# Commentary on Husserl, *Zur Phänomenologie der Intersubjektivität. Zweiter Teil: 1921–1928* (Hua XIV)

The volume collects research manuscripts, not a treatise, and Husserl often overrules himself in the margin; those later notes belong to the argument and are treated with the unit they qualify. Several misreadings recur in the reception and are guarded against throughout. Empathy (Einfühlung) is neither Lipps's feeling oneself into an expression nor sympathy: it names the experience in which another subject is given as such at all. The likeness between the other's body and mine does not operate as an inference; it is a passive transfer of sense, and Husserl attacks the argument from analogy directly (Nr. 12, Appendix LXVII). The "solipsistic" or "original" sphere is an abstractive stratum reached by reduction inside an already intersubjective experience, not an earlier phase of life and not a Cartesian private theatre. "Monad" means the concrete transcendental ego with its stream, habitualities, and surrounding world; Husserl takes Leibniz's word and rejects windowlessness (Nr. 13 §4). Constitution is not production: the other is constituted in me precisely as what is not mine. The distinction between lived body (Leib) and physical body (Körper) carries the whole volume, whose central puzzle is how my lived body, given to me from within as no thing is, comes to count as a physical thing among others. Finally, original givenness of the other is not perception of his inner life; what is originally given is that life's inaccessibility to me.

Leib is rendered "lived body", Körper "physical body", Leibkörper "bodily thing", Vergegenwärtigung "presentification", Appräsentation "appresentation", Umfiktion and Umfingieren "re-imagining", and the editor's Beilagen "Appendices". Latin and Greek terms Husserl uses (alter ego, solus ipse, ego cogito) are kept. Page numbers in parentheses are those of Hua XIV.

## Editor's Introduction

(XVII) Kern presents the volume as the continuation of Hua XIII. It rests mostly on Husserl's research manuscripts, characterized in the introduction to that volume, and follows the same principles of selection and arrangement. The reader therefore meets written monologues that correct themselves as they go, not drafts for print.

(XVII) The texts continue the chronological series of 1905–1920 into the years 1921–1928, two dates that mark turns in Husserl's work. In 1921 he began preparing a "great systematic work", which replaced the plan to continue Ideen; 1928 is the year of his retirement and essentially closes his teaching. Kern's note documents the abandonment of Ideen II and III. When Landgrebe revised and typed Edith Stein's 1916–18 compilation in 1924/25, Husserl regarded those texts only as "material", and he considered publishing parts of Ideen II only briefly, as a letter to Ingarden of December 1927 shows. The intersubjectivity manuscripts of the 1920s occupy the place the unpublished Ideen II was meant to fill, which is why Ideen II is the constant backdrop of Section I.

### I.

(XVIII–XIX) Section I comes from the preparations for the systematic work. In November 1921 Husserl told Ingarden he had spent months working through his "all too large manuscripts" and was planning a work that, building from the bottom up, could serve as phenomenology's foundational work; he had kept the summer semester of 1921 light for it. Kern reconstructs how the plan arose. In autumn 1920 Husserl sent the Sixth Logical Investigation to press almost unchanged, dropping the revisions of 1913–14 and leaving open whether his new insights would appear as a recast Investigation or as a new book. In winter 1920/21 he lectured on logic, which he called "in truth transcendental logic, the most general theory of constitution, starting from a theory of original time-consciousness, theory of judgment, etc."; the course, repeated in 1923 and 1925/26, is partly printed in Hua XI and underlies Experience and Judgment. Kern conjectures that this transcendental logic convinced Husserl that his frame had to widen into a systematic foundational work. The genetic theory of passive synthesis and the intersubjectivity texts of this volume thus belong to one project, which is why Nr. 2 and Appendix I treat genesis and individuation as problems of the monad.

(XIX–XX) For about a year Husserl's work stands under this plan, with an especially creative phase at St. Märgen in the Black Forest from 31 July to 26 October 1921; he always regarded the "St. Märgen manuscripts" as fundamental. By spring 1922 his confidence faded. On 1 February 1922 he wrote to Natorp that most of his work lay in manuscripts, that he almost cursed his inability to bring things to a finish, that the universal systematic thoughts had come only now and forced him to rework all his special studies, "everything in the stage of recrystallization", and that he might be working only for his Nachlass. An invitation to lecture in London (6–12 June 1922, "Phenomenological Method and Phenomenological Philosophy") displaced the plan. Afterwards Husserl meant to expand those lectures into a new introduction, the course "Introduction to Philosophy" of 1922/23, deepened in "First Philosophy" of 1923/24 (Hua VII–VIII). This narrowed the 1921 plan to a systematic introduction followed by thematically bounded studies, announced in letters to Ingarden and Daubert as meditationes de prima philosophia. Only around 1930 did Husserl again pursue a plan of the 1921 scale, again without success (Hua XV). The Cartesian way of First Philosophy is thus a retreat from the monadological system sketched in Section I, not its fulfilment.

(XX–XXI) The preparations of 1921/22 are extensive but contain no piece worked out for publication. Kern explains this by Husserl's method. Rather than draft a plan and execute it piece by piece, he tried in written "monologues" to master the whole subject in thought, hoping then to write the presentation quickly. Husserl told Albrecht in 1931 that the system had first to be finished "in the head"; the logic book had been written in about two months, Ideen in six to eight weeks. What survives from 1921/22 are "meditations" aimed at the system but meant only as preparations. To Albrecht, on 1 September 1922, he described the year as "a time of great reflections" in which he thought through "for a last time" the basic ideas and guidelines of phenomenology. The self-corrections in these texts are therefore part of their content, not lapses in a draft.

(XXI) During the summer semester of 1921 Husserl reread and revised his 1910/11 lectures "Basic Problems of Phenomenology", which had extended phenomenology to intersubjectivity, and added several Appendices to them (Hua XIII, Appendices XXVII, XXVIII, XXX). Their result, a phenomenological plurality of monads in empathic relation, seems to have framed the 1921 studies. The earliest precisely dated texts (Nr. 1, Appendices I–IV) aim at a phenomenological monadology, and the long Nr. 13 of January/February 1922, which surveys the systematic horizon once more as if in conclusion, treats "absolute monadology as an extension of transcendental egology". Kern locates the main significance of these manuscripts here: Husserl tried to think the idea of monadology through consistently by reflecting on the connections of consciousness. The monadology is a result of descriptive reflection, not a metaphysical premise.

(XXI–XXII) Kern documents the horizon with three quotations. Empathy creates the first true transcendence: a second stream is co-posited, indicated rather than formed by mine, and here consciousness first really goes beyond itself (Nr. 1, 8f.). Every ego is a "monad", but monads have windows, although no other subject can enter really (reell) (Nr. 13, 260). Absolutely speaking there is only the ego and its life, connected with other egos through the nature each constitutes and through confirmed sense-givings that lead to an identity of sense and being; the in-itself of nature has its sense in this intentional identity; absolutely there is nothing but spirit and no connection but a spiritual one, and independent absolute "substances" can be connected only without abolishing their independence (Hua XIII, 232f.). Within this horizon Husserl considers the concept of monad (Appendices II–IV); principles of genesis and individuation (Nr. 2, Appendix I; other genesis texts remain in Ms. B III 10, one printed in Hua XI, 336ff.); separate subjects with separate worlds and the conditions of coexistence (Nr. 4); intersubjective objectivity (Nr. 5); empathy with "anomalous" subjects (Nr. 6); psychophysical dependence (Nr. 3); and the conceivability of other egos through re-imagining my own (Nr. 7–8). The three quotations already state the double thesis the volume's monadology must hold together: no real entry of one subject into another, and yet a real connection through a common nature.

(XXII–XXIII) Foreign experience as such is analysed less extensively than in 1914–15 (Hua XIII, Nr. 8–13), though it plays the decisive role throughout. Among the empathy texts of 1921/22, Nr. 12 is the most comprehensive and takes a position on Husserl's earlier reflections. Nr. 11 is also important: it calls the experience of a human being in my surroundings originary, although the other's subjectivity is "not originally given"; later Husserl speaks of "original interpretation" (Hua VIII, 495) and "original manifestation" (352, 355). The concept of originality (original perception, original sphere), which Husserl distinguishes from originarity, grows steadily in importance. Another new contribution is the reflection on empathy's distinctive kind of confirmation (249, 284). Kern's distinction is the key to the volume's vocabulary: "originary" (originär) qualifies givenness in person, which the other human being can have; "original" (original) qualifies what belongs to my own sphere of experience, which the other's life can never do.

(XXIII) The texts Husserl grouped under the title "communal spirit" (Gemeingeist; Nr. 9–10 with Appendices) deserve particular attention. In them he approaches basic sociological concepts on which Tönnies, Simmel, and Max Weber were working, studying in detail what Nr. 13 calls the various possibilities of connecting monads by connecting their ego-subjects (270). The central notions are the social act, which Husserl distinguishes from mere empathy and mere understanding, and the supra-individual "personal unity of higher order", worked out far more precisely than in Ideen II (§51). The social act turns coexistence into community, and these texts supply what Ideen II only announced.

(XXIII–XXIV) Section I does not include all the manuscripts of 1921/22, which are scattered across convolutes, mostly undated, and often hard to identify; even among the securely dated ones Kern selected by the theme of intersubjectivity. That theme largely coincides with the monadological thread of the period, but some texts belong to other volumes. Kern lists smaller pieces on representing and acting, on hexis and person, on transcendental idealism, on the closure of physical nature, and on the ego of capacities, and above all a closed group from October 1921, the last weeks at St. Märgen, on "the constitution of space" and on the "pregivenness" of objectively existing objects and their constitution as identical and in itself, all within the methodical frame of the "solitary monad". The solitary-monad analyses of space are the background against which the 1927 texts (Nr. 33–36) reopen space constitution for empathy, so the volume shows only one side of a two-sided inquiry.

### II.

(XXIV–XXV) Section II gathers texts written after 1921/22 and before winter 1926/27. Husserl first worked from the London lectures toward a new systematic introduction through the courses of 1922/23 and 1923/24. Letters to Ingarden show him still at it at the end of 1924; he probably dropped it early in 1925, having meanwhile also worked up his Kant lecture of May 1924 (Hua VII, 230ff.). The summer course of 1925, "Introduction to Phenomenological Psychology" (Hua IX), absorbed him entirely. These three were the only wholly new courses between summer 1922 and summer 1926; the rest repeated earlier courses on logic, ethics, and the history of philosophy. From spring 1926 Husserl again planned a new systematic work. Section II therefore covers a gap in his sustained work on intersubjectivity rather than a phase of its own.

(XXV–XXVI) The idea of monadology stays present. First Philosophy ends with the sentence that phenomenology leads to the monadology Leibniz anticipated "in a brilliant aperçu" (Hua VIII, 190), and the 1925 psychology course points to a phenomenology of intersubjectivity extending to "the totality of monads" (Hua IX, 216). Several research manuscripts of the period sketch a phenomenological monadology (Hua VIII, Appendix XXXI; Hua IX, Appendix XXV; here Nr. 18 with its Appendices). But there are no extensive studies of intersubjectivity comparable to those of 1921/22 and of January–February 1927, so Section II offers only single shorter texts. Nr. 14, perhaps from the end of the 1922/23 course, briefly discusses the intersubjective validity of phenomenological truth. Nr. 15 (1923 and later) resumes the Bernau problems of September 1918 (Hua XIII, Nr. 15), the relation of empathic experience and self-experience in the apperception of body and soul, and also clarifies kinds of thematizing the other. Nr. 16 and its Appendices test, through imaginative reflections on "ghost appearances" probably prompted by seminar discussions, Husserl's thesis, opposed to Scheler, that foreign subjectivity can be posited only on the basis of experiencing an external body. Nr. 17 and 18 sketch in excellent formulations his idealist monadology: the connection of monads in their independence and their mutual "unexperienceability" in "original manifestation". Nr. 19 groups texts on originality, a concept fundamental to Husserl's problem of intersubjectivity; they show the concept wavering and lead to "primordinal originality", which becomes "primordiality" in the Cartesian Meditations. The opposition to Scheler fixes a thesis that runs through the volume: the other is given only through bodily expression, never through an immediate perception of mind, which makes the ghost thought experiment a test of principle.

### III.

(XXVII–XXVIII) Section III contains texts from the second part, from January 1927 onward, of the four-hour course "Introduction to Phenomenology" of winter 1926/27. Kern stresses its novelty against the courses of 1922/23 and 1923/24. It does not begin with the idea of apodictic knowledge and the reduction to the apodictic ego cogito, but with the idea of universal science; given the unclarity of the sciences' basic concepts, it reflects on what precedes all science, the world of pure experience. This ground is analysed structurally "in the epoché regarding all sciences". Natural (purely physical) and purely psychic experience are set against each other, and as counterpart to natural science the idea of a pure psychology is developed, which excludes all nature and rests on pure self-experience and experience of others. Nature and spirit first appear as mere opposites. The further course cannot be reconstructed unambiguously, since there is no continuous manuscript, only many separate meditations (Nr. 22–37). Husserl perhaps meant to overcome the worldly opposition of nature and spirit in transcendental consciousness and to present transcendental phenomenology as the realization of the idea of philosophy as universal science; at any rate he introduced the transcendental reduction through the psychophysical problem and the return to "original experience" (Nr. 26), and then analysed the experience of others at length. Kern here applies the distinction his 1962 essay on the three ways to the reduction made explicit: the Cartesian way through apodicticity, and the ways through the ontology of the world of experience and through psychology.

(XXVIII) The course as a whole, with its return to the structures of the pure world of experience motivated by the unclarity of the sciences and its route to transcendental phenomenology through pure psychology and the problem of nature and spirit, recalls Husserl's last work, the Crisis (Hua VI). Kern's comparison makes the Crisis way not a late innovation but the descendant of a 1926/27 prototype that already contains the full theory of empathy.

(XXVIII–XXX) After abandoning the introductory work planned from the London lectures, Husserl seems to have conceived this new kind of introduction around 1925 or 1926. In 1926 he was again occupied with a new work: many manuscripts carry the note "for elaboration 1926", and on 16 April 1926 Malvine Husserl wrote that after thirteen years of effort he had begun on 8 April, his birthday, the definitive literary version and hoped to have the first part printed by autumn. It did not happen. Husserl found again that the greatest difficulties gather around the problem of an "introduction", a natural beginning and ascent from the pre-philosophical standpoint, and ill health hindered him too. The 1926 manuscripts, which Kern lists, show his attempt to open the way into phenomenology through a structural analysis, an ontology, of the "pure world of experience" or "natural concept of the world", the life-world of the Crisis. An old envelope in which Husserl gathered convolutes "for elaboration" outlines the plan: first, empathy, alter ego, a second phenomenological reduction (of intersubjectivity) from 1922, and ghost appearances; second, memory and empathy, reproduction and phantasy in relation to position-takings; third, phenomenology and ontology, the new and the older conception of transcendental guiding clues; fourth, the idea of a transcendental aesthetic and the natural concept of the world. In red pencil Husserl referred to the 1910/11 lectures and their October 1910 preparation, where he had extended the reduction to intersubjectivity. Kern dates the envelope by elimination to 1926/27. Empathy stands first in a plan whose goal is a transcendental aesthetic of the experienced world, so the 1927 analyses of foreign experience belong to the theory of the life-world, not to a separate chapter on other minds.

(XXX–XXXI) The 1926/27 course matches these preparations. It is that year's most systematic attempt at the way into phenomenology through the ontology of the world of experience, and in its second part it develops at length the intersubjective dimension that, judging by the envelope, Husserl meant to stress in the planned work. Overall it links the 1910/11 lectures, which also started from the natural concept of the world and led phenomenology into intersubjectivity, with the Crisis. Kern calls this line a quite "special Husserl" that can hardly be harmonized with the "Cartesian Husserl" of Ideen I or the Cartesian Meditations. On this thesis, Section III is not a set of preliminary studies for the Fifth Meditation but part of a different way into phenomenology.

(XXXI–XXXII) The 1926 plan was not realized either. The new summer course of 1927, "Nature and Spirit", still follows it, starting from the classification of the sciences and demanding an analysis of the world of experience. From mid-1927 other tasks took over: the Encyclopaedia Britannica article, then the Amsterdam lectures (Hua IX). Only in winter 1928/29 did a systematic work succeed, Formal and Transcendental Logic. Its ontological and science-theoretical perspective and its lack of any Cartesian motivation bring it close to the 1926 plan, but it was not that plan's realization: it was written as an introduction to Landgrebe's compilation of older logic manuscripts. How much Husserl suffered from the repeated failure of his publication plans in the sixteen years between Ideen I and this work appears in a letter to Pfänder of 6 January 1931. Trying to improve the draft of the second and third parts of Ideen from 1912, he says, he became entangled in new and extremely comprehensive investigations: the phenomenology of the person and of personalities of higher order, of culture and the human surrounding world; the transcendental phenomenology of empathy and the theory of transcendental intersubjectivity; "transcendental aesthetic" as phenomenology of the world purely as world of experience; time and individuation; the phenomenology of association as theory of the constitutive achievements of passivity; the phenomenology of the logos; the phenomenological problems of "metaphysics". The manuscripts grew almost unmanageable, and the work brought setbacks, depressions, and a dangerously sunken self-confidence. Husserl's list maps the contents of this volume almost item by item: person and higher personalities (Nr. 9–10), empathy and transcendental intersubjectivity throughout, time and individuation (Nr. 2, Appendix I), metaphysics (Nr. 13).

(XXXII–XXXIII) The manuscripts for the second part of the course begin with an overview, given after the Christmas break, of the first part, which had led to the "method of pure self-experience" for a pure psychology (Nr. 20). From there Husserl passes to the "method of purely subjective experience of others" and develops the idea of a pure psychology of intersubjectivity (Nr. 21). After this the course is no longer clearly traceable, because Husserl wrote no continuous lecture manuscript. Instead there are many single texts, scattered through convolutes and mostly written as personal meditations rather than for delivery; several are dated to the day and marked "preparation", so the course can be roughly reconstructed. Husserl also demonstrably drew on older manuscripts on empathy. He seems to have reached problems on which he could not at once write a continuous text for delivery and had first to clarify them in free reflections. Section III therefore has the same monologic character as Section I, though it was written under the pressure of a running course.

(XXXIII) After setting out a pure psychology that includes intersubjectivity as counterpart of the science of mere nature, Husserl probably turned to the relation of the psychic and the natural, the "psychophysical nexus". He studies it in "original experience", the "original sphere", excluding all appresentative moments of sense that psychophysical experience owes to empathy. He develops anew, as already in 1918 (Hua XIII, Nr. 15), the thought that self-apperception as the objective psychophysical unity "human being" rests on empathy (Nr. 22) and is not an "original" apperception. Having studied the psychophysical in original experience and subjected that experience to the transcendental reduction (Nr. 23–26), he set the direction of the remaining lectures by a question: what does the motivation look like which, within original experience, drives forth presentifications serving to constitute new present objects whose essential determinations cannot be originally perceived, the determinations of the "other" (474)? All further preparations to the end of February are guided by this question, whose sense, whether a question of genesis or of static analysis, is itself under discussion (Nr. 27, Appendices LXII–LXIII). The 1927 problem is the one Nr. 1 opened: my own humanity is derivative, the reduction to the original sphere makes that visible, and empathy must explain it.

(XXXIII–XXXV) These are Husserl's most thorough reflections on the experience of others, the "perception of a human being", since 1914–15, and in them he brings his problem, posed from a quite particular standpoint, to a certain solution. When Formal and Transcendental Logic (1929) says that the main points for solving the problem of intersubjectivity and transcendental solipsism had been developed in the 1910/11 lectures, but that the "difficult detailed investigations" were completed much later, this completion may be seen in the studies of January and February 1927. Here Husserl's theory of empathy first takes a closed and thoroughly worked-out form that his later treatments do not essentially surpass; the Cartesian Meditations fall far short of the 1927 reflections in penetration and depth regarding the perception of others. Working constantly against his earlier texts, Husserl analyses the perception of others in the realization of its possible fulfilment. He shows that it cannot be an "apperceptive supplementation" ("direct appresentation") but only a mediate appresentation (Nr. 29, Appendices LXVII–LXVIII), and he grounds it constitutively in "pairing association" (Nr. 35). The greatest achievement of these reflections is to solve a long-standing problem: how the likeness between my own body and an external body, on which association depends, is constitutively mediated despite the difference in principle between their possible modes of appearance. The answer lies in an analysis of space constitution, specifically in the constitutive reference of every external distance and movement back to my own "kinaesthetic movement" (Nr. 36). Recourse to an imagined external picture of my own body ("moving it out there") becomes superfluous. Kern's verdict sets the volume's arc, from empathy as analogy grounded in resemblance (Nr. 1) to the grounding of that resemblance in kinaesthetic space, and ranks the 1927 texts above the Fifth Meditation as the place where the theory is worked out.

(XXXV) Kern closes by thanking H. L. Van Breda, director of the Husserl Archives in Leuven and of the edition; Ludwig Landgrebe, for information about Husserl's work plans in the 1920s; Rudolf Bernet, for correcting the proofs of the critical apparatus; and Eduard Marbach, for checking all proofs against the originals and for help with manuscripts, the presentation of the texts, and their dating. Landgrebe, Husserl's assistant in exactly these years, is the source of much of the work history reconstructed above.

## I. Texts from the Preparations for a "Great Systematic Work" (Spring 1921 to Spring 1922)

### No. 1. The Apperception of One's Own Body as the Original Bodily Apperception and the Mediacy of the Apperception of the Other's Body. The Constitution of the First True Transcendence through Empathy (Summer Semester 1921)

(3) Husserl starts from the result to be analysed. An external body is apprehended as a lived body, not merely as a physical body, because it typically resembles mine. Events at it correspond to events at the outside of my own body; since those have a counterpart in my interiority, the body over there calls for a corresponding interiority. The formulation already contains the volume's problem: the resemblance holds between the other's body seen from outside and my body seen from outside, yet my body is primarily given to me from within. Kern's note dates the text. Husserl wrote it in summer 1921 while rereading his 1910/11 lectures on the "extended reduction" and empathy (Hua XIII, Nr. 6), not for a course but for a planned systematic work, and he reread it in 1927 for the second part of the lectures *Introduction to Phenomenology*, whose materials form Section III of this volume.

(3–4) Like every apperception, empathy is a construal that clings to something impressionally given and goes beyond it, and it realizes itself in regulated series of intuitions. For a thing, the intentions that go beyond are expectations that can turn into perceptions. For empathy, the empty intention can be "fulfilled" only by presentifications: I can make the other's interiority more or less intuitive to myself, but never live through it as an experience of the type "interiority". Husserl at once doubts this use of "fulfilment". A perceptual apperception is fulfilled only by further perceptions, and presentifications count only as annexes of perceptions. He therefore relocates fulfilment. The other's body is perceived as a lived body and as a human being; his outward conduct, continually understood through the interiority that belongs to it, confirms expectations and arouses new ones. The interiority itself is demanded as co-present, bound in coexistence to the body, but not as anything perception could give. That is the specific trait of empathy. The demand arises because my own outward body is constantly bound associatively to impressional interiority; the foreign exterior shares the phenomenal type of mine, so the matching interiority is co-posited. Not every co-demand, then, is a demand for perceivability. The distinction between what is demanded and what is perceivable becomes the formula of the Fifth Cartesian Meditation: the other is given as the confirmable accessibility of what is originally inaccessible.

(4–5) Husserl tests the distinction against two neighbouring cases. Memories demand earlier and later memories, but these refer back to what I once perceived; empathy refers to something co-present. External perception also demands something co-present, the unseen side of a thing. That side is perceivable later, or earlier if it has not changed, but not as it is now, since whatever is perceivable is reachable only through a process. The difference from the thing thus narrows to one point. The same real thing is perceivable in another phase of its time, and its present determinations, accessible now only by presentification, stand under a valid counterfactual: had I moved freely in such and such a way, I would have had impressionally the very determination I now presentify. The other's co-present life admits no such counterfactual. It cannot be thought as something that could ever have been, or could ever be, given to me perceptually, for then it would be mine. The "if I had moved there" that grounds the thing's co-presence returns at the end of this text and, in 1927, as the "as if I were there" (Nr. 30); Husserl here establishes that it yields the other's place and view, never his interiority.

(5–6) Empathy therefore consists of analogical demands: motivated within my interiority, they run toward an analogue of it that does not and could not lie within it. Whatever lies within mine is given impressionally, or in memory as past, or in expectation as coming through impression. Much remains unsettled inside my own sphere: connections between what appears bodily and my own mental life still have to be found out empirically. The child already has the bodily phenomenon but does not yet know that a certain food upset its stomach. Yet all such connections link items already experienced as mine, as what I do and what affects me; the gap is empirical, not a gap in givenness. A further mass of knowledge comes only by taking over others' experience and knowledge, which indicates possible experiences this ego has never had. Husserl brackets that layer because it presupposes empathy and cannot explain it.

(6) The connections to be instituted, like those already instituted, therefore attach to my actual stream and to my own body, not as an arbitrary thing but in the peculiar modes of givenness Husserl calls the body "in inner attitude" (Inneneinstellung). My body is a subjective-objective formation of its own kind. It is not an in-itself in the sense of an intuitive spatial thing, even one constituted solipsistically; it resembles things but is set apart, and firmly bounded, by the modes of appearance it admits. The physical body it contains arises only in a further, supervening objectivation that requires the lived body to be equated fully with other things in the nexus of physical causality. The physical body is therefore not co-constituted with the givenness of the lived body. Husserl marks this as a task for constitutive analysis; it becomes the 1927 problem of how the null-body is levelled with external bodies (Nr. 33, Appendix LXX).

(6) Every foreign body, by contrast, is first given in exteriority, born as an external thing, and must then be translated: it undergoes a "second birth" in being apprehended as a lived body, constituted in interiority, carrying a whole conscious interiority and an ego, and so completed into an animal or human being. The two births fix an order of founding, which the end of the text will qualify. So many bodies, so many separate human beings and streams, each exterior to every other, each related to the same world.

(6–7) For mere physical things the order of constitution is indifferent. Since some thing must be constituted first in the knowing ego, any one could have been first; once one is apperceived, every other follows by analogical transfer. Husserl states the thing case to set up the asymmetry that follows.

(7) Bodily apperception differs in principle. The original givenness of a lived body can only be the givenness of mine. The apperception "my body" is essentially first and the only fully original one. Every other body is apperceived mediately, since its sense, a co-presentified body in inner attitude, presupposes that mine has already been apperceived. Every other body, like every other ego and inner life, refers back to mine as the only one given in the original in the most actual and final sense. The other still has a certain originality: his physical body is given originally, and empathic co-presentification creates a peculiar apperception through which something like a human being reaches the only givenness possible for it in principle. Husserl then reverses the direction. I am not given to myself originally as a human being but as the "I am" that has its body in a wonderful way of its own, as functioning centre of its surroundings. Once I have posited others as human beings, essential law leads me to apprehend myself as one too, under the aspect others must have of me. My humanity is a derivative self-apperception borrowed from the other's view, a thesis worked out in Nr. 26 and Appendix LIX as the "secondary self-apperception I-human-being".

(7–8) Husserl now places empathy in a stratified account of reality. A physical thing is constituted as an intentional unity of aspects. Aspects are constituted only through associatively arising expectations of the form "if this, then that", which are fulfilled in immanent perceptual data of a single ego-stream. The fulfilment is always provisional, because the expectation series that constitute aspects and all-sided phantoms imply an infinite further intention. The reality first constituted, in the ideal order, is therefore solipsistic and rests wholly on the rule of the solipsistic stream. Intersubjective reality, objectivity for "everyone", is constituted only through empathy. The solipsistic thing is the abstract stratum Ideen II had isolated (Hua IV, §18).

(8) The constitution of an animal or a human being is again the constitution of a reality. In the ideally first order, the solus ipse's own body and material surroundings are constituted solipsistically, each as a mere rule for immanent connections in his stream. When a "foreign body" appears among these solipsistic things, a wholly new kind of transcendence enters. All earlier transcendences were intentional unities of manifolds falling entirely within my stream. They were not experiences, yet they belonged to immanent manifolds and were only units of sense with a rule of rational validity. The solipsistic world was in this sense an "immanent" world: my formation of sense, posited in my appearances and rationally confirmable by me, not leading beyond me. The thing's transcendence counts as immanent not because things are experiences but because their sense and legitimacy are exhausted by rules in my stream.

(8) Empathy creates the first true transcendence. On an immanently instituted world that includes a body of the type "foreign lived body", a reality of a new kind is founded. Its upper stratum co-posits a second lived body, given as I have mine from within (my "primal body"); a second oriented world of appearances; an appearance of the very world that was given to me solipsistically; and second constitutive manifolds with a second ego acting and suffering. What is co-posited is not a second world but a second appearance of the same one, which already sets the identification problem of the next units.

(8–9) A second stream of consciousness is co-posited, not as a mere formation of sense in my stream but as something only indicated by my formation of sense and its legitimation. In that second stream a world is constituted solipsistically, in the constitution indicated to me, and the very sense of the indication requires that this world be identified with the world constituted "in me". Husserl shifts from constitution to indication: my stream does not produce the other stream but points to it, and what it points to has a constitutive life of its own.

(9) Here consciousness for the first time really goes beyond itself, and what it reaches is the other ego and his consciousness. With this, every rule-governed constitution of thing-realities in my sphere also gives a rule for constituting matching, identical realities in the empathized sphere, and conversely, so that in the "normal" case the regulated manifold is exactly the same. When I put myself in the other's place I find "the same" that he finds when he puts himself in mine. Reciprocity arrives with transcendence: the other's world is from the first a rule for mine, and normality, which Nr. 6 and Appendix XII will put in question, is defined as this matching.

(9) What motivates the identification? Husserl finds the source in the other's body, which is two things at once: for me an external body in my world of things, for the other the primal body given to him alone and empathized by me. That primal body centres an oriented surrounding world, which as empathized is an inner view and for me is the surroundings of the external foreign body as given in my orientation. The coincidence depends on how empathy gains its content in relation to my body and to my inner views of "I move here and there": I can transfer myself mentally to any place, and each place has its own aspect of the world. The other's body is the hinge where my outer view and his inner view of the same surroundings meet, and my free possibility of moving there supplies the bridge.

(9) The foreign body cannot be grasped as a lived body, cannot acquire its own primal mode of appearance, unless I understand it as the body that is there and that, "seen from within", looks much as mine would look from within if I had moved there. To that inner look belongs the surrounding world as seen from there, indicated by the foreign body just as it would be by my body functioning perceptually from there. The other is understood as an embodied "there" I could occupy; the 1927 texts ask whether this requires picturing my own body out there at all (Nr. 34, Nr. 36).

(9–10) Husserl declares the account provisional: the whole systematic order of motivations, and the necessary formation of one sense to which I and the other both have access, must be worked out anew. One constraint stands. I cannot have the other subject before having the other's body, and I can have that body only in an act that grasps in one the other ego living in this body, looking out through it, surrounded by these things. Hardly any stages can be found here. Only in the order of regard is anything first: the inner body with its oriented aspect of the world from there. This is what is "experienced", "beheld", and it co-presents an ego and an ego-life that are grasped only by a reflection carried out "in" the empathic presentification. The other's ego is reached as my own is, by reflection, but inside a presentification. Husserl withdraws the sequence that the stratified account of the preceding paragraphs had suggested.

(10) The conclusion corrects the construction outright. It is not that I first constitute my things and world solipsistically, then empathically grasp the other ego as constituting its own world, and only then identify the two. Because the indicated foreign manifold is not separated from mine, my unit of sense is eo ipso the same as his empathized one. Only when I consider the other as a constituting ego for himself do I find the formation of sense as constituted by him and ascribe it to his immanence. The identity of the world comes first; its distribution into two constitutive achievements comes from a later reflection. Husserl's footnote, "But here is the problem", concedes that he has stated the result without showing how a manifold not separated from mine can still be another's. The "windows" of Nr. 13 and the 1927 concept of pairing are later attacks on exactly this point.

### No. 2. The Personal Ego and Individual Character. The Problem of Genesis and of Determinacy in Genesis: How Can a Monadic Subject Be Univocally Determined and Knowable? (St. Märgen, 1920 or 1921)

(11) Husserl's own table of contents frames the text. A monad is a unity of genesis, a unity filling time through which a nexus of necessity runs, the unity of a development with one monadic ego. The question how such a subjectivity can be determined leads to "sufficient reason" as the mode of cognition proper to individuals, to the ego's freedom and individuality, to rational and irrational regulations, to the personal ego as principle of intelligibility, and to the contrast between the inner manifestation of individuality and its outer knowledge, by empathy or by association and induction. An inserted elementary account sets the ego-pole against the object-pole and treats the ego as functional centre; the end considers re-imaginings of my subjectivity that leave the functioning ego intact and those that do not, and the problem of the double. The text belongs in a volume on intersubjectivity because the individuation of egos is the condition for there being several.

(12) The opening principle, set off by spacing, is Leibnizian: that something exists prescribes in each case a domain of compossibility, the totality of what can or cannot exist along with it. Existence is never a bare fact but a constraint on what else can be. The principle governs the later deduction that there can be only one world and one time (Nr. 4) and the thesis that monadic possibilities are relative to monadic actualities (Appendix XXI).

(12) Every individual being is either a concrete individual or a dependent moment, part, or property of one. Every concrete individual endures, and it is what it is by passing continuously from one presence to the next. It thus has a being in states, and as a continuous series of states stretched over a duration it has its temporal essence: the duration concretely filled with its states. Individuality is defined through filled duration, which prepares the contrast between things and monads in how their durations cohere.

(12) A filled duration can be divided into temporal pieces, but it does not follow that the pieces could count as concrete individuals, or that they could be the filled durations of independent individuals. Temporal divisibility is not ontological separability: a stretch of a life is an abstract part of it.

(12) For a subject, a concrete monad, the unity of being in becoming is the unity of a development, genesis in a special sense. The content of a later stretch, and of each phase in its place, cannot be thought without the earlier ones. An apperception arising at a given point is motivated there and nowhere else and bears the inseparable mark of a genesis. This is the volume's first statement of the genetic principle that every sense carries the history of its institution, developed in the same years in the lectures on passive synthesis (Hua XI).

(12) Hyletic data, taken apart from their apperceptive function, behave otherwise, and so does a physical thing with respect to its perceptual fill of duration in objective time. The pure visual thing does not, by its content, point back to an earlier state, to a history. The physical thing as real, with real properties, does point to other actual or possible things, but its properties are causal: they refer outward to circumstances, not backward to its own past. Husserl separates two ways of reaching beyond the present, the genetic, internal to monads, and the causal, external among things.

(12–13) For any individual, or any stretch of its duration, one can ask what a priori conditions make a filled duration possible. There cannot, for example, be infinitely many discontinuities; the filling of time is not free of all constraint, and every individual must satisfy these conditions. Husserl's note limits their reach: heterogeneous contents prescribe nothing to each other in coexistence, and one filled duration prescribes nothing at all to another in succession. These are formal laws of time-filling, not laws of what fills it.

(13) Beyond these there are essential laws of a wholly different kind, governing individual becoming and the connection between earlier and later in the filled duration, such as the essential laws of monadic genesis already glimpsed above. Husserl separates the formal conditions of any filled time from laws that tie a monad's phases together by their content.

(13) Causal laws for things might seem to belong to the second kind; Husserl shows they do not. If we had the individual essence of a physical thing, we would have its properties and its states as states of those properties, and the causal laws would give the whole system of its possible causality toward other things, apart from the counter-things that fix its present causality. To be a point of reference for such real possibilities belongs to a thing's "essence". But this essence is not given with the single thing and what lies in its filled duration. What the thing contains as intuitable essence includes no causality demanded by its temporally distributed contents, no necessity running from earlier to later. Where causal necessities connect parts of a thing, no essential connection is there to carry the conditioning. Causal connection is not rational and causal necessity is not insightful. Husserl grants Hume's "old observation" for nature in order to claim, for the monad alone, an intelligible necessity of a new kind.

(13–14) In a monad a nexus of necessity runs through the whole filling of time as a nexus of genesis. How far does it reach, and is the determination of the later content by the earlier, interwoven with the essential relations among coexisting dependent contents at each moment, univocal or capable of being so? Genesis is necessary; whether it determines is the question of the whole text.

(14) Every monad contains the unity of an ego, identical over the whole duration together with everything egoic, and an ego-alien sphere that is nevertheless "subjective": a domain of hyletic objects extended through immanent time, and possibly a domain of transcendent objects presenting themselves in these. Past hyle prescribes no essential necessity to future hyle. Hyle comes contingently, subject only to the most general laws of continuous time-filling, and every anticipation developed in the monad is legitimate only as presumptive and carries by essence a horizon of possible non-arrival. The monad's passive sphere is as unpredictable as nature; whatever determinacy the monad has must lie elsewhere.

(14) The guiding question is stated with spaced emphasis: how can a monadic subject, taken in full concretion, in its essentially correlated contents of egoic life and ego-alien surroundings, be univocally determined and knowable in its full determinacy?

(14) As far as the "inner" or "own essence" of an individual goes, everything about it that can be given intuitively, specified, and grasped eidetically, no individual's essence prescribes its future becoming univocally and with rational necessity. It is even open whether it prescribes anything for the future. Eidetic insight reaches only generality, and generality underdetermines the individual.

(14–15) It must first be investigated whether a monadic individual can cease, or whether continued becoming in infinitum is prescribed by its inner essence, and likewise whether a monad can begin. For a thing it is evident that its inner essence, the continuum of its momentary phantoms or its temporally extended phantom, prescribes nothing for the future from any present or past. For a monad, too, the future is certainly not univocally determined in content by the past, since the essential laws governing coexistence at each moment leave the lowest differences of what coexists mutually open. The questions of beginning and end, raised in passing, become in 1922 the thesis that the ego can neither arise nor perish (Appendix XX).

#### Sufficient Reason

(15–16) Must there not be a sufficient reason why an individual, existing in becoming, becomes just so and not otherwise as it moves into the future, and must that reason not make the future knowable in advance from any past? The demand is made of the beginning too, and for an individual without beginning it would remain for the infinite fact that just this duration has elapsed and will continue just so. Husserl reinterprets the principle. "Sufficient reason" is the correlate of a mode of cognition in which the indeterminate generality attaching to every eidetic cognition of the singular is supplemented until the singular is determined as an individual: the cognition proper to the individual as individual, in which what fully individuates comes to givenness. An individual is necessarily given as being in becoming, within an already filled stretch, however small, and with an open future horizon prefigured by the past in an essential style, whose determinate fulfilment awaits determining perception. Breaking off may be among the open possibilities, yet a positing runs toward the future through all indeterminacy. That one of the possibilities "must come", though the knower cannot say on essential grounds which, is absolutely certain. Hence the idea of an indeterminate but certain future, and of an individual closed in itself, determinate over its whole time but unknown, belongs to the sense of individual experience. Leibniz's principle survives as a structure of experience: every individual is experienced as fully determinate in itself, not as foreseeable.

(16) Does it follow that an individual must be knowable in advance, while still becoming, in its whole duration? And, along a different line, that it must be knowable as existing in itself as against the experience that happens to be had of it? Husserl separates predictability from being-in-itself so that denying the first will not force denying the second.

(16) Recourse to cognition is unavoidable. Every individual refers us back to present, past, and future and is necessarily a present one, and nothing determines the present but subjectivity. The subject exists in becoming and constitutes in itself its own presence, its own past, and the open future toward which it is directed as a horizon. Only because it constitutes presence in itself, and finds itself present with a past that was present, can other presents, pasts, and futures be given to it, receiving their original sense from it and its presence. The question of an individual's determinacy thus leads back to the subject's temporal self-constitution, the theme of Hua X.

(16) The first task is therefore the essential analysis of the subject as "monad", of the concrete essence monad in general, in all its egoic and ego-alien structures and in the possible forms in which monadic generality can be typically particularized. This is the programme of an eidetics of the monad, sketched in Appendices I–IV.

(16) An objection: a univocally determined life course seems excluded by essence. A monad includes a free ego, free to think this or that, to call up this or that memory, and such free activities bring new experiences into the monad that help determine its further life.

(16–17) Husserl answers with questions. Do free acts arise unmotivated, out of nothing? Is "individuality" not already a law governing them? I, as the one I am and no other, decide in a given case thus and not otherwise; with my original dispositions and abilities I have this idea and invent this train of thought, which with other dispositions I could not. Granted, he replies, but this shows only that there are principles of regulation, and irrational ones, not a priori. He then corrects his own identification of "irrational" with "not a priori". Original dispositions may be called irrational. The central ego of personal acts is different: as person it is a principle of intelligibility and so of rationality. Acts that spring from the ego, and are seen to spring from it, are understandable and are known as determined, as necessarily done by this ego in their determinacy. People deceive themselves about themselves as about others, but only because they have not exhibited the real situation of motives and made each motive intuitive in its origin in the ego. When I put myself vividly into a situation, even an imagined one, and let myself be quasi-motivated as ego, I can see that my decision would be firmly determined, that I as this ego would and must act so. Freedom and determinacy meet in motivation, the fundamental law of the personal world in Ideen II (Hua IV, §56): the decision is necessary because it is understood as mine, not because it is caused.

#### The Personal Ego, Personal Individuality

(17) Can I then say that my ego is given to me as an "essence" and that I know its essential connection with the decision, a rational necessity grounded in a specific essence? Certainly not. Still, judging myself or another "empirically", from outside, inductively, expecting behaviour and framing rules ("he was often unreliable, so he will be again"), differs from judging from within by putting myself into the ego's doing. But what is the ego that can be grasped only by living into an actual cogito, into the connection of past position-takings that have become habitual, and into the connections of motivation? What do I really find as the identical person I am? The necessity Husserl seeks is neither eidetic nor inductive, and its bearer is reachable only by inward re-enactment, not by inspecting an essence.

(17–18) What then is an inner transformation of the ego, a conversion (Umkehr)? Before it I say with inner evidence that I would act so in such a situation, or will act so in the coming one, provided it really is that situation as a situation of motives. After the conversion this no longer holds, and I would no longer say it. Conversion is the test case: if decisions are inwardly necessary, a change of person must appear as a change in what is necessary for him, not as a break in necessity.

(18) I as person am not a momentary component "ego" of an act but the ego that has performed all its earlier acts and shows in them its manner of letting itself be motivated. For this ego no motivation in its interwoven motivations is contingent and unintelligible like the arrival of a colour sensation; each is necessary and "intelligible". The motivation in each case is univocal, but not by essence: univocal for this ego. That phrase, a necessity indexed to an individual, is what the rest of the text tries to make precise.

(18) The person, as the "individuality" of a subjectivity or monad, is related to the sphere of specific activity in the form ego cogito. In the manifold of cogitos the one and same ego unfolds, develops, changes, and may convert, as an individuality. It is a unity that constitutes itself, and constitutes itself for itself, as it actively performs its acts out of itself, so that it can come to know itself in such free acts (evaluations, position-takings) as the unifying substrate of personal traits of character. Acts, by contrast, are passing personal comportments in which the individuality manifests itself momentarily, in actuality or in imagination, doing or having done. "Momentarily", because it exists even when it does not manifest itself, as in sleep, and could manifest itself equally in other acts, future or merely possible. A real manifestation occurs even when the ego imagines itself into situations and decides in imagination, though the act is an act of phantasy: a stance of neighbourly love taken in imagination is not an exercise of it. Husserl's note adds that the ego is this individual ego from the start and yet constitutes, unfolds, and transforms itself as this same individual in its free motivations. The ego as self-constituting through its own acts anticipates the Cartesian Meditations doctrine of the ego as substrate of habitualities (Hua I, §32); the claim that imagined decisions manifest character licenses the method of re-imagining used below.

(18–19) The personal ego as individuality is the identical element in the whole manifold of position-takings actual or possible for it, and for this ego that totality is fixed and manifests itself "inwardly" in single comportments. What would a complete manifestation be, and is it possible? Is the person, for itself and as the one it is, so firmly bounded that its decision is prefigured in a unified way for every possible circumstance, and can it be certain of this? Two notes sharpen the problem. The first indexes the totality to time: the personal individual at a stage of development is the ego-unity manifest in the fixed universe of position-takings actual and possible from that moment; the individual of the whole monadic life runs through all these momentary individuals and has as its universe the continuum of momentary universes. The second asks how far the person as it now is prefigures the person it will be, under what conditions this prefiguring follows lasting traits of character the person can state, and how personal self-shaping arises and works. Self-shaping presupposes self-experience and self-evaluation, which Husserl says are evidently conditioned by the empathic experience and evaluation of other persons and by acting on them. The note closes on "the great ethical problem": can self-positing determine self-shaping otherwise than revocably; can a willing of oneself according to an idea of one's own self be so absolute and central that it is a decision irrevocable for eternity? The absolute life-decision of the Kaizo articles of 1922–24 (Hua XXVII) grows here out of a question about individuation, and the person's self-knowledge is rooted in empathy, which ties the ethical problem to the volume's theme.

(19) A further problem is what empathy can achieve: whether it can grasp the other's individuality, and grasp it as the other can grasp it himself, supposing he can. Empathy's reach is measured against the person's inner self-manifestation, which the preceding units made the standard.

(19–20) Inner manifestation is opposed to an outer, associative-inductive one. Association rules within a person's manifold of experiences, and since acts issuing from the ego appear in the stream as experiences with outward analogues, they too fall under the laws of association and found expectations of similar acts under similar circumstances. I can therefore expect in advance, without putting myself into the situation as the ego that must decide, that as soon as the image of the situation arises passively the "corresponding" behaviour will follow. This associative anticipation is no genuine manifestation of individuality, at most an anticipation of a manifestation still to be produced. When I actually presentify the situation, similar to one in which I decided for a, I may find that as this individual, and precisely because I decided so, I must now decide otherwise. The disappointment of the expectation is then not contingent, as when a melody fails to end as expected, but has "inner" grounds. There is thus an "expectation", a prior certainty about future behaviour without a preceding decision, that is not external and inductive but motivated from within, from the ego's individuality. Having decided once can itself be the reason for deciding differently; no inductive regularity registers that.

(20) Individuality shows itself in what it loves and hates, not in sensory pleasure or pain; in what it values more or less and how it prefers or subordinates in valuing; and then in what it actively sets itself as a practical goal, what it "would rather will". Passive drive reveals nothing of individuality, any more than sensory feeling does. Practical position-taking stands opposed to drive. A purely passive satisfaction of drive does not manifest the person, but yielding to sensory drive from the ego (the habit of compliance) or resisting it does. The person lives in position-taking and in the order of preference, the domain where Husserl's ethics lectures of the period (Hua XXXVII) locate it, not in inclination.

(20–21) Nor does individuality manifest itself in passive belief, where a sensory datum simply stands there as present or past in perception or reproduction, or in the passive play of what suggests itself and what seems likely. It manifests itself in active doxic weighing and decision, in active thinking and all intellectual activities with their position-takings by an ego that decides according to "reasons". The ego subject to rational assessment is the personal ego, and this points to essential laws of norms addressed to all personal egos as standards. Because the active ego answers to reasons, its individuality stands under universal norms without being derivable from them.

(21) First, though: the personal ego is individual, a "character" manifest in its position-takings that sets this ego apart, and yet we speak of person in general, bring persons under type-concepts, and also have an essence-concept of person. Clarity about the essence person in general can be gained only by fully clarifying how personality is constituted, which must itself be exhibited eidetically. The person is irreducibly individual and yet an instance of an eidos and of types; Husserl defers the reconciliation to constitutive analysis.

(21) The personal ego is free: it decides as it decides but could decide otherwise. It is therefore no passive stage for experiences among which decisions also occur, all running off by a fixed order that could be read off them through essential or empirical laws. Husserl admits it is very hard to say the right thing here and offers a formulation. There is no order in the temporal sequence of experiences in immanent time that would permit fixed rules of induction and found firmly ordered empirical expectations within the stream. Nor does the essence of a stream's experiences prescribe the acts: it is not as if two streams with equally ordered hyletic data and other original passivities (if that is compossible), forming the same associations, would have to contain the same acts. Could an act incompatible with the one that occurs have occurred instead in the same stream as it was up to then? What kind of principle is the ego, the personal individuality? The thought experiment of two passively identical streams isolates the ego as an independent factor.

(21–22) What holds by essential law holds for the contingent on account of its "essence", because the this-here can be raised into the sphere of pure possibilities, where something holds for anything of its kind. If I hold the this-here fixed and try to grasp its determination as a possibility, all other singularizations of the essence stand beside it as equally legitimate possibilities. For an individual red and some possibility for its future, I have one among all possible colours. If instead I posit a cogito for some now or the near future in a stream with its ego, then by general essence every other cogito of the same specific sense (including exclusive ones) is equally possible, and no essential law fixes one, as with the colour. But this ego as it now is singles out one of them in advance, as what it must become in the posited situation and as its own, and not by way of empirical expectation. Husserl notes that this needs finer elaboration. Eidetic variation treats every possibility of a colour alike; applied to an ego's possible acts, it meets a preference that the eidos does not supply.

(22–23) Every decision issuing from the ego of a stream, as that ego now is in its development, therefore occurs neither as a mere fact, even an inductive one, that could as well have been otherwise, nor on general essential grounds, by laws that govern totalities of pure possibilities above the individual. The decision is determined from mere possibility, "a priori" in a new and peculiar sense. The cogito weighed as a possibility in a given context is already decided in possibility; the actual decision is evidently the only possible one and could be decided already in weighing the mere possibility, in phantasy. Every act in a stream, in a determinate subjectivity, has its necessity from pure possibility, and in this the identical ego of the stream has its individuality, by which it can be identical with no ego of another stream. Only when the ego is taken as this ego of this stream in this now is every possible decision for every situation in the stream determined a priori, and every actual decision therefore univocally necessary. The ego does not have its character as the uniqueness of a singularized specific essence, as though many egos of this specific essence were possible and it merely singularized along with the essences singularized in a stream. Husserl coins an individual a priori: necessity drawn from possibility yet valid for one ego only, the role Leibniz gave the complete individual concept in the divine understanding, here assigned to the ego's own phantasy.

(23) An ego has no general material character; it is entirely empty of one. It is merely the ego of the cogito, which supplies the whole content, and it is related to a stream on which it depends, as the stream depends on it. Yet it has a character in its freedom. What is possible for an ego in general, an empty generality showing no differences in anything specifiable, varies; but for each ego only one thing is possible in a given case, and that is therefore necessary for it. Each ego has its necessities, not of external determination by imposed rules knowable empirically, but inner, insightful necessities, "a priori" possibilities that determine actualities. A note restricts this to each actual now. The empty ego of Ideen I (§80) is retained, but its emptiness now coexists with a character located in the way it chooses.

(23–24) One might say that the ego of the cogito, empty of material specific essence, is comparable with other egos only as an empty form, "individuated" by the stream only in the sense of uniqueness. From this individuation by uniqueness, which the general form ego (specifically the same in all monads) undergoes, as do all single act-experiences, Husserl distinguishes individual character. Character is the determinate totality of possible acts belonging to each singularization of the form ego at each now, prefigured univocally and "a priori" for the single ego as ego of an individually determinate stream at the given moment, in a way that belongs to this ego and monad alone. In this stream every act is univocally necessary a priori relative to its presuppositions, conscious or unconscious, at the actual now. Before an act is performed, a manifold of possible acts is conceivable and intuitively representable for this ego itself; yet already in this free fiction one position-taking is prefigured as necessary for this ego in this context and is given to it as evidently necessary. I who am, the one I was, the ego of this stream so far, now buried in obscure undeveloped habituality, can do only this one thing now and must. The determinate "I will" is an "I shall", an individual necessity. I can imagine my future life in many ways, but for each possibility I can tell whether it is a possibility for me, when I construct my life concretely as mine from the present into any future in view of my life so far, or one that re-imagines me into another ego and another monad, incompatible with me as I am. Husserl's note specifies that this is spoken from the now: "if I remain who I now am". Holding myself fixed, I find already in phantasy, when I posit an imagined cogito as continuing my concrete active life, whether I can carry it out intuitively; and I can carry out only one. Re-imagining becomes a method of self-knowledge: varying my situation in phantasy reveals not what an ego in general could do but what I, as this one, must do. Nr. 7 and its Appendices turn the same method toward the possibility of other egos.

(24–25) When I look into my past, entering its dark realm by reproduction, I can also imagine it otherwise than it actually was. Each past act is thus univocally determined at its place in its actual having-been, as against possibilities which, posited as actual, would mean losing our actual monad and positing another incompatible with it, with another ego. In this respect acts are no different from hyletic data, and the past no different from the future, which needs only to be posited to be thought as determinately filled with life. Once any stretch of time is posited as concretely filled and existing, every other positing is excluded as conflicting with it. Determinacy here is the incompatibility of rival fillings of one time, the opening principle of compossibility applied to the monad's own history.

(25) In general a concrete content that has elapsed prescribes no univocal necessities to what is coming. If it was regular, it can carry only empirical anticipations for the knower and prove itself, by the norms of induction, a continuing empirical regularity under empirical laws. But a monad is not an arbitrary connection in time, though it is given to itself (and, through empathy, to others) as temporal and as filling a time series with all its "experiences". For the hyletic contents of that series and for what is given as ego-alien surroundings, nothing new arises; for the ego and the egoic it does. Apart from the empirical regularities that also govern acts as events in immanent time, acts have a law of their own, an individual law. This law prevents two monads from being entirely alike, even though they could well be thought alike in their hyletic contents and in everything that belongs originally and passively to a subjectivity: two monads alike in this, and in every developmental content of passivity, still cannot be alike in their acts and in what their acts determine. Kern reports Husserl's later marginal verdict on these sentences: "That cannot be right." The retraction falls on the inference from inner necessity to the impossibility of two alike monads, which the notes on pp. 32–33 also reject; the division of the monad into a passive and an active stratum stands.

(25–26) Alternatively: monads do have something general, a specific essence. In everything they contain, acts included, they have something specifiable, and they ground essential laws of generality, a generic a priori. On the other hand, in their "I am" they have a principle that includes nothing specifiable, not a "content" that would join the general element common to every ego as an individual difference. Any such content would be repeatable, a specific difference, at last a lowest difference, but still something general. Whatever individuates by content becomes universal, so what individuates the ego cannot be a content.

#### An Elementary Account of Object-Pole and Ego-Pole; the Ego as Functional Centre

(26) Husserl approaches the ego through the intentional object, which is "the same" and recognizable as the same in all the experiences in which it is meant. As temporal occurrences those experiences are different: a perception and a memory linked by a continuity of memory are two, "alike" only in bringing something identical to appearance, alike in their "direction". The identical is not a like moment in each, nor the identity of a common individual moment, which could be thought as the limiting case of a likeness, as when equal parts of two wholes are brought to coincide by altering one. It is a unique identity that only intentional experiences can show with respect to what they mean: not something really (reell) identical, a component they share, but something intentionally ("ideally") identical, a pole toward which each consciousness is "directed" as that of which it is conscious. Husserl's own note marks this as a separate consideration running to p. 30, after which he returns to his theme. Intentional identity is sui generis, not a degenerate case of resemblance between real parts.

(26–27) The object, as the same in all experiences directed to "it", is empty, an empty form, and only as this empty identical is it the same everywhere, determining a likeness among the experiences. But in one experience this empty something is meant as αβγ, in another as α′β′γ: it is empty and identical, yet meant variably as "bearer" of these or those "predicates" (predicables, prior to conceptual grasp), which are themselves new, dependent, borne identities, attribute-poles belonging to the bearer. The X meant once with these predicates and once with those differs in its modes of determination and is yet the same X. The "pure X" of Ideen I (§131), the determinable bearer of the noematic sense, is restated so that its structure can be set against the ego.

(27) A consciousness, and an act-consciousness above all, has a counter-pole. Being directed toward the object-pole is the ego's being directed "in" consciousness toward that pole, and this ego too is a pole. Intentionality is bipolar: the act stretches between two identities, neither of which is a part of it.

(27) Within one monadic stream the ego-pole is identically the same for all acts, whatever X, X′ they are directed to and whatever attributes of these bearers they are co-directed to. It is not an identical "experience" or moment of experience in the stream. That would be absurd, as though a monadic stream, constitutive flowing through and through, the constitution of a becoming in which there is "being" only as what is intentionally identical across constituted becoming, could contain the same piece in different stretches. The ego's identity cannot be the persistence of a real component, because the stream contains no persisting components.

(27–28) It is the incomparable peculiarity of a monadic stream, unlike any ordinary "stream" and any object, all of which are thinkable only as constituted in monads, that it is and can be only with an ego-polarization. Every "consciousness" in a monad, in this universe of connected consciousness that is itself a consciousness, can be only consciousness of "the same", absolutely identical ego. Whether I live in the actual present, experiencing, relating, thinking, and valuing my surroundings, or reflect on my subjective modes of givenness, sensations, feelings, and position-takings, or roam into a reproduced past and as it were live there again, I find, moving from past to past, not a mere succession of "presents" in the mode of the past but at each point an "I was active thus". All activities at that point are centred in the form cogito in the identical ego, and across the continuum of phases I find not only like centring but centring on the same ego: I who am now am the one I was, and every past counterpart is the counterpart of this same ego, not merely of a continuous "stream". Everything is centred, actually or potentially: it is my theme, or affects me and is noticed in passing, or affects me unnoticed, or can affect me and become the theme of an act. This identical ego-pole, the centre of affections and actions (and reactions), is there for itself, and for it every affection and action that has "occurred" is there, like everything ego-alien, as something that can in turn affect it. It "lies" in each consciousness as a pole differently from the intentional object toward which the consciousness, or the ego directing itself in it, is directed. Two later revisions sharpen the account. "Is there for itself" becomes "becomes there for itself by entering into function": the ego's self-presence is achieved in functioning, not standing. "The ego directing itself" becomes "the ego directed", with the note that the ego is first affected and then turns toward what affects it; the actus as a self-directing in a mode of position-taking must be distinguished from the directedness that persists in the act-consciousness through immanent time, and acts as a rule form syntheses.

(28–29) A second, reflective consciousness is required to grasp the pole. It grasps it as the pole of the first consciousness, which has now been constituted as a temporal object (ontically), or rather has become affective. In this new consciousness the pole, which Husserl later qualifies as "constituted", becomes an object-pole, while the subject-pole of the new consciousness is not objective; and yet it is the same pole. Every objectification of the ego leaves a functioning, unobjectified ego behind it.

(29) The ego lives in ego-life as affectively or actively functioning, and as such it comes before the polar opposite function of affecting itself and becoming a theme like anything else that can affect and become thematic, ontic. That function is in itself second and necessarily subsequent, though the sense of "subsequent" is difficult and cannot mean ordinary succession in time along a single line. One must always distinguish the primally living pole in the primally living act, which by being, that is by functioning, institutes a presence for itself without being a present that stands over against it, from the pole that has become a counterpart and is no longer the living pole but is there for a new primally living pole. The new one is still the same, absolutely identical pole in new primally living functioning. The ego is a functional centre that can at any time be the centre of a function making the same ego, "itself", thematic as a centre so functioning or having functioned, and so is there for itself. The later revision adds that the ego, even when not active, goes out from itself toward something and can become an active centre. The functioning ego that institutes presence without being a present, and the subsequence of reflection that is not temporal succession, seed the late manuscripts on the living present (Mat VIII) and Fink's problem of the anonymity of the functioning ego.

(29) The identical ego is thus nothing for itself: taken abstractly in and for itself it is wholly empty of content and has only what is specific to it and general, to be a functional centre in general and, here in this stream, its functional centre. Description of the pole confirms the earlier dilemma: whatever individuates the ego cannot be found by inspecting the pole.

(30) The functional centre is what it is in some function, passive (affective) or active. Accordingly the ego appears as "I suffer from" (am affected by), "I sense this", "I experience that", "in sensing I suffer pleasure or displeasure", "I am passively drawn, striving", and also as "I think, I value, I take pleasure in, I am glad of, I am sad about, I desire, I love, I will". The two lists map the division between passivity and activity drawn on pp. 20–21 onto the pole: the same ego is patient in the first and agent in the second.

(30) This is somewhat analogous to the object-pole, which is what it is as bearer or substrate of changing accidents and attributes, and yet wholly different.
