The ego-pole is not a bearer or substrate of affections and actions but precisely ego: a point of in-radiation, a functional centre for affections; a point of out-radiation, a centre of activities, of acts. It is "in" its states, reactive states and passive realizations included, and "in" its acts as they direct themselves into what is ego-alien and has affected it. A marginal gloss names the ego as pole of affection and action related to objects immanent and transcendent. Husserl refuses to read the ego as a substance with modes: the schema of substrate and property belongs to the object-pole, and the later notes on substance and monad (Appendices XXXVII–XXXIX) take up whether the ego is a substance at all.

(30) This centre of manifold functions can therefore be grasped originally only after the fact, from the same centre, in functions of the same kind directed toward the ego and thus toward functioning, within the unity of one stream of experience in which every function carries the moment of issuing from the ego or being aimed into it. The ego's original self-knowledge is reflexive and internal to one stream; another's ego-pole can therefore never be grasped in this original way. Kern's note marks the end of the elementary account here.

(30) Returning to the main theme, Husserl considers the ego that, in peculiar functions of re-imagining, entertains other possibilities of sensory data, surroundings, and experiences of action and affection relating to them. Manifold variations of the actual stream result, which the same ego can find as its actual realm of subjective experience. A note qualifies the realm: it is that of immanent time, of "experiences", immanent noeses and their noemata; but immanent time is only the guiding thread toward what is absolutely subjective in originally constituting life. The note concedes that the stream of immanent time is itself constituted, the result of the Bernau manuscripts of 1917–18 (Hua XXXIII), so the variation operates on a constituted level.

(30–31) Two fundamentally different kinds of variation now appear. The first changes my sphere of subjective experience, in conflict with what is actually mine, without disturbing the identity of the ego as functional centre. I would not be changed in the least if the surface I now experience as red were green, or if the instinctive drive I now feel as hunger for the fruit before me were missing, or replaced by a drive to eat this piece of wood. The second concerns the active functions, those subject to "rational assessment". Setting aside whether they meet the norm, as soon as I try to imagine them freely together with re-imagined affective contents, I find myself bound. It becomes evident that I can remain the same identical ego with arbitrary affective contents only if I would behave in certain ways; and holding my affective contents fixed as those I actually had, I could perform in each case only the acts I did perform. The affective contents that my acts awaken, along with the whole inheritance of apperceptive contents in my habitual underground, are presuppositions for exactly the acts I perform; if I re-imagine them, I can no longer preserve my ego's identity. There are therefore transformations of the concrete monad that preserve our individual ego and transformations that abolish its "individuality". Whether two coexisting monads, among which the variations were distributed, could share identical affective contents in the hyletic and originally passive sphere is a separate question. And not everything affective can be shared, since everything active turns into something affective. The variation sorts the monad into a stratum indifferent to the ego's identity and a stratum constitutive of it; the last remark blocks the assumption that passive contents could be cleanly shared, because acts sediment into passivity.

(32) A principal theorem for the active sphere is announced, but a principle must come first.

(32) Two coexisting monadic unities cannot have the same ego. Moreover, the ego of one is not only not identical with that of another; each ego has its character, as described above, and in each this character is a univocally necessitating way of taking positions, of deciding so and so under the presuppositions of affection, which include previous action. A note limits what is shared: two monads have specific commonality only in that each has a functional centre, a common form, and in that the basic kinds of functions, like other structures of consciousness, have an essential typology identical for all monads. Numerical distinctness of egos and difference of character are kept apart, and only the formal structure of egohood is common.

(32–33) To re-imagine this character is to re-imagine the ego. That two egos cannot be alike in character, in the univocal necessity of their action, is not a metaphysical assertion but an evidence given in re-imagining my ego. As soon as I re-imagine my character and construct, as I can, a systematically closed character as a possible one, I necessarily have another ego, independently of hyletic contents. In re-imagining I hold all possible monads and egos in individual coincidence, and there I see the closed character as the principle of "individuation", what makes the monad necessarily unique. Husserl's later note calls this "hasty and a fallacy". In individual coincidence my individual ego is incompatible with every other individual kind, as my hyle is with a re-imagined hyle, and the two are independent; all that follows is that there is a genus ego with lowest differences, differences of personality, a priori, which every poet makes intuitive. Incompatibility within one coincidence shows only that two characters cannot be one ego, not that two egos cannot share a character. The text continues. I could have other surroundings, which as my intentional surroundings would change my subjective contents. But not every change in my subjectivity, if that covers everything a psychologist would thematize as "subjective", is a change in my subject, the ego; other surroundings do not mean another ego. Keeping my surroundings, I could also imagine that I behaved and would behave otherwise than I do, and be intuitively certain that I would. Then I have a consistent possible re-imagining of my ego, but it would no longer be I; I would have re-imagined myself into someone else. The same holds for re-imagined surroundings, into which I can imagine myself either as the ego I really am, unchanged, or as transformed and therefore as another.

(33) One might argue that a second monad cannot contain the individually same ego because it would then have the same functional character, and two monads with the same functional character are unthinkable. What could prove this inversion of the earlier claim? Above it was taken as given that two coexisting monads cannot contain the same individual ego, which implied that the centres could be called non-identical without asking about their "character", just as within one monad identity was taken as given before that question. Husserl's note rejects the inversion: "No." If there cannot be two human beings with the same personality, that must have quite other grounds. It cannot be a necessity drawn from direct intuition, or poets would not treat the "problem" of the double. But the poets make it worse, for the double is experienced as the same self. Literature serves as evidence about eidetic possibility: what imagination presents consistently, intuition cannot exclude, and the double shows that shared character is imaginable.

(33) Several monads require a common constituted world. Could several people, or even all people, walk about in their world with identically the same personal character, "the same" egos in different bodies? Each would have his particular experiences, stand in other circumstances, and therefore have other habitualities and knowledge; yet they would recognize each other as "the same". Growing up in roughly the same surroundings, like children of the same parents, we would feel ourselves psychic doubles, and with the same outward body, which is entirely possible, doubles outright. We would face the problem of the double. Individuation is now posed within a shared world, where character is recognized through empathy and what is in question is sameness of character, not of ego.

(33–34) Husserl judges that these last hasty remarks lack clarity in principle. The important task is to formulate the genuine problems sharply and to prepare them correctly by phenomenological means.

(34) The closing sentence states what survives. What is individual in an ego's active behaviour lies in its univocity for that ego, which holds for every ego and is not grounded in each ego's having a different passive underground, as the text has tried to show. Husserl keeps the individual necessity of decision and its independence from hyle; in the margins he abandons the claim that it secures the uniqueness of character across egos. The ego's individuality lies in how it decides, not in what it undergoes, and what distinguishes one ego from another remains open for Nr. 4 and Nr. 7.

### Appendix I. Phenomenology of Monadic Individuality and Phenomenology of the General Possibilities and Compatibilities of Experiences. Static and Genetic Phenomenology (June 1921)

(34) Husserl names two disciplines. The first is a phenomenology of the possible "phenomena", phenomenal connections, and constitutive achievements that can occur in monads in general: an eidetics of consciousness indifferent to which monad has them.

(34) The second is a phenomenology of monadic individuality. It investigates the laws added to the laws of experiences that fix what the individual unity and closure of a monad require: what belongs necessarily to an individual monad as its own essence, what universal form it must have, which genera of elements that form includes, and what secures their unity and closure in it. If the monad is necessarily a unity of becoming, of unceasing genesis, its concrete structure is built only from "elements" that are themselves unities of becoming, with an abstract structure of phases like the whole monad. Each phase has necessities of its own, not merely compatibilities. Every experience that stands out for itself demands a background, a horizon; every moment of a phase makes demands on the becoming; and each demands, for further genesis, the flow that constitutes temporality. Naturalistic concepts must be kept away. The monad is a living unity that bears an ego as pole of acting and suffering (Husserl adds: and as pole of personal characters), a unity of waking and hidden life, a unity of capacities and "dispositions"; and the hidden, the "unconscious", is a mode of monadic containment whose necessary sense must be drawn originally in ways of its own. The distinction between compatibility and demand carries the text: an eidetics of possible experiences says what can coexist, an eidetics of the individual monad says what each part requires of the whole. The "unconscious" enters as a mode of the monad, not as a psychological hypothesis, which opens the way to Husserl's later analyses of sleep and sedimentation at the limits of phenomenology (Hua XLII).

(35) Husserl finds the first title insufficiently determined and specifies it. The phenomena are investigated with "transcendent" reality transcendentally bracketed. That includes the thing-world in quotation marks, with the necessities and possibilities it carries as intuitive, and experienced nature as such. He would describe orientation in time and space (perspective), givenness by sides and the appearing of sides, sensations as adumbrations, apprehensions, and the connections of perceptual appearances that constitute unity and sameness; the relation to the ego, grasping, relating, explicating, conceiving under generalities, predicating; the meant states of affairs, propositions, inferences; the modes of attention, affection, and turning toward; the thinking "I do", as when I judge premises and, motivated by them, the conclusion. All these are occurrences in "immanent time", the time of "experiences". In considering the monad we have its nexus of immanent time with its experiences and the unities constituted in them. That whole nexus, a "further step", has its constitution in the original flow of time in the corresponding primal experiences. The programme is the descriptive content of Ideen I, but the parenthetical "further step" places the constituting flow of Hua X beneath it, so that the eidetics of Ideen I describes a constituted level.

(35) This prescribes a definite path for phenomenological study once the reduction has provided its starting point. I must proceed step by step: at first I do not yet see that a stream of consciousness constitutes itself inwardly, I have not fixed it scientifically, let alone the monadic individuality and the ego of capacities constituted in it. The order of discovery runs opposite to the order of constitution: what is constitutively deepest is reached last.

(35) Must the study then not go on to show a unity of genesis in immanent time, and in it the unity of the monad temporally constituted for itself, which must be traced back to the primally living monad whose absolute being consists in a manifold flowing in which the immanent phenomenon of filled immanent time, the phenomenal immanent monad, constitutes itself? Husserl doubles the monad: a phenomenal monad, constituted as a unity in immanent time, and an absolute monad that is the constituting flow. Appendix II returns to the distinction as the monad "in its last, absolute or primal form".

(35–36) The investigation of individuation then concerns both: the individuation of the immanently constituted monad and, by going back to the laws of the primally constituting flow, that of the absolute monad. It aims at the necessary form of this unity of filled immanent time, at what gives all content a necessary unity in the togetherness of each phase and in succession, and all individual components a single unbreakable nexus. The monad is a "simple", indivisible being that is what it is as continuously becoming in time. Everything belonging to it lies at some point of this becoming, has its being as a fill of this filled immanent time, and is nothing for itself, since the filling is continuous and related to one and the same ego-pole. Whatever is related to one identical ego-pole belongs to one continuous stream of becoming of a single filled time with a single ego. Two monads mean two streams of becoming of the same time-form, but not of the same time, with two egos. An ego's immanent time can never be unfilled, have gaps, or break into several streams separated by pauses. In the monad everything is connected with everything. Leibniz's simplicity is reinterpreted as the unbreakability of one filled time around one pole; and the criterion for two monads, two times of the same form, is where the problem of one common objective time in Nr. 4 begins.

(36) Under the title monad Husserl has so far considered the unity of its living becoming, its history. The monad also has its living present, in which it has become and from which it at once becomes further. This present is, on one side, primally impressional, the newly welling moment of life in the form "impression"; on the other, as heritage of the past, it has together with the impression its dark and sometimes brightening underground, and carries its history in every now as a horizon into which it can look and which it can run through again, as if reliving it, in single or connected recollections. Every phase of monadic becoming has this structure, "with all the wonder that belongs to it". The sequence of primal impressions yields a filled unity of immanent time, but not everything that was and is in the becoming. Each phase also contains its sedimented history, its hidden "knowledge", its habitual. Now this, now that was recollected; the past came alive and entered relations with the present. The monad is not only what it now is but also what it has been; it can know its past, want to roam in it, perform acts that link present and past. A note asks whether all this concerns only the passivity of the stream and, for the ego, a general potentiality, the general "capacity" to have a field of affection and action in it. The ego also has its individuality in a special sense, a principle regulating acts from the ego by which new experiences enter the stream. Is this individual ego, in the unity of its individual capacities, not the counterpart of the unity of a thing, whose individuality is likewise not circumscribed by general laws of constitution? Sedimentation adds to the sequence of impressions a second, non-linear dimension of the monad's time; and the note returns to Nr. 2, now putting the ego's individuality on a level with a thing's individual character, which general constitutive laws leave open.

(36–37) In this direction one can study the monad's unity and the essential demands it contains, even though the stream contains contingency: all sensory data, even when they arrive as expected by empirical motivation, are contingent and could come otherwise. A note draws the consequence for the ego. The sensory datum is contingent, and so is the fact that sensory data are regulated toward forming thing-apperceptions and constituting a nature and a world. But the individual ego, though determined by the contingent, is not contingent in the same sense in its individuality. Am I not a "necessary fact", my contingency determined only by the incomprehensible matter that co-determines my psychic, monadic development? The necessity lies in my being uncancellable and in my intelligible unity under these presuppositions, which under other presuppositions would still be the same individuality and never another. Whatever contingency there is, and however much the idea of a colour sensation prescribes only that it belongs to some sensing ego, leaving open an indeterminate infinity of possible egos, an individual colour sensation is different. Its individuality is not a feature added to its general features. The datum is what it is only as constituting itself in this monad within its lawfully formed nexus of time; it has its being as a unity of flowing life, intentionally one in it and repeatedly identifiable by the monad's ego, and its form of individuality is its unique position in time, the index of originally constituting life. Nothing constituted in a monad belongs to it as something that could exist for itself, enter the monadic nexus as a member, and as easily enter another. Everything immanent is individual but dependently individual; only the monad is independent. The stream supplies the distinguishing individuality through its phases, its immanently objectified order of experiences, but all such particular individuality is as dependent as a phase is relative to an independent concrete whole. Everything concrete in the monad is dependent, and the independent cannot be identified with the concrete, as Husserl had done in the Logical Investigations. The Third Investigation had equated "concretum" with independent content; in a monad even a full concrete experience is dependent, so the theory of wholes and parts must separate two concepts it had merged. The "necessary fact" of the note anticipates the late manuscripts' ego as primal fact (Urfaktum).

(37–38) I can, however, study the formations of the stream noetically and noematically in their general typology, their possible variations, their essential connections, without pursuing the laws of a monad's individuality. The phenomenological-eidetic reduction places me on the ground of a possible monad in general, not of one held as individually identical, with the task of circumscribing its individual identity in its possibilities and necessities. I can also set this new task, using the eidetics of acts and constituted formations. I can likewise describe single geneses and laws of genesis without systematically taking up the universal genesis of a monad and the essence of its individuality. Individuation is a third problem, distinct from eidetics and from genesis though it draws on both.

(38) Husserl now draws a threefold division. Investigations may be called static that trace the correlations between constituting consciousness and constituted objectivity and exclude genetic problems altogether. From these he distinguishes investigations of the typology of the various forms of experiencing and of genesis, by their essential possibilities and compatibilities, still without treating individual problems in context. Finally there is the phenomenology of monadic individuality, which includes the phenomenology of a connected genesis in which the unity of the monad grows, the monad being by becoming. This is among the first explicit statements of the static–genetic distinction, and its middle term is easily overlooked: a typology of geneses that remains eidetic and general, between static correlation-analysis and the genesis of one individual monad.

(38) A systematic phenomenology as Husserl had conceived it follows the levels of possible constitution. At the bottom lies the always necessary constitution of the immanent stream of time and of monadic being as an immanent temporal unity; then the genetically higher levels of transcendence, phantoms, the constitution of a nature and of animals in it, everything "aesthetic"; then the achievements of thinking, which could begin at any level and take different shapes accordingly (the ego's activity). These are genetic considerations, and the description of finished formations and their constitutions is placed within genetic research. One can also describe the correlations for themselves, in their typology and in the necessary belonging-together of their correlates; in genesis their becoming from constitutive lower levels becomes intelligible. Static description is not abandoned but subordinated: it supplies the finished correlates whose formation genesis explains. The ladder from time through phantom to nature and animal is the order of Ideen II, now read genetically.

(38) Systematic procedure also lays the ground for a systematic doctrine of levels of monads, according to whether or not they achieve higher development by advancing to higher constitutions. Every higher monad has developed from a lower one and was itself lower at an earlier stage. The individuation of a monad would still need its own treatment, though it is doubtful whether that must be a comprehensive doctrine; the problem must be kept in view. The levels of monads are at once a hierarchy of kinds, such as animal and human, and the developmental history of each monad, which prepares Nr. 6's treatment of empathy with children and animals as interpretation by dismantling my own levels.

(38–39) Husserl separates the questions that move the inquiry. First, what belongs to the possibility of a monad, to its own essence, in ideal possibilities and necessities? Second, what belongs to a monad that is to be able to constitute a nature? Third, what belongs to a monad that is to have other monads given to it, to experience and know a plurality of coexisting monads, and what belongs to those monads themselves if they are to stand in commercium? The questions are graded by added conditions: monad as such, monad with a world, monad with others; and the third splits into the conditions of experiencing others and the conditions on the others themselves, the two sides Nr. 4 and Nr. 13 treat.

(39) Fourth, among a monad's essential possibilities as monad is conceptual knowledge. What kinds and forms of it "are there", what consistent possibilities hold for the concepts, judgments, and connections of judgments to be constituted, and what for truth? Here possible knowing in general, possible meaning, and possible true being as knowable for the knowing monad are considered in generality, always within the frame of a possible monad in general. Such knowledge about monads is not like general truths about numbers that hold for each single number. It resembles the knowledge that every pure number belongs in the number series, alongside which special laws for primes, sums, and products arise that need not concern every given number; or the geometrical laws for kinds of figures that are not essential traits of every figure. The essential laws found for possible monads likewise need not express properties that every monad must have. Not every monad must think logically or perform moral acts, yet the essential laws of logical and of moral consciousness belong to the general domain of the science of possible monads. Laws of the genus monad are separated from laws of species within it, so that an eidetics of reason does not become the claim that every monad is rational.

(39) Fifth, a different question concerns the systematic possibilities of monadic consciousness, its possible basic forms or genera, and the essential laws regulating possible occurrences, acts, and states and the formation of systematic connections constituting objective unities and contents of thought. Different again are the laws governing a monad's individual identity, and further those governing a compossible plurality of monads that are to motivate one another and be spiritually determining for one another. The two kinds of law are connected. But not everything essentially possible for ego and ego-experience is compossible in the individual unity of a monad, and every essential incompatibility also excludes something in that unity. There are also laws that prescribe positively what belongs to a monad's necessary formal structure and what must come to be once a determinate individual content is present. The primal law of genesis is thus the law of original time-constitution, together with the laws of association and reproduction and the laws by which the monad constitutes itself for itself as a unity. Husserl's list of genetic laws names the three themes of the lectures on passive synthesis written in the same period: time, association, and self-constitution.

(40) Are the specific laws of genesis the laws of individuality, or only one branch of them, those concerning the monad's becoming, while another branch concerns coexistence? Husserl suspects a bad approach. Every law of compatibility in coexistence also prescribes a law to possible genesis. Laws of compatibility in temporal coexistence already presuppose the constitution of time, and they stand beside laws of compatibility in succession; together they are general laws of being able to be together, simultaneously and successively. There are also laws concerning not compatibilities but necessities of sequence: the former say that if a is, a coexisting b cannot be; the latter that if a is, b must be, at the same time or afterward. But the temporal constitutes itself, and we arrive at primal connections of the flow in which both kinds of law again play a role, in an altered sense. Dividing laws by coexistence and succession fails because time is itself constituted; below constituted time both kinds of law recur in a sense still undetermined, the problem of the primal connections of flowing in the Bernau manuscripts (Hua XXXIII).

(40–41) These are basic questions for distinguishing and ordering the necessary phenomenological investigations, which Husserl always frames as static and genetic phenomenology. What was the guiding viewpoint? He describes his practice. I can start from external perception, take this type of experience, hold on to the intended object, set its meant features against the sensations and adumbrations found in the perception, pursue the possibility of further perceptions continuously one with the first as perceptions of the same, describe the changing sensations, the forms of apprehension and synthesis, and follow the correlation between the unity of the appearing object and the manifold of appearances that combine noetically in agreement. I construct essential possibilities for such experiences and their connections, and so for a monad in which they may occur: a monad is possible as bearing such possibilities. I do not ask about genesis in the monad, about how such phenomena arise. I follow the idea of a consistent course of experience related to a natural object that maintains itself as identical, and also the branching off of discord at some point as another possibility, which of course changes the monad and its genesis. Or I start from purposive objects, spiritual formations, books, from objectivities of every kind, including ideal ones such as concepts and mathematical propositions, and ask how consciousness of them can look, how manifold consciousness of them is possible, and how they "constitute" themselves as self-given in consciousness. These are constitutive questions throughout. Constitution concerns essential correlations between the object of cognition and cognition, the noetic connections in which ontic connections, including those between objects and concepts or truths, are constituted. I always hold objects under the idea "existing for me", even when I aim at the immanent, and study the noetic-noematic modes that function constitutively, or modes of activity such as grasping, contemplating, and comparing, which in turn constitute higher objectivities. Even with their existence excluded, the objectivities stand before me as intentional and guide the ordering of the connections. To trace constitution is not to trace genesis, which is the genesis of constitution and moves as genesis within a monad. Is static phenomenology not precisely the phenomenology of guiding clues, of the constitution of leading types of objectivity in their being, and of the constitution of their non-being, of mere illusions, nullities, discords? Its essential belongings are those of correlation, not a conditioning in genesis: nothing conditioned comes to be from what conditions it. Genetic phenomenology traces the original becoming in the stream of time, itself an originally constituting becoming, and the genetically functioning "motivations". It shows how consciousness comes from consciousness and how constitutive achievement is constantly accomplished in becoming: in the conditioning between motivating and motivated, or in the necessary transition from impression to retention, in which the consciousness of this very becoming and, correlatively, the change of the now into the just-past constitute themselves. Static phenomenology is defined by its method, the object as guiding clue (Leitfaden); genetic phenomenology by its relation, the dependence of later on earlier consciousness. Correlation and conditioning are different kinds of belonging-together, and the first does not yield the second.

(41) Statically, though, Husserl describes not only constitutive possibilities relative to an object as guiding clue but also the typology of the connections of consciousness at any stage of development. Ideen I did this, describing the structures of pure consciousness as structures of phenomena that may occur in the unity of an immanent phenomenal nexus. A note asks whether the structures of pure consciousness must be regarded from the outset as constitutive occurrences. Static analysis thus has a second form besides the guiding-clue method, a typology of consciousness; the note asks whether that typology too presupposes constitution, which would make the "structures" of Ideen I themselves results.

(41–42) If the individuality of a monad is to be held fixed, however, all possibilities must be selected: what is individually one demands itself in existence. It can do so only by laws. It is also a law that whatever occurs within the form of unity fits into the unity by laws of kind, and that through the law of unity what fits is demanded by the context (Logical Investigations, Third Investigation). Individuality converts compatibility into demand: in a possible monad things may coexist, in an individual monad each is required by the whole. The doctrine of foundation is now applied to a life.

(42) Is it not the case, then, that there are laws of possibility and compatibility in monads as such and, distinct from them, laws belonging to the unity of a monad as an individual unity? Individual unity stands under laws of genesis. The phenomenology of absolute individuality, of the monad as individual unity, would then have to explain how the individual phases develop from one another, each with its own law of individuality. And would there be general laws of the individuality of these phases? The closing question restates Nr. 2's problem methodologically: individuality calls for laws, laws are general, and Husserl leaves undecided whether general laws of individuality are a coherent goal.

### Appendix II. On the Concept of "Monad". The Concretion of the Ego (Probably June 1921)

(42) Husserl builds the concept of monad in numbered steps. First, the pure ego: the ego in ego cogito, I who am directed in perceiving at the perceived thing, who was affected by it before turning to it, who feel pleasure or displeasure "at" it, who compare one thing with another, desire something, suffer grief. This ego is itself an object of the specific experience of the ego in which the ego directs itself at itself; how the ego can be an object that affects itself and awakens its own turning is set aside. It is uncancellable and given in self-experience as now existing and enduring, sometimes as turning back, while it endures, to its own past, to itself as subject of past acts or affections. In such turnings it grasps itself as enduring and as the ego of the elapsed phases of its duration and of elapsed acts, absolutely identical while performing ever other acts, past, present, and future. The pure ego is defined by uncancellability and by an identity found in reflection; the question set aside, how the ego can affect itself, is the one Nr. 2's elementary account had treated.

(42–43) Second, this pure ego is given as enduring, in self-perception as now enduring, and it can also be given as the identical subject of a recollected duration, a recollected lifetime; it is the same ego in all phases. Its identity differs fundamentally from that of something that endures in another sense, which is identical by having momentary phases that fill its duration, "extending" over it and changing or remaining unchanged, different in content in different phases or alike in all. A tone changes or stays the same, and we can distinguish two correlative objectivities, the filled duration and the one that endures and changes or does not. The ego endures, but it is not properly self-same, as though it stretched through the duration and each phase continuously repeated something equal; nor could it really change as ego; nor could it, if a stretch of time were thought empty, fall apart into two merely equal egos, as a tone falls into two separate equal tones. As pure ego it is absolutely identical, belonging to every point of this time, and yet not stretched. Two models of persistence are separated. The tone's identity is made of its filled phases and can therefore be interrupted and doubled; the ego's identity is not made of anything and can neither break nor be duplicated, which Appendix III calls a "non-founded" unity.

(43) Third, the ego has its ego-life in acts and affects. It fills time only in an improper sense, insofar as its time belongs to it as the field of its life, and that life fills the time. A note says the first step should have been the ego's relation, within its field of time, to surroundings, to objects given straightforwardly and unreflectively, sensory objects and objects of transcendent experience, and only afterward "consciousness" with its objectivity and temporality. Every act begins in time and stretches through it, as does every affection. The ego is wholly identical and without any qualitative differences, and yet it differs from moment to moment as differently active and suffering. In this unique relation of ego to act or affection the ego gains a stretching content. But the acts are not like other temporal events in the ordinary sense, whose extension would yield a unity that would then be the ego. The ego is nothing but a quality-less pole of acts and has all its determinations from this polarity; the acts are something incomparable, not something beside the ego that would first have to be related to it. The ego lives in its acts, releases them from itself, or undergoes something from outside, is affected and lives as affected. Its condition has an incomparable sense that forbids taking the ego as something for itself or as qualified in itself, while it is determined by its acts and affects, from which it has all its "essential" properties. The ego is empty and yet determined by its acts. Husserl avoids both the substance with accidents and the bundle of events by making acts the ego's own radiations rather than items related to it.

(43–44) Fourth, we may speak of the concrete ego, which is as it is in determining itself through its actions and passions. This is the real ego of inner experience, since what is experienced in the full sense is concrete, whereas the pure ego is an abstract identical, though not in the ordinary sense an abstract "moment" running equally through the acts. The concrete ego extends through immanent time and changes according to its "spiritual" content, its acts and states, carrying always the absolutely identical ego-pole and living itself out in its life, the concrete connection of acts centred identically in the pole. A later note disowns the result: the whole consideration yields no concretion, since the ego is always constituted, in a wholly peculiar way, as a personal ego, the ego of its habitualities, capacities, and character, and Husserl asks where the right place is to discuss the habitual and capacities. The note marks the turn from the pole-ego of Ideen I to the ego as substrate of habitualities: a concrete ego without its sediments is still an abstraction.

(44) Even so, the concrete ego in the unity of its ego-life is not really concrete unless we add what such a life cannot be concrete without. The unity of ego-life as doing and suffering relates essentially to a changing counterpart: to a constantly filled immanent time, to a filled "objective" time with "objective" objects that are there for the ego "in consciousness" as the immanent ones are, and to occasional objects of phantasy, there only "as if", hovering before it in "mere fiction". A note specifies that the counterpart is first that of "objectivity", to be divided into immanent and transcendent, and that "object" means what is thrown over against, not an intentional experience. The active "I think", the cogito (I grasp, I compare, I take pleasure in something), is cogito of a cogitatum; every I-do is a relation of the ego to something conscious to it. Something must already be conscious for the ego to turn to it, and without turning there is no activity regarding it. Turning presupposes affection, and only what is conscious can affect, exert a greater or lesser "stimulus" on the ego. Concretion requires the world side, and the order of presupposition runs from consciousness to affection to turning to activity.

(44–45) Husserl leaves aside whether the gradation of affection signifies only a modality of a general passive having-in-consciousness that precedes possible activity, turning, and any "interest" of the ego. In any case having something in consciousness is, in its generality, having something conscious over against oneself, having it pregiven. This may take the form of exerting an affective stimulus on the ego, or of being turned to, acted on, and grasped, and so given in a special sense; in these forms what is conscious has entered special relations to the ego and its actions and passions. Moreover, one having-in-consciousness can enter with others, endlessly many, into the unity of one consciousness in which the same something is conscious as identical, conscious many times and in many ways. Every having-in-consciousness, every lived experience (intentional experience, not necessarily intending in the active sense) is itself conscious, and all the experiences of an ego are spanned by the unity of an inner consciousness and ordered in the unity of one immanent time, which they fill in each phase. Three layers are distinguished, pregivenness, affection, and active grasp, and beneath them inner consciousness, which makes every experience itself conscious and orders all in one time. The question left open, whether affection is a degree of pregivenness, is answered at the end of Appendix IV by separating the two.

(45) The word "lived experience" (Erlebnis) expresses this being-lived, being conscious in inner consciousness, through which an experience is pregiven to the ego at any time. Unless it is a hyletic datum, an experience is itself consciousness-of, intentional, with an intentional content, meaning everything that is not an immediate component of the experience, not inwardly conscious, but conscious secondarily through the intentionality the inwardly conscious experience exercises. A note settles a recurring hesitation: hyletic data may not be called experiences, nor their immanent flowing the life of the ego, "of course not!" An intentional experience may have various "contents" pregiven through it as something conscious in it without being real (reell) components: in external perception the perceived object, in recollection the recollected one, and also the perspectival appearances. When the ego in turning performs its relating, connecting, and other activities, a connected experience builds up in the course of the activity, a unity of consciousness with a unity of sense, a proposition for example, which in its founding includes relation to a substrate object, a subject of the proposition. These experiences arise because the ego functions as active, producing formations of sense and thematic unities that are conscious as produced and relate to what is conscious without being produced. Consciousness lies before all producing. The ego is subject for its universal consciousness, for the unity of manifold intentional experiences each of which is itself consciousness; this does not mean that it is always active, producing, interested, or even always affected. The unity of consciousness and of the stream of intentional experience it contains is rather the medium in which the ego lives, the medium of its active and passive participation. Through the intentionality of these experiences the ego gains possible relation to their contents, which are thereby there for it and can become objects of its contemplation and reflection. If the ego sleeps, it performs no action and perhaps undergoes no stimuli; then it is not even passive, if passivity means being affected, undergoing a stimulus from something that stands out. "Medium" is the new term: consciousness is not the ego's doing but the element in which it lives, so a sleeping ego still has a stream though it neither acts nor is affected.

(45–46) Hence a double concept of the ego's life. First, the universal consciousness of the ego with its stream of experience: the life in which the ego constantly lives, the intentional medium through which it acts or is affected, and which it enriches with new experiences in doing so. Second, the manifold doing and being affected themselves: the life of the ego's participation, of its stepping onto the scene, enriching the stream through action and passion that occur "in the ego" itself, though through moments of that general medium. A note specifies that universal life contains nothing of objects, not even immanent ones, the hyle, though immanent objectivity belongs essentially to life as its correlate; alternatively one might distinguish the ego with all non-hyletic contents of inner consciousness, the ego and its intentionality, from the ego and the whole of immanence, which would then be the medium of life, a concept Husserl expects to give offence. The double concept separates living-in from taking-part; the note shows Husserl undecided whether hyle belongs to the ego's life, a decision the next units reverse.

(46) The centre ego is always present, whether it emerges, wakes, is awake, or not, and the stream of experience or consciousness always flows, whether or not it contains specific acts of the ego. Their mutual relatedness is essential; they are not a whole made of parts but an absolute unity. A certain potentiality connects the "egoless" consciousness, for which the ego, perhaps awake elsewhere, sleeps, with waking consciousness, the consciousness of a waking ego. The ego can wake to everything lived in its stream, which is why the stream is called its own, and can turn to its contents. "Egoless" means without the ego's participation, not without ego: the stream's ownership is defined by a potentiality of waking, which is how Appendix IV treats sleep and the "naked" monad.

(46) This unity of universal life in experiencing, with or without the ego's participation but always with its possible participation, Husserl calls monadic life. Taken in full concretion, together with the essential fact of the ego of this life, the ego related to its experiencing and the experiencing related to the ego taken in one, it is the monad. A note adds that the stream of hyletic data is here included in universal life, reversing the exclusion of the preceding note. The monad is defined by possible, not actual, participation, and the hyle is now inside it, as it must be if the monad is to be concrete.

(46) Does this exhaust the concept of monad? Not yet discussed are the ego as subject of its capacities, the ego and its habitual, and the ego and its "surroundings", its counterpart. Husserl names the two gaps he will address: the habitual ego, deferred once more, and the surrounding world, taken up in point five.

(46–47) First a supplement on monadic consciousness. After the study of the consciousness that constitutes inner time and constitutes all experiences as unities of immanent time, two continuities must be distinguished: the continuity of inner consciousness, which has its own form and is primally temporalizing for the constitution of immanent time; and the continuity of the inwardly conscious experiences, as continuity of conscious life flowing through the form of immanent time. Accordingly there is the monad in its last, absolute or primal form, and the monad as unity of "inner sense", constituted in the form of immanent time. The monad in the latter sense has its own kind of immanent, absolute truth of being and its own kind of concretion. The doubling of Appendix I recurs: primal monad and monad of inner sense correspond to the absolute flow and the constituted stream of Hua X (§34), and even the constituted monad keeps an absolute truth of being because it is immanent.

(47) Fifth, the surroundings. Every experience is consciousness of something and has intentional contents as distinct from its real ones. Husserl had distinguished the objective sense, the object in quotation marks, and this sense in its noematic modes. Everything that belongs in this way to the experience as consciousness-of, without which the experience is unthinkable, must be included in the monad. So must the enigmatic feature of possible conscious life that an object stands there in "belief" as existing, as actual, as something the ego in turning finds as an actuality over against it; the same holds for acts whose achievement is an "actual" formation, given in a single-rayed turning as an actuality over against the ego, such as an actual state of affairs or an actual content of judgment, a truth. The noematic correlate belongs inside the monad: what the reduction bracketed is retained as meant, and the monad's concretion includes its believed world.

(47) Through its intentional experiences the ego in any case has pregiven realities over against it, objects and objective connections in actuality, but also in probability, doubtfulness, possibility, and among possibilities as mere phantasy possibilities, ficta. The surroundings are graded by the modalities of belief, so the monad's world includes the doubtful and the fictive as modified forms of the same counterpart.

(47) However problematic and in need of clarification, we find the ego related by essence, through its consciousness, to "actualities", to surroundings in the widest sense. These surroundings are immanent insofar as immanent objects are constituted by intentionality, and transcendent insofar as they are transcendences. Surroundings in the widest sense reach into the immanent: they are defined by standing over against the ego, not by lying outside consciousness.

(47) If surroundings are the totality of what is "over against" the ego in the pregnant sense, given or pregiven without reflection, they must be distinguished from the totality of what can become objective for the ego at all. It belongs to the essence of the constituted monad that it can itself be given to the ego as an objectivity of inner consciousness, and that inner consciousness is constituted for itself and can come to givenness for the ego; otherwise we would know nothing of it. Every experience therefore not only is but is also an actuality for the ego belonging to its immanent surroundings, and so the whole monad and the ego itself belong to the ego's surroundings. These immanent surroundings are necessary for the ego. Transcendent surroundings can exist for it only through immanent experiences that perform transcendent sense-giving and positing of reality and that, with these features, themselves belong to the immanent surroundings. Two concepts of world are separated, the straightforwardly pregiven counterpart and the wider field of what can be objectified in reflection, which includes the monad and the ego; transcendence is always mediated by experiences belonging to the necessary inner world, which Appendix IV turns into a new concept of subjective and objective.

(48) Hyletic data have a special place in immanence. They are constituted in inner consciousness as immanent unities, are not themselves intentional experiences, but become the "material" for intentional experiences in immanent time. They enter external perceptions, for instance, as real components, as material of apprehension; they gain an intentional function there but have, as sensory data, nothing intentional in themselves. The intentionality of inner consciousness that constitutes them is foreign to them as what is constituted. Husserl's final note concedes that the "ego" has remained abstract and indeterminate throughout, abstracted from its being necessarily "personal" as a monadic ego. Hyle is immanent but ego-alien, constituted but non-intentional, which lets it count both as "subjective" and as the first "object" in Appendix IV; and the closing note admits that the whole construction has abstracted from the person, the very dimension Nr. 2 made central.

### Appendix III. The Self as a Non-Founded Unity (June 1921)

(48) Husserl wonders whether he should always say "self" instead of "ego". The identity at issue is the reflexive identity of a person with itself, not the identity of a pole.

(48) He quotes Leibniz's New Essays (II, 27, §9) in Kuno Fischer's German. A person is a thinking, intelligent being, capable of reason and reflection, that can represent itself as one and the same subject thinking at different times and places and that does everything with the consciousness of being itself the ground of its actions. This consciousness always accompanies our present sensations and representations when they are distinct enough, and by it each is for himself what is called, in the reflexive sense, a self; the identity of the person reaches as far as consciousness extends back over past actions and thoughts, and the self is now the same as it was then. Kern notes that the words are those of Philalethes, that is, of Locke. Husserl thus starts from Locke's memory criterion of personal identity, which the following units correct: identity cannot consist in extended consciousness, because the ego's identity precedes the contents consciousness extends over.

(48–49) One might try an analogy. In the external attitude I find the same thing in the continuous succession of its manifold states, or the same sensory datum, the selfsame concrete red as unity of now equal, now changing moments of red, its quasi-states. In reflecting on the self I would likewise find it as a unity, though of a wholly different kind, of its conscious states in the flowing succession of conscious life, which is in truth the life of the self. But there is an essential difference. "In" the external states the unity runs through them, constituting itself in their flow. The flowing experiences form the unity of the stream, but the unity of the self does not lie in them in the same sense. Against the succession of "delimited" acts and appearances we find the self, inseparable from them, but as the self that "performs" them or is affected by what appears, and all background with its potentialities is related to it too. The self is not another "phenomenon"; only the representation of the self, the grasping of it, is. Experience of an ego, experience of an experiencing self, has an essential pole of unity that is not constituted in the experiences the way, in all temporal being, a changing or unchanged unity is constituted in the continuity of a filled duration. The self is not the unity of its states, as a thing is of its own, because it performs the states rather than being what they add up to; it appears only as represented, never as a phenomenon in its own right.

(49) Yet the self is abstract relative to its experiences, and whoever says "I" or "my self" means something concrete. So the ego that lives itself out in its stream, together with that stream, is the concrete "self". The pole is abstract and the self concrete, the same pairing as pure and concrete ego in Appendix II.

(49) In this respect the case does resemble the thing. Looking at the continuously filled duration and running through its phases is one thing; looking at the unity of the red, or of the thing that endures and is the unity of its changes or changing states, is another. So with the self: when I speak of it I am directed at the ego as ego of its life, and to direct myself at the life itself is a different direction. The analogy holds for the direction of regard, which can rest on the identical or on the flow, and fails for the mode of identity.

(49–50) The ego is essentially related to every delimited experience and every objectivity: the reflection possible on any experience necessarily brings it into the form ego cogito. Kern notes that Husserl later put a question mark beside this sentence. If I reflect on this reflection, the form recurs, "I reflected on this cogito", and the ego appears twice, as ego of the reflection and as ego of that cogito; I recognize the identity of an ego posited twice but existing once, each time as ego of a different cogito. In continuously running reflection I find the ego continuously identical through all its acts and states, which are given as temporally extended experiences of immanent time. But the self, what is identical "throughout", is not temporal in the same sense, still less extended, as the experiences are. Because acts issue from the ego and affections enter it, they have, so to speak, a side that reaches out of the temporal extension and only stretches along with its continuity, running parallel to it in unity. One might say that the self is adumbrated in the constitution of inner consciousness as the enduring red is adumbrated in its retentional modifications. But the same holds of any ideal object, which comes to givenness in a temporal spread and so appears spread over a duration, yet is evidently irreal and does not endure as something real does, as a unity of contents each different through the duration. As pure ego the ego is identically the same in every phase of its enduring givenness, and not in the way a changing thing or a changing red is the same, constituting itself as the same only through the continuity of a content filling the duration. The pure ego has no different contents in different phases; it is absolutely identical in all of them. The concrete self has a changing content, but here the identity of the ego, as the identity that creates unity for all contents, precedes them; the unity of the self, even of the person, is not a mere identity in change, in a transition of phases. All changing contents entering the duration presuppose the ego's identity: what flows into time from the ego's activity has the ego before it, and whatever affects the ego presupposes it in order to affect it anew, and perhaps otherwise, from moment to moment and so give it a changing content. A note adds that the question of an ego and self in a dull, "naked" monad is a question of dynamis: for such a monad the ego would not be really on the scene, not "constituted" in immanent time; Leibniz is right that self-consciousness and delimited experience necessarily go together, a point worked out on a separate sheet (Appendix IV, 53f.). The ego is nearer to the ideal object than to the real thing: like a meaning, it is given across time without being made of temporal phases. The note turns the question of the ego's presence into a question of potency, with Leibniz's naked monads as the test case.

(50) In one case, the thing, we have a one-sidedly founded unity, found only in a continuous transition from different content to different content; what is separated, lying discontinuously in time, is not one but several. With the ego there is no such founded unity: it is one from the start and only enters into relation with what differs, though it cannot be separated from it. This shows in the fact that, given two disconnected intuitions of red, I can assert likeness and similarity but never identity; but when I "compare" myself in childhood and in manhood on the basis of disconnected single intuitions, I find myself, absolutely and numerically the same. This is the decisive objection to Locke's criterion. Personal identity needs no continuous chain of consciousness, because the ego is recognized as identical across a gap, which is possible only for a unity not founded on its contents. "Non-founded" uses the Third Investigation's term: the ego's unity does not rest on what it unifies.

### Appendix IV. The Monad in Its General Structure (June 1921)

(50–51) The ego as pole, as centre of its acts and affections, is necessarily the subject of intentional experiences and therefore has consciously "objects" of its acts, objects it is turned toward and busy with, or objects of its affection, what affects or could affect it. Being a pole entails a world: the ego is a pole only as a centre of relations to objects.

(51) Ego, consciousness, and object therefore belong together by essence, and the ego is an identical of a wholly different kind from its object. What is conscious as identical in manifold consciousness is wholly different from the ego that, as identical, is the subject of everything conscious as identical, of what persists as one through manifold consciousness or is actively identified. The triad ego–cogito–cogitatum is restated with its two identities kept apart: the object is identical across the manifold, the ego is identical as subject of that identification.

(51) Under the title "object", however, we also have everything distinguished here. The ego has an inner consciousness of its manifold consciousness, a consciousness related to itself that can itself become conscious; it can have a particular consciousness "directed" at particular consciousness, at single intentional experiences; and it is also conscious of itself as the identical ego of manifold consciousness. In the formal sense everything, consciousness and ego included, can become an object, so the formal concept does not separate the three, which forces the narrower concept that follows.

(51) Necessarily, though, there belongs to the ego a realm of objectivities that are neither consciousness nor ego. As centre of objective relatedness in general, the ego is specifically the centre of relations to "objects"; a note calls this the widest concept of object, the object-pole. The subject-pole has over against it manifold object-poles, each conscious but not consciousness, conscious straightforwardly and not through "reflection". A second note says that what is meant is the ego-alien individual, the object as individual, though it is unclear why eide and similar "formations" should not be included. "Object" in the narrow sense is what is conscious without being consciousness and is given without reflection; Husserl hesitates over whether ideal objects fall under it.

(51) Everything we call consciousness is, as the ego's life-medium, egoic and subjective in a special sense, a different sense from that of doing and suffering. The objective is what is thrown over against (Gegenwurf) the ego and everything subjective, and yet it is conscious in the subjective of consciousness and, in this intentional manner, a "correlate" in conscious "relation" to the ego. A note observes that Gegenwurf fits better the external "substances" in whose constitution empathy has played a part. Three grades of the subjective are distinguished: the ego, its doing and suffering, and the medium of consciousness. The note on Gegenwurf ties full objectivity, the counter-standing in-itself, to empathy, the thesis of Nr. 1 that intersubjective reality is the first true transcendence.

(51) The objective that potentially harbours objectivities as objects, a potential counter-pole, differs from the objective that has actually become an object. Every object conscious for itself, object in the special sense, is lifted out of a "field" of possible objects, a field "confusedly unitary" and open, which is not nothing. The field is the passive pregiven ground from which affection lifts objects out, the field of the lectures on passive synthesis.

(51–52) The objective has levels. First, the objective that as immanent already belongs to inner consciousness and makes something ego-alien conscious inwardly: the hyle and hyletic sensibility. Second, the objective that as transcendence is the object of outer sensibility, of intentional experiences that are themselves conscious in inner consciousness; it can be conscious only in this mediate way, through "appearances", as the unity of manifold appearances, with hyletic data serving as "adumbrations" and bearers of "apprehensions". By means of the immanent ego-alien objects as primal objects, transcendent objects constitute themselves for the ego, appearing in adumbrations and meant through appearances. Whether every ego must be conscious of transcendent objects as existing, presumably existing, or possibly existing must remain open. Hyle is the primal object and transcendence is founded on it; leaving open whether a monad without transcendent objects, a merely sensing monad, is possible keeps room for the lower levels of monads raised in Appendix I.

(52) This yields a new concept of objective and subjective. Since an ego with consciousness but without hyle is unthinkable, and hyle is given as really one with the egoic, hyle too is called subjective. Nothing transcendent can be given as really one with the subjective; it need not exist even when meant and experienced in person; it gives itself as independent of being meant and perceived, if it truly is. So the transcendent is called objective in the pregnant sense, standing over against the ego and everything subjective, everything inseparably and really one with ego and ego-life, as non-subjective. The criterion is real inclusion: hyle is ego-alien yet subjective because really one with the stream, while the transcendent is objective because it can be meant without existing and is meant as independent of being meant.

(52) The concrete nexus of everything in consciousness that is really and inseparably one, single intentional experiences, hyletic data, and backgrounds, existing in itself and for itself in this unity, makes up the monad. Defined by real inclusion, the monad excludes the transcendent world, which is only meant in it. This narrows Appendix II, which had included the noematic correlate.

(52) One difficulty remains. The ego's capacities and habitual subjectivity also belong to the ego or the monad. And one would have to discuss the ego as it finds itself in actual life as now present, with its now present and actually experienced content, and the ego that finds itself as subject of a past and a future, related to an endless time. Husserl must proceed further systematically, and all this belongs to the basic problem. What is the residuum of the phenomenological reduction, what remains uncancellable as pure ego and its cogito? Do I gain myself as monad, as mens, as animus, as substantia cogitans? In what sense, and how is a series of fundamental concept-formations to be carried out systematically? The residuum of the reduction is the question all four Appendices have circled: Descartes' terms are tested for whether the reduced ego is a monad with capacities and history or only a present cogito.

(52) Husserl judges that what all these sheets contain is not yet satisfactory. The verdict covers Appendices I–IV as one attempt.

(52) In the reduction, in purely immanent consideration, he finds ego, consciousness, and objectivity, but only immanent objectivity, without quotation marks. He finds the unity of the stream of experience as an immanent intentional unity, not in quotation marks, yet constituting itself in infinitum and uncancellable, with the difference between the primally impressionally given now and the now that is not given. The immanent is given without bracketing, but the stream's infinity is constituted rather than given, which already limits what the reduction secures with full evidence.

(52) He finds the "world" of transcendence, conscious in manifold consciousness, believed, confirming itself, presenting itself through hyletic adumbration. The world enters the inventory in quotation marks, as meant and believed, not as part of the residuum's real content.

(53) He finds the ego, himself, as active and suffering. The ego is found, not deduced, and found in the two modes of the elementary account in Nr. 2.

(53) He finds genesis: the way consciousness issues from consciousness in passive motivation, and necessary laws of reproduction, association, and the sedimentation of past experience in present experience. Genesis belongs to the residuum: the reduced field is not a timeless structure but contains its own history as a datum.

(53) But he also finds "sediments on the ego", what is peculiar to his ego. What issues from him in "I do" and what affects him in "I suffer" hangs together with the ego and has its own kind and rule, not conditioned by the passivity of association: resolution, will directed to the future, the ego intervening in its own life. From there one must go further, to the understanding of an ego and the understanding of it as this individuality. Two kinds of sediment are distinguished, those in the stream, governed by association, and those on the ego, governed by its own rule, Nr. 2's individual law of acts. "Understanding an ego as this individuality" is the step to empathy as knowledge of persons.

(53) The monad's general structure is therefore not easy to know. At the start everything must be kept open; there are orders of procedure and of givenness, orders of constituting and constituted, and first of all come the pure ego and flowing consciousness, not even the endless stream of consciousness but only the phenomenon of the ego cogito in its "living" present. The starting point narrows to the living present, the only thing not already constituted as a stream. The living present appears here for the first time in the volume as a methodological starting point, the role the late manuscripts give it and the one First Philosophy (Hua VIII) tests in its critique of apodictic evidence.

(53) One more point is important. In the dull, "naked" monad the ego is not really present as a central pole to which everything would be related. There is properly no "everything" there, since everything is a flowed-together unity. But what "really present" means, whether a mere potentiality or something else, is open. The naked monad is Leibniz's, but the criterion is phenomenological: without differentiation there is nothing for the ego to be the pole of.

(53–54) In any case one would have to say that if the ego woke and could catch a stretch of its previous unconscious state, it could not find itself in it, by reflection turned backward, as the ego that had belonged to it. That is questionable, one will say. But this much is certain: self-consciousness and delimited experience necessarily go together. Only where experiences stand out is the ego "there", pregiven, not only experiencing but experienced. Only where experiences stand out, where hyletic data lie ready "for themselves", or representations for themselves, even empty ones, can affection occur, and there it necessarily does. What stands out is related to the ego, and the ego as pole is in function and is there for itself; only then can there be reflection. The ego is given only through a reflection that starts from something objective for the ego, conscious or represented for itself, but then it is always possibly given. It is evident that wherever an object is there, the ego that was directed at it in representing it is reflectively graspable. Delimited representation, unitary self-enclosed representing in the widest sense of Leibniz's perception, what is represented as an objectivity standing out for it, and the centring on the ego belong necessarily together. There are thus two reflections starting from the object: onto the ego directed at the object (being affected by it and turning anew toward it are already reflections), and onto the "representing", the consciousness and its features "in" which the ego was directed, perceiving, reproductive representing, and so on. The ego's presence depends on differentiation in the field: no standing-out, no affection, no ego for itself. Husserl reads Leibniz's distinction between perception and apperception as a correlation between contrast in the field and centring on the ego.

(54) This is plainly important for the doctrine of innermost consciousness as well. If a manifold stream of consciousness is there with things standing out, its ego is awake: it is there with them, itself standing out, in constant function as identity-pole of affections and actions, pregiven for itself and given in a reflection possible at any time. All "consciousness", every intentional and every hyletic experience, is conscious; it is graspable for the ego, which is itself conscious and in this respect on a par with experiences, and graspable as already conscious before being grasped. Even the unconscious that comes to consciousness has its way of having been there for me and pregiven. The ego is itself one of the things that stand out, which is why it can be grasped like an experience; and "conscious before being grasped" extends even to what was unconscious, treated as a mode of pregivenness rather than an absence.

(54) May one not say that in the contrast between swoon and waking, the previously ungrasped and unseparated experience, the hyletic for instance, also comes to stand out, and that I too was already the same, only "self-consciousness" was impossible, my standing out for myself and being pregiven for a possible reflection? Sleep would be just this: nothing affects for itself, and so the ego is not for itself as the identical of changing affections and actions. The proposal revises the claim two units back that the waking ego could not find itself in its unconscious past: the ego was there and the same, but not for itself.

(54) Then standing out and affecting would come apart, or the concept of affection would have to be reconceived. What stands out has different affective force, and the ego has different receptivity; it is affected when, in a given case, the force suffices for the object to come to proper "consciousness". Affection becomes a relation between two variables, the force of what stands out and the ego's receptivity. This answers the question Appendix II left open and applies to sleep the theory of affective force from the lectures on passive synthesis (Hua XI, §§32–35).

### No. 3. Lived Body – Thing – Empathy. Linking Soul and Body (St. Märgen, Summer Vacation 1921)

#### §1. The "Subjectivist" Attitude and the Naturalistic Apprehension. Self-Experience and the Constitution of the Bodily-Psychic Reality as a Psychophysical Unity

(55) Husserl opens with two apprehensions of the human being. In the first, the living apprehension of the ego, the ego-subject and everything subjective are grasped in the "subjectivist" or personal attitude, and the human being counts as a personal being. In the second, the naturalistic one, the human being counts as a natural being, a "psychophysical" being. These are the personalistic and naturalistic attitudes of Ideen II (Hua IV, §49), and the text asks which of them is original for my own case.

(55) On the first: I find myself as an ego that has things over against it through appearances whose course I can influence, that can turn by choice to this or that thing around it and take pleasure in it. I find myself as having this body as a thing over which I have power, with organs I move freely, which serve as organs of perception for all other things (and for the body itself) and as practical organs for reshaping other things. Self-experience starts from power, not from observation: the body is first what I can move and use.

(55–56) This ego, behaving passively or actively, and especially relating to its body as the ego that puts it into function and exercises power over it, is not given in reflective self-experience as psychophysically connected with this body, not as a psychophysical reality one with the physical body. When I reflect on my person I do not find that reality. My body is given to me on the one hand as a physical thing (a real, causal spatial thing) and on the other as a lived body. The thesis of the whole text is stated at once: the psychophysical unity "human being" is not found by reflection on oneself, so it must come from somewhere else, and Husserl will locate that source in empathy.

(56–57) Of the body we have physical experience, perception of it as a thing, and also somatological perception. For the latter the main problem is its structure of sense: which strata does the lived body have by essence, such that if they were fully given in perception the body itself would be given originarily? The stratum of sensitivity yields what is localized in the body: hyletic data, kinaesthetic data, sensitivity to pain. Then free mobility and the articulation into freely movable organs over which I have immediate power, functioning as organs of perception, of voluntary and purposive intervention in physical nature, of instinctual satisfaction (nutrition, reproduction). A note asks whether the body as outer organ can be distinguished from the body as inner organ. The body is perceived without all these functions being actually in play, so with an open but articulated horizon. Somatological perception is given a layered noematic structure, sensing field below and organ-functions above, the layers of Ideen II's "aesthesiological" and "volitional" body (§§36–38).

(56–57) The body can also enter experiential relations to subjective spheres and acquire horizons regarding them that are extra-essential to it. Drinking coffee stimulates my thinking and phantasy; reaching the goal of a willing, with the satisfaction it brings, "acts" perceptibly on bodily activity. The horizon of experience widens. But does the apperception "lived body", the sense of the perception as such, widen? Do new basic determinations accrue to bodiliness? What is perceived of the body immediately and non-relationally? However much subjective material enters the body's horizon of experience, it does not constitute what we call the psychophysical unity of body and soul, in which the body is taken as a physical thing, even in the sense of physics and biology. Learned correlations between bodily states and mental life are not yet the psychophysical apperception: they relate the lived body to my experiences, whereas the psychophysical unity relates a physical thing to a soul.

#### §2. The Phantom Perception of the "Body" as Foundation of the Apperception of the Lived Body on the One Hand and of the Apperception of the Bodily Thing on the Other

(57) In all experience of things the lived body is co-experienced as functioning, not as mere thing, and where it is itself experienced as a thing it is experienced doubly and at once, as experienced thing and as functioning body. A note restricts this: the body functioning as organ of perception functions only for the constitution of the phantom. Does this not suggest that in original experience, as my body and before all objectivation through intersubjectivity, my body is not experienced with a lower stratum "physical thing-body" at all? That can only mean that the perception of the body as lived body does not draw on what constitutes the bodily thing as real, as a substantial-causal thing. Two higher apperceptions therefore radiate from the phantom perception "body": the apperception of the lived body, and, in another attitude to the actual experience, the apperception of the bodily thing as a physical thing. A second note withdraws part of this, referring to the correction printed as Appendix VI: if that correction is right, the two apperceptions interpenetrate, but the apperception of the physical body is not essentially the foundation of the apperception of the body as functioning organ; constitutive analysis must go deeper, and the account as stated is questionable. The phantom, the spatial-sensory schema without causal properties, is made the common root, so that the lived body is not founded on the physical body but both are founded on the phantom; Husserl's own hesitation concerns whether the two branches are as independent as this suggests.

#### §3. The Lived Body as What Is Most Originally Mine. The Union of the Ego with the Body in Solipsistic Self-Experience

(57–58) In the natural attitude, as a living, functioning ego not reflecting on myself and my experience, I am an experiencing, thinking, willing ego, directed theoretically and practically at my natural themes, the themes of "straight regard". The analysis of the body as mine starts before reflection, in functioning.

(58–59) Straight regard faces above all the physical world, the spatial world oriented around the zero point of orientation, and everything spiritual, culture, has physical sides or supports (poems, artworks) and so also lies "outside". As functioning ego I relate to my spatial world as constantly active, guided by goals I attain or miss. Everything attained is appropriated; I am with it, and it remains mine as long as I have not lost or given it up.
