Among all spatial things in my practical sphere, "my" body is what is most originally mine: originally and constantly my own, always at my disposal, the most original and the only immediate thing at my disposal, the first thing I appropriated (as a child) and now the organ and means for appropriating everything in the world of straight regard. Husserl's note defines it as that in which I function immediately in space and through which I function for everything else: I "live immediately in the body", and through it I am near or far from other things. (How spirit must be expressed in the physical and then embodied is reserved for separate study.) The body thus has the most original character of what is mine and belongs to me, contrasted with what is alien, in which I take no practical part. The greatest alienness here is that I merely experience external things, in pure passivity; as soon as I want to know them, my intention aims at appropriating them in experience, and their being and truth become my own, mediated by my body as active organ of perception. Of all things my body is nearest to perception, feeling, and will, and so I as functioning ego am one with it in a special way. It is the centre, the object standing in the middle; though itself an object over against me, it becomes the functional centre for all other objects and all my functions toward them, that from which and through which I reach, appropriate, and shape everything else. "Mine" is defined practically, as appropriation, and the body is the first appropriation and the means of every later one; the ownness of the body is the root of the later concept of a sphere of ownness (Eigenheitssphäre) in the Fifth Cartesian Meditation (§44).

(59) Reflecting on myself as ego of these functions, I find this special belonging of the body to the ego as "mine", mine as nothing else can be, and necessarily involved in every appropriation. Before reflection, however, the ego is already constituted as functional centre and as habitual ego of the functions it has actually performed and that have settled on it, and it develops further in this respect. It is constituted as constantly "one" with its body, having it present and exercising all other outward functions through it as organ. Over against it stands the partly known, partly open surrounding world: things already appropriated, bearing the stamp of my functioning subjectivity, sediments of meaning from such functions that point back to my functioning and to my bodily activities; and things that touched me only fleetingly, or things of the open horizon, wholly unknown and not drawn into my purposive activity, yet unknown utilities and harms, and so related to a possible horizon of function. The surrounding world is constituted as a field of sedimented and possible bodily functions, so that even the unknown is pre-structured by the body's practical horizon.

(59–60) Solipsistic self-experience prefigures by essence a series of structures: ego and ego-alien; practical ego and ego-alien at its practical disposal; specifically, I as practical ego and the spatial world. Dominion over the spatial ego-alien is exercised through a spatial thing immediately available and always ready, the spatial body. A division therefore necessarily arises: I together with my body, and the world outside me, outside my body, which has been constituted as a physically real, causal world in my functioning, a functioning that, turned back on my body, has constituted it too, starting from its phantom content, as a physical thing. But if the ego "unites" with the body in this way and, so united, faces its outer surroundings, this unity does not constitute a double reality, body-ego plus my subjectivity, set against the rest of the world. Still less is it what "external" psychophysical experience posits as the unity of physical body and soul in the unity human being or animal of natural-scientific psychology. The functional unity of ego and body is real and primary, but it is not a composite of two realities; the composite belongs to the external view.

#### §4. The Influence of the Experience of Others on the Self-Experience of the Body. The Primacy of Somatological Perception over the Physical Perception of My Body

(60) Leaving solipsistic experience, Husserl asks how we experience the other and how this affects our self-experience apperceptively. In empathy I perceive the foreign body physically as a physical thing given to me externally like other things, and so at first not as a lived body at all. But an ego-subject expresses itself in it, experiencing this same thing as its body (as I experience mine) and united with it as described above, a functioning subject with its constant organ there. Since the foreign subjectivity, an ego with its body related to its appearing surroundings and living a full life in them, expresses itself in the body appearing externally to me, the physical thing identified with that "body for him", and is appresented through this expressive thing, the foreign ego, analogized in empathy with "me", is localized in my space and world of appearance. Kern notes that Husserl later struck this and the next paragraph. The other is placed in my space through his body; this is the first localization of subjectivity in the volume, and it happens only for the other.

(60) My own ego does not localize itself in my space of appearance; it does not localize itself in my body. The asymmetry is the crux of the text: localization of an ego in space is achieved for the other before it is achieved for me, and Husserl's later deletion probably reflects doubt about the flat claim that I am nowhere in my own space, since I am already "here" as the body's functional centre.

(60–61) The foreign body is physically perceived by me as a physical thing, and only as one; as a lived body it is apperceived by appresentation, in which its specifically somatological features are there for me only appresentatively. These features are apperceptive sediments of the functioning foreign ego and are originally constituted, and so "perceivable" in their way, only for that ego. The whole foreign ego and its life is appresented, however indeterminately, in one with the appresentative apprehension of this thing as a lived body by virtue of its "analogy" with my body and of its movements with my functional movements. Once a piece or moment of a subjectivity is appresented, the necessary categorial form of unity of any subjectivity awakens a whole subjectivity with all its categorial components and correlations. It is thus represented appresentatively as a modification of my subjectivity, from which the awakening proceeds. Two theses here govern the later theory: appresentation is holistic, because subjectivity has a categorial form that one appresented moment calls up entire; and the other is presented as a modification (Abwandlung) of me, the formula of the Fifth Meditation's "intentional modification" (Hua I, §52).

(61–62) For me in the solipsistic "world-view", every external thing, as experienced, is experienced as physical, since only so is it constituted and made a theme of experience. My own body I can also apprehend as a physical thing, and in the developed ego it is constituted as one too; but for it I have somatological perception as lived body, which evidently comes first and is in itself first for me as functioning ego, while apprehending or "perceiving" it as a physical thing is second. To regard my body purely as a thing like others I need an artificial attitude, and although it counts for me as a thing and has long been habitually constituted as one, this sense plays no "role" while I function. Its givenness as lived body, as functional organ, is not founded in its givenness as physical thing; a note limits this to the general sense in which practice everywhere precedes "theory". The rest of the passage, which Husserl struck with a reference to Appendix VI, argues constitutively. Above all for the body as organ of perception, a body must already be constituted and functioning as perceiving body before the apprehension "physical thing" could arise, since the phantom must be constituted before physical things, and for that a most primitive "body" must already precede. Once phantoms are constituted, for the body and then for other things, the perceiving body founded in the phantom functions as organ for the constitution of the real thing; once external reality is constituted, the body too becomes apprehensible as a physically real thing and is so apprehended. Even apprehending the subjective movement of the whole body, locomotion, as movement in space (geometrical and mechanical) is secondary and complex, posterior to the constitution of the movement of external things, which are the first things simply. In developed consciousness every apprehension of one's own body as a thing "like another" is complex: "like another" signals a detour. An arbitrary thing need not be apprehended "like another"; it is simply perceived. If my body is simply perceived, I have not perceived it as a thing unless I make the detour "like another thing" (as if). One may think here that the body can be represented intuitively as moving, and so as a proper spatial thing, only by imagining it outside, "seen from a there", which already presupposes a doubling of the ego and so leads over to empathy. The struck argument is that the physical body is a late, detour-constituted sense of my body, reached only through an imagined external standpoint; Appendix VI challenges exactly this, and Kern's introduction names the "moving out there" of the body as the device the 1927 texts render superfluous.

(62–63) In any case, my own body cannot be genuinely perceived as a physical thing by me, only by another. Since my body is a perceptual given for me that presupposes no one else (as constituted other), a solipsistic perceptual given in this sense, it cannot have a founding lower stratum of sense "physical body". Husserl's note anticipates the objection that in my body's unique modes of givenness the specifically somatological element of the sensing body is inseparably one with something constituted as in any intuitive external thing. Then I would first have to abstract, and abstraction is the problem here: what is abstracted is not something that could be removed and inserted into arbitrary things, not even into the foreign body; "empathy is not insertion" (Einfühlung ist nicht Einfügung). In the solipsistically constituted world there are necessarily an external world and a body, and all being relates to the solus through its possible modes of givenness; the necessarily functioning body has a stratum of physical properties, but inseparably from the somatological; only in the objectively, that is intersubjectively, constituted world does the stratum "bodily thing" become the appearance of an in-itself in objective nature. The text concedes that solipsistic perception does not yield the reality of the body and so no fully constituted real world. At this level the external world is constituted as real, though without intersubjective objectivity, and my body is constituted as in "a certain way" spatial and thing-like, but with such differences that it is something wholly its own and, in that ownness, the founding lower stratum for perception as functioning organ. A note specifies this stratum as the passively constituted aisthēton, sensing field, and subjective moving, which only in spiritual relation to the ego becomes organ, spiritual body, the primally spiritual object. Only with intersubjectivity would there be, if this is confirmed, a full nature even for the single subject, who then stands in an intersubjective association of persons. The formula "empathy is not insertion" rejects the view that I build the other by abstracting my psyche from my body and inserting it into his: there is no detachable stratum to transfer, because in my own case the somatological and the physical are inseparable. Full objective nature waits on intersubjectivity.

#### §5. The Mediacy of the Experience of One's Own Body as a Physical Thing. The Constitution of All-Nature, or of Objective Subjectivity

(63–64) On this account the first body experienced as a physical thing is the foreign body, experienced by appresentation in its specific bodiliness, as aisthēton (sensory bodiliness) and as spiritual body, which in turn is perceived originally by the appresented ego. Only by the detour through the other and possible others do I learn to experience my own body as a physical thing, wholly mediately. It is physically experienceable for every other and a possible expression of my self-experienced bodiliness, and the other actually facing me is experienced appresentatively as experiencing me externally and physically in my bodiliness. Once I have performed this "apprehension" on my own body and given it the sense of a physical thing, I have a universal nature in which this body is a thing among others, and the relation of the physical thing "my body" to my subjectivity is for me what it is for the others I experience. All-nature is constituted as objective by the constitution of a nature for all, in which all bodies become physical things like others and lose the distinction each has for its subject (as spatial object, apart from its function). My body in its physical truth is the unity of the possible perceptions that any other could have of my body. My self-perception of it is an exceptional, merely subjective perception, objectively significant only when interpreted through recourse to empathies. This self-appearance is itself empathized and serves to ascribe a subjectivity to the intersubjective physical thing "bodily thing": it indicates for everyone a subjectivity that experiences this bodily thing as lived body and through it relates, in consciousness and act, to the intersubjective world. Husserl inverts the natural order: the objective truth of my body is the set of its possible appearances to others, and my own self-appearance becomes the exception that must be interpreted through theirs. The thesis of Nr. 1, that my humanity is borrowed from the other's view, is here worked out for the body.

(64–65) Only in external experience, then, is a bodily thing given as a mere physical thing: for me, the foreign bodily thing at its objective place in nature and space, in causal connection with other things. This physical body I find united by appresentation with a foreign subjectivity, a functioning ego living in its stream of life and experiencing this same bodily thing in its subjective way, somatologically, as organ of its perceptual and other functions, related to a surrounding world experienced by this ego in such and such modes, normal or distorted by illusion. That experienced world is, in its truth, there for me too, experienceable or experienced in my experiences, a world containing things and persons. So I experience the foreign subjectivity as related to the objective world, and through its body. When I posit this bodily thing as intersubjectively experienceable, true nature, a member of physical all-nature, there accrues to it, as to all similar "bodily things", a surplus over what characterizes and constitutes every thing as a physical body according to external experience. It expresses a subjectivity, with everything subjective belonging to an ego as described above, by appresenting it. The body is a physical thing plus a surplus of expression, and the surplus belongs to the thing only as posited in intersubjective nature.

(65) In the spatio-temporal-causal order of nature, at the place of each bodily thing, something co-belonging and co-existing appears, a subjectivity, which is thus localized in objective nature in intersubjective truth, a "second nature", an annex to the natural, and yet not physical. The physical as such has its own constitutive sense-giving, to be confirmed by actual perception and for everyone, and nothing psychic is constitutive for the physical. The soul is localized in nature but not natural in the physical sense: it is an annex, which leaves physics complete in itself and prepares the question of §8 about how far psychophysical regularities reach.

(65) The objective physical thing of nature is the thing for everyone, the unity of appearances that are anyone's possible appearances, "exchangeable" through mutual understanding and identifiable through their synthesis of unity. So with physical all-nature. Objectivity is defined by exchangeability of appearances, which makes communication, not a view from nowhere, the criterion of the objective.

(65) Objective subjectivity is subjectivity as object for everyone. It is an object for everyone through the identification, carried out on the basis of empathic appresentations, of externally experienced "bodies" as physical bodies with the appresented, somatologically experienced lived bodies. Through the further appresentation of the subjectivity interwoven with the somatologically experienced bodiliness, the empathizer co-apprehends it, and it is recognizable as identical for everyone who experiences the same bodily thing and understands it as expression. Everyone can experience everyone's body as the same thing and everyone's subjectivity as the same subjectivity. Everyone can identify his self-experienced body with the one the other experiences as a mere thing and recognize this identical intersubjective body as a member of physical nature; then his subjectivity too belongs to this thing, as any other's belongs to his bodily thing. Objective subjectivity requires a double identification: of the outer body with the appresented lived body, and of my self-experienced body with the body the other sees. The second is how I become, for myself, one subject-object among others.

(65) So we all have one objective world, one all-nature, and different subjectivities belonging to its different bodily things. This is the result in its naturalistic form; the next section asks what "belonging" means.

#### §6. The Connection of Body and Soul

(66) How is this belonging to be understood and described? How are bodily thing and soul "connected"? Only the constitutive sense of the apperception can tell, the apperception in which objective nature and objective subjectivity "in" nature are constituted and with them their "connection". Husserl answers the metaphysical question of the union of soul and body by constitutive analysis, not by choosing among interactionism, parallelism, and identity.

(66) Objective all-nature is a spatio-temporal-causal unity, and all bodily things belong in it. Souls are the objectified subjectivities: the contents appresented through each bodily thing, but ideally also appresentable. To them belong all my experiences, everything I can establish in myself purely phenomenologically, by practising the reduction and excluding nature as an existence I have accepted as actual and now presuppose in cognition. I retain all experiences, all hyletic data, intentional experiences, acts, all noematic contents and everything ontic in brackets, exactly as I can find them "in me"; "I myself" am always the identical ego for all this. All of it I find related to the form of immanent time, which now "coincides" with the space-time of nature. The soul is the reduced subjectivity reinserted into nature: the contents of the reduction are exactly what objectification localizes at a body. This makes the soul a constituted sense of transcendental subjectivity, the "parallelism" of psychology and phenomenology that the Encyclopaedia Britannica article (Hua IX) later states.

(66) To the constitutive content of my body for me belong, on the one hand, the moments that make it the appearance of an objective natural thing, the bodily thing, and on the other, parallel subjective, somatological strata: subjective movement and mechanical movement. With this we have "causal" (conditional) factual relations between the bodily and the psychic. How far this "causality", constituted apperceptively from the start, reaches is open. The psychophysical relation begins as the parallel of two descriptions of one body, which makes the "causality" between them conditional dependence within one apperception, not an interaction between two substances.

(66) A line of "causal" connections between physical bodiliness and the psychic must therefore be traced, including whatever enables bodiliness, as against other things, to express. Every expression is an indication by regular empirical connection, which, Husserl remarks, is really a tautology. A note asks whether this is really causality, since causality seems essentially to involve a necessity of change in the consequent. Treating expression as empirical indication puts it on the side of induction; the note's doubt about "causality" is the one Nr. 2 raised about causal necessity in nature (13).

#### §7. The Regulations in the Constitution of Things. The Lived Body as Index of Psychic Normality and Anomaly

(67) In subjectivity a physical thing is constituted through regulations of associations relative to circumstances. The thing's existence indicates a definite such regulation relative to definite circumstances; as objective existence it indicates a universal regulation, spanning all experiencing subjects, of the coexisting and changing phantoms of the thing relative to those of other things, which have their unity in such regulations too, among them physical bodies. This universal regulation, in which alone lies the self-preservation of the concordance of all realities that are to exist in the form of coexistence (space) and succession in space (objective time), is linked with a second regulation; a note points ahead to the insertion of biological regulation. Objective reality is defined as a rule across all subjects' phantoms, so that the concordance of nature is a fact about intersubjective experience.

(67) The first regulation is that of normal communicating subjects, thought of as anomalous in no respect, not even falling into transient anomalous states. It is therefore an ideal abstraction. Objective nature as physics describes it presupposes idealized normal observers.

(67) Admitting the anomalous means that every subject, not as subject of acts but with respect to its passivity, has possible normality and anomaly, and the index of these differences is the lived body. A note marks that a basic structure in objective real nature must first be supplied. Normality and anomaly belong to the passive stratum, the stratum Nr. 2 had declared indifferent to the ego's identity; a subject's acts are not "normal" or "anomalous" in this sense.

(67–68) The lived body is an "organic individuality", not merely a physical-physicalistic one. The universal physical regulation of nature is not the only one; the regulation of biology also governs. In nature there is a supra-physical order according to which being arranges itself by organic life and life-sustaining circumstances, and nature is a realm of organic individuals that preserve their individuality relative to their typically persisting and changing external circumstances and also serve as circumstances for one another. As objective, this regulation is also an indicating regulation for the subjective constitution of nature in the subjects. Biology's order is not reducible to physics, and it matters for constitution because the organism, as index of normality, regulates how nature appears.

(68) My body is above all the organ of external perception, and every bodily change that alters it anomalously also alters the perceptual appearance. Anomaly enters constitution through the body's role as organ.

(68) Normality is a form belonging to constitution, governing the constitution of spatial givens, of external things and of one's own bodiliness. Deviation from the norm can affect single organs in describable ways and can be ascertained through the normal function of organs that remain normal. From there the description of anomaly continues. Anomaly can affect the modes of appearance an organ "yields"; the organ can become useless for perception; several or all organs can be affected. Anomaly, and the discord it produces in the appearing world and in the courses normally to be expected, is first something the subject itself can ascertain, even the solipsistic subject; then also something intersubjective, intersubjectively knowable. Moreover, an organ is something constituted, known through experience, but only to a certain extent. The child has organs but knows nothing of them. We all have organs, countless ones, functioning in thousands of ways, that do not fall within our experience. Are organs that function "unconsciously", that I do not experience even in imperfect appearances and cannot steer deliberately, organs in the same sense (ganglion cells, nerve fibres versus hand, eye, ear)? Here lie the enormous problems of a theory of anomalies. Normality is a constitutive form, not a statistical average; anomaly is first detected within one subject by the concordance of its other organs. The distinction between experienced organs and physiological organs opens the gap between the lived body and the body of science that §8 exploits.

(68–69) In consciousness a world of normality is constituted as the first true world, and over against it anomalous appearances of the true world, related to modifications of experienced bodiliness, of the organic individual, or to the biological anomalies of the real biophysical body constituted within normal experience. These biophysical anomalies are intersubjectively knowable, within the normal, intersubjectively exchangeable system of experiences, as is biophysical normality, the structure of the body in its normal biophysical individuality. But the anomalous appearances I have are bound to my anomalous body not for everyone but only for me, the subject of this body, who has it as my organ of perception and action. Others understand me through empathy as subject of anomalous appearances when they recognize the anomaly objectively. The anomalous body is public, the anomalous appearances private: others reach them only by empathy guided by the objective finding, which is how colour-blindness, for example, is known.

(69) For every subject of world-experience a system of normal experience is thus constituted as an intersubjective system. Everyone is subject of a body that appears to himself. If it is a normal body, a normal biophysical individual, he has, in its normal biophysical "functioning" and the parallel normal subjective functioning, normal world-appearances in which a true world appears to him. The normal body can become anomalous in various ways, and then anomalous appearances arise; finally there is an anomaly that destroys the biophysical individual, biophysical death, in which the body ceases to be a lived body and to appresent any psychic life. Husserl adds that he should first have said that the body is not only an organ of normal and anomalous perceptions but a lived body in general and the expression of a psychic life, and that disturbances of the biophysical body's normality also yield "psychic disturbances" beyond the perceptual function, deviations of psychic types from the norm. Subjectivity has a normality in having normal systems of experience relative to a certain normality of the corporeal body; it also has normality in pleasure and pain, instincts, drives, and conduct, and these too show relations to the normality of the body. In experience the human being is thus constituted from the outset as a psychically typical individuality with an idea of normality and anomalies branching from it, related to a typical organic individuality, to a normal and anomalous body. Death enters the volume as the anomaly that ends appresentation: the corpse is a body that no longer expresses, defined phenomenologically by the failure of empathy.

(69–70) This empirical constitution always results in an apperception with open horizons, which the further course of constituting experiences determines and enriches. We learn to know bodiliness ever more completely in "biological" respects, as normal and anomalous; in dependence on that, the normal soul with its normal psychic life and the anomalous deviations; and how anomaly of bodiliness conditions anomaly of soul and vice versa. One might speak of a parallelism in typology, related on both sides to a normal idea: physiological psychology of humans and animals. The parallelism Husserl admits is a parallelism of types relative to norms, not a point-for-point correspondence of physical and mental events.

(70) It must now be considered how far this reaches and how, within its range, the psychic "expresses" the physical and conversely. There is also physiognomic expression of foreign psychic life, expression in linguistic communication, and the manifestation of personality and its character in thinking (and style of thinking), valuing, and acting. Expression in the strict sense, physiognomy and speech and style, exceeds psychophysical correlation; the person manifests itself as Nr. 2 described, through its position-takings, not through organs.

#### §8. Physiological Psychology and Its Limit: The Intellectus Agens

(70) We have a natural science: a universal physics, physical science in the usual sense, physical zoology and anthropology as biophysical sciences. As such they presuppose intersubjective experience and therefore empathy to a certain extent: the exchange of mutually empathizable external experiences, but also personal achievement and the mutual understanding and taking over of achievements (tradition). Natural science depends on empathy twice, for the exchange of observations and for tradition, the theme Husserl develops in "The Origin of Geometry" (Hua VI, Appendix III).

(70–71) Mutual understanding presupposes "expression in bodiliness": bodiliness in inner and outer total appearances, like other spatial objects, identifiable again and again in changing appearances in the course of experience, above all thinking. Expression presupposes typical similarity with one's own appearing body as a whole and in many individual traits and articulations standing out within it. In the relation of subjectivity to subjectivity, with their appearances, acts of thought, and the like given to each other in relatively determinate ways, the objective world and the true objectivity of science are constituted. So an objectively true animality and humanity are constituted as zoological-physical, together with a "parallel" objective psychic animality, an "objectively true" psychic being, such that what is scientifically known in biophysics can be allowed to indicate a correspondent in the objective soul. Scientific objectivity depends on expression, and expression depends on the typical similarity of bodies, the problem that runs from Nr. 1 to the 1927 analyses of pairing.

(71) Physics can be pursued without psychology. What of the objective "soul"? Is it not the nature of physiological psychology to seek, for the bodily as objectively known or at least experimentally experienced in the direction of objectivity, the corresponding element of the soul, the psychic parallel? A note specifies that this is a theoretical attitude presupposing physical nature as its theme. And, conversely, to take psychic anomalies as a starting point in order to seek the physical parallel and "explain" them by it? Physiological psychology is defined by its direction of inquiry, from the physical to its psychic parallel, and so presupposes nature as already constituted.

(71) In the objective-theoretical attitude, directed at the one ego-alien nature first given intersubjectively in experience, physical bodiliness is the prior objectivity. Nature is the first object of objective scientific research, and when the subjectivity connected with physical bodies, psychic being in nature, is in question, the first result is the objective regulation: biophysical structures of bodiliness have psychic parallels to a wide extent, and changes in such biophysical features bring about psychic changes, as voluntary experiments can show. Typology and its change on the one side (typology belongs to the essence of biophysical individuality) have their "causal" correlate on the psychic side in a corresponding typology. Psychic types can often be discerned without a universal study of subjectivity, as on the physical side. In the end one needs neither ultimate knowledge on one side nor on the other, only relative and approximate knowledge on both. Husserl concedes the legitimacy and practical sufficiency of psychophysics within its range: it works with types and approximations and needs no transcendental foundation to be useful.

(71–72) But how far does this objective, natural-causal psychic research reach, in which the soul is regarded as tied to physical bodiliness, joining and changing in parallel with the body in its biophysical typology, "causally" interwoven with it? Everything given, all objectivity, presupposes functioning subjectivity, and subjectivity is pregiven to itself before all objective cognition, which is itself only a function of subjectivity; only because it is, with all the formations that make empathy possible, can it empathize. How far it can be capable of knowing foreign psychic life, and what the conditions are for knowing it in infinitum, is a transcendental question, as is how far a psychophysical "parallelism" of the factual kind is a condition of the possibility of scientific knowledge of human beings, an objective and unlimited science of the soul. In any case the knowing subject finds itself inwardly, before all objectification, as person, insofar as it is already, solipsistically, a subject of acting and creating, of "achieving" in freedom, on the underground of affection and of the genesis of ever new intentionalities in which objects pregiven to the ego and stimulating it are constituted. The ego acts on its body and through it outward, in its solipsistic world. That world then becomes the subjective givenness of an intersubjective, objective world presenting itself in the ego insofar as it experiences others; and as the ego enters into relations with others, communicates, acts on them spiritually and undergoes their effects, the intersubjective world is constituted through science, and a cultural world with other cultural forms. The limit of physiological psychology is that it presupposes the subjectivity that constitutes its objects; the person is already a free agent before objectification, so that personhood is not a product of the psychophysical apperception but its precondition.

(72) How can the personal subject enter the nature it itself constitutes, become a psychophysical parallel, something dependent on the constituted objective body? What is it that stands, and can alone stand, under the title "psychic parallel" in the objective causal world of nature? Can constituting, active thinking and the like function in it, and how? This is the paradox of human subjectivity, constituting the world and being part of it, that the Crisis (§§53–54) makes the central difficulty of transcendental phenomenology; it is posed here in 1921 in the vocabulary of psychophysics.

(72–73) The other is a full subjectivity, an ego-subject with intentional experiences and acts, with its world-phenomenon, its body-phenomenon, and its secure empirical knowledge of its world and of its body as thing and as organ. This ego acts on its body and on its surroundings. It can act only if the body is passively constituted and has certain structures; and if the body changes passively, naturally, the ego's possibilities of action are different. The ego's action depends on passive conditions without being reducible to them: the body sets the range of possible acts, not the acts themselves, the distinction Nr. 2 drew between passive underground and individual decision.

(73) Can this be cancelled by the attitude toward objective nature? Can the ego's acting ever be explained away, as acting into its true surroundings and therefore into objective nature? Does the domain of "physiological psychology" not concern exclusively the regularities governing the passivity of all subjects? Physical nature is already a general title for regularities of constitutive passivity that concern all subjects equally. Bodiliness is a further special title, insofar as nature as such includes an empirically lawful necessity of organization (perhaps also a corresponding a priori, since physical lawfulness is transcendentally insufficient) and an empirical necessity of a development of organisms up to the human body, up to the height that is the condition of possible empathy, of progressive knowledge of human beings, and of human culture; this still involves many unclarities, doubts, and gaps. In any case bodiliness is the condition of the possibility of a passivity in subjects through which an intersubjective world can constitute itself passively and be mastered actively. But the intellectus agens, which presupposes the psychic underground with its regularities and so with its physiological boundness, cannot itself be grasped physiologically. On the other hand the intellectus agens also exists together with the natural body and is ordered into objective nature. Why do we not call it nature itself; why do we say it stands above nature, is ordered into the world and yet is the subject of the world? Husserl confines physiological psychology to passivity and names the active intellect with Aristotle's term (De anima III.5, nous poiētikos), the separable, active mind that the body conditions but does not explain. The closing question is left open; the answer the volume develops is that the ego is "in" the world only through the objectifying apperception empathy confers, which Appendix VIII states.

### Appendix V. Subject as Object and Functioning Subject (St. Märgen, September 1921)

(74) I find a world before me, the universe of objects of my actual and possible experience, in a structure, an unavoidable form of experience: I with my body, and the external world. My body is the organ of my central ego, organ of perception for all external things, my organ of action into the external world; all appearances of things relate to it, and it is interwoven in many ways with all my experiences. This structure is to be studied in the solipsistic domain. The note compresses Nr. 3, §3 into a programme: the ego–body–world triad is the first, solipsistic stratum of analysis.

(74) Then empathy: foreign subjects appear with their foreign bodies. An objective world is constituted as correlate of an intersubjective experience and knowledge that necessarily develops through communication, in which the communicating persons take part as connected subjects: a communal cognition, a communal subjectivity, as correlate of the objective world. The correlate of the objective world is not a single knower but a community; this anticipates the "transcendental intersubjectivity" of the Cartesian Meditations as the subject of objectivity.

(74) The objective world meant by everyone contains everyone along with each body. Everyone is included in the world everyone means, so the subjects become parts of their own correlate.

(74) But everyone is also a subject of cognition: subject of corresponding pregivens with passive intentionalities, a subject that, empathizing, stands in intentional connection with the other and is related to others in consciousness, that experiences and thinks actively, and not only thinks for itself but thinks with the other, follows his thinking, co-experiences his experiences, takes them over. Every subject functioning communicatively is at the same time an object in this cognitive function. The objective world is a world of objects and includes the subject-objects. As functioning, however, the subject is not subject-object but subject-subject, functioning subject. Husserl separates the subject as object in the world from the subject as functioning, and he locates functioning in thinking-with, a co-functioning of subjects that is not itself an object in the world.

(74) In objective consideration, though, every subject is an object together with its functions, so the objective world also contains subjects experienced and known as functioning, as knowing, valuing, acting. I as functioning subject, and we together, thus posit the world as nature and as human and animal world. Objectification reaches even functioning, which it represents as a fact in the world; this is the circle the closing question of Nr. 3 raised.

(74–75) We can direct theoretical interest at objective nature as a realm of associative apperceptions, a logical realm of induction. In this attitude toward associative empirics we can also take the empirics connecting bodies and subjects and investigate human beings in "external" experience, which here means using empathy only as the basis for inductive apperceptions and thoughts, as it originally contains them. But we can also regard the subjects as functioning subjects and feel ourselves into them in another sense, acting with them, valuing with them. What connections result if I remain, in doing so, a researcher of the world? Two uses of empathy are set apart: as a source of inductive data about humans, and as co-performance of their acts, the difference between naturalistic psychology and the human sciences of Ideen II. The question whether the second can remain a science of the world is the problem of a "pure psychology" of the 1925 lectures (Hua IX).

### Appendix VI. The Problem of the Constitution of the Lived Body as a Physical Thing in Solipsistic Experience (Correction to pp. 61ff.) (1921 or Somewhat Later)

(75) Husserl judges the attempt on pp. 61ff. imperfect and probably incorrect. Before any function as an external body, the body is constituted for itself in a special way. First comes the ego's functioning in kinaesthesis, with groups of sensations that associate with it empirically (moving the hands, feet, head about), which yields a first constitutive system. Second, one hand touching the other, one limb touching another, first as involuntary mutual meeting and contact, then as intended and controlled, which yields a second system combined with the first. Third, then or simultaneously and crossing with it, the touching of other things, first by chance meeting and then by voluntary exploring. But when one hand meets the other, there is mutual touching and possibly voluntary exploring (double sensations). It already follows that a part of the body is constituted from the start with a field of touch, and another "thing" without one. The correction rebuilds the constitution of the body from three tactile-kinaesthetic systems; the double sensation of Ideen II (§36) is the point at which a body part shows itself as both touching and touched, and so as different in kind from a mere thing.

(75) Every limb gives itself as moving "itself", moved by me, and at the same time, for the other limb touching it, as "mechanically" moved. Both "kinds" of movement thus coincide and are identified with each other here. Every "I move my hand" is at once a mechanical movement. Within limits, every experienceable movement of the body, every movement genuinely experienced and perceivable, is also a "felt movement", possibly an "I move myself"; a seen movement of the hand is a felt, subjective movement. Subjective and mechanical movement are identified within solipsistic experience, through touch and sight of one's own limbs, without any detour through the other. This contradicts the struck argument of Nr. 3, §4.

(75–76) What is constituted in one is a functioning body that is at the same time a thing, touched by the functioning of the body in other organs, and the touched thing, when touched, gives sensations in the touching, which "mere" physical things lack, as they lack subjective movedness. If a stone lies on my hand I feel pressure, and the stone is heavy; if my other hand lies on my hand, I feel the same, but I also have a correlative sensation of pressure in the hand lying on top. The thing there is warm or cold, and so is my hand, warmth and cold given as physical properties through touching and with the specific tactile properties; but I also "feel" warmth and cold in the hand itself or in single fingers. The body's peculiarity is a surplus of localized sensation on top of the same physical properties other things have; it is not a different set of properties but a second layer over the same ones.

(76) When I push with my hand, the movement is subjectively produced. But it can also be apprehended as objective movement, since it indicates such movement relative to its possible apprehension through other organs, and so "I push the stone" comes to be apprehended as of the same kind as "one stone pushes another" (subjective pushes and mechanical pushes). Causality is constituted with a subjective parallel: my pushing is experienced as one push among pushes, so the body enters the causal order of things already in the solipsistic sphere.

(76) However primitive these exploratory analyses, it is already visible that the lived body is constituted together with the physical thinghood outside it, and that in and for itself it is constituted doubly and is, or can be, double in everything that belongs to it as body. Every phantom has a subjective stratum, a field of sensation; phantom movement, geometrical movement in space, has subjective moving as its parallel; physical causality has subjective acting and suffering as its parallel. We have the visual thing (sense-thing) and the intuitive physical thing, and above them, as their parallel, a sensing body that senses, feels pleasure and pain, and moves itself reflexively. A note calls this the body-phantom, the full schema of the body, in its double aspect of "sense-thing" (visual thing) and "sense-body". The ego's will and its acting can still be left aside. Can one nevertheless say that, as long as we remain in solipsistic consideration, we have only one level? For while the external thing is constituted as a full physical thing, the body cannot be: it cannot be experienced (perceived) as moved in space as a whole, and even where parts of it are experienced as moved, only within essential limits. Is the body then, by its constitution as spatial object, an analogue of the external physical thing, and yet in solipsistic intuition not really a thing? But in solipsistic space the body can be at any place and can be experienced as occupying each place in turn. The body is double from the start, physical and somatological at every level; the remaining problem is that it cannot be seen moving as a whole, which seems to deny it full thinghood, while locomotion seems to restore it.

(76–77) Considering the objects near me and their distances from distant objects, I can, by functioning movement (walking) in space, have these distances in countless other modes of givenness, bring the distant objects near, hold them fast as identical, and come to know them better. As I walk, the visible distance between the parts of my body I can see and the distant objects changes, and "I" as bodily, my whole body, comes "itself" near to each distant object; my foot, my visible hand are now close to it, and the distance can be touched directly and so known by touch. Can I not experience while walking that a thing moves along with me while it "seems to rest", and can such experience not lead to the apperception that my body is moved in space although, in the first way (change of orientation), I see no movement? Locomotion yields evidence of my body's change of place without my seeing the body move: I infer my movement from the changing distances of things, and the thing that "moves along" while seeming to rest supplies the contrast that makes the inference explicit.

(77) Can I not gain, solipsistically, the representation that I move, am moved, can come to any place in space, and that my body is a spatial thing that, like every spatial thing, can take any position and be moved mechanically anywhere, and so is a physical thing like any other, even though certain modes of givenness are impossible for my body? Of course I can. More exactly, the constitution of solipsistic space proceeds from the start and by essence both through the kinaestheses of single organs and through the kinaesthesis of walking, the locomotion of the whole body. Correlatively the surrounding world of things is constituted in its unitary space, and the body is constituted as a spatial thing in this world. But in what way is my body solipsistically constituted as a spatial thing? Not as a thing like the others, like external things; body and external thing are still characterized differently. I can never perceive my body's movement in change of orientation the way I perceive the movement of a thing from near to far; I can never see it "approach" or "run away". Only when I see myself and my body with the eyes of another, or from "over there", are my locomotive movements representable as approaching and receding. Husserl now grants what the struck text denied, that the solipsist can constitute his body as a movable spatial thing, but he keeps the asymmetry: only from an external standpoint can my movement be represented as an approaching and receding thing. The 1927 texts (Nr. 36) solve the problem by grounding every external movement in my kinaesthetic movement, without the imagined external viewpoint.

(77) The other difficulty is that my subjective moving, including locomotion, has a perceptual function, and every thing is the same in change of orientation only because I can compensate for the change in its appearance by subjective moving. The body cannot be simply one moving thing among others, because its movement is what constitutes the sameness of things across changing appearances; to treat it as an object of that constitution would make it presuppose itself.

(77) So there is a fundamental problem to think through from the ground up: how the body is constituted as physical body, what its second "intuitive" stratum, the specifically somatological, looks like, and how this becomes the basis for power over the body, for its ego-functions and the spiritual apperceptions connected with them, through which the body is constituted as the primal tool, the primally spiritual object. The correction ends by stating the problem the volume carries to 1927, and adds the top layer the analysis has not reached: the body as tool and "primally spiritual object", the body as bearer of the ego's will.

### Appendix VII. As Description: The Human Being in Pure Experience, as Object in the Objective World (Around 1921)

(78) We must try to take the human being, the animal being, as we find it purely as experienced: to grasp the sense belonging essentially to animal experience as such, unclouded by all preconceptions, interpretations, and theories, to exhibit it intuitively as the essential content of the type "animal experience", and then, by explicating it, to gain its essential constituents and express them faithfully in concepts. The method is that of the "natural concept of the world": describe experience of the human being before any metaphysics of mind and body.

(78) We find the "unity" human being and distinguish in it the body-thing and its "animation", or soul. We do not find a genuine connection of a thing with a second thing-like res cogitans, nor a genuine "outside" and "inside"; yet taken figuratively these expressions point to a truth. Of the bodily thing we have experience of a thing; the corporeal body-thing is the "outer". But it indicates a "soul" in empathic experience, to me who perform the empathy. In empathy, unlike mere experience of things, I am involved. I am subject of my body and of my surroundings centred on it, in my "inner" view, "solipsistically". Accordingly I feel into the "foreign body" among those things another ego, experiencing the same nature as surroundings "from there", on the whole the same things in other aspects, and as surroundings of another ego with other feelings, another conscious life in general. This experience confirms itself in its own way. As I behave perceptually toward my physical surroundings, as this experience of nature confirms itself and determines me practically, as I busy myself in familiar ways and my true surrounding things take on new purposive shapes, so I understand all this in the other: I understand his handling of things, his achievements and works as such, and then I understand things by their shape as purposively formed, without having observed the forming. Descriptively there is no Cartesian union of two things, but the metaphor of inner and outer is kept as a pointer: the "inner" is what I contribute in empathy from my own case. Understanding artefacts as purposively made extends empathy from persons to their products, the root of the theory of culture.

(78–79) The human being as object of experience is always and necessarily over against an ego: a "thou" (counter-ego, other, alter), apperceived in the image of the ego, subject of a counter-body, of meant and actual objects that are partly experienced empathically as the same ones I have given in my ways, with the common nature as their necessary core under all circumstances. From the outset we are subjects of this same nature and of other objects, but for me these are the objects appearing to me, meant by me, given to me so and so and given to me in primal presence; for the other they are those appearing to him, meant by him, originarily given to him, but empathized into him by me (what is empathized has the mode "other"), meant by him in his modes of consciousness with their presentations and founding apperceptions. All objectivity is objectivity for subjects, and as intersubjective it is something identical, identified or identifiable in reciprocal empathy between the I-modal and the thou-modal. Every ego is the ego of cogitationes, of meaning and the meant, related to an objective surrounding world, merely supposed but also true; and that it is the same supposed or true world expresses an identification made possible by empathy, and a legitimate one, justified by evident confirmation: that is the true surrounding world with truly existing objects. Givenness to me is "primal presence", to the other "originarily given to him"; what I empathize carries the index "other". Intersubjective identity is identity across these modal indices, the "I-modal" and "thou-modal", which gives the "thou" a constitutive role before any sociology.

(79) After empathy my surrounding world also contains human beings, who are themselves objects, given as unities in the experience we call empathy. "Souls" express themselves in foreign bodies and are apprehended in one with the bodies as belonging to them, expressing themselves in them and in what they indicate. And "we" are there for one another, I, this human being, for him, and he for me, and we apprehend "ourselves" as human beings with the eyes of others. We have a common world as surrounding world that includes us human beings. "Each" is there an empirical fact, a title for a manifold of empirical facts standing under rules. "We" can investigate the physical things of "the" world common to us on the basis of the experiences we have and exchange cognitively, and also the human beings themselves in their empirical existence. The constitution of the "we" and of self-objectification in the eyes of others ("with the eyes of the others") are given as one event; the human being becomes an empirical title, open to inductive research.

(79–80) We can investigate objectively and empirically the connections between things, specifically things functioning as bodies, and human subjects as souls of these bodies. The empirical relata are the intersubjective things, the counter-relata the whole subjectivity intentionally related to these and other things and other true objects. But we do not have human beings, or ourselves, as beings that contain things really (reell) but as subjects to whom these things are "given", "stand over against" them, as meant and perhaps grasped in person, as progressively confirmed, and so on. In the natural attitude we, the researchers, posit the things as true, as bearers of their true properties, insightfully identifiable in correct cognition, confirmable in experience, and known to us to such and such an extent. To the subjects we posit in one with the bodies, these things may be given, experienced, known. But the being of things, as objects of actual and possible cognition, is one thing, and the being of subjects another. The things are "in themselves" over against the subjects, and only in empirical consideration (as subjects indicated by the body there) does it belong to the subjects that they mean, experience, or know these objects, or some of them, or none. To the human being and his soul belongs conscious relatedness, but not being-in-itself. This relatedness may be called being-experienced, progressive confirmation in experience, natural-scientific theorizing, insightfully legitimate theorizing: these are factual components of the total subjective content of the animal being, of what is called its soul. The soul contains the intentional relation but not its object; even within naturalistic psychology the distinction between act and object, set out against psychologism in the Logical Investigations, governs what counts as psychic.

(80) It is no different with objects of categories other than nature. But only with things given intersubjectively as individual factual existences are souls co-given, by appresentation, as factual. Souls exist as facts only by attachment to bodies; there is no appresentation of a soul through an ideal object, a theorem or a melody, as such.

(80) A scientific investigation of the connections between the bodiliness found in nature and the souls objectively co-existing with it (empirically and legitimately indicated for every subject of possible empathic experience), within the frame of empirical fact, finds no empirical connections between souls (or human beings) and ideal objects. It finds that human beings orient themselves by ideals, strive for truth, try to appropriate true being subjectively, succeed in this, and so on. These too are facts with their place in the unity of the whole empirical nexus of the physical and psychophysical world, under empirical rules. We can say that human beings come to stand in a relation of cognition, or of realizing works, to ideal objects; but they are ordered into the "world", into existence among factual individual existences, only through their bodies, not through ideal objects. They have psychological properties, factual subjective properties, in being directed at these ideal objects in such and such noetic and noematic contents and in realizing them subjectively in such and such ways. Naturalistic psychology can study the striving for truth, not truth: ideal objects enter the soul only as the noematic correlates of its acts, which keeps the anti-psychologism of 1900 intact inside the psychophysical view.

### Appendix VIII. Stages of the Constitution of the Soul as a Natural Unity, and of the Solipsistic Person, the Human Person, and the Psychophysical Unity Human Being (Around 1921)

(81) Husserl's summary states the thesis: the "objective world" is constituted only through empathy, and only through empathy does the merely egological subject of its objective surroundings become the objectified subject in the world. The natural unity "soul" contains psychic life, the ego and its experiences, as co-existing with one's own body, and so as something "transcendent". Kern notes that Husserl marked the first sheets as a copy: probably at the end of the 1920s or the beginning of the 1930s he rewrote the first two sheets of this manuscript of around 1921 (up to p. 83, l. 3), and the old sheets are lost. The soul, as natural unity, is a transcendent object constituted in empathy, not the immanent stream; and the late rewriting of the opening means its terminology (as in the "original" insertions below) reflects Husserl's later usage.

(81) The first problem is to clarify the situation in the inner attitude toward one's own body and one's own psychic subjectivity; then, in the outer attitude, to clarify the outer unity of body and soul in others and in oneself. The order of inquiry follows Nr. 3: inner attitude first, outer attitude second, self as well as other.

(81) Put differently, the task is to clarify the self-apperception motivated in the solipsistic inner attitude and possible before empathy, and on the other hand the apperception after empathy. It is the task of clarifying the origin of the "naturalization" of subjectivity. "Naturalization" names the apperception that makes subjectivity a soul in nature, and the question is genetic: where it arises.

(81) In the inner attitude I find my surroundings centred on my own body, which is also a thing. The psychophysical is in a certain way co-constituted here. If I move my open eye while the body otherwise rests, the modes of givenness of the thing seen change in a regular way; if I close my eye, the "optical images" vanish. I find that each of these images of things is lived only when the eye has a certain relative position to the thing and no covering thing lies on the line from eye to thing, and so on. Likewise, objective contact between finger and external thing normally conditions a "touch sensation", a touch image of the object, which again is lived only when such objective contact occurs. Solipsistic experience already contains dependencies between bodily states and appearances; the question is whether these amount to a psychophysical apperception.

(81) Here we have objective relations, relations of spatial position among things. These stand in relation to subjective occurrences, sensory data, aspects of things, and so on, and to the "I can" give my body such and such a position. The dependencies are spatial and kinaesthetic, tied to the "I can", not to physiological processes.

(81–82) It should be noted, however, that although we find regular relations between the real unities thing and body on the one side and the constitutive phenomena on the other, no natural apperception between the subjective and the natural is thereby grounded. In the play of eye movements, or rather of kinaesthetic courses, and the visual data running along with them, the visual thing is constituted as a unity. Dependencies of coexistence obviously follow: if this unity "exists", at rest or moving in space, certain motivated possibilities belonging to the possible perception of what exists must obtain in the subjective sphere. If my body is constituted as a visual thing along with the visual thing, the conditionalities described must result: the visual thing can be perceived only if it stands in a suitable spatial position to my visual body, and so on. But these are not relations constituted in a special inductive-empirical apperception; they are essential states of affairs belonging to the apperception of thing and body. Every constituted unity stands, by its existence, in essentially necessary relations to the corresponding constituting appearances. The dependencies of appearances on bodily position are not empirical laws discovered by induction but essential correlations between a constituted unity and its constituting manifolds, so they cannot ground a causal psychophysics.

(82) The same holds at a higher level, when full things are constituted through apperceptive syntheses of visual things and the like as constituting manifolds. Things are constituted in causal relations to one another. The corresponding relations of dependence of things (causes and effects) on subjectivity, within the possibility of experience, are not causal relations but essential relations belonging to the constituted causality of things, in an empirical turn conditioned by the facticity of the given causal relation. The relation between constituted causality and constituting subjectivity is not itself causal; Husserl repeats the anti-naturalist principle of Ideen II at the level of real things.

(82–83) It is clear that in the solipsistic inner attitude, in a consideration abstracting from all contributions of empathy, a localization of the subjective in the body is still lacking, and so is any constitution of my soul as a unity belonging to the body and connected with it psychophysically and causally. As solus I have no soul "of my" body. I, the experiencing subject, have no soul belonging to this body here (which I call "my" body only later, in contrast with the bodies of other human beings) as long as I have not apperceived other human beings as such, or as long as I have not imagined my body "moved out" and freely mobile in space. In doing that I have apprehended it like a second, foreign body and as an empirical-inductive bearer of psychic life, empathically. Empathy, of course, presupposes my own inner apperception of the body. With it there is given by essence the type of constitutive relations between bodiliness and other surrounding things, and finally the whole unity of inner life that a priori accompanies all experiencing by virtue of experiencing and its subjective connections. The localization of the subjective, its ordering into nature, presupposes empathy; only through empathy is the subjective objectified. The thesis is sharp: as solus I have no soul. Imagining my body "moved out" counts as already empathic, a self-empathy; and empathy presupposes the inner apperception of the body, so the solipsistic stratum is necessary but not sufficient for my being a human being.

(83–84) How does this come about? I apprehend the foreign bodily thing as the body of a soul. What is appresented is a conscious life, a sensuously appearing nature appearing in a certain orientation around a body in inner givenness, which is identified with the bodily thing appearing to me externally, as that nature is identified with the nature appearing to me. But the appresentation is only partly determinate, roughly typical: I know only that the appearances the other has must be roughly those I would have "from there", from his place in space, in a suitable position to his things. Whatever goes beyond this is represented in an indeterminately general way, within a general frame: it is a subject and a conscious life, an analogue of mine. My own is for me actual lived life (a later insertion adds: "originally given or to be disclosed by self-perception, self-recollection"); the foreign one first needs determination through "mediate experience". What does this experience look like? Every closer determination rests in turn on empathy. If in my inner experience I find an associative relation of indication established between violent bodily movements, a shouting voice, and the like, and anger, then the apperception of the corresponding foreign bodily expressions, already understood as expressions of an inner view of these bodily movements, can undergo, in the co-connected and apperceptively indeterminate interiority, a closer determination: the other is in a rage. This presupposes many empirical connections already formed between inner processes, between experiences of consciousness such as affects, feelings, thoughts, and bodily processes, the latter as appearing, and appearing in a certain inner view. This association, established within the solipsistic restriction (the many relations of empirical interweaving by which typical occurrences in one's own body, running their course in certain modes of appearance of a closed circle and so as appearing, are regularly and empirically one with occurrences of consciousness that in themselves, by essence, have nothing to do with them), does not create a new apperceptive-transcendent unity of the kind of the unity human being. In that unity the bodily thing is a transcendent reality in itself, connected with a second transcendent reality, the soul, by psychophysical causality through which each member receives its psychophysical properties. Associations running purely inwardly in the subject between what is own-bodily and its own conscious life do not create an empirical unity "I as a human being with body and soul". Empathy proceeds in two steps: a typical, rough appresentation of a perspective "from there", then closer determination by transfer of my learned associations between expression and feeling. My own associations between bodily expression and affect are indications, not a psychophysical unity, because their bodily side is the body as it appears from within, not a transcendent physical thing.

(84) Why does no apperception of this kind arise there? In the inner view my whole inner life is a unity centred in the ego-pole, but an immanent unity (later: "original"), a unity of immanent time. Within it "external" things appear, a world stretching endlessly in space and space-time around the body as central member of orientation. Here the ego separates itself out: the ego that performs acts, has appearances, lives in its stream, finds sensory data, has things over against it, relates to them attending, judging, desiring. The empirical unity "I-person" also forms here, the solipsistically constituted I-person, which is not yet a person in the usual sense. I have my customary ways of being motivated by things in my surroundings: some stimulate my desire as food, others as the sweet scent of flowers; when they are brought into certain relations to my body, pleasure or displeasure arises; they gladden or sadden me. I react in empirically regular ways to things as I experience them, as appearing in certain appearances, and I have my peculiarity in how things receive their subjective characters, their agreeableness, beauty, usefulness. But this empirical unity I-person remains over against all things; they form its surroundings and have characters assigned to them from it. This relation between the empirically personal (and solipsistic) ego and the things it relates to is not a natural relation, as if this ego were a reality in real (substantial) connection with things as realities. The ego is not an object among objects but the subject of all objects. Everything that is an object, that is in itself, a substrate of lasting predicates, is in itself over against the subject. The solipsistically constituted person is not yet a reality related to a body, not yet a person in nature. The solipsistic person is real as a person, with habits and a character, but not as a reality in nature; personhood precedes naturalization, as Nr. 3, §8 had concluded.

(84–85) The ego itself has no "in-itself" in the same sense; I am not a substrate of identical predicates, of substantial properties. It has habitual and character properties, but in constant development, and if its development has a style, that style cannot be reduced to a system of absolutely identical properties. As long as we stay within the inner relation between ego and surroundings (the nature constituted in the ego's stream of consciousness and for the ego, appearing to it or lying in the indeterminate, unknown horizon and freely accessible to it), the ego with its conscious life, to which it can also relate consciously, is not in nature and not naturally connected with it. It is the ego that has nature as its surrounding sphere, as theme of its representing thought, theorizing, valuing, practical shaping, and acts into it, while not being a cause in the sense of natural causes, nor having effects in the same sense. The ego can reflect on itself and make itself the theme of thinking and valuing, but it is never an "object" and can never become one in the inner attitude. It is passive in its states, active in its acts. It lives, but it has no existence (Dasein). With its life it lives into immanent time; it is not in immanent time, the time of its experiences. It is supra-temporal and yet related to time. In its temporal experiences a nature with its space-time appears to it, and the two times coincide stretch by stretch, moment by moment. The ego's regard runs through immanent time and, in the attitude toward the natural, through natural time; and as the ego "acts" on its own life, not shaping it, since consciousness is not a thing, but determining the stream of future life by will, so it "acts" on the course of nature and into it in its own temporality. The person is not a substance: its traits form a developing style, not fixed properties. The formulas "it lives but has no existence" and "supra-temporal yet related to time" give the pole-ego of Nr. 2 its temporal status, the "standing" ego the late time manuscripts analyse; the coincidence of immanent and natural time is how a non-natural ego can still act into nature.

(85–86) Now we pass to the outer attitude. We had empirical connections of association between bodily occurrences and subjective states and acts which, transferred by empathy, make foreign bodily occurrences apprehensible as indications of similar subjective acts and states. The bodily thing there indicates, through its general shape, in its similarity to my bodily thing, and through its particular expressions, a subjectivity in general and then particular determinations of one. I, the experiencer, find in the nature around me things that are bodies of another subjectivity. This subjectivity is posited in one with the existence of that body-thing and belongs to it lastingly as long as the body exists in nature in the normal type "living organism", inseparable from it during that time of nature. The bodily thing is changeable and changes like other things, and empirically its changes correspond to changes in the subjective element attached to it. A note summarizes the inner constitution: I as pole and habitual ego related to an (original) experiential surrounding world constituted in me and centred on my body, through which all experiencing and outward acting proceeds; this yields a universal apperception arising from association, original and wholly unified, and in empathy this whole is apperceived analogically. The first "psychic" element is the egoic, primarily conscious to me egologically: I see, I hear, I am affected, I push, I shove, I meet resistance and overcome it, I suffer from things, fear them, take pleasure in them, deal with them in action, occasionally appearances of them; I as subject of intentional relatedness to objects. In the outer attitude the soul is posited with the body and dated by its life as an organism; the note fixes the analogical object of empathy as my whole egoic world-relation, not a list of feelings.

(85–86) In my inner sphere I find empirical-associative connections between processes of my bodily thing and states and acts of the ego, and regular constitutive relations between bodily things and my perceptions of them, with everything belonging to such perceptions in sensory data and appearances. This understandably determines my apprehension of foreign bodies. The psychic element belonging to them is now localized and temporalized by its persistent attachment through the changing movements and alterations of the foreign body. It is lasting, a unity closed in itself, standing in manifold reciprocal relations of dependence to the bodily, relations of dependence that run in spatio-temporal objective time (a later insertion: "first of the nature original for me"). Localization and temporalization of the soul are achieved through the persistence of the other's psychic life attached to his moving body; the soul acquires a place and a date only as the other's soul.

(86) For me, the experiencer, the foreign subjectivity together with the foreign body is as objective as any thing (later: "of my original spatial world"), an in-itself that is as it is whether or not I have knowledge of it, whether or not it presents itself in my appearances. For the other, likewise, my body and my subjectivity are objective in this sense. The subject that can never become an object for itself in immediate self-experience, in mere reflection, becomes an object for the other, and then becomes an object for itself through the identification of the subject of inner consideration with the subject-object that the other, in his outer consideration of my body, assigns to it: an object in the nature common to me and him (so the concept of object changes). This is the precise mechanism of self-objectification: I become an object for myself only by identifying my inner self with the subject-object the other assigns to my body. The "change in the concept of object" marks that the object now means the intersubjectively in-itself, not the ego's counterpart in the solipsistic sense.

(86–87) Only through empathy is an "objective" nature in the full sense constituted, an objective world that is not only physical. A note defines objective as intersubjective nature together with the objectification of subjectivity, so that an objective world is now constituted. Before, there were only the subject and its surroundings as spatial-temporal-causal surroundings (later: "as universe of my original external experience"). Now there is a nature that is not only my surroundings but the common surroundings of an open plurality of existing subjects. If the subject was formerly a supra-natural subject, so to speak, related to its nature but not included in it, now every subject is a natural object, the soul of a body; for every other subject it is a soul existing in itself on a body existing in itself, ordered into space through its body and into objective natural time as enduring with the body. A priori, nature is first constituted as merely physical, and also only as surrounding nature over against the one non-natural subject that constitutes it in experience; a note calls this the "first" nature, the solipsistic, the realm of original spatial experience, and the first ego, the solipsistic, not yet naturalized. At a higher level, with the nexus of empathy, nature is constituted in an extended and altered sense, as intersubjectively identical nature, and further as the objective world "in" which all subjects have existence. The objectification of nature as nature for everyone takes place in inseparable unity with the objectification of subjects as subjects for everyone. Subjects can stand over against me, empathically, only as subject-objects, subjects one with bodies and, as human beings, members "in" the one intersubjective nature with its one space and one time into which all objects are ordered: physical bodies and, when they are lived bodies, the souls connected with them. Causality and substantiality exist already in the solipsistic sphere for physical nature; in the communicative sphere this physical substantiality becomes intersubjective and is taken over in that form. Naturalized subjectivity connected with bodies introduces a new form of substantiality and causality. The soul, the subjective naturally ordered into nature, is a unity closed in itself and one with the bodily, a double unity. The soul in this connection can be considered purely insofar as it shows factual occurrences that are regularly dependent on the bodily-corporeal and on the further connections of physical nature. To apperceive the soul as human soul, to perceive a human being, to posit one in experience, is eo ipso to apperceive it to some extent in such dependence.
