To be directed at nature, even physical nature, is eo ipso to be directed at the empirical dependencies of corporeal events, here called physical causality. One can take this attitude toward the body-soul relation as well, attending to the bodily insofar as it indicates the psychic, which then presents itself as occurring in dependence on it, and conversely. If one pursues these empirical regularities, apprehensions of the body are necessarily constituted with components of sense related to such regulated dependencies. The body is apperceived not only with physical properties (causal properties related to physiological-physical conditions) but also with new physiological-psychic properties: it has the property, for example, when touched by other bodies, of delivering sensations to the soul (or the human being has the property, when his body is touched, of reacting psychically with sensations), and conversely. So new conceptions of substance and causality arise for body and soul, and corresponding investigations analogous to those of natural science. This psychophysical doctrine of the soul, linked from the start with the physical doctrine of the body, naturally takes in everything psychic that enters the circle of psychophysical conditionality, and if there should be, within the factual connections inside the soul, anything revealing regulated connections of natural facticity, it too would be included; a note asks whether constitutive connections are meant. The main point is that the psychic is localized in space and time, incorporated into and attached to nature in the physical sense, and that the issue is rules of the objective spatio-temporal order. The supra-natural subject of the solipsistic stage becomes a natural object through empathy, and nature and subjects are objectified together. Psychophysical "substance" and "causality" are new conceptions produced by this objectification; their range is open, and the note asks whether constitutive relations might be mistaken for natural ones, the confusion Appendix XLIX later names as the root of metaphysical dualism.

(88) But the psychic also has its immanent "own absolute essence" and the essential laws belonging to it. The last line reopens what psychophysics cannot reach: the absolute, immanent essence of the psychic, which phenomenology, not physiology, investigates.

### Appendix IX. Empathy and the Lived Body (Around 1921)

(88) As many organs, as many functional units as particular unities within the body, so many strata of empathy. Here we have phenomenological foundations. If the eye is atrophied and apprehended as blind, the other does not see; if the hand is burned, the corresponding region is missing in the field of touch. Empathy is stratified by organs: each organ I recognize in the other's body opens a corresponding stratum of his experience, and an organ's visible defect closes it.

(88) Physiology supplies more precise determinations of the sensory functions, of the function of the retina and its parts, an ever finer analysis that traces the corresponding "dependences" down to the sensory data and fields, providing foundations for ever new empathies. This psychophysical "causality" or conditionality is studied in every direction, first in the sensory sphere relative to sense organs, sensory nerves, connection with the centre; then also the relations to reproductions and so on: body and soul, "brain and soul". Physiology refines empathy rather than replacing it: knowledge of the retina determines more finely what the other can see.

(88) Identity of biological function and functional form, identity of an organ: what kind of objectivation are organism, "organ", and organ-function? What "ideas" govern talk of normal and anomalous organs? The self-preservation of an organism, the normal organism? Biological concepts rest on norms, ideas of function and self-preservation, whose constitution must be clarified, the question Nr. 3, §7 left open.

(88–89) All this is of course the foundation for the theory of concrete empathy and its legitimation. What, then, is "animation"? Can an organ be an independent organism? Does a system of organs correspond to a system of souls bound into one soul? How can "functions" combine, and can souls combine at all, and how? If my hand is cut off and grafted onto another man, would my field of sensation, this part of it, pass into his? If foreign eyes could be "implanted" in a blind man, would a piece of the other's soul, his visual field of sensation, become a piece of the blind man's soul? And what of parts of the brain, association areas, speech areas? Polyp division and reunion. The thought experiments test whether a sensory field belongs to an organ or to a subject; if the field went with the organ, the soul would be divisible like the body.

(89) But what of the unity of a consciousness, of affection and action: is it thinkable that one subject, one "ego", combines with another into one ego? The answer prepared by the elementary account of Nr. 2 is negative: sensory fields may be tied to organs, but the ego-pole, the unity of affection and action, cannot be divided or merged; Appendix XLI returns to the possibility of fused monads and a divine supra-monad.

(89) Perspectival adumbration; objective "images" of things on the retina; the law of refraction and so on, physical; subjective "perspectival appearances". The retinal image is a physical fact and the perspectival appearance a subjective one; they correspond without being the same, so the phenomenology of adumbration cannot be read off optics.

### Appendix X. Personal Ego-Spirituality and Sub-Egoic, Bodily Life (1920s)

(89) When we as persons are directed at persons and their personal life, our regard is not specifically directed at the psychic element that, in the proper sense, gives bodiliness a specifically bodily life beyond its physical-organic happening, or, if one likes, beyond its organic life. Does this not point to a difference between personal ego-spirituality and sub-egoic, bodily life? On one side the ego-spirituality that radiates activity from a centre: grasping attention, comparing, distinguishing, determining ends and means, and that from this centre is also the governing part (hegemonikon) of bodily life, acting in the body or through it, directing its organs of perception or setting in play voluntary bodily movements and bodily effects in the external world, pressure, push. On the other side the sub-egoic life of the body, which runs on as subjective life in flowing experiences without the ego's active participation, though it always presupposes the ego. The Stoic hegemonikon names the ego's rule over the body; the sub-egoic life is the passive, sensory stratum of Nr. 3, §7, which belongs to subjectivity yet runs without the ego's participation.

(89) Yet for the specific life of the ego we must also constantly claim what, speaking purely subjectively, we call stimuli on the ego, the ego's being affected, wherever something appears in the subjective field that arouses attention and interest and to which the ego answers with activities. Affection is the hinge between the two lives: sub-egoic in origin, egoic in its address.

(89–90) Evidently we must also count among the ego as ego the marvellous peculiarity that distinguishes subject-realities from other realities. They are not only realities in connection with other realities, with a real external world surrounding them outside themselves; they are realities that have consciousness of an outside, that have a world surrounding them in consciousness, of which they find themselves the centre, and from which they see everything that is outside them and consciously there for them in modes of near and far. The ego-centre is the constant central point of the world-consciousness of each subject, and in experiencing, this ego finds its body as the thing specially belonging to it, the primal field of its governing, the immediately practical object through which every practical relation to other things is mediated, the central object of spatial localizations. A subject-reality is defined by having a world, not merely being in one; the body is the "primal field" of the ego's rule, the formula of Nr. 3, §3.

(90) Here, in any case, a main theme is marked out for a science of the natural concept of the world: to study precisely how subjectivity relates to bodiliness, how the specifically somatological differs from the higher spiritual element and the specific functions of the ego, and how both nevertheless remain inseparable. The "natural concept of the world", Avenarius's phrase taken up in the 1910/11 lectures, names the descriptive science of the pre-scientific world in which this relation must first be studied, the programme Kern's introduction traced to the Crisis.

(90) Finally a further theme opens: on the side of personalities, a new and higher level of formations of wholes among realities, specifically among spiritual realities. Personality and bodiliness are of course inseparable. But when human beings enter into relations, this does not happen only in bodily-corporeal respects, as when they jointly load a railway carriage, no differently from a heap of stones. Rather, while their bodiliness always co-functions somehow, they can unite really into personal wholes, act with and in one another as personal subjects through purely personal activity; not only does something come about outside the persons, even a communal work such as a building, but they unite inwardly and personally and finally become a personality of higher order, an association, a state-people, and the like, in which one will, one purpose, one body of practical convictions, political or scientific, lives: a will that is a communal will, a purpose that is a communal purpose. The railway carriage contrasts aggregation by weight with union by will; the "personality of higher order" (Ideen II, §51) is announced here and worked out in the "communal spirit" texts Nr. 9–10.

### No. 4. Can There Be Separate Subjects Related to Separate Worlds? Conditions of the Possibility of the Coexistence of Subjects. Deduction That There Can Be Only One World, Only One Time, One Space (St. Märgen, 1 September 1921)

(91) Can two or more subjects exist and be related to entirely separate immanent or transcendent times? Husserl's note points to the recapitulation on pp. 98ff. The question extends to subjects the argument of Ideen I (§48) against a second world in principle inaccessible to ours: there it concerned things, here it concerns monads, and its outcome is the thesis of the Fifth Meditation that there can be only one community of monads (Hua I, §60).

(91) If there are two subjects, there must at least be the ideal possibility of a cognition that each of them exists, concretely, with its whole immanent content, and that both exist. This ideal possibility presupposes that we can think the possible knowing subject in a connection of empathy with each of the two; and that presupposes that each of them has constituted a common world with the knower. What is common between the knower E and S1, and between E and S2, need not be quite the same. But at any rate E must have constituted a transcendent world that contains the worlds of S1 and S2 as identical. Then S1 and S2 must also be able to stand in a possible connection of empathy, directly or by way of E. The argument rests on a premise about being: to exist is to be possibly known; and since a plurality can be known only by someone in empathic connection with all its members, plurality implies a shared world. Empathy is transitive through the knower.

(91) Can it be shown, then, that several subjects, if there are several, must necessarily be able to stand in a possible commercium and so must have constituted a common world in themselves, the same realm of "possible experience"? The thesis is stated as a question of necessity, with Leibniz's term for interaction among monads.

(91–92) Husserl reconsiders the premise. If A is posited, the positing is unthinkable without thinking along with it a subject of possible "perception" (experience). If A is something immanent, that subject is the subject of the immanence; the immanent datum and its subject are inseparable in being and necessarily coexist. If A is a subject, the subject is itself the subject of its possible self-perception; here the two coincide in existence, subject as existing and subject as knower. If A is something transcendent, it is by its sense related to a subject in whose immanence the sense-giving for this transcendence has been constituted, whether actually, or in the connection and open horizon of what has already been constituted, or habitually, or as a potentiality within a development. The premise is specified by kinds of being: for each kind, a correlated subject, and for the transcendent even a merely potential or developing one, which keeps the argument open to the undeveloped child.

(92) Thinking an A assumed to exist thus necessarily leads me back to a subject, whether that subject actually thinks the A in the sense of a belief, or confirms the belief by perceiving or having perceived it, or expects it, and in any case has it within the range of its possible experience. Husserl's note puts it more briefly and better: one cannot think an A as existing without thinking along with it a subject, also existing, that is a subject of possible experience for this A. The subject cannot be merely imagined, a subject invented and added to the posited A. Positing the existing A does not merely mean that I can posit or think along with it a corresponding subject; an existing subject of constitution with its constituting consciousness is necessarily presupposed. Only when I posit an A as fictive do I have a fictive subject with it. As soon as I posit the A, I posit the subject as existing. The decisive step: possible experience requires an actual subject. The correlate of actual being is not an ideal observer but an existing one, which is what makes the argument bear on coexistence rather than on mere conceivability.

(92–93) It will be objected that something transcendent can be objective, the common property of many subjects: the possibility of an experiencing subject belongs to the objective-transcendent, but not of this one or that. But if A is objective, it is intersubjectively constituted and knowable as experienceable for everyone. Then a universe of subjects, possibly communicating with one another and identifying through mutual understanding, must be posited along with A; and however indeterminate these subjects remain, they are not wholly indeterminate. They are not mere ficta; at least I exist and have my open horizon of personality. One or another must be determinate, and the rest form its attainable horizon. For an objective A, then, a co-existing universe of subjects as subjects of possible intersubjective experience is co-posited. "Everyone" is anchored in me: the universe of possible subjects is an open horizon around an actual subject, not a class of possible minds.

(93) It always holds that the possibility of experiencing what exists is not the possibility of imagining experiences of it. As actual being and ideally possible being differ, so "real" possibility of experience, a possibility motivated in an actual subject, differs from "ideal" possibility, merely fictive real possibility related to purely fictive subjects. The distinction between real and ideal possibility, motivated versus imagined, is the hinge of the deduction: only motivated possibilities in an actual subject count toward what exists.

(93) Experience is the "realization" of something existing in a subject's cognition, and what exists is an intentional pole, the pole of an "actual" intentionality that consists in actual subjects as actual experience or as actual disposition, capacity, ability. What exists is therefore an index for constituting subjects, a real possibility of turning to the pole, the "object", grasping it, and bringing it, in one stroke or in regulated processes of progressive "realization", to realizing constitution. Being is defined as an index of real capacities in actual subjects, the formula of Cartesian Meditations §21 that the object is a "transcendental clue".

(93) This holds for every possible object to be considered as existing, including a subject, a soul, a concrete human subject. It is a subject and at the same time an "object", an intentional pole for the ego that is in it and constituted in it, for which the concrete subject itself is constituted and can therefore become an object of possible cognition. The monad is not a stream flowing for no one; within it lies the someone for whom the stream can become an object of knowledge. The italicized sentence answers the first case of the premise: the monad is self-knowing, so its existence needs no external knower, and the problem of plurality begins there.

(93) But what of a plurality of subjects? Can each subject be an object only for itself, while the plurality as plurality is there for no one, neither actually present in actual experience nor experienceable for any existing someone? The objection is that monadic self-knowledge makes each monad satisfy the premise alone, so a plurality of mutually isolated monads would violate nothing.

(93–94) Husserl replies that a subject can be thought in principle in only two ways. The first is to start from our "I am" and freely re-imagine everything subjective we find evidently in it. Every such possibility of variation yields a possible subject, and the evidently existing ego then also becomes one of these possibilities, which can happen only by positing a varied ego and regarding the original one from there as a possibility of phantasy. Under the title "an ego" we then have nothing but mutually incompatible possibilities of subjects: a plurality, an extension, but not a plurality of subjects in the natural sense of a coexisting plurality or a possible coexistence of subjects in the plural. A note calls this the universe of the possibilities of an ego, in individual coincidence with the factual ego I am. Eidetic variation of myself yields a range of possible egos, each a possible me; they exclude one another and so never form a coexisting plurality. The eidos "ego" is reached by variation, but plurality is not.

(94) The second way of representing an ego, a concrete subject, is to represent it as over against me, keeping my own ego untouched, so to speak. That happens when I experience a human being in space outside me or represent one intuitively. This, one will say, is a factual mode of representation, factually possible for me. But now let us vary freely, re-imagining in every possible direction while holding fast only the duality of subjects, keeping identically that a coexisting duality remains, one subject (me) and another. We can then transform each of the two subjects for itself in the only possible way just described, producing the infinity of possible subjects belonging to it as "a subject in general". Are we free in this if we want to keep a coexisting duality? If I start with myself, my subjectivity includes the manifold of the constitution of my world of experience. In free variation of my subjectivity, everything that makes possible for me the legitimate, concordant experiential positing of the physical world, the one actually and legitimately experienced by me, may finally vanish; the re-imagining of my ego (in its whole concrete content, in which I am constituted for myself through concordant inner reproductions) may leave no transcendent physical world at all for me as a world of concordant experience. Variation constrained by a fixed duality is the new method: it tests what the coexistence of two subjects requires, and the first discovery is that variation of myself can destroy my world.

(95–96) The possibilities here are evidently very different. In re-imagining the nature given to me, which is at once a re-imagining of my ego, there is a circle of incompatible natures, each possible for itself as experienceable for "me", while I myself am re-imagined as to my experiences and motivated connections of experience and so turned into another subject within the circle of my incompatibilities. Each such re-imagining would mean that the world given to me, who undoubtedly exist, in consistently confirmed certainty of experience need not exist and need not be as it is; it has many directions of free variation that would keep it a world experienceable for me. A note fixes what lies in my conviction that my world of experience exists: past experience was often discordant, but every discordance resolved into concordance through a motivated change of apperceptions that retroactively changed earlier, legitimately motivated apperceptions; it can go on so, but the idea of a definitively concordant experience holds in legitimate motivation, and my consciousness stands under an inviolable rule of possible absolute concordance of all my experiential apperception, which is the correlate of the existence of this world. Every such variation of the world also varies me, as to the whole system of experiences enclosed in my stream of life that I call the constitutive system of actual nature (the actual nature, of which alone I speak and can speak). That has inseparable consequences for the rest of my concrete subject, so I am evidently another if nature is another, or would be another if it were another. But I can also re-imagine my concrete subject, as it lies before me as actual in its present and its whole past, or can be reconstructed, so that no physical nature comes to constitution at all; and in the same sense, moving forward into my open and variously possible future, I can steer my stream in fiction so that nature never comes to constitution, or so that it was legitimately experienced and concordantly constituted but falls apart in infinitum into discords and my concordant experience loses its force. Then I would all the more be, or have been, another. There would be no surrounding world at all for me; and as my experiences would undergo these variations, and with them a concrete part of my subjectivity, so all the "affections" acting on me from what was constituted as transcendent physical reality, and my active reactions and modes of behaviour motivated by "the" world as experientially actual, would vanish. This is the thought experiment of the annihilation of the world from Ideen I (§49), now turned against the ego that survives it: the ego survives, but as another ego, since my identity includes my world. The note defines the world's existence as a regulative idea of final concordance.

(96) Someone may say that "the" physical world, or "a" world, could still be objectively actual although I no longer experience it or no longer gain concordant experience of it, or that it or some world could be actual in itself without ever coming or being able to come into my experience. Much relevant to this will be said later. Here only this is certain: a world of which I can speak with any legitimate ground must have its motivation in my experiences, and the actual being of this world posited in my motivation means, as remarked in the margin, an ideally inviolable rule of the possible ("final") concordance of my experiences of it. The realist objection is deferred; the only world at issue is the motivated world, which leaves the argument as a thesis about the sense of "world", not about a world-in-itself.

(96–97) Having made of myself, in free fiction and constructing pure possibilities, everything that is possible, I could clearly do the same, with the same freedom, for my counter-subject. He was given to me as a fact, as the human being over there with the body there, through empathy. It lies in the sense of empathy, by essence, that it is an analogizing appresentation of the foreign ego-subject in my subjectivity and that it must necessarily agree with one of the free variations of a subject I can perform on myself. According to the sense in which I now posit him appresentatively, the other has, for instance, the experiences of physical things (the same ones that are there for me) that I would have now (but cannot have now, since I have others incompatible with them) if I saw these things "from there"; and this there is freely accessible to me at any time unless it is physically occupied by the foreign body or another thing. In other respects too, according to my empathy, the human being there has a content of experiences and acts other than mine, indicated by the foreign body and its expressive signs as an analogue of my own experiencing and doing. All this is represented, and representable only, as a variation of my own subject and subjective life; and although it is posited by appresentation as existing with the body there, it necessarily belongs to the range of possible re-imaginings of my own ego. Evidently each of the possibilities we gain by re-imagining him must agree necessarily with one of the possibilities I gain by re-imagining my actual ego, just as every thing experienced as fact must agree with one of the pure possibilities of phantasy I gain by running through the totality of the (mutually incompatible) possibilities of a thing in general. Empathy is situated within eidetic variation: the other is given as a possible variant of me, the variant "if I were there", but posited as existing alongside me. The other is an eidetic possibility of my ego actualized as coexistent, which joins the "intentional modification" of Nr. 3 to the method of variation of Nr. 2.

(97) It is therefore certain that if I put myself into the foreign ego, intuit his subjective peculiarities, and then re-shape everything freely in fiction, I gain exactly the same circle of variations of a subject as I gained for myself, but only if I do not bind myself by being there along with him, or by having an ego, sprung perhaps from my re-imagining, there beside or "over against" one of the egos sprung from his re-imagining. What this indicates must be examined more closely. Free variation of either subject alone yields the same eidos; the constraint arises only when both are held as coexisting, which is the condition the next units analyse.

(97) If I vary my subject so that my body is no longer constituted and so no longer there for me, the other also disappears from my sphere of experience, since I now have nothing to make empathy possible. I can of course think my body varied freely in infinitely many ways; as long as I have a body for myself, of whatever shape, I can experience another through empathy. If I re-imagine myself so that the other body disappears or loses its analogy with mine, I also lose the other from my range of experience. But the possibility of another in general persists as long as I keep a body and an open nature in which I could still find "bodies", analogues of mine. And others are actually there for me when my empathic experience, analogizing, confirms itself consistently in its way. The body is the invariant of coexistence: vary it as I like, but abolish it and the other goes with it; and the other's body must remain analogous to mine, so analogy, not identity of shape, is the condition.

(98) In a parenthesis Husserl asks what happens if I keep my body and the rest of nature disappears, or keep a nature and my body disappears. It would have to be shown that a nature is possible only together with a body for the subject experiencing it, and that a body by itself, without a far-reaching nature, is scarcely thinkable; but for present purposes this can be dispensed with. The mutual implication of body and world is asserted but not proved, which leaves a gap the 1927 analyses of the null-body and kinaesthetic space (Nr. 36) fill.

(98) What has all this shown? A second subject is experienceable as over against me, as a second subject distinct from me, only if I am the subject of a body and of a nature outside the body in which I can experience a second body as such. This is an a priori connection. It is unthinkable that the knowing ego could experience another ego without experiencing itself and the foreign ego as animal beings. Must I not then say, correlatively and a priori, that it is unthinkable that besides me, and in general besides one subject, another subject should exist if I, the first subject, had no body and were not the subject of a physical nature oriented around that body, in which a second body, that of the other ego, coexisted? The inference passes from conditions of experiencing another to conditions of the other's existing, by way of the premise that existing means being experienceable. The conclusion makes embodiment a condition of plurality: disembodied subjects could not be several.

#### Recapitulation, or Rather a Better Repetition

(98–99) More precisely than before, Husserl states what has been shown. First: if I, the knower, run through the possible variations of my ego, my concrete subjectivity, in free re-imagining, I obtain a closed system of possible subjects. Strictly, all these possibilities are incompatible, because they are in individual coincidence. The ego that exists, I who am, therefore says: I think, I suppose, I were another or otherwise than I am. In this individual coincidence one subject, actual or possible, and another possible one are brought to individual unification. In it the immanent fields of time on both sides evidently coincide, and every doubly occupied place is a place of conflict. The two subjects are therefore incompatible. Each subject, in everything it is, is an individual, unique. Every individual variation is as such irreconcilable with what is varied; positing one cancels the other and conversely. Incompatibility is located precisely: in coincidence the two immanent times overlap, so each moment is claimed twice, which is the principle of compossibility from Nr. 2 applied to rival lives of one ego.

(99) But each is a possibility for itself, and the totality of possibilities of variation that can arise from the fact, taken as any such possibility, by free variation is the totality of possibilities of "a subject in general", as the totality of possibilities arising by variation from any possible thing yields the totality of possibilities of an individual thing in general. Every "extension" of a general concept referring to individuals is gained in the synthesis of incompatibility under individual coincidence. Yet the possibilities can also be thought outside this synthesis, as separate and then compatible possibilities: in the synthesis of plurality, of coexistence. Every such coexistence can be rethought into an overlapping coincidence and a synthesis of individual unity, which then "explodes" in mutual cancellation. The account of eidetic variation later given in Experience and Judgment (§§87–88) is here: extension arises from conflict in coincidence. Husserl adds the converse operation, from coincidence to coexistence, which turns the eidetic question into the question of compossibility.

(99) The question, then, is whether subjects, singled-out possibilities of subjects, can be thought in the plural as coexisting with one another as things can, how they can be, and whether and to what extent they stand under essential laws of possible coexistence. The problem is now formulated exactly: coexistence of subjects is not guaranteed by their being instances of one eidos.

(99–100) Second: one form in which we can represent a coexistence of subjects, or what is the same, set it before our eyes as experienceable, is the one given to us as a fact of experience, that we, or say one subject, I as the experiencer, experience another subject as "outside" me, as over against me. When I do that, I distinguish my subject and that of the other, and so I have a duality (and possibly a plurality) of subjects as a possibility and, with consistent confirmation, as confirmed actuality. Evidently the other is in the same situation as I: as he is in my range of experience, I am in his, and we are not merely coexisting at all but there for one another as coexisting. In this case each of us, in his subjective experience, and both of us in a possible reciprocal experience, have constituted a common world. Each experiences not only a physical world but the foreign body inserted in it, and with it the other as an animal being; each also finds his own body in this same world, finds through empathy (actually or possibly) the other as experiencing my body and me myself as human, as animal; each so inserts himself into this world as animal, as bodily-psychic being. In this case it is one world in which both subjects are spatially, and perhaps also temporally, separate, a world constituted in each subjectivity for itself and identified in each, in the relation of empathy, with that of the other. The factual model of coexistence is reciprocal empathy within one world, in which separateness is spatial: the world both unites and separates its subjects.

(100) We submit this fact to essential consideration. We recognize that if two egos are thought as coexisting in this way, inserted into one world and thereby separated, a rule is imposed on each, and both together are spanned in this regulation by an overarching regulation, so that the rules for the two subjects are not independent of one another. This is expressed in the fact that each subject is not only the subject of a world experienceable for it, but the two are subjects of a world common to them and experienceable by them in common. Let us vary what can be varied: let us vary the factual rule under which an ego stands in virtue of a world being there for it, so that no world can any longer be experienceable for this ego and so none is a confirmable, true actuality for it. It then appears that such a variation abolishes the possibility that another ego is experienceable for this ego. Then the two would also have no common world, and there would be no world for them in which both could find themselves. The constitution of a common world, and a correspondingly fixed rule for each ego for itself and for both in relation to each other, is the condition of the possibility of empathic experience, of the being-for-one-another of separate subjects, and of their coexistence as a possible experience and cognition for themselves. The transcendental conclusion is stated with emphasis: a common world, as a rule binding each stream and linking the two, is the condition of the possibility of empathy, the converse of Nr. 1's claim that empathy constitutes the common world. The two are co-constitutive.

(101–102) Now consider this. If a world is constituted as common for any plurality of subjects, the plurality is, as shown, experienceable for each; in this style a coexisting plurality of subjects is thinkable. Conversely, assume that there is a plurality of subjects. Then it must be thinkable and so, in principle, representable as an experienceable plurality. Every representation that achieves this points to a possible subject, a possible subject of a possible experience of this plurality as plurality. That subject would have to stand in a connection of empathy with each of the several subjects; a common world would have to be constituted for it and for each member; it would have to be a bodily, animal subject, and so would each member. The world of the subject possibly experiencing the plurality would have to contain all the bodies of the several subjects and, with them, their surrounding things. Each of these subjects would share in this world; it would have to be constituted for each, or, as for an undeveloped child, constitutable as a real possibility. They would all be related to one world, the conditions of their possible empathy would be constituted, and so, given the appropriate course of their motivation, they would find themselves related, or possibly related, to one another, over against one another. A possible experiencing subject would therefore itself have to belong to the plurality it can experience, for which a world is necessarily constituted, and it would coexist with them only thanks to the form of the world that connects them but also makes separation possible, functioning here as the form of coexistence. It is therefore unthinkable that several subjects coexist unless they are animal subjects for whom, in the rule of their course of consciousness, a world is constituted actually or in real possibility, so that they all "belong to one world". It is also unthinkable that several subjects exist without lying mutually in their ranges of possible experience. Each subject can be thought in and for itself, and we can think an ego as we please in any fictive (only consistent) world to which other egos belong or not. We can further think as we please another world with other egos. These are free possibilities, to be kept carefully apart. Side by side we can think many equally possible worlds with separate groups of ego-subjects belonging to them or lacking. But a priori such worlds, or such pluralities of subjects compossible and possibly coexisting relative to a co-constituted world, are not compossible with one another. There can be only one world, only one time, only one space with one nature and a manifold of animal beings. The deduction concludes by separating possible worlds from compossible worlds: many worlds are each possible, none is compossible with another. The knower who would know two worlds would have to belong to both, which is impossible since he must be embodied in one nature. One time and one space follow as the form of that nature.

#### Result

(102) World and animal beings are inseparable from each other. A nature is unthinkable that is not an animal nature, as subjects are unthinkable that are not animal subjects, that are not related to a nature, and so are not there for one another, "objects" for one another, each lying for the other within the range of possible empathic experience. The result states the double implication with emphasis: no nature without animals, no subjects without nature. The first half claims more than the deduction shows, since it was derived from the existence of several subjects; it makes nature essentially a nature for embodied subjects.

(102) At first one would like to find it obvious that there can be a plurality of subjects absolutely isolated from one another. Each, as existing, is necessarily an object of possible experience; but it satisfies this condition in itself, since as a subject it is experienceable for itself. It need not on that account be an "object" experienceable intersubjectively. On the contrary, each could be endowed with a regulation of its process of consciousness, belonging firmly to its individuality, such that by essence it could in principle experience no other subject. That would be so if, for example, we thought of each as constituting a nature for itself (which would mean a fixed rule for its course of life), but each a different nature, so that the conditions of possible empathy between the subjects in question would have to remain unfulfilled. Yet all such apparently compatible possibilities are a priori incompatible; nothing but nonsense results. The objection is the strongest one, isolated monads each with its own nature, and Husserl's reply depends on the premise that a plurality must be experienceable as a plurality by someone; whoever rejects that premise is not refuted.

(102–103) It is just as wrong to think that if a subject does not exist in my nature, or if I am even so constituted that I have no nature as surroundings, that subject could still exist for itself and then perhaps have another nature and another animal world around it existing for it. All subjects that exist, that coexist, are there for one another and necessarily objects for one another in one time. "Coexist" is fixed as being-for-one-another in one time, which turns Leibniz's pre-established harmony into an intersubjective condition of experience.

### Appendix XI. Notes on the Problem of the Coexistence of Subjects (Probably September 1921)

(103) All non-being presupposes a being. If something is not, something is with whose being the non-existent conflicts. The non-being of something individual is non-being in a duration that has a temporal position. What then does the cancellation of the non-existent by the existent mean? Cancellation by something existing in this duration, in the duration the non-existent as it were claims? Or can the cancellation also mean that something exists in an earlier or later duration? The causality of nature may exclude that something is at such and such a place in space-time because something definite was at such and such a place earlier, and likewise for the future. But can there be empty time? Can there be a nature with empty regions of space-time? Negation is grounded in conflict with something positive, the doctrine of the passive origin of negation in the Analyses concerning Passive Synthesis (Hua XI, §§5–6). Applied to time, it asks whether a stretch can be empty, which would make the non-existence of a second world undecidable.

(103) We are led back to constituting subjectivity. The question of empty time can be settled only by asking how time is constituted, not by asking whether time can be empty in itself.

(103) The coexistence of a plurality of subjects first means "simultaneity", even before we know anything of a common world: being-there-with a subject. The being of both is equivalent to the possibility of a "perception" that perceives both; and then we have simultaneity and relation to an objective world, a world in a presence in which both subjects are present, together with the horizon of objective past and future. A note specifies the presence as "mobile": being in present becoming, with flowing primal present, primal past, and so on. Simultaneity of subjects is defined by a possible perception of both, and the shared present is a mobile, flowing present, a constituted one rather than a fixed point.

(103) In this horizon past bodies can be "inferred" and given, in a peculiar experiential intuition, as having been experienceable, and with them a past subject as "coexisting" in a new sense with the present one. Earlier past. Coexistence across time is opened: a past subject coexists with me through the inferred experienceability of his body, which is the root of Husserl's later analyses of generativity and the community of the dead and the unborn (Hua XV).

(103–104) Questions follow. Can a subject begin? Can a subject cease? Does a subject of a coexisting plurality not require an organic body? Such a body can and must begin and end. What then of the subjects? If subjects in Leibniz's "slumber state" are bodiless, several subjects, how can they be experienceable as a plurality? Does this not bring an unnoticed difficulty into our deductions? The deduction of Nr. 4 required embodiment for plurality; birth and death, and bodiless monads, threaten it.

(104) Or should one refer here to the case of several sleeping subjects? The question is then how sleep, as a "state" of a subject, is given to the subject itself and to another, as a "pause" in waking. But no dead man "wakes". As this human subject he is dead. Sleep is no model for death: the sleeper's body still appresents him, the corpse appresents no one, as Nr. 3, §7 said.

(104) The experienceability of a subject for itself does not mean the free possibility of making everything subjective, the whole stream of experience, really objective for itself. Inhibitions belong to the essence of subjectivity, and certain conditions must be fulfilled to overcome them. The subject is constituted for itself insofar as, by essence, in existing, it is pregiven to itself with a horizon of "possible experience". So too for empathy into others. Self-experience has limits: the stream is not transparent to itself, and access requires overcoming inhibitions (forgotten pasts, sedimented layers). This weakens Nr. 4's premise of a monad satisfying the condition of experienceability by itself, and Husserl notes that the same horizon-structure applies to empathy.

(104) But is everything in principle experienceable? The question is left as a question, the point where the premise "to exist is to be possibly experienced" meets its limit in the unconscious.

(104) A human organism is built up out of organisms, and these again. Does there correspond to empathy, which presupposes a distinctly and properly experienced objective nature, a lower level of intentionality from lower subject to lower subject, from one egoically sleeping subject to another, and does the psychic underground of the soul of the whole body contain a mirroring of those lower "monads"? But what would all that mean? Mutual obscure indications, each feeling what is going on in the other. But does a comprehensive whole feel what is going on in the partial organisms? Husserl entertains Leibniz's hierarchy of monads within organisms and a sub-egoic empathy among them; the question whether the whole "feels" its parts tests whether monads can compose.

(104) There is something psychic that is distributed, as the sensory spheres are, over different bodily organs (if we interpret it so) and that belongs to one soul and one ego because the organs are organically connected in a definite way. But if cutting a worm creates two organisms, the soul is cut into two souls. What of the reproduction of past experiences? Is it cut off, and can a monad divide? Descartes' doctrine of the indivisibility of the ego: did it not mean an indivisibility of the concrete subject, the monad? Kern refers to Appendix XLI. The worm forces the question Appendix IX raised with grafted hands: if the sensory field follows the organism, the monad would be divisible; Husserl suspects that Descartes' indivisibility, properly understood, applies to the concrete monad with its memory, not only to the ego-pole.

### No. 5. The Solipsistic Subjectivity (the Solipsistically Immanent and the Solipsistic Surrounding World) and the Stages of the Objectivation of the Solipsistic. The Constitution of a Communicative Subjectivity, Which Is Called Objectivity, Objective Being, etc. The Categorial Form of Objective Experience (St. Märgen, September 1921)

(105) Physical objects are constituted through hyletic data, and the manner of this constitution implies an essential restriction for different subjects of possible physical experience. To have the same physical nature within the range of their possible experience, several subjects need not agree entirely in their hyletic data, but they are not entirely free in this either. The same physical object is constituted for several subjects only if the identity of this object, which each subject has as a unity of its own experiences, can be constituted. For that we need a knowing subject that stands in possible connection of empathy with these subjects (and may also be identical with one of them from the outset); and if it does, it is easy to see that they themselves must be able to stand in possible empathy with one another. The universe of subjects for which a physical object or a physical nature can be constituted, which can have it within their range of possible experience, may therefore be called a universe of possible mutual understanding. Nr. 4's transitivity argument is restated for objects: sameness of the physical thing for several requires that they be able to understand one another. The constraint on hyle (agreement need not be total, but cannot be arbitrary) is the condition Nr. 6 will study as normality.

(105–106) A transcendent objectivity in general is the correlate of possible mutual understanding, and this is possible only through a constitution of transcendent objectivity that, under demonstrable general and more specific conditions, makes mutual understanding and then objectivity possible. Genuine or pure objectivity stands under conditions of necessary, not merely contingent, agreement, which needs further investigation. An immanent object (the subjective in the sense of data of immanent time) is excluded from this insofar as it is properly, originally, experienceable (perceivable) by only one subject. For every other subject it is appresentable only through empathy, or rather accessible only to other subjects in a connection of empathy with the sensing subject. Then the immanent element of this subject, like the subject in general, becomes experienceable in the new sense, and so does everything subjective in any other sense in its relation to the subject in question. Objectivity and mutual understanding are correlates; the immanent is excluded from objectivity in the first sense because it has only one original experiencer, yet becomes experienceable "in a new sense" through empathy.

(106–107) Regarding objective cognition, which is intersubjective cognition within the ideal universe of subjects standing in actual or ideally possible agreement, of something immanent belonging to the subject S, it must be noted that S has its immanent content given absolutely non-relationally, and what it states on the basis of its "direct" (straight) experience in direct description is stated by it simply, as plain truth. By its sense this truth is "subjective": sayable by no other subject. Yet in itself, as directly descriptive, it expresses no relation to S, or at least not necessarily. S can always relate its truths subjectively in the form "I have the view that...". It can also want to state specifically egoic truths about its various personal properties. But all subject-relations entering a statement are themselves subjective in the same sense as truths stated without relation to the ego; they are not "objective", have no objective sense, like the truths, the statements, that the physicist and psychologist want to make. "Objective" is what everyone (from the designated universe) can experience and state in an identical sense, and what everyone also means in this identity-relation to everyone; this identity itself belongs to the objective sense as the categorial form of objectivity. What I have immanently myself and grasp in immanent experience only I can grasp so (which is itself an insight of objective character, since I think of it as a true statement that everyone must recognize). I can recognize that everyone who stands in actual empathy with me could recognize that I have such and such immanent contents; then the knowledge "I have these sensory contents, and that I have them anyone of this group can recognize after me" is already on the way to objectivity. When would we have objective cognition, in the strict sense, of the immanent? What are its conditions? Answer: if I could know that every subject belonging with me to the ideal universe could at any time convince himself, by actual empathy or by mediate ways (which presupposes a possible reproductive empirical intuition based on earlier empathies), that I had the sensations in question at the place in question in my immanent connection of time, and if this held for the immanent in general, then there would be possibilities of "objective" statements about my immanent contents. If I can know them as knowable after me by everyone, and if this holds as for me so for every knower in relation to every knower and his contents, we have "objectivity" for cognition of the immanent. Kern adds a slip from another manuscript of 1921/22: "The immanent becomes intersubjectively knowable only in connection with its whole subjectivity, the whole monad." The categorial form of objectivity is identity for everyone, which belongs to the sense of an objective statement as its form, not as its content. Psychology as an objective science of the immanent is therefore possible only through this form, and Kern's slip adds that the immanent becomes objective only with its whole monad, never as an isolated datum, so a psychology of isolated sensations has no objective object.

(107–108) Another domain of objectivity, closely interwoven with the possible objectivity of statements about the immanent, is that of statements about physical things, and for both domains the ascent to the height of pure objectivity (the ideal categorial form pure objective truth, whose correlate is the pure objective world) has its stages. In itself the outer evidently precedes the inner, the physical precedes subjective, spiritual objectivity. The physical things I experience, and posit beyond actual experience in thinking mediated by language and concepts, are not subjective in the sense of my immanent sensory data and all my immanent experiences, my ego-acts, and so on. Care must be taken: when we set these objects of external experience as "objective" against the "subjective", the givens of immanent reflection, this concept of objectivity is quite different from the one in question when we speak of the objectivity of truths in themselves and of "objective sciences". "In itself" is also ambiguous, since in a certain way the physical "object" as we are now considering it is already in itself. Here we consider it as we simply experience it and think it in accordance with experience, without any consciousness of a relation of identity to other subjects and experiencers. For me the thing is there and so constituted, for me it is so large and has a measured size, so many times larger than this rod. "For me" here means no relation brought into play in experiencing and thinking, only that in straight regard toward the experienced or thought object (of my possible experience), and toward the relations and conceptual grasps it offers in that regard, I think of no "someone" and no "everyone" who would have a say. Two concepts of objectivity are separated: transcendence to the immanent (the thing as not my datum) and validity for everyone (the objective sciences). The solipsistic thing is already in-itself in the first sense, and "for me" means only that no one else is thought of, not a relation to myself.

(108–109) For us human beings, of course, raised not only in almost constant empathic relation to others but also in language, a stratum directed toward "objective" apperception is already present in the use of the words almost never absent from conceptual (stating) thought, or of the obscure intentions and apperceptions aimed at words. The same holds in the use of many things stamped in advance, so to speak, in this direction. The centimetre measure, the ell, the balance with its calibrated weights, money: these are all "objects". Words bear the stamp of "everyone": "I like everyone" express it so, it is expressed so for everyone, everyone can speak so and understand it. The weights are something generally customary and generally used, understandable for everyone in their "value". Language, measures, and money are institutions of objectivity: things that carry "everyone" in their sense, which places objectivity in culture before science, the argument of the Crisis about the life-world's measuring practices (§9).

(109) However ready the attitudes directed at objectivity may be, we can always abstract from them, and they are not always and necessarily decisive. This is not, of course, abstraction in the sense of disregarding inseparably necessary moments. By this "abstraction" we gain what Husserl also called the "solipsistic" world and view of the world. The solipsistic world is reached by abstraction from a separable stratum, which confirms against misreading that it is a methodical stratum within experience, not a prior stage of life.

(109) Physical things I experience I can without further ado experience as objective: I experience them and at the same time think them as identically experienceable by everyone. I "presentify" to myself an "arbitrary" human being in my surroundings and recognize him, without any linguistic-conceptual thought, as a possible subject for the same things I see. I recognize, in intuitive general cognition, that a physical surrounding I have must be findable by "everyone" assumed to be in it, and that anyone who can stand in empathy with me, who could find me bodily and psychically in his surroundings, could experience my surroundings as well as everyone's. "Everyone" is here an intuitive generality of presentified others, not a concept, which makes the objectivity of things pre-linguistic.

(109) Objective apperception, then, is an apperception of things that has taken up from such thinking a categorial apprehension, not a new stratum of experience, a stratum of "apperception" proper. This is not the first objectification and moreover only an imperfect one. The objectivity of things for everyone is categorial, a form added to thing-apperception, not a new perceptual layer; Husserl will place the first objectification elsewhere.

(109–110) What of the first objectification of subjectivity as ego and person, soul and psychic content? It was discussed above for immanent contents (the subjective in general), which originally are not yet "psychic". Consider soul, the psychic, and its objectification. First we have the formation of the apperception "human being" and in it the appresentation "human soul". This is objectification in the other sense; the double meaning is again felt. The human being there is an "object" for me, over against me, given as experienced through empathy without any "thinking". The "apprehension" of the physical body there as substrate of appresented properties, somatological and psychic, is a basic kind of experience. What is so experienced, the human being, is merely "apperceived" and so merely subjective, insofar as it is an object of my surroundings and its physical body is part of my own environmental content; at first the body there is my solipsistic object. But here already we have not merely my own. It is another, who exists in himself and experiences the same body that is my counterpart as his body and in the null mode of appearance. So the foreign body is the first intersubjective item, and specifically the body of the other. The first understanding of a foreign body as lived body is the first, most primitive step of objectification and constitutes the first, still "imperfect" object, the first thing identified intersubjectively across the experiences of different subjects. Only after it comes the first objectification of the physical surroundings. The second objective item is physical thinghood as not merely mine but "ours". A note asks whether the second is rather the other subject, the other human being facing me as a human being. The order of objectification is fixed: the first intersubjectively identical object is the other's body, given to me externally and to him in the null mode; physical things become "ours" only afterward. This reverses the common view that shared things come first and others are found among them, and it is the step the Fifth Meditation takes when it makes the other's body the first "objective" item (Hua I, §55).

(110–111) As for the subject, it is not only my counterpart and belonging to the "objectified" body, but also a subject to which I am counterpart and which has my body, given to me myself in its subjective mode of appearance, in an external mode of appearance. So the human being too is now constituted in a first objectification, before all conceptual and general thought. The human being is a subject that has another subject over against it, to which it is itself over against; a subject given to itself in an "inner" (solipsistic) way and to its counterpart in an outer (communicative) way, and reciprocally. Again a terminology for this "inner" and "outer" is lacking. Each human being then has his solipsistic surrounding world, and as concordantly experienced and experienceable it is the same as that of the other and of every normal other. But each has the world in his mode of appearance, his solipsistic inner world oriented around his solipsistic inner body (the world in his S-givenness); every other human being has his other S′-givenness; and each grasps the other externally through empathy by grasping his body and things "from himself", while he grasps his own body and things inwardly. For things, however, all modes of apprehension yield the same thing. The human being is defined by reciprocity: inner to himself, outer to the other, and conversely. The S-givenness and S′-givenness are perspectives on one world, and things are what remains the same across all of them.

(111) Two concepts must first be distinguished: objectivity as intersubjectivity, related in its apprehension to a "universe" of subjects, which may however be limited, for instance a community of normal subjects; and "strict" objectivity, related to the actual unlimited "universe", the totality of all knowing subjects in connection with the knower. The distinction opens Nr. 6: relative objectivity for a normal community and strict objectivity for all possible knowers, between which the problem of anomalous subjects, animals, and other species lies.

### No. 6. Differences in the Ontological Structure of the Surrounding Worlds of Different Subjects. Empathy with Children and Animals as Interpretation by Dismantling (St. Märgen, 1921)

(112) Husserl's own summary states the problem. Since the intuitive world, constituted solipsistically, can look different for different subjects, must ontology also differ for each? The consideration leads deeper into the relations of thing, lived body, and subject and contributes to the theory of empathy, specifically with lower animals. The danger is a relativism of regional ontology: if the eidos "thing" is gained from my own experience, a subject with other senses would gain another eidos. "Dismantling" (Abbau) is the method the text introduces in response, the same word Heidegger uses for the destruction of ontology (Hua XIV predates *Sein und Zeit*), here meaning the systematic subtraction of strata from one's own apperception.

(112) For the ontological analysis of the thing as thing of possible confirming experience, Husserl notes that I, the single knower, can start from experience and freely produce concordant courses of experience, and so clarify for myself a thing, and a thing in general, in its necessary eidos as embodied unity of possible experience. Eidetic variation is anchored in my own experience: the eidos thing is gained by varying my actual experience of things.

(112) Ontology exhibits here the essential necessities belonging to the objectivity of possible experience, intuitive objectivity, and specifically to mine, since it is I who bring a totality of possible experience to intuitive synthesis. The ontology of nature is, on this account, the ontology of a nature intuitable for me, and the question is what happens to it when others enter.

(112–113) What happens when we pass to a plurality of subjects connected by communication? Each has his closed experience, his universe of the experienced and experienceable. Insofar as those in a connection of empathy can "introject" one another's experiences in all their correlations, nothing new can come: what the other experiences I could experience myself, and I can take over what he has experienced. But he can have a sense content of intuitive things that I lack. He can experience anomalies I cannot follow. How does this affect the ontology of experience? Can I accept ontological propositions the other knows but I cannot know after him? "Introjection", Avenarius's term, names the ideal of perfectly transferable experience; the difficulty is experience I cannot reproduce.

(113) Ontology, however, investigates the form of possible nature in general; I have a nature given, and I can gain the form of a nature from it by the appropriate method. I must then keep phenomenologically to the form and not count hyle as form. No hyle, even within a hyletic genus given to me, can I invent; I must have experienced it. Beyond what I have experienced I have analogical anticipations of possibilities of a plus ultra. Here, as Hume recognized with genius, there are open problems. So the other too can have sensory spheres "unheard of" for me, and corresponding modes of appearance. A note specifies what belongs to the form: how each sense contributes strata to the constitution of the natural object, how these strata correspond and point to one another, how normality and anomaly belong together. I could not empathize anomalies or new senses into the other if I did not myself experience "anomalies", or experience how one sense functions alone and another is absent (where sight is lacking), and so could also be absent permanently, or from the start. Essential features belong to the ontological form of the thing and to its constitutive form. That the form can be filled differently and separably is given; so it remains that everyone can and must grasp the formal adequately in himself, and the formal element of intersubjectivity in himself through empathy and in community with others through mutual understanding. The answer is a separation of form and hyle: the ontological form of nature (spatiality, causality, the stratified structure of sensing) is accessible to every subject; the hyletic filling is not. Hume's "missing shade of blue" is the limit case of analogical anticipation of unexperienced hyle, and the note grounds my understanding of other sense-worlds in my own experience of losing a sense.

(113) The form of nature is not affected by this. For cognition it can mean a great deal, in the higher as in the lower sense. The other can state many facts I could not; his senses yield differences in things that mine do not and allow connections to be exhibited that I cannot exhibit. Formal ontology is shared, material knowledge is not: the extra sense extends the facts accessible without changing the eidos of a thing.

(113–114) But when can I say that I experience nature, that I have a realm of possible experience of nature in which it could be given to me materially, if in my own way? If I were a jellyfish, would I already have an experience of nature? If I had only smell, like Condillac's statue, would I have experience of nature? The jellyfish and Condillac's statue (from the *Traité des sensations*, 1754) mark the lower limit at which "experience of nature" becomes questionable.

(114) Would the thinking jellyfish also have an ontology of nature? One might almost say that we have the idea of nature in advance and are therefore sure that "our" nature is a fully sufficient nature. That would be to accuse us of a circle. The circle is real if the eidos nature is taken from our experience and then used to judge whether other experience is experience of nature.

(114) Of course we have the idea of nature "innate", Husserl replies. But that only means that we have experience of nature and the capacity, by free variation of actual experience, to form the ideas of possible experience, noetically and noematically. We grasp with insight the eidos experience of nature and nature in all its essential connections. Having that, we have in it the absolutely valid norm by which we can measure even the most remote datum of empathy: whether, for instance, a jellyfish's experience belongs to the idea nature. The circle is broken by the claim that variation reaches the eidos, which is then independent of its factual starting point; "innate" means acquired through the capacity for variation, not given before experience.

(114) That a jellyfish has experience of nature is then seen to be ambiguous. The two senses that follow separate full apperception of nature from a lower stage contained within it.

(114) In the first sense it may mean that the jellyfish has an actual, full apperception of nature, so that, if it had the capacity for categorial thought, it could procure for itself the idea of nature and the whole ontology of nature and of the experience of nature. This sense the dismantling method rules out for the jellyfish.

(114–115) In the second sense, varying the apperception of nature, we can see that within the system of possible variations relatively coherent systems can also be formed that likewise constitute (transcendent) unities of experience, since the apperceptions in question keep the type of apperceptions of nature as their variations. But a distinction is needed here. All possible apperceptions that fit into a full apperception of nature share a type: they always constitute unities of appearance, unities of presentation through adumbrations, optima, and so on. But what is constituted need not be a "full nature" as we have it. It can be an apperception of a lower level, one lacking certain apperceptive horizons, and otherwise contained with its whole content in our apperception, as it were. With its horizon it indicates a coherent system of possible experience contained in our total experience, which in ours, however, constantly carries further horizons and points to more comprehensive systems of fulfilling experience, whereas such references and possible fulfilments could be lacking in "jellyfish experience". Lower experience is our experience with horizons subtracted: it keeps the type (adumbration, optimum) but lacks the references to further systems. The relation is containment, so animal experience is interpreted as part of ours.

(115–116) This can also be discussed genetically. In our experience, in each of our perceptions of things, lies a horizon of possible experience, which points to a genesis of this experience; that genesis must have its levels, in which lower apperceptions and modes of experience with restricted horizons develop, then new unities of experience through new connections, experience of a higher level through higher horizons, and so on. We can dismantle our full experience (perception, the originary apperception of experience) systematically in a certain way: we can consider what perception would have to be like in its horizons if certain experiences were excluded from the genesis, that is, if certain groups of experience had never been possible. For example, we restrict the kinaestheses: we suppose that we could not move from the spot and never could have, that we could never have "approached or moved away from an object". We can then come to say that optically we could have constituted only an "oculomotor" world of things, and so on. If any of the strata of apperception, or of horizons, belonging to nature and the idea of nature were missing in my development, I could of course not say that I know nature, and that what I had given as a transcendent unity of possible experience was a lower stratum of nature, even in a good sense experience of nature. I can say this legitimately only as a fully developed human subject, but I can very well say it of my (legitimately interpreted) embryonic or childhood development with its transcendent apperception. I could say, and do say, that the child perhaps sees the same things as I do but does not yet have the fully developed apperception of them; it still lacks the higher horizons, its experiences have not yet been organized, have not yet taken up the motives through which the child would see the things as what we see them as. The same holds for lower animals. Dismantling is genetic analysis run backward: remove a group of experiences from the history, and see what world remains. The "oculomotor" world refers to the stage analysed in *Thing and Space* (1907, Hua XVI), where a visual field with eye movements alone yields a two-dimensional manifold before locomotion adds depth. The judgment that a lower world is a lower stratum of nature can be made only from the developed standpoint, which makes the child and the animal interpretable only retrospectively, from the adult.

(116–117) Through empathy we apprehend an organic individual as animated and relate it to the same surroundings in which we live. But within our motivation we do so legitimately only by empathizing into it the level of apperception corresponding to the modification of its bodiliness as against ours. Somatological apprehension too has its levels. According to the indications we have, we grasp the sensitivity to stimuli of "touch surfaces" and movements in response to stimuli, which we interpret by analogy as subjective reaction movements. If systems of movement are lacking that can be interpreted as kinaesthetic and that have essential constitutive significance for certain strata of our apprehension of nature, we cannot ascribe those strata to the living being. What we may and must ascribe to it is determined by a variation of our apperception of nature corresponding to the varied bodiliness, or to the stock of perceptual organs whose function we can understand analogically, however indeterminately. The understanding that concerns the somatological is thus functionally connected with the understanding of the kind of "surroundings" there for the subject in question (the jellyfish subject, say), and scientific determinacy, as far as it is possible, is given only by dismantling our own apperception on the basis of its phenomenological system, by excluding the strata for which kinaesthetic motivation, or motivation through sensory organs such as eyes, is lacking. If there are "light-sensitive" spots, eye-like insofar as they can be apprehended as subjectively sensitive to stimuli, the question is what kinaesthetic system we can interpret as "at the disposal" of the unknown psychic subject. And according to the rudimentary kind of that system (obtained by dismantling the motivations accomplished by our richly organized system) we interpret the seeing of this animal: we understand what "things" are constituted visually for this being, what the optical stratum of the whole "thing" looks like for it. The rule of legitimate empathy: ascribe only the world-stratum that the observed body's organs and movement systems could constitute. Bodily similarity governs psychic attribution, and the kinaesthetic system is the decisive variable, because kinaesthesis is what constitutes the thing's spatial strata.

(117) Empathy carried out along an often extremely remote analogy (without following the gradation of analogy in the series of animals) is extremely vague; it is not an experience with a fully determinately formed horizon. Our external experience of natural objects, originally acquired and built up in a systematic genesis, may be ever so indeterminate and even vague in its way. But its apperception prefigures in all circumstances the full ontological form of a thing, a process, a plurality of things, and so on; it is only unfilled, or burdened with a hybrid character, an overlapping of different possibilities: then we have two overlapping horizons, but each with its ontological structure. It is a "thing", only it is unclear whether a horse or a donkey; or the shape is determinate and the colour unclear, ambiguous. Two kinds of vagueness are separated. The vagueness of an unclear thing is vagueness within a fixed ontological form; the vagueness of animal empathy affects the form itself.

(117) It is quite different with the apprehension of an animal, and pre-eminently with a lower animal, into which we cannot, as with a higher animal, nestle and stretch ourselves bodily, so to speak, with the essential organs and groups of organs and the corresponding kinaesthetic systems coinciding. Already with higher animals "dismantling" is needed, but the necessity is not noticed at once. As children we first insert a human being and expect confirmation in the animal's behaviour as if it were human behaviour. We developed humans, regarding a horse, perform analogizing coincidence in richly articulated detail "almost" as with fellow humans, unclear here and there, where coincidences fail and other determinations are needed. The vocabulary of nestling and stretching into the other body describes empathy as a bodily coinciding of organ systems, the "pairing" of 1927 (Nr. 35) seen from its motor side. The child's anthropomorphism is the uncorrected first stage, not a mistake to be explained away.

(117–118) With human beings too it is at first "tout comme chez nous", and only in experience does the other become particularized, and a certain dismantling takes place. But here somatological apperception contains the experience of a body identically of the same type, the same organic "species". To every organ that functions constitutively for the constitution of our own world (our surroundings, not mere nature), every member of our body constituted for us as a bodily organ, there corresponds just such an organ in the other, and to the whole arrangement and connection of our organs, as functional arrangement and connection, there corresponds such an arrangement in the other, at least when the other is not conspicuous through visible bodily anomalies; this is the apprehension. It follows the apprehension of our own body, which is not a mere thing but given as a functioning system of organs (functioning not in secret but in the publicity that alone counts and is alone constitutively significant), given in the manifold of its experienced changes in the individual identity of a functional type. It is just this identity of the functional type that determines the species homo apperceptively. The species is defined phenomenologically by the identity of a functional type of body, not by anatomy or descent: the organs that function in constituting my world, functioning "in publicity", visibly as perceiving and grasping, make the other a member of my species.

(118–119) For a higher animal this species, the type of bodiliness as bodiliness, is already changed: for the ape very little, but hands that function as feet and feet that are also hands require a variation, and still more a tail, an organ wholly foreign to us. The apperceptive prefiguration must be extended there by varying the analogy with our grasping organs. With lower animals the determinate ontological prefiguration is quite minimal, and phenomenologically the limit of the animal (as a being that can still be experienced as a bodily being through empathy) lies precisely where some analogy with our bodiliness is still apperceptively effective, even if only an analogy with sensitivity of the skin and with reaction movements, like a twitch in the skin, resonates, and at the sight of it we feel, as it were, touched or stung in our own skin and twitch, as when we frown on being touched or stung there. A note adds that when we apprehend a plant as psychically animated, it is really only on loan; children do not seriously regard tables as animated either. This indeterminacy of empathy is quite different from the indeterminacy of a thing in vague experience, or from that of a human being whom we do not recognize well (purely somatologically, which is what is at issue here). Whereas a determinate apperception has grown up originarily, as with us the apperception of our body and then that of a fellow human, the experience of animals is mediate, an experience through variation of our bodily type in possibly very remote analogy, and its closer determination has a quite different method. It is not the filling-in of an ontological structure already lying in our horizon but the new formation of a suitable horizon with a suitable formal structure. The limit of empathy is fixed by felt bodily resonance: where seeing the animal's twitch makes my skin twitch, animation is still apperceived, and beyond that (the plant) it is only "on loan".
