Animal experience is mediate, constructed by forming a new horizon, which marks the difference between interpreting a vague thing and interpreting a strange life.

(119) One will probably have to say that a worked-out apperception is created not through progressive mere experience, which would mean that an actual apperception is gained in original generation, but through a construction in the method of intuitive "dismantling" of our own body-surroundings apperception. By excluding motivations of our apperception for which experience offers no support, a variation of our system of motivation is produced, a degeneration, an atrophy in certain directions, and the unity of the atrophied system of motivation is constructed intuitively, as far as this succeeds. It is not mere phantasy: through the experienced supports, a doxic prefiguration is carried out in the experiential motivation. Animal empathy is a construction, not a perception, yet it is not fiction, since the observed body anchors it with belief. The method is the phenomenological basis of comparative psychology and of Jakob von Uexküll's theory of animal *Umwelten* (1909), which Husserl's "the kind of surroundings there for the subject" closely resembles.

### Appendix XII. Notes: Normal Apperception and Apperception of Anomalies. Child, Animal, Variations of Madness (Probably Around 1921)

(119–120) How can empathy with the newborn child and with lower animals take place? How is the child's body apprehended as a lived body while the child's ego is not apprehended as that of a spatial surrounding world? Should one say that it depends on the distinguishable strata of the constitution of bodiliness as such? But is the lowest level of the constitution of things, with spatiality, not already presupposed? If the apperception of the body here is mediate, presupposing that I myself have a fully constituted body with a fully constituted spatial external world and can already apprehend foreign bodies as human bodies in the normal sense, then the question is how I can still hold on to foreign bodies as such while interpreting them through an anomalous human (and animal) subjectivity. The newborn sharpens the paradox: I see its body in space, but its ego must be interpreted as one without a spatial world. Empathy must therefore separate the spatiality of the perceived body from the spatiality of the empathized surroundings.

(120) How can my pure subjectivity, which is factually of the "normal" type, be varied so that the spatial world is lost, or becomes thinkable in a genesis in which spatiality is not yet constituted? If I start from clarified intersubjectivity, constituted in normal empathy as a community of normal human beings, then every human subject can be varied in such and such ways as to its psyche. What variations are possible if I am still to apperceive a foreign body as a lived body, or to carry out the human, or a more general animal, apperception as a "variation" of the normal human one, that is, to confirm it consistently as an apperception of psychophysical objects in the surroundings? The question is the range of variation that preserves empathy, the same question Nr. 2 asked about the range of variation that preserves the ego.

(120) This extraordinarily important sphere of problems also includes the apperception "mentally ill human being", and also "mentally ill animal". In a certain way child and animal are an "anomaly", and yet they belong to the system of the normal world; indeed the human being becomes normal in a normal stage of development, with the embryo and so on as normal starting stage. But everything normal at every stage and within every limitation has its horizon of possible anomalies. The child is anomalous relative to the adult yet normal as a stage; normality is therefore relative to stage and kind, each with its own horizon of anomalies.

(120) The basic problem, then, is the apperception "anomalous objectivity", anomalous animality, anomalous human being, anomalous cultural objects, and their intentional mediacy lying in their very sense, their back-reference in sense to the corresponding normal apperception. Anomaly is a sense that refers back to normality: to apperceive something as anomalous is to apperceive it as a deviation from a norm co-given in the apperception, a structure of "intentional mediacy" that also governs anomalous cultural objects (a broken tool, a misprint).

### Appendix XIII. Normality and Animal Species (Evidently St. Märgen, 1921)

(120) Husserl's summary records the preceding topic, normal bodiliness and normal psychic life, for each organ, for the whole of bodiliness, and for each species, all considered constitutively and later also transcendentally, and names the problem: the transcendental necessity of a system of organic and psychic development. The appendix moves from description to a transcendental question: is a developmental system of organisms a condition of the possibility of a world?

(120–121) Norm. A world is constituted as universe of possible spatio-temporal experience in relation to a bodiliness: a constitutive system of experience has been formed and is familiar on all sides. Already here there are differences of presentation relative to the different organs of perception. The same determinations of things (intuitive ones, belonging to the phantom) present themselves in different "senses" by means of different organs; in general the modes of givenness differentiate in parallel and are connected by mutual indication. Not only do optical and haptic presentations differ (the thing, as to the determination in question, gives itself differently to this sense and that), but within the same sense there are differences again: in the field of touch, the special touching organs (various fingers, lips) by no means agree qualitatively, and the richness of differentiation varies. Some things do not present themselves to one sense that present themselves to another; what presents itself to the touching finger does not present itself to the touching foot, much is lost there. In the normal system every presentation here has horizons, including some that point to the parallel senses (single organs within a sensory genus) and their knowing better, so to speak. That is why I pass from touching with the back of the hand or the foot to touching with the fingertips. "Normal" bodiliness includes a system of possibilities of presentation, worse and better, and to this belongs the idea of an optimal mode of givenness or a possible optimal system of modes of givenness, which at a certain level stands for the truth. Normality is first an internal order of my organs: each sense, each fingertip, ranked by richness of presentation, and the optimum "stands for the truth" at a level. The optimum of *Thing and Space* and Ideen II (§18) is now the basis of the concept of normality.

(121) If my bodiliness becomes anomalous in one direction or another, or functions anomalously, under anomalous conditions or influences affecting perception, the new modes of appearance are apprehended as related to changed circumstances and as belonging to them; the constitutive system expands, and so do its horizons. The original normality, to which a certain typical constancy in the behaviour of bodiliness belongs (a typical position within the whole typology of bodiliness), is broken through; the "world" seems to change, quite unexpectedly; but after the expansion of apperception it is said to be mere appearance. One may not notice that this type of bodily normality has undergone change here or there; as soon as one notices it and recognizes the empirical relation of the changes in the view of the world to it, everything is in order. Apperception expands by new systems of "if–then". The idea of the optimum may also expand. Many deviations are not merely changes but diminutions in the richness of the properties of things that still present themselves, as in "pathological" changes. But if, as a man born blind, I am operated on, understood as solus, the content of the world is enriched for me: the same world has more determinations than I knew, and they now appear. As I gain control over the operated eye, the system of my normal aspects of the world changes, and so do the idea of my normal bodiliness and my optimal givenness of the world. Anomaly is absorbed by expanding the "if–then" system to include bodily conditions, so the world stays the same and only its appearance is attributed to the body. The operated blind man (Molyneux's case) shows the converse: normality itself can be raised, and the optimum is revisable upward.

(121–122) So far we have imagined ourselves as soli. Now I enter into relations with others, intersubjective constitution arises, and it proceeds in the same way. Apart from the anomalies I can empathize intuitively into the other, which belong to the system of constitution that has constituted for me the normally and anomalously experienced world as a unity of concordant experience, I find there colour-blind people and the like, or am myself "colour-blind" and recognize others as colour-normal. I perform an extended constitution with the help of "indirect" representation. It belongs to the world that it presents itself not only in my original constitutive system, systematically so and so in relation to bodily conditions, but also, for everyone, in other ways, and it is the unity of all such actual and possible presentations. To this again belongs the idea of the correct determination, which we can all gain together. But that is no longer an intuition for everyone, and a priori it is always open that new subjects enter this connection and bring still more of things to experience. This experience always concerns the "qualities", the contents of the phantoms, on whose experience all causal experience rests. The same world can be maintained concordantly for ever "higher" subjects, who experience ever more determinations of "it", and I can never say, no experiencing subjectivity can say, that it is absolutely certain that no one will experience more determining content, perhaps whole sides of the world hitherto hidden, sides to which a "higher" bodiliness would belong. The colour-blind man's case introduces "indirect representation": he knows of colours he cannot intuit. Objectivity then outruns intuition: the true world is a unity of presentations no one has all of, and is open to "higher" subjects with senses we lack, which makes the qualitative world essentially incomplete.

(122–123) What then remains of a normal experience of the world in relation to normal bodiliness? Constitutively we originally have, with a vague horizon, the "intuitive world" in its possible variations correlated with variations of bodiliness, both solipsistically, as anomalous changes of my body and its functioning, and partly intersubjectively, as variations of the "view of the world" that I can find or suppose in dependence on changed species of physical animal bodiliness. Let us look around in the animal world in general and follow the analogy. We have a biological-physical normality: the biological individual, the physical-organic, "maintains" itself and develops normally under the normal conditions "favourable" to its preservation and individual development; unfavourable conditions yield atrophy, disease, and so on. To this corresponds, for an animal being in psychic respects, the normal functioning of the perceiving body, a normal "view of the world", a normal constitutive system for a "world" that stands there constantly as experienced and experienceable for it. Deviations through disease and the passage back to health, or the removal of physical anomalies through "operation" and the like, mean something in consciousness for the constitutive system of the experienced world. The system necessarily acquires an appendix of anomalous deviations, which must be fitted in with respect to their conditions, or an expansion through which it signifies, objectively and finally subjectively, the establishment or re-establishment of the normal view of the world. Biological normality (self-preservation under favourable conditions) and constitutive normality (a normal world-view) correspond, and healing restores both at once.

(123) Biophysical normality and anomaly thus have a constitutive counterpart, for the system of constitutive experiences of the world. On the biophysical side only the "perceiving bodiliness" is relevant. This parallel of biophysical and psychic development, here of the constitution of the view of the world, is given in experience and belongs to the constitution of the experienced world itself. We therefore have an idea of normality that is also intersubjectively dominant. All "normal human beings" have normal bodiliness with normal organs of perception, are then also psychically normal, and experience the same intuitive world. But all also stand under the rule of anomaly: as the physical organism can maintain itself despite single injuries, lose its full normality and yet vary, deviating from the normal type, in coordinated anomalous types (corresponding to the coordinated anomalous external conditions of life), so it is to some extent psychically too. There is thus a system of anomalous "views of the world", determined as the human system of possible anomalies, to which my human system of anomalous perceiving bodiliness belongs as correlate. Something similar must be assumed for every animal species. Anomaly is not chaos: it forms a system of types correlated with types of bodily deviation, so each species has a normal world-view and an ordered family of anomalous ones.

(123) In this parallelism, the biophysical optimum (as against the deviations) corresponds to the constitution of the normal view of the world, the system of possible normal perceptions, as health is, so to speak, the optimum. It lies in the line of organic teleology. Anomaly has its gradations according to the deflection of the teleology. What holds of whole living beings holds of single organs and systems of organs and their normality and anomaly. Health and constitutive optimum coincide under an organic teleology, the first appearance in the volume of the teleology that dominates Husserl's late manuscripts (Hua XV, Hua XLII).

(123–124) We now see the being of the world related to the co-existence of normal bodies that form a system of levels: factually, as the animal species show, but also ideally prescribed, since a priori the idea of a normal constitution of a world includes the possibility that it contains human-like beings (or animals) with a richer and better intuition "of this same world", and others with a poorer one. All these beings of different levels are related to this world in concordant experience, and to all belong anomalies that fit in. But is there not also intellectual and other madness, including a madness of perception, in which the subject can no longer maintain a unified world in its perceptions at all? What possibilities exist here? Is it possible that, becoming normal again, I remember my perceptual madness solipsistically? Is self-preservation possible with madness of this kind, through the care of others perhaps? This presupposes that a world exists. How much madness is compatible with the existence of the world? Everything depends on the answer to the question what the correlation between the being of the world, and first of nature, and possible cognition of the world includes. If normal human beings, or normal intuiting beings at all, exist in the world, a world is constituted, with reservations, in concordance for them, the world in which they themselves live. But this constitution has different levels even as normal constitution, and each level has higher levels above it as open possibility. What a being or species experiences is "its" world, a subjective-objective world, with itself as animal species in it, in its way and with its open horizons. A species of higher intelligence finds a more richly determined world, and, finding the lower species in its world, recognizes its superiority and at the same time understands, as well as it can, the form of givenness of the world as subjective for the lower species, with much poorer contents of determination, though normal for a normal species of that lower kind. When a madman faces a human being, even this anomaly (which can from the start be apprehended as a bodily anomaly, parallel to other illnesses) can fit into the normal system of human experience. But what does the possibility of still performing empathy presuppose as a minimum? Perceptual madness, a subject unable to hold a world together, tests the correlation of being and experience: the world survives only if the madman's incoherence can be understood from within the normal system, as an illness. The question of the minimum for empathy is the question at what point another subject ceases to be understandable as having a world at all.

#### Normal and Anomalous Empathy (Here as Purely Somatological Experience)

(124–125) Must we not distinguish normal and anomalous empathy? In normal empathy I apprehend the body as a human body and appresent an analogue of my subjectivity and of my intuitive surroundings, only "seen from there", and this can confirm itself continually as empathy proceeds. If the appresentation is not confirmed, that does not yet mean that this is no lived body but a wooden or wax doll or the like, something that recalls a body and is none, a sham human being and not a real human being, not a real psychophysical being. A note distinguishes non-confirmation by cancellation of the empathy, striking out the appresented soul, from non-confirmation by other determination; and it marks a special case. Normal (fully intuitive) appresentative "perception" consists in perceiving the other as perceiving such and such in the surroundings, and this includes "the same that I perceive from my side". The anomaly consists in the latter not holding: he sees the same as I but sees it qualified differently. But, Husserl asks, can one "see" that? Empathy may still be confirmed: it is a human being, but an anomalous one, and this includes anomalies of sensibility and the like, which manifest themselves in their way within the framework of an empathy that is itself no longer normal. The question is then what anomalies of empathy are possible, what variations the fulfilment of an empathy first performed normally permits through the disappointment of its expectations without ceasing to be empathy. Two kinds of failed empathy are distinguished: cancellation (the wax figure, the soul struck out) and redetermination (a human, but anomalous). The doubt "can one see that?" concerns whether a differently qualified seeing of the same thing can be given in appresentation, or only inferred from behaviour.

(125) Here a human body of its specific type lay before us. The problem recurs when we follow the analogy of organic species and ask, along this series, about the limits and minimum of possible empathy or of empathic contents, perhaps about the closed system of empathizable contents to which, corresponding to a normal organic species and its normal behaviour, we assign a normal inner life, and about the madness of each such species. To gain an interpretation that can be grasped scientifically, is it not necessary to enter the realm of the ideally possible variations of the solipsistic givens of one's own inner life, to construct eidetic types there, to distinguish strata and vary them, to consider founding strata as to their possible one-sided detachability and relative independence, as the ancients already did in a certain but crude way with their distinction of parts of the soul? A note sets out the basis: somatological empathy and, probably together with it, empathy of the surrounding world of things, already stratified as sensory sensibility, sensibility of feeling and drive, the affective ego, the active ego. The parts of the soul in Plato and Aristotle (nutritive, sensitive, rational) are taken as a crude anticipation of an eidetic stratification by one-sided founding, which is the phenomenological reformulation of the scala naturae.

(125) Only from one soul, that of the knower, which alone is a priori primally given to him, can every other soul and every possible content of empathy be investigated, and then only by the method of eidetic variation, which, ideally speaking, must have all possibilities within its horizon. The methodological solipsism of the inquiry is stated: every other soul is accessible only as a variant of mine.

#### The Apperception of "Animals" Already Presupposes My Own Apperception as I-Human as Primal Apperception

(125) Empathy is an apperception. In every apperception the origin prescribes a norm of sense. Once a determinate sense-giving has been carried out in one case, like material for apprehension is apprehended in like sense. The object is new, but it coincides in sense with the earlier one or with the chain of those apprehended so earlier. Similar material too can be apprehended in a similar sense, and the structure and articulation of sense can be the same if it is expressed in the material to be apprehended. The concrete concept is thereby prefigured, and its variations through variations of single components of sense while the general form of sense is preserved. But apperception can also take place through anomaly on the one hand and through remote analogy on the other, instead of through identity of sense or of a kind of sense. Apperceptive transfer from an original institution (Urstiftung) is the general law to which empathy belongs, the principle the Fifth Meditation (§50) applies to the other. Anomaly and remote analogy are deviant forms of the same transfer.

(126) I see a human being, but he has no arms. I recognize him, I perform empathy, but I discover that he is blind. The frame of sense has a hole that is unfilled, quite differently from how it was apperceived as filled. Anomaly is a gap in a frame of sense that the apperception had filled; it presupposes the full frame.

(126) But what when I, a human being, see an animal? First a higher animal. It still resembles a human being, a distorted human being, so to speak, in bodily respects, changed and malformed in particulars, "anomalous": the hands lengthened and turned into feet, the fingers into claws. This "degeneration" can be understood. I can think not only that limbs look different outwardly and change, but also that parts with their sensitivities and functions drop out, that whole limbs drop out, finally that it is a mere head with a trunk, but mobile. How far I can understand, how far I can empathize, is determined by the ideal variations of the primal type human. I, who am a human being, perform normal empathy as perception of the other; a similar foreign bodily thing is thereby supplemented without further ado with the same supplementary stock of sense that belongs to my body under corresponding circumstances, and with the possible variations belonging to mine. The animal is a "distorted human", interpreted by subtracting and transforming my organs; this anthropocentric method is not a prejudice but the only access, since the primal type is the one given from within.

(126) My body in "inner experience", the solipsistic, is thus the primal apperception and gives the necessary norm. Everything else is a variation of this norm. Every variation that occurs in many exemplars in experience then designates a new type, normal relative to its anomalous variations. How is it in the purely physical sphere of things? There no primal thing is distinguished in consciousness, and there is no normality or anomaly. There are no anomalous things, but are there anomalous crystals, fir trees, roses? Empirical kinds, purely physical. All this is important for clarifying the idea of empirical genera and species. My own body, given from within, is the primal norm, and every type is a variation that becomes a norm for its own variants. Physical things have no primal exemplar, hence no normality, which marks the difference between the logic of empirical kinds in nature and the logic of living species.

(126–127) I exist, and exist as a person in relation to my surroundings, and an infinite nature is given presumptively in concordantly maintained experience, but in empirical certainty and continually confirming itself. In this world I find my body, as functioning related to the world in its modes of givenness, and I find other human beings and also animals, normal and anomalous, the normal experiencing the same world in correspondingly normal ways. I find normal human beings perceptually in normal empathy, in appresentative intuition, intuitable and understandable on both their sides and given with empirical certainty as beings like me; the other beings in partial and analogical, remote and improper intuitiveness. The factual starting point of the eidetic question is restated: normal humans are given "on both sides", body and mind, other beings only analogically.

(126–127) What are the essential necessities if I take the existence of these beings experienced by me as exemplary cases for pure possibilities? What variations are possible? Can a physical nature be held fixed, varied but still a physical nature, if all "souls" are struck out? To the actual world we experience and posit with reason belong we ourselves, human beings who experience it, and their experiences themselves, human beings who reflect on it and project sciences of it. We vary the world into a possible one if we vary human beings so that they no longer think logically and are directed at no objective truth and science. We who vary them so in thought belong to the actual world, in which we now project a phantasy of a world possible in thought. What do we do to human bodiliness thereby? Can we think a world wholly without human beings, and an intuition of the world without human bodiliness? Varying the world to remove knowers is a variation performed by knowers within the actual world; the question whether a world without humans is thinkable is answered at the end of Appendix XIV, negatively.

(127) If we consider the possibility of a world and find that it must be intuitively constituted, it can still be intuitively constituted at different levels, perhaps simultaneously, with the different intuiting beings, intuiting at different levels, able to stand in possible empathy. But if the world intuited at different levels exists, it must be knowable in its truth. Let us think a "human being" who has never thought but merely intuited, if we want to call that a human being: subject of his intuitive surroundings, experiencing himself bodily in them and so finding himself there too, with lower animals around him that he understands, and likewise fellow humans. Does the ideal possibility that these "human beings" can be thought as fully thinking ones suffice? Does the empirical view of the world and its empirically continuing concordance suffice? Is it not only active thinking and active reason, which make their projects, experiment, observe, theorize, and so work out true being, that first know the true being of a world and dismiss its non-being as irrational, as they must? Certainly. But in essence the situation is not changed on either side. The concordance of mere experience in its progress constantly increases the force of anticipation and constantly widens the range of constitution. Pre-scientific experience and science differ in degree, not in kind: both are anticipation strengthened by concordance, which is the continuity between the life-world and science that the Crisis asserts.

(127) Thinking, science, increases the force of anticipation quite incomparably, and likewise the range of constitution; each of its steps has, so to speak, the force of infinities of actual experience and opens up new infinities constitutively. But it necessarily remains anticipation. All we can say is: if a world exists, it is thinkable only if a corresponding monad is posited in which the conditions of possible concordant experience of this world and possible cognition of it are realized. Science remains anticipation, so the existence of a world cannot be proved by it; what can be said is the conditional, that a world requires a monad in which its knowability is realized.

(127–128) One monad? Perhaps rather a system of monads standing in the unity of a developmental connection, which realizes the various possible intuitions of one and the same world in stages and developmentally, rising to the human being and finally to the human being of ever-increasing intelligence and science, who has so rich a presentation of the world and so developed a power of reason that he can know scientifically what the world is in truth in every respect, and at the same time understand all monads through empathy and through phenomenological-eidetic research into the possibilities of consciousness, and find them so determinately indicated by their insertion into the world, that he can realize the true being of the world according to all possible intuitions in his experiential knowledge. The monad required is a developing community culminating in the phenomenologist, who understands all monads by empathy and eidetic research and so realizes the true world as the synthesis of all intuitions, a teleological picture close to Hegel's absolute knowing.

(128) A monad can exist only by developing, and a view of the world can exist only as its product of development. The true world as intuitively realized is thinkable only as something developed in the self-development of consciousness. True being is only an index for consciousness and, for every existing monadic consciousness, an index of possible development, but of really possible, not merely imaginable, development: a rule for development. Every consciousness necessarily has hyletic data. But if they came lawlessly, that would contradict a development of the view of the world. Every consciousness must therefore be furnished with hyletic data, they must come and go so, that the necessary development moves in the direction of a view of the world, however low at first, and that of every monad must agree with that of every other. Every monad must constitute a body, and this body must be really given in the view of the world of each, or implied in its horizon. Empirically speaking, every development is limited, bound by the organic kind of bodiliness. Is it a mere fact that every body develops only to a certain height (the mature animal), and could it not develop in infinitum so as to become the organ of knowledge of the full world? Or is there a transcendental necessity that a world can be constituted only together with a developmental system of animals and animal monads, with a certain parallelism of psychic and bodily development (here pursued on the side of cognition)? Does the world itself not develop in this system in which ever new animals and species are "generated", though it keeps a pervasive structure under the title physical nature, which retains its fixed lawfulness and with it lasting being through all changes of its absolute elements? Or could physical nature also develop, only so that a complex of laws is preserved and a pervasive identity? What could development mean there? The appearance of new laws that do not disturb the old? Of new elements? Or rather the appearance of ever new organization in new types, which must, however, fulfil transcendental conditions? Being as "a rule for development" requires that hyle not be lawless: the contingency of sensation that Nr. 2 asserted is now limited by a transcendental requirement that hyle be ordered toward a world, and that all monads' hyle agree. Darwinian evolution is reinterpreted as the development of the world's own constitution, and the closing alternative locates development in organization rather than in physical law.

(128–129) "Our" world has its determinate structure. Subjectivity is objectively there in it in the form of animal and human subjectivity, in a system of reproduction, embodiment, and development in which ever new monads become objectified, appearing newly as souls, and old ones disappear as souls. And as the bodiliness of the world is really a single unity branching typically in infinitum, constantly dying off and reproducing itself in its individuals, so are the parallel psychic monads. It seems as if, in reproduction, together with the physical germ, a psychic germ, a germ-monad, branches off from the lower passive psychic underground and in development constitutes in itself an individuality of its own, perhaps a higher personal and rational one. Husserl entertains a "germ-monad" splitting from a parent's passive underground, a psychic counterpart of germ-plasm continuity; generativity enters transcendental philosophy as the problem of how monads begin.

(129) All consciousness standing in this world-connection is then, it seems, a unitarily connected consciousness, or better psychic life, with an ontogenetic and phylogenetic unity. But the question is whether that is a correct and possible interpretation: whether a "germ" branches off from a monad (Leibniz's concept already implies that this can be held impossible), instead of something branching off from its body, an organic germ that, like every partial organism that is itself an organism, bears a soul developing in the new conditions in parallel with the organic germ and its physical conditions. Leibniz's monads cannot split; so the germ must branch from the body, and the new soul is the soul of a new organism, which preserves monadic indivisibility at the cost of making souls attach to organisms.

(129) If one then tries to regard the whole world as one universal psychophysical organism, one sees that this does not work and that the concept of organism fails here. This universal organism would have no external world; physically it would no longer be a body developing under surrounding conditions, and psychically there would no longer be an ego that could be active, as animal, in relation to surroundings in the animal-human sense. The world-soul fails because organism and animality require an outside, surroundings, which the whole cannot have.

(129) The existence of a common world for subjects presupposes the objective animal existence of the subjects in the world, as conversely the coexistence of several subjects presupposes a common world for them in which they themselves are animal. If, then, subjects of a world, or subjects who are there for one another and coexist, must have a body in their world and so be animal in the widest sense, what does this idea of animality necessarily prescribe, and what can be grounded here on transcendental grounds of the possibility of cognition? Nr. 4's result is restated as the premise for asking what animality must include.

(129–130) Could a world exist that lacked knowledge of itself for all eternity for empirical reasons? Would it suffice to say, on transcendental grounds, that if a world exists it must be an organic world in which organisms bear souls? But the level of organization may be arbitrarily low, and organization may remain eternally at low levels; nothing like development need exist; the organisms of our world happen to have the basic properties of reproduction, ontogenesis, and phylogenesis. A lived body means only a system of indication of the psychic, and that need not reach further. Must a body be a thing of metabolism that maintains itself only as a typical individuality? Could such an individuality not also be unoriginated and imperishable (meaning by origination the organic coming-to-be of a new individuality), although each such organic individuality is constantly in origination and in typical self-maintenance, enduring like a rainbow? The minimal transcendental requirement may be only that a world contain bodies indicating souls, with development a contingent addition; the rainbow image, a form persisting through constant replacement, describes metabolic identity.

(130) Can one not deduce a priori, in formal propositions, how far this must reach in forma? One would at first think it quite possible that there be a world that contains, for all eternity, only the lowest "animals", and these enduring eternally, if the world is assumed eternal. But that is already a problem. Must a world be eternal? Must consciousness, spirituality, endure eternally, if the world, if nature, is eternal? Here in general lie the problems of time and infinity. The question of the eternity of the world and of mind is raised as a transcendental question, the antinomy Kant posed, here bound to the conditions of development.

(130) Husserl had said that every subject is in development and its world is a product of development, so a stage of development must have preceded experience of the world. But hyletic data had to be there already. If they were there from infinity, they were so either without change or with change. Without change, there could be no development of the subject; the subject would have to sleep, be a sleeping monad. With change, the change was either chaotic or regulated. Can one see that in the latter case a world had to constitute itself, and the world would have had to be constituted long ago; and in the other case that no world could ever constitute itself, since the world includes the existence of everything spiritual as psychic in all the past, and a world with an infinite time in both directions is and must be constituted? The dilemma tries to deduce a beginning of world-constitution from the structure of hyle, sleeping, chaotic, or regulated, and stalls on the infinite past.

(130) But all these are somewhat arbitrary attempts. According to Leibniz there are sleeping monads that, if Husserl understands rightly, need not be monads of bodies. Leibniz constructed the world in intuitive possibilities, but that is no scientific attempt at a systematic eidetics, which alone could help us. Husserl judges his own speculation by the standard he set Leibniz: intuitive construction is not eidetic science.

(130) One will not say that the existence of the world requires that in every period of its being there be human beings or still higher knowing subjects; already human beings with science are higher than human beings without science. The requirement of knowers at every time is dropped; science itself marks a level within humanity.

(130) But does the possibility of a world, as intentionally constitutable, as the identical surroundings of possible experience for its subjects, not require a unity of the course of development of its subjectivity that can never be lacking, so that the world has a beginning as the beginning of development for its subjects, and then the whole typology of organic and psychophysical development with ever new individualities? But the beginning may itself be the death of an earlier development (a world period). Husserl entertains cyclical world periods, each beginning as the death of an earlier development, which defers the problem of a first beginning.

(131) Must the creating God try out all possibilities, and is this trying something like the constitution of the "true" world, for which the world periods are the "appearances"? Order must now be brought into such transcendental trains of thought. It must be possible to set fact and ideal possibility and necessity in a scientific relation. The theological image, God running through possibilities as constitution of a true world appearing in world periods, is set aside with a demand for method: the relation of fact to eidos must be made scientific, the problem of facticity that Husserl's late metaphysics (Hua XV, Nr. 22) addresses.

### Appendix XIV. The Problem of the Identity and Truth of the World in the Experiences of Different Kinds of Subjects (St. Märgen, 1 October 1921)

(131) How is it possible that my solipsistically experienced thing, characterized descriptively so and so, is knowable for the other through empathy as the same, while the other is known by me as experiencing this object in a descriptively different way? How can we legitimately say that we experience the same thing while we convince ourselves that one of us is colour-blind? If I experience a red house and experience that the other experiences the same house, how can I experience that he experiences the same house differently? The problem is identity across qualitative difference: I must posit the same thing and a different appearance of it for him.

(131) One can also ask why in normal empathy, which rests on analogizing, not merely something similar, a similar surrounding world, is empathized but something like, indeed identical: the same nature for all, despite discrepancies in the intuitive, concordantly experienced nature each experiences in himself. Analogy should yield similarity, yet empathy yields identity; this asymmetry is the problem the whole volume circles, and its 1927 answer is that the other's surroundings are given as mine "from there".

(131–132) An exact analysis of motivation in empathy, and of its solipsistic basis before empathy, is needed. First, it must be shown that the solipsistic motivation in which the thing, the sensory world, is constituted apperceptively has its necessary norm in a normal apperception, which is also the necessarily original one but is at first not recognized as a particular apperception. In it the normal thing is constituted, the thing that truly is in concordant experience, a truth that in later development is characterized as subjective truth. Then, not necessarily preceding empathy, there is a further level: single anomalies for single organs of perception stand out as "diseases" of them, with anomalous changes of intuitive things bound to them, "as if" the things themselves had changed, while the other organs, functioning perceptually, continue to give the true thing unchanged. In general there is a kind of change in "appearance" related to changes in bodiliness, a change recognized as a true change through the normal functioning of the other organs. The solus ipse thus identifies sense-things that differ as sense-things and apprehends them as "appearances" of the same thing. The sense-things that, in the normal perceptual functioning of the body, did appear in relation to it but were posited simply, without thought of this relation, are now relativized: certain bodily changes, and perhaps other unknown ones, draw the sense-things into conditional sympathy. The normal thing is the norm to which all deviating appearances must point. For the solus ipse it is the true thing, the true subjective thing. A note asks whether "norm" means more than "reference system" and judges the discussion insufficient: the idea of normality, its ambiguity, and a classification of the basic kinds of anomaly must be clarified. Normality is invisible until anomaly appears: the normal apperception is first simply perception, and only the diseased organ relativizes appearances to the body. The note's question whether a norm is more than a reference system decides whether normality carries truth or only privilege of position.

(132) Second, once this has been established ontologically and recognized as genetically necessary in its motivations, the ontological and motivational analysis of empathy can be considered. The normal sensory-intuitive thing, the solipsistic one, is presupposed in empathy. If no anomaly is seen in the foreign body, it is empathized without further ado as the same. Likewise every anomaly is empathized as one producing the same anomalous appearances I know as solus. This also means that the subjects who form a universe of reciprocal communication with me necessarily apprehend one another as subjects whose world of experience is related to the same norm, the same idea of a normal bodiliness, and whose intuitive truth is exactly the same for all, however many may not know that truth face to face. For the colour-blind man the normally colour-seeing man is the norm. Identity in empathy rests on a shared norm of bodiliness: I empathize my normal world into a normal-looking body, and the colour-blind accept the colour-seer as norm. The norm, not the analogy, carries identity.

(132–133) What of animals and the animal surrounding world? We do relate animals, with their sensory intuitions, which we think with reason to deviate presumably very far, to the same things, the same world we experience. Are we and our normal human bodiliness the norm, and the animals' sensory intuitions anomalous deviations from it? At any rate everything must be thought through carefully here, and it must be observed that animals too, all beings we relate even possibly to the same world that is there for us, are bound to a norm as a distinguished system of reference. A long note asks whether a normal system, which must be constituted originally, must always remain the system of reference, and works through the ideal possibilities. The perceiving body is at my free disposal; insofar as that means influence on its functioning, the free activity belonging to functioning itself, the body remains unchanged as body in every such functioning, and correlatively the world that appears, as experienced, does not change; it only looks different again and again, offering other aspects. If the body changes without my doing and outside the system of changes subject to my free activity as perceiving (passive movement of my perceptual organs), then "everything looks different" in a way that concerns not contingent aspects but whole systems of aspects, the "appearing thing" itself. But I can also exercise power over my body in another way, as I do through it over other things: by injuring myself, striking my eye so as to cause disturbances of vision. Why do such interventions not belong to an extended system of perceptual functions? If by such doing we gained new courses of appearances, had free power over them at any time, and gained them so that they fitted into a concordant constitutive system, we would also do this constantly and call it perceiving. It must be described what is missing here and why all such doing, up to santonin, yields nothing constitutively essential except the "anomalous". Then, in the intersubjective system of experience, the constitution of the intersubjective intuitive and then true world: everyone has his bodiliness (bodiliness is always the counter-member), functioning normally and anomalously, in a normal state. But must all normalities correspond exactly; is not a connecting common normality presupposed as an idea? It must be shown for the solus ipse as for the communicative system that we always stand in systems of concordance in which the true is something identical and everything qualitative is relative, and which yet permit the identity of object and property to be maintained; and how this is the basis for the idea of a logical determination of things. The note asks why self-induced anomaly (a blow to the eye, the yellow vision induced by santonin) does not count as a new mode of perceiving. The answer implied is that perception requires free, repeatable control fitting into a concordant system; the anomalous is what falls outside free kinaesthetic disposal. The final demand, identity of object and relativity of quality, sets the problem of the physical thing as the logical determination that survives all qualitative relativity.

(133) What also emerges is that we must strictly distinguish two universes: a universe of possible subjects empathizing reciprocally, subjects who know themselves related to the same world and experience themselves as so related; and a universe of subjects that I, or a determinate subject, can "reach" through empathy, a universe of one-sided empathy. It then necessarily experiences or knows all these subjects as related in consciousness to the same world, so that in their solipsistic surroundings they have appearances of things of this same world. But that does not mean they can grasp me and all others empathically and assign the same world to all. The proposition holds that such a universe of one-sided empathy, constituted for one subject, can count as constituted or constitutable for every subject that forms a universe of reciprocal empathy with it. One-sided empathy, the human understanding the animal that cannot understand him, is a distinct relation, and humans share their one-sided universe among themselves. Animals belong to the world as experiencers without belonging to the communicative community.

(133–134) Great problems arise from here. If we start, in eidetic and in a certain way formal consideration, from any subject that has constituted a world on the basis of a normality, that normality is something relative. A people of colour-blind persons is thinkable, whose children constitute the world as it looks to the "colour-blind" as normal. If this people enters into relation with us and our normality, it will accept our normality, as the single colour-blind person among us does, who, if colour-blind from birth, accommodates himself to us in the necessarily solipsistic constitution of his world without being able to gain our intuitions themselves. He recognizes indirectly that his world is not the optimal one, that ours is, as it were, the "truer". Different normalities thus collide through empathy, as already happens in human life (not every anomaly bears the stigma of pathological degeneration, and how "disease" is to be defined formally, logically, is itself a problem), and all the more when we relate human beings and animals. Do ants, some birds, and so on, to which new kinds of senses or senses heightened beyond ours have been ascribed, therefore have a truer experience of the world? Are there not, ideally speaking, infinitely many normalities, and where is the limit, in the series of "better" normalities, of the "best"? A note works through the colour-blind case. The colour-blind man always keeps his normal world as the reference system of intuition, but recognizes that the normally sighted have another normality and that they experience the same world (the colour-blind world of experience and that of the normally sighted coincide in one stratum), but with other visual determinations, richer ones: they too have the colour-blind man's nuances of grey, but for other things on which the two agree, and besides this they have, in a regulated way, the colour qualities in corresponding aspects for the remaining things. That yields a concordant system for them with richer determinations of things, much as for everyone as solus the richness diminishes when a finger is burned and so anomalously changed. The mode of constitutive expansion is thus analogous; only the knowledge of the greater richness of qualitative contents in the normal person's world, which is the same world, makes the plus be taken up. As I, born blind and then operated on, change my intuitive world and my normality because the new one is better and richer, so the colour-blind man changes his apperception through determinations of thought in the direction of greater richness. The development of constitution always proceeds in the direction of concordance and of the greatest possible expansion of determining content, that is, of the better, and everything expanded becomes the norm. Normality is relative and ranked by richness, and the rule of constitutive development is maximal concordant enrichment; each enrichment becomes the new norm. The colour-blind people's acceptance of our norm is what makes their indirect knowledge possible, the model for how a species could know a world "truer" than its own intuition.

(134–135) Is there a true world as idea, and as idea a kind of subjectivity, absolutely normal, related to a kind of bodiliness that experiences the world in the ultimately perfect way as simply true? No one, Husserl says, will answer yes. Different normalities and different "best" intuitive worlds may, if we widen the circle of empathy suitably, stand relatively equal and unite through mutual penetration into a better one that takes up everything relatively better in each. They cannot be wholly unconnected; they must at least be mediated by worlds that have intuitive structures of sense in common with the one and with the other, since otherwise no identical world could be constituted mediately as manifesting itself in appearance in those "lesser" worlds. But it can be foreseen that we stand here in an endless relativism of possibilities. We are, it is said, the "highest" beings on this earth, also in our intuition of the world, all in all. But there may be "higher" beings, not only in particulars but on the whole, and nowhere is there an ultimate truth as truth of intuition, an intuitive descriptive world that would have to be the final norm for all conceivable beings that could stand in empathy with us, beings that might be infinitely higher and descend to us in one-sided empathy as we descend to animals. A priori no one, no species, can say that it has in its system of experience the optimal experience in which all properties of things present themselves. A note adds that "anomalous organ" is a title for a certain supplementary conditionality to the normal conditionality body–thing; the foreign body in its deviations from my bodily normality is likewise included in constitution; and the thing itself in its truth is the concordant unity of appearances related to my body in all its possible variations, insofar as they function in this conditionality, and to all bodies in all their "fluctuations". The necessary structure of the construction means that each has his subjective reference system as normal, but none is in itself truer, although there are differences in the richness of the actual phantom-properties given in this or that system. A human being experiences more of things than a jellyfish. There is no absolute intuitive norm: the true world is an idea of the unity of all appearances to all bodies, and every species has its reference system, none truer, though richer or poorer. The "infinitely higher beings" descending to us in one-sided empathy are the exact mirror of our relation to animals, which keeps the intuitive world open above as well as below.

(135) What, then, are the conditions of the possibility of speaking of a true "objective" world, however it may look, presenting itself "normally" as the "true world" in one way for this single subject, and not only for it but for this single species of subjects, and otherwise for another? And what, in phenomenologically absolute consideration, are the conditions of the possibility of the coexistence of all such species, and then of one world for all of them and for all that are to be compossible, that are to be able possibly to coexist? A world is related only to a compossible totality of subjects and, among them, to a compossible totality of distinguishable species of subjects, and then also of species of bodily subjects, of "human beings" and animals; and it stands, if it exists, as would have to be seen, necessarily alone: there can be only one world. Any other may be compossible in itself, but not with a second world, since the two worlds would have to be knowable as a duality. Kern's note refers to Nr. 4. The plurality of species-worlds is reconciled with one world through Nr. 4's argument: the species-worlds are compossible only as appearances of one world, since two worlds would have to be known together.

(135) The considerations on compossibility carried out elsewhere do not yet suffice. Nr. 4 established one world for subjects of one kind; species with incommensurable senses need a further argument.

(135–136) We distinguish human beings and animals. We ascribe an intuitive world of the same "perfection" as ours not to animals in general but only to "higher" ones, and only to human beings the "capacity" to know by thinking, in the logos, and to constitute from intuitive nature, the world of mere experience, a logically true world, a world of theoretical reason in the specific sense, and then a world of reason in general. Is the grouping "human being" as rational being and animal, with a possible series of levels of animals down to a lower limit of a sensory world of the lowest level, a necessary grouping under the idea of a knowable and true world? Or is that world rather the correlate of human beings, where it may remain open how many "different" species of humans there may be, not fundamentally different but different in bodiliness, below and above the moon? But is it not then again necessary that these "human beings", insofar as they communicate with one another, have corresponding organizations? At any rate a body, an organism with its system of organs of perception and so on, belongs with formal necessity to the spiritual "human being". The same belongs to the animal, however great or small its wealth of organs. But the human being has reason, and to what extent reason too must have its organ is a question. The empirically given must be put under the eidetic magnifying glass; we must ask what essential necessities we can read off it, how we can rise to eidetic necessities by its free variation. "Human being" is redefined transcendentally as the rational subject of the logically true world, which allows "humans" on other planets ("above the moon") with other bodies. The open question whether reason has an organ is the limit of the psychophysical parallel that Nr. 3, §8 drew at the intellectus agens.

(136) The existence of a world presupposes subjects and the constitution of normal intuitive worlds, and presupposes constitutively a great deal of what is necessary so that there is not merely some contingent identification of intuited unities but such courses of "appearances" that an objective truth can be worked out of them for every rational subject, provided it is so willing and empirically so capable of thinking logically correctly. The world's existence requires appearances that make objective truth attainable by reason, which ties being to rational knowability, not to mere intuitive concordance.

(136) A world can exist only if there are human beings in it. A world is thinkable only as spatio-temporally infinite, and only as a developing world and a developing animal kingdom of organisms up to the human being, just as in the human being experience of the world is possible only in a development from childhood, and knowledge of the world in the development of science on the basis of experience. Subjectivity is absolute, but it has its absolute development within itself, and the necessity of possible cognition means only, since the present is not everything but is the present of pasts, that the stream of development must lead to the human being and to science, to culture. The question left open in Appendix XIII is answered positively: a world requires humans, development, and science. Absolute subjectivity has a development, which makes transcendental subjectivity historical in principle, the thesis of the late teleology of history.

(136) The world can exist only if it develops constitutively, if absolute subjectivity develops, if it develops the world itself, so that it develops to self-consciousness in the form of human self-consciousness and then further to scientific self-consciousness. Without a tendency toward truth there is no truth; without development toward knowledge there is no true being. Being, truth, and the development of reason are identified in a formula of strongly Hegelian cast: absolute subjectivity comes to self-consciousness in humanity and science, the programme of the *Crisis* (§§6, 73) read back into 1921.

### Appendix XV. On the Doctrine of Evidence. Every Human Being "Can" Think Logically, etc. (From 1920/21)

(136–137) I can think every human being, every ego-subject, as subject of every evident judgment, every one as a subject judging every possible truth. Every human being, every empirical ego-subject, has its bodiliness and its individual development, which prescribes what can factually occur in it. Nevertheless I can think of every truth that I could understand and see it. The question is what this really means. Not every ego can factually sense colour, and yet every law of colour is "accessible" to it ideally. The first says that the psychophysical regulation of nature exists and perhaps must exist a priori in some type (nature could be otherwise) if certain transcendental requirements are to be fulfillable, which points to some higher teleology, and of course what this means is not a matter of indifference. The second says that this psychophysical regulation is not an essential necessity of a theoretical kind; perhaps it is not theoretical but practical, axiological necessity. A great deal depends metaphysically, then, on becoming clear about all these necessities in their relation to one another. Two kinds of accessibility are separated: factual, bounded by body and development, and ideal, open to every ego. The suggestion that the psychophysical order is axiologically rather than theoretically necessary, required for reason to be realized rather than by essence, anticipates the late metaphysics of the "teleological" necessity of the factual world.

(137) One can also say: every ego-subject can empathize into every other only if the conditions of empathy obtain. But every one can see the possibility that, despite all given nature, every thing could appear and fit in by its appearances in any context whatever, and so every sensory datum; and further that every ego in general could have every sensory datum, could have the appearance of any nature that another has, could have, or imagine (in the mode of imaginative givenness), every empirical assertion and empirical evidence of anyone. Every subject can think its actual course of consciousness continued, against all passive motivation of its previous expectations, so that all motivation would arise in it that makes any assertion whatever intelligible and any truth whatever (including subjectively relative ones) groundable for it. Ideal accessibility rests on the variability of the ego beyond its factual motivation: any ego can be imagined continued so as to acquire any evidence, which is how truths remain truths "for everyone" though not everyone can reach them in fact. This is the ground of Husserl's thesis in Formal and Transcendental Logic that truth in itself is correlated with an open, ideal intersubjectivity.

(137) Husserl notes that he has carried out such considerations repeatedly; they must be seriously brought to an end, so that perfect insight may finally prevail here. The admission closes Section I's first cluster of texts on subjects, worlds, and normality; the systematic answer was never written, and the problem passes to the 1927 analyses of the original sphere.

### No. 7. As Introduction to the Theory of Empathy: The Conceivability of Other Egos. The Universe of Possible (but Mutually Incompatible) Ego-Subjects through Re-imagining My Ego (Probably 1921)

(138) Husserl's summary announces a careful consideration of how far my ego can be re-imagined, and returns to the question how egos I can conceive, which are only my variations, could reach compossibility and so possible coexistence. What is to be learned from re-imagining the ego I am, and from the system of other possibilities of my ego, which are struck-out possibilities? How can possibilities of an ego that are incompatible, because they coincide individually, become a system of compatible possibilities? How is a plurality of ego-subjects possible? Kern notes that the summary probably also covers Appendix XVIII. The problem of Nr. 4 is posed here at its root: eidetic variation of the ego yields only rival versions of me, so the concept "ego" is first given with an extension of mutually exclusive possibilities, and plurality must come from elsewhere.

(138–139) It is evident that I, who am and evidently am, could also have been otherwise: could have lived through other things, had other objects in my surroundings, performed other acts, shown other traits of character. Through this re-imagining of my ego an infinity of possible concrete egos (monads) arises, and here they form a system of incompatible possibilities. If one is actuality, as I who now actually am and am with absolute indubitability, every other is a re-imagining that actuality shows to be a necessary nullity. All these possibilities of an ego, possibilities of concrete monads, stand in relations of necessary coincidence, much as when I imagine a red surface and then the whole system of surfaces I obtain by imagining the same surface recoloured in every possible way during the same duration. There, however, all these incompatible possibilities can combine into the unity of a change if I think each distributed, in suitable order, over a phase of the duration instead of the whole. This coincidence is one of incompatibility. The red surface marks the difference: incompatible colours can become compatible as a sequence of changes, whereas two lives of one ego cannot be serialized, since each occupies the whole of its time.

(139) "An" ego, any such possibility, is in general and by essence originally present to itself with all its living. Its past living is originally present to it again in the form of recollection. For every experience, every immanent being, being (individual being with its individual temporal position) coincides with being present, or having been present, to the ego; it is the identical element of possible recollections following the presentation that primally institutes its existence. It belongs to the essence of everything immanent that it cannot be present twice, in two separate presences. Connected with this is that every monad is originally present only to itself, that it is only by being for itself, that every monadic ego is only as subject-ego of its experiences, its surroundings, its affections and actions. The impossibility of a second presence of one experience grounds monadic separation: what is immanent is present once, to one ego, which is the phenomenological content of "windowless".

(139) An ego is for itself only in the form of the cogito, as its ego; and if there are two egos, the one cannot be given to the other in this form, the life of the one cannot be presented or represented (recollected) for the other. When I re-imagine myself, I imagine that instead of what I actually live through I live through something else, have it in original presence; that instead of thinking or willing this I think or will something else, where the thinking is again my original thinking, consciously present to me as such. What is re-imagined is incompatible with what is assumed as actually present, as immanently existing. The various possibilities, as incompatibilities, separate the concrete possible egos but connect them in the form of necessary exclusion for actual being, ego coinciding with ego in conflict. The other could be given to me only in the form "cogito", which belongs to its owner alone; re-imagining gives possible egos only as versions that exclude me.

(139–140) How is the conflict and this coincidence cancelled and compatibility established? Better put: if two egos are separate, each has its existence in and for itself, and the two can have no experiences in common. But if they have no experiences in common, how can the surrounding worlds each has constituted for itself, given to each as unities consistently confirmed in separate manifolds of experience, be anything but wholly separate and purely subjective surroundings? How then can the two bodies be mutually experienceable, and how can the two subjects be there for each other? Separation is complete at the level of experiences, so community must be at the level of constituted unities, which seems to require what is excluded.

(140) Husserl puts it another way. Every ego not only is but is also a fact for itself with its whole content of life, and it has facts transcendent to itself in the unities constituted for it as unities of its possible experiences, which we call the surrounding world of things. How can one ego be there for another? Through knowledge? But legitimate knowledge presupposes experience. Of myself I have self-experience; of things too I have experience. How can I, on this basis, have mediate experience and knowledge of other egos that are a priori not really experienceable for me, that cannot be given originally as egos (present and then recollected)? That an ego is there for me necessarily also means that its experienced and experienceable surroundings are there for me. But can what is constituted for him, the other, also be constituted for me? If this identity holds, it would have to be knowable; but how, unless the knower had given both the surroundings constituted in the one ego and those constituted as such in the other, and so had experience of the one and of the other ego, of its experiences and the unities experienced in them? A third ego of this kind presupposes the solution of the problem we are trying to solve. If we let this third ego coincide with one of the two, the question is how one ego can make sure of the other's surroundings and find them identical with its own without first getting hold of the other ego. The transitivity argument of Nr. 4 is turned against itself: a third knower presupposes the empathy it was meant to explain, so the identity of the world cannot be established without first reaching the other.

(140–141) What can give me an indirect experience of the other, apart from a direct experience that is impossible in principle? Experience is either an originally self-giving experience or one co-giving along with a self-giving one: there is direct presentation or appresentation. Is an appresentation conceivable whose appresented content is another ego? What could such an appresentation look like? The alternative is exhaustive: if the other cannot be presented, he can be given only as co-given with something presented, and the body is the only candidate.

(141) Husserl sees that this way of considering the matter does not come at the right place. First the achievement of empathy must be exhibited directly, and then it would have to be considered to what extent this achieved appresentation is the only possibility for another ego to be there for an ego, and in one with it for his surroundings to be identifiable with mine, so that together with the appresentation of a foreign ego and the origin of the concept alter a common nature, an identical space with identical things, must also be constituted. The method is reversed: describe empathy first, then ask whether it is necessary. This is the order the 1927 texts follow, and Appendix XXII confirms it against Nr. 7's own procedure.

### Appendix XVI. On the Theory of Empathy. I and Other Egos (May 1919)

(141–142) How is a plurality of egos given, and can it be given in original intuition? How is another ego given to me intuitively in "empathy"? I am given to myself in reflection as I-me-reflection. The other is given in a presentification attached appresentatively through the other's body, apperceived as living, in which the ego of what is presentified necessarily coincides with my living ego, as I coincide with the ego of my past in the presentification of my recollection. Then a reflection within the presentification is needed, a transfer of the I-me-reflection into the presentification. Wonderfully, then, I can find the other intuitively only in coincidence with me, the other's experiences only in conflict with my experiences, the other's stream only in conflict with my stream, but only by way of presentification. It is as if I re-imagined myself completely, imagining a stream other than mine; but then I would no longer be the same ego, not even a changed one, as when I hold fast a piece of my stream from the beginning and then imagine a different continuation, as far as it goes concordantly, in conflict only with facts that could also have been otherwise and were not motivated by the earlier piece. In truth every ego that, against all motivation, devises something psychically its own is another ego, and that should probably be put first, before I have foreign bodies over against me and then foreign human beings. How then do I have many egos? In a coincidence of conflict with the actual ego, and struck out as fiction by the actuality of the absolutely given stream. So too I have a fictive spatial thing struck out by the actual spatial world, which is why fiction and nullity become equivalents. Only the striking out has other sources here. With the ego it is grounded in the fact that, imagining myself into a new life, I continue to take myself as this individual ego, whereas the individual ego gains its individual sense from its stream, which shapes it ever anew. Only when I subtract this apperceived sense, take the pure substrate ego, and lend it indeterminately the form of an individuality, determined further only by this ego's living through the imagined experience or performing the imagined act, do I have a possible representation. It remains that coincidence is the original thing here, and that I cannot have the other ego and my actual ego side by side or separately in order then to perform an overlapping coincidence. Intuitively there is only coincidence here. But what grounds the conflict? Must there be conflict? This 1919 text, earlier than the volume's main body, states the structure that Husserl's later analyses keep: empathy is a presentification modelled on recollection, in which the presentified ego coincides with mine as my past ego does, and a reflection within the presentification grasps it as ego. The difference from recollection is conflict: the other's stream excludes mine, as an imagined life does, so the other has the logical form of a struck-out possibility of me. The closing question, whether conflict is necessary, opens the way out.

(142) Husserl considers presentifying a wholly new ego in its stream and, beside it, another wholly new one with its new stream: two series of experiences, acts, phenomena are imagined. If a world is constituted, that makes demands, and it may be that the one world and the other conflict if both, with their streams, are to be possibilities that coexist. But they need not "truly coexist". Each ego lives as a possible one. When are they coexistent possibilities? Passing from one to the other, I have not a transition between things separately represented "side by side", but the transformation of one into the other, with identity of the ego-pole. Even two imagined strangers are reached by transforming one into the other through the same imagining pole; imagination cannot place two egos side by side, so coexistence must come from the world they share, Nr. 4's answer.

### Appendix XVII. The Mode of Givenness of Several Egos in Intuition (Probably 1921/1922)

(142–143) It matters, when one asks how many possible egos are given, that this givenness is presupposed for forming the general concept ego-subject. What are the conditions not only of the possibility of thinking many possible egos but of thinking a plurality of egos, the conditions of the possibility of compossibility? Finally comes the question when an open infinite totality of egos, thought in a thought leaving infinitely many possible and coexisting egos open, is given as a true object, a true totality for cognition, and when such a thought can be logically admissible. For this, the fundamentally different mode of givenness of a plurality of subjects in experience, as against a plurality of things, is of interest. Three questions are graded: possible egos, a plurality of egos, an infinite totality of egos as a legitimate object of thought. The last is the question whether "all subjects" is a well-formed totality, which Appendix XX raises with Brouwer.

(143) Non-subjects, say things, that are "alike" or "similar" to one another are in a certain way "sensuously" unified in our field of intuition: they form a sensuous community of the similar, the "figural" unity. They can then also come to overlap in "comparison". One may also be given alone intuitively while another stands out in the background of memory ("of" which the intuitively given one reminds us), and there may be coincidence before full intuitiveness. But nothing of this kind is necessary. The "figural moment" goes back to the *Philosophy of Arithmetic* (Hua XII, ch. XI), where sensuous groupings of similar items are grasped at a glance before counting; things can be grouped this way, by sensuous similarity.

(143) A foreign subject, however, if it is given to me at all, can be given only in a coincidence; I need not first compare, and on the other hand I have in principle no unity of form here, no "sensuous" similarity. I cannot have only one subject given if I have a foreign subject at all. For with the one foreign subject I am there too, and I am always there; I can never vanish from my field of pregivenness. Ego cogito. The foreign subject is in coincidence with me, in a unity of conflict with me. Whereas elsewhere I convert a sensuous similarity into coincidence, here I have only coincidence, and comparison on the basis of coincidence. If I have two foreign subjects, I "transfer" myself into each, I perform as it were his acts, live through as it were his experiences, and the comparison is here mediated by the coincidence of each with me. Plurality of subjects is never a figural group: subjects are compared only through me, by coinciding each with myself. Every plurality of others is thus at least a plurality of three, me and them, the structure of the Fifth Meditation's "pairing" in which the ego is always one member.

### Appendix XVIII. The Re-imagining of My Stream of Consciousness and the Possibility of a Plurality of Egos (Probably 1921)

(143) Husserl's summary names the essential bindings belonging to the question of re-imagining my stream of consciousness, or the essential laws of the coexistence and succession of experiences (recollections, phantasies) that are to be able to belong to the unity of one stream; the fundamental problems of the theory of horizons; and the question whether horizons are to be regarded like determinate experiences, with no genuine conflict between them. Variation of a life is bound by laws of the stream itself, which the appendix tries to state, and the problem of horizons enters because variation that ignores horizons produces illusory possibilities.

(143–144) The problem: every ego can imagine anything whatever at any point of its stream; it can re-imagine arbitrarily. It seems, then, that the question here is how far what occurs successively in the stream is contingent. The whole individual stream is a fact, despite the motivation running through its unity. Everything hyletic, for instance, is contingent, though it can be motivated by the preceding consciousness and its earlier hyletic content. But can one say, even for hyletic data, that this doxic motivation is never a motivation of necessity, characterized by the essential content of the preceding stretch of time being incompatible, in the unity of succession, with the essential content of the following stretch? The question is whether there are laws of succession of content, not merely of coexistence, within a stream; Nr. 2 had said that past hyle prescribes nothing to future hyle.
