(144) Or, better, can one state as a primal law of the stream and its immanent time the following (Husserl's note asks whether it holds and points to the answer below): concrete essential incompatibilities are incompatibilities of coexistence, of being simultaneous; every piece of the stream of consciousness (the stream of immanent time and its "experiences") can also be represented otherwise. It may then stand in a relation of incompatibility, of a conflict grounded in essence, with the actual piece in question, but never in that relation with the concrete content of the preceding and following durations. What we call material essential laws would then be a priori temporal laws of coexistence, concerning the fillings of one and the same duration. This does not, of course, include the temporal laws themselves, nor the essential laws of the constitution of time. The candidate law confines material incompatibility to simultaneity: red and green cannot fill the same place at once, but any filling can follow any other.

(144–145) So it seems. But can it be right, and what is right in it? Suppose a recollection arises in the stream and a process of complete clarification transforms it into an explicit, fully clear recollection. Is that possible without the corresponding impression, the perception, having occurred earlier in the stream? It follows that in a stream lacking a certain perception the corresponding clear recollection is not possible; the content of the stream prescribes an a priori rule to what can enter it. Memory can be falsified; moments of memory can be falsely present. But when we speak of phantasy working its way in, is it not also an essential law that nothing can appear in phantasy, "reproductively", whose like had not already appeared originally, impressionally, in the earlier stream? So a phantasy cannot occur in every stream either. Primal impressions, such as the sounding of a tone, can occur anywhere. But even there we must distinguish: a pure tone occurring for the first time has another character than a like one occurring impressionally later. The tone is the same, and as far as this like content is concerned it can occur at any temporal position, but not in the character of familiarity, not as "a" tone. Reproduction refutes the candidate law: recollection and phantasy require earlier impressions, so the stream's past constrains its future. Hume's principle that ideas derive from impressions becomes an essential law of the stream, and familiarity (Bekanntheit) is a character that only a second occurrence can have, the type of Experience and Judgment (§8).

(145) The laws of reproduction are therefore essential laws that regulate the sequence of experiences in the stream, though not univocally. Just as we cannot represent a spread of red as at the same time a spread of green, or a loud tone as at once soft (in coexistence, as duration), so we cannot represent in succession a stretch of the stream without a certain impression if we represent a following stretch as beginning with a corresponding reproduction. Laws of succession exist after all, but they are not determining: they exclude certain sequences without fixing which one occurs, the distinction Nr. 2 needed between genesis and univocal determination.

(145) The first conflict, incompatibility in coexistence, turns into compatibility if we convert red and green into a succession, letting the spread of red become a spread of green, since it is the same spread; here one must ask whether becoming must mean continuous becoming, or whether there can be a really discrete jump, or whether that is a mere limit-idea that itself remains excluded. Whether a discrete change is possible in experience or only a limit is the question of continuity that Hua X raised for the flow and that Brentano's theory of the continuum had treated.

(145) The latter incompatibility turns into compatibility if we insert such an impression somewhere into the stretch empty of it, changing that stretch in thought. But before too the change was only in thought. We can think anything at all otherwise; we posit during the conflict. Every thought alteration is an alteration posited against the actual, so conflict is the medium of all variation.

(145) There is therefore no primal law of the kind assumed above. I can think "anything possible" into any stretch and point of my stream, but it is not thereby said that everything is really (realiter) possible there, with regard to what precedes and follows in the stream. What then does "anything possible" mean? There are "thought" possibilities, representabilities in themselves, prior to the question of possibilities within the unified connection of a stream, of real possibility. It is of such possibilities that we speak, since all such considerations hold not only for inner time but for objective time too, in the ordinary cases where objects or events are declared possible. The distinction between thought possibility and real possibility (Nr. 4) is derived here from the stream itself: thought possibility is representability in isolation, real possibility is fit with the whole stream.

(145) Everything intuitively representable, quasi-perceivable, is "possible", even if the representation conflicts with an actual experience: the being-together of what conflicts is impossible, but each member of the conflict is possible for itself. So too every intuitively represented succession, every immanent course I represent as unified and intuitive, is possible, to stay within this sphere. If I re-imagine my inner life at any point and stretch, what is imagined is as such a possible: possible in itself, representable, thinkable in itself. Intuitability is the criterion of possibility in itself, the principle of Ideen I (§4) that intuitive phantasy yields pure possibility.

(145–146) But care is needed here. I can re-imagine any point and stretch of my stream in infinitely many ways, and each such fiction is therefore a possibility. It gives itself as a possibility of "my" living at this temporal position, which here evidently means a possibility of my living within the connection of my life in general. The new stretch of life coincides with the actual stretch in question, and precisely thereby my life is re-imagined, and I myself am re-imagined as subject. With the re-imagining of any piece of the stream the ego itself is re-imagined. It is similar to re-imagining a thing: instead of having a like thing in pure phantasy, I have replaced, at the objective position in time and space within the objective connection of nature, a material content of determinations by other conflicting ones, but so that a unity of intuition results. Husserl's note adds that the re-imagined thing is nonetheless really impossible, and so is the re-imagined ego, if the connection of reality is held fixed. Varying any part of my life varies me: identity of the ego is global, as Nr. 2 had found for acts and here extends to every experience.

(146) What is possible in the full, evident sense is in fact only the imagined experience and its ego, as far as they can really become intuitive. Insofar as essential demands proceed from the past stream of life with which the new experience conflicts, the fact of these demands means that I cannot truly imagine this experience into my stream of life. That is to say, I can indeed re-imagine my actual experience at the temporal position in question; and insofar as I know that this piece is a piece of my determinate stream, though not now intuitively present, its re-imagining is also a re-imagining of the whole stream (in the mode of conflict, a note adds), such that the stream remains as a whole and only this point is re-imagined. What actually takes place, however, is only that the factually filled piece remains as it is, and that afterwards in the stream there newly occurs that re-imagining, a fiction that relates intentionally in a peculiar way, as "coincidence", to this piece but changes the stream here and nowhere. A note compares imagining into some position in the world a thing that is not there, or one changed from reality, in conflict with the causal connection: re-imagining is likewise burdened with a hidden conflict that emerges when what is obscure is made intuitive. Re-imagining is itself a new experience, later than the piece it re-imagines, related to it by "coincidence"; it changes nothing in the stream. The distinction prevents the paradox of altering one's own past by imagining it.

(146–147) As soon as we demand more, assuming that what is imagined is, that my life here was otherwise, we must also re-imagine the whole past life explicitly and concordantly; only by doing so suitably can we gain an intuitive representation of the unity of the one life that terminates in the present fictum. Then I am not really I, the factual ego, the subject of the fictum, but a correspondingly changed ego, another ego, imagined only in coincidence with mine. So too I can re-imagine any thing; but I do not notice at once that I have thereby imagined another world, which I have imagined only in a certain coincidence with the given one. A note corrects this: I have really re-imagined only what actually appears; the obscure horizon of what appears is reshaped in consequence. By essential law an intuitive possibility, regarding its empty horizon, must be completed by fitting intuitive possibilities, in the sense of a comprehensive unity of intuition fitting together, and these again in infinitum. Every immanent or transcendent phenomenon, everything appearing intuitively, has its determinate or indeterminate but necessarily prefiguring horizon. Every reshaping of the appearance requires, for the pure possibility to be preserved, a binding to the demands of the horizon, and without a functionally corresponding change of horizon there is no re-imagining. If the horizon is kept identical, there is coincidence in a hidden conflict still to be explicated. These things, and this world, could in fact be otherwise; but that only means that another world is thinkable that is not merely similar to the given one but stands in a certain coincidence with it, a coincidence through conflict. Insofar as in rethinking the one thing I have rethought only its phantom intuitively, while the other phantoms still in my field of intuition can remain unaffected, it seems as if the change of a thing, its fictive transformation, could leave the rest of the given world untouched. A second note answers that a phantom is not reality; if reality is really re-imagined, that concerns me too, since another world would have motivated me otherwise; though phenomenal nature is a stratum of its own. Variation is holistic: to vary one thing in its reality is to vary the world and, through motivation, me. Only the phantom stratum allows local variation, which is why careless variation seems to leave the world intact. The rule that no variation is possible without a corresponding change of horizon is the condition for eidetic variation to be legitimate, made explicit in Experience and Judgment (§87).

(147) The difference must always be observed between empty re-imagining, carried out only on superficial components with empty horizons and backgrounds, and fully explicit re-imagining, which alone exhibits the whole sense, content, and range of the possibilities. Two grades of variation are separated, empty and explicit; only explicit variation yields genuine possibilities, which limits what eidetic intuition can claim from casual imagining.

#### Identity of the Ego and the Various Possibilities of Being Otherwise, of the Ego's Development

(147–148) I can rethink myself and my stream of life in infinitely many ways. But when can I keep a concordant representation of myself in doing so, and when is it a coincidence through conflict, burdened with absurdities, the same ego being the same only through coincidence in conflict? There are essential laws of the unity of an ego and its stream of consciousness, including the laws of development, reproduction, and so on. According to them, an ego and its stream can take different paths of development from a given point, and it can be touched by different sensations that determine its experience from then on. If we follow the different lines, we gain in each case a concordant ego, and at the same time it is evident that the same ego, which up to a selected turning point W finds itself concordantly as ego and says its ego sum, finds itself evidently as this same ego in the further course as long as the concordance lasts. From the turning point on, then, the ego remains the same ego, numerically identical, in each of the branches of ideal possibility. But the ego that lived up to the turning point can, after developing along one of these lines and now saying "I am the same, but I have developed in this way meanwhile", only say that it could have developed differently from W, for instance along this or that other line. Had w′ occurred then instead of w, it would have had different consequences in me, and I would have become this or that other. For every individual development, contingencies occur, events in its stream that, taken purely for themselves, could have been quite different, and everything has its after-effects and so determines the development. What determines development, consciously and "unconsciously", in the upper and lower spheres of the psyche, is a field of the greatest problems. Branching identity is admitted: the same ego persists into each branch from a turning point, so numerical identity is compatible with alternative histories. The ego is not identical with any one future, only with its past up to the branching, which gives counterfactual self-reference ("I could have become another") a precise sense.

#### The Divergence of the Actuality of an Ego, through Re-imagining, into Many (Mutually Incompatible) Possibilities of This Same Ego

(148) At any rate there are essential laws for the becoming of an ego and for the content of a unified ego-life. Closely connected, there are essential laws for the possibilities of a unified ego-life, the a priori of unity and the essential laws of such unity for all levels and strata of temporal connections. As soon as what is thought as one falls apart through conflict, we no longer have one unified ego but several egos in coincidence of conflict, each to be followed into a sphere of experience of its own concordance: each can be if the other is not. Every exclusion of what conflicts yields a concordant ego, and since the conflict is mutual we have several, infinitely many, evident possibilities of concordant egos, so many possibilities of egos, or possibilities of "an" ego in general; Husserl's note says: many possibilities of my ego. The extension of the concept ego arises by splitting one life along its conflicts into concordant branches, which are possibilities of me before they are possibilities of "an ego".

#### On the Other Hand, the Possibility of Many Egos

(148–149) But this does not yet give the possibility of many egos, more precisely the possibility of a plurality of egos. A plurality means an individual coexistence of different objects, or more generally a collective compatibility of non-identical objects, objects that conflict in unity with one another. If we have an infinity of individual possibilities that are incompatible in the unity of an individual object (and likewise for eidetic objects), it must first be shown when or how this infinity can harbour coexisting pluralities, what the conditions of the possibility of the coexistence of separately existing objects are, objects that distribute in their separation, each to an individual, what is incompatible in the unity of one individual. The step from extension to plurality requires distributing incompatible contents over separate individuals, which for things is done by space; for egos it remains to be found.

(149) In general one can say: where different possibilities conflict as individual ones and thereby coincide in conflict, the unity of a generality of species corresponds to them, whose extension they form. When does a plurality of possible individuals of such an extension become the possibility of a coexisting, simultaneous plurality of individuals? Husserl states the general logic of species: a species is the unity of its conflicting instances in coincidence, and coexistence is a further condition on top of membership.

(149) In our case, then: what are the conditions of the possibility of the coexistence of a plurality of ego-subjects and streams of experience? The question is Nr. 4's, whose answer (a common world through bodies) serves as the distributing medium that space provides for things.

#### Later Addition

(149) When I remember any past phase of life, I can imagine that I could have thought of this or that I did not think of, could have taken this or that line of thought; I consider what would have happened if I had expected B instead of A, if I had known this or that proof, if I had not succumbed to a temptation, if I had let myself be motivated practically in another way. I seem to have the freedom to imagine anything whatever, intuitively, into my past life. Counterfactual memory seems unrestricted, and the following section shows why the appearance deceives.

#### Deceptions about the Possibilities and Impossibilities of Subjective Being, in Particular of the "I Could Have Thought, Done, Let Myself Be Motivated"

(149) It follows from the foregoing that I easily fall into deceptions here. In setting an imagined experience into an actual stretch of past life, perhaps in place of a definite experience that really occurred, I hold on to my empty horizon of the past; and when I enter into it in the form of intuitive recollections, I bring out single items without really realizing the complete unity of my past stream of life with my ego-affections and ego-actions, my connection of passive and active motivations. And now I think that I, the same who lived the determinate life obscurely indicated in the potential horizon, could also have represented, thought, felt, done this or that; and further that I, who have this "original character", this individuality, could have chosen what another chose according to his individuality. In understanding him by putting myself into him, I coincide with him, and so it seems that intuitive empathy is equivalent to "I myself could also have done so". The deception is diagnosed: empty horizons let an imagined act fit my past because the motivational whole is not made intuitive, and empathy produces the same illusion, since coinciding with the other feels like a possibility of mine. Empathy is not counterfactual self-possibility; the other's choice is not one I could have made, being his by his character, which preserves the individual necessity of Nr. 2 against the levelling effect of empathy.

#### On the Theory of Horizons and Empty Presentations Proper. Between Mere Horizons There Is No Conflict; They Flow Together into Pseudo-Horizons, etc.

(150–151) There are evident problems here, above all the following. To a phenomenon belongs its horizon, and everything that appears and is intuitively represented has its non-intuitive horizon. When I re-imagine what appears, the horizon as the "halo" of the phenomenon remains unmodified (this is where the deception arises); at any rate the empty consciousness of horizon need undergo no change. So every phenomenon seems compatible and combinable with any horizon belonging to any other phenomenon that can enter into the unity of a conflict with it, in the form of possible re-imagining. That cannot possibly hold. The solution probably involves this: what is re-imagined is what is covered over by the overlay of fiction; it has not vanished from consciousness but is there in covered form and continues to bear its horizon. Only thereby does the overlaying item, which in the conflict is identified, numerically unified, with the covered one as to its point of identity, its substrate, have this horizon as acquired. It is in truth not its own horizon; it also has its own. But why do the doubling of horizons and their conflict not become noticeable? Here one feels that the fundamental theory of horizons must be carefully worked out, together with the theory of dark (empty) "presentations". The horizon is not itself an empty presentation; one emerges from it, is awakened in it, and an affection from its intentional object reaches the ego. An empty horizon is not a self-enclosed and determinate consciousness of something objective represented indeterminately or determinately; it is a mode of consciousness attaching to a presentation, with the character of potentiality for a manifold of corresponding particular consciousnesses, delimited intentional experiences, standing in synthetically unified relation to what is already actual as presentation, the empty or intuitive presentation in question. A consciousness of conflict is possible only where presentation is set against presentation: where, within a horizon of consciousness or a complete consciousness in which an intuitive presentation is established and a non-intuitive one has been actualized from the empty horizon, intuitive and non-intuitive, or in other cases non-intuitive and non-intuitive, come to coincide in conflict. Unexplicated horizons do not conflict; they do not separate at all; they flow into one another without flowing together into a real horizon. Other recollections can shine into a recollection, come to unity by association, and yield a unified quasi-horizon for a mixed recollection. But it is called a quasi-horizon because it represents a unity of potentiality for presentations such that its actualization leads to things not belonging together, to the bursting of the apparent unity of the recollection, to presentations that reveal the unified horizon as a fusion of horizons not belonging together. This must be clarified more carefully and cleanly. Horizons are potentialities, not presentations, so they cannot conflict until explicated; this explains why imagined variants seem to fit any background. The fused "quasi-horizon" of a mixed memory, which bursts on explication, is the analysis of false memory in the lectures on passive synthesis (Hua XI, §§ on empty presentation and the "confusion" of recollections).

#### The Strata in the Structure of the Stream of Consciousness. The Stratum of Nature and the Stratum of Higher Spirituality. Re-imagining in the Stratum of Nature and in the Higher Stratum

(151) Another problem is this. For an ego, the constitution of nature takes place genetically in a stratum of its own, as against the stratum of higher achievements of acts and what is constituted through them (and as against the stratum of feeling, already at a lower level). For example, whatever the individuality of the ego may be, and so its free motivation of acts, nature constitutes itself, in general and in the normal human community, in the same way. Precisely for that reason one and the same thinghood is there for many subjects, possibly even in the same modes of appearance as to sensory qualities; only the same appearances motivate different egos in different ways. The re-imagining of a sensory thing and of a real thing therefore does not affect the personal ego in its specifically personal content, or at least there is a relative independence there. This too needs closer consideration. The stratification resolves a tension within the volume. Nr. 4 found that varying nature varies me, while Nr. 2 found that the ego's personal identity is indifferent to its sensory world. Both hold if nature is constituted in a stratum common to all and indifferent to individuality: varying it varies my concrete monad but not my person.

### Appendix XIX. Necessity and Contingent Facts. The Evidence of the Ego and of "I Live", as to Being and Being-So (Around 1922)

(151) I grasp myself, and grasp myself in apodictic evidence. The consciousness in which I am conscious of myself is my consciousness, and, taken concretely, consciousness of myself and I myself, likewise in full concretion, are identical. To be a subject is to be in the mode of being conscious of oneself. If I grasp myself in reflection, I grasp my identical ego as the pole of my life; or, advancing from life to life and reflecting ever anew, I grasp myself as an identical unity and my life itself as the unity of a multiform stream. Self-consciousness is constitutive of subjectivity, not added to it; being a subject is being for oneself, the formula Henry will radicalize against the reflective model Husserl keeps here.

(151–152) Throughout, the being-so is contingent insofar as I could re-imagine it and imagine that I lived through other things, that the experience that is so were another. One must be careful here. There is a great difference between taking hyletic data (I see red and could have had blue) and intentional position-takings (I wish and could also not have wished). At any rate I have the phantasy possibility of being otherwise as to my life. But what of the uncancellability of my present life in the unity of its living presence? It is apodictic necessity; yet there is also the possibility of its being otherwise, but a possibility cancelled by this apodictic necessity. Two kinds of contingency are separated, hyletic and positional, the distinction of Nr. 2; and the living present is apodictic yet contingent in content, its alternatives possible only as cancelled.

(152) A quite different case seems to obtain regarding the ego itself, and regarding any life at all. That the ego should not be at all, that I who am aware of myself in reflection should not be, is simply unthinkable for me, and so it is unthinkable that I should have no life at all. That I should not be is thinkable for me only in the form of a "possible" cognition, an "intuitive representation", in which I with my stream of life would be cancelled by conflict with something existing. But for that I would myself have to be again and would have to have the conflict given in another stream of life; the being of the ego and stream to be cancelled would have to shatter against my absolute being. That is an absurdity. Every possible stream of life I represent as a variation of my given one is my stream of life, mine, the same ego's. I can indeed say that if this were given, if I were the ego living in this life, I would not be the one who lives in my factual life. But all conflict here is between one life and another coinciding with it a priori, and between me as subject of the one and me as subject of the other life. The apodictic necessity of my ego as subject of my possible life is the highest; but my possible life is only a variation of my actual life, of which I am apodictically certain. My non-being cannot be represented, because cancellation requires a cancelling being, which would again be me. This is the phenomenological form of the cogito's indubitability: not an inference but the impossibility of representing my absence except as my being otherwise.

(152–153) Husserl sets out the result in three points. First, I am given absolutely to myself with this content of presence. Second, against this "experience" I can represent myself concordantly as subject of another life, in evident conflict with my experience. But the possibility exists insofar as I can carry it out concordantly in itself as pure possibility, apart from this conflict with the fact. It exists as pure possibility, as something concordantly imaginable. This means that I can detach myself from the fact and move into pure phantasy; then of course I also have a phantasy-ego as subject of the phantasy-life. But as soon as I posit existence, I conflict with experience. In general, every phantasy possibility I form, I form as a possibility related to a phantasy-ego with a quasi-living to which this phantasy and its phantasy objectivity belong. Phantasy-ego and actual ego, phantasy-life and actual life, phantasy reproduction and memory necessarily coincide, and only for that reason can phantasy be posited as existing, but in conflict, and cancelled as "fictum". Every phantasy has a phantasy-ego that coincides with me; that is why phantasies can be posited at all and, when posited, are struck out as fictions. The analysis of the quasi-ego of phantasy belongs with the texts collected in Hua XXIII.

(153) Third, in the sphere of pure possibility and eidetic consideration I recognize that an ego in general, one of the resulting possibilities of an ego, if it were posited as existing, excludes every other of these egos and its life, as happens for my phantasies in relation to my actual ego and life. For every ego in general, each of its pure possibilities can be posited, and is either an actuality experienced by it or a null fiction conflicting with its experienced actuality. The structure is universalized: every possible ego has its own system of possibilities cancelled by its actuality.

(153–154) We have here a double positing of existence. Positing existence in phantasy: fixing a possibility in phantasy and considering other possibilities from this possible ground, held fast. One changes ground when passing from one system of possibilities to another; when one "posts" oneself on one, every other is a cancelled possibility relative to the fixed ground. To each such ground belongs an ego. Or we understand by positing existence the connection of a phantasy possibility, or an empty possibility of thought, with an already given, actual ground of existence. In our case that is the ground of my self-experience, an "impression" giving me myself in original selfhood with certainty. If I have no actual ground of existence, no basis of experience, I also have no possibilities. My imagined variations of ego and life are my possibilities, my contents of fiction, and presuppose that I am. But how can I then say that I could have been otherwise? And can I not see that if I were otherwise, all the variations of possibility of my ego would again form a system, an ego-system, which is the same as the one I have as the now actual ego? But in pure phantasy I can cover over my actual life, re-imagine it entirely, and then from such a fiction imagine again that, if I were this living ego, I could cover and re-imagine this life again, and so on. All these egos would be in coincidence as the same ego, but a coincidence of conflict. Only through empathy can my possibility also be the same possibility for another. All this must be thought through carefully. At any rate this system has a remarkable function here: I "could" be another, I could be one of this possible system of egos. But if I were not this one, I would have to be another. And that I am this one and not another presupposes the system. It is absolutely necessary that every non-being for an ego and its life presupposes an ego with its life as basis, and that every ego that exists has its absolute system of possibilities, in which every possible striking-out gains sense. Accordingly, of my life not yet present or no longer present, and of the egoic determinations it motivates, I know in part nothing and in part doubtful things; I can very well be deceived. But it is absolutely necessary that I not only am in the present but have been and have before me a horizon of the future, will be. It is unthinkable that I begin, one might say. For I can surely represent, in pure possibility, a life lying before the beginning. But how could the positing of the non-being, or of the being, of any possible life before the beginning come to confirmation or striking-out? How could a beginning show itself as a beginning? Likewise for the end and the possibilities after the end. Possibilities exist only relative to an actual ground; the system of an ego's possibilities is the same whichever possibility is actual, which makes "I could have been otherwise" a statement about position within a fixed system. The note that only through empathy can a possibility of mine be the same possibility for another anticipates Appendix XXII's reversal. A beginning of the ego is undecidable from within, since nothing could confirm or strike out a life before it.

(154) Husserl concludes that he has the apodictic evidence of the I-am as primal evidence, and is the subject of a two-sided life that is absolutely necessary as to its actual being (existence) but could be otherwise as to its being-so, in such a way that the system of possibilities of being otherwise is a closed infinite one. Of the being-so, experience gives apodictically the flowing presence and a horizon of indeterminate but necessary existence, and of an existence determinable, though only with reservations, whose essential form is prescribed, as is the form of every possible confirmation and rejection. Existence necessary, essence contingent: the reversal of the scholastic contingency of creatures, for which essence is necessary and existence contingent, makes the ego the one being whose existence is apodictic.

### Appendix XX. The Universe of the Possibilities of My Being Otherwise Coincides with the Universe of the Possibilities of an Ego in General. The Ego Cannot Arise or Perish (7 and 8 January 1922)

(154) The necessity of the ego's existence: I am, and I have the evidence not only that I am but that I necessarily am. It is unthinkable for me that I should not be. So it is also unthinkable that there should be no ego at all. And I also have the general evidence that for no ego is it thinkable that there be no ego. The necessity is first-personal and then generalized: no ego can think the absence of all egos, which makes subjectivity as such a necessary being for any thinker.

(154–155) I am who I am. But I could be otherwise. It is a mere fact, contingent, that I am this one, or as I am. I have the evidence of being able to be otherwise, and the domain of possible being otherwise lies a priori "in" me; I "could" run through all possible variations of my ego intuitively. And this universe of the possibilities of my being otherwise "coincides" at the same time with the universe of the possibilities of an ego in general; this idea, a regional idea arising here, is "innate" in me. To every possibility of another ego separate from me there corresponds a possibility of my being otherwise; every foreign ego can be "brought to coincidence" with mine; each has another individuality, but each has the same "essence". In other words, each has its stream of experience spanned by the form of immanent time; each is the pole of affectivity and passivity relative to this stream and what is intentionally constituted in it; each is, with respect to it, a centre, a substrate of habitual properties. And insofar as all this has its essential law, the ego itself has its essence. It is evidently not intuitive in the way a simple experience is; its essence is analogous to categorial essences. The thesis of the title: the eidos ego is exhausted by the variations of my ego, so every possible other is a possible me. The ego's essence is "categorial", a formal structure (stream, pole, substrate of habits) rather than a sensuous content, which explains how it can be shared while individuals differ.

(155) I can think that I were otherwise. I need only think my passive experiences or my active position-takings otherwise, which I can do intuitively and originally. Even my individual character I can think otherwise in this way. "Otherwise" also designates possibilities of transformation, of becoming otherwise, in particular of a complete "conversion". However much I can always think, within this a priori frame of possible being otherwise, that I were otherwise than I actually am, it is always unthinkable for me that I should simply not be. The text now allows variation of the character itself, against Nr. 2, where varying the character produced another ego: here it is a possible conversion of mine, which reflects the later marginal criticisms of Nr. 2.

(155) I can think other egos, and think myself otherwise. I can think that other subjects do not exist, for they can be given to me only through empathy, through an apperception that presupposes that my body has been constituted for me as a lived body, and psychophysical connections related to it as my own. That makes foreign bodiliness apprehensible by analogy with mine, and foreign psychophysical subjectivity by analogy with mine, in an empirical apperception that is cancelled if the bodily-psychic indication is not confirmed. A note calls for a special investigation of how the evidence of empathy becomes overwhelming in reciprocal intercourse, and of the role this plays for the evidence of the givenness of nature. Others' existence is empirical and cancellable; mine is apodictic. This is the asymmetry the Cartesian Meditations will try to overcome by an "apodictic" foundation of intersubjectivity; the note points to the cumulative force of mutual confirmation as the source of its overwhelming evidence.

(155) So I, and every ego, can think that no other ego exists, but no ego can think that it itself does not exist. It can only think that it could be otherwise than it factually is. I am always contingent only as to my being-so, not as to my being simply. My being-so is bound only by the general frame of essence that spans all possibilities of my ego and of an ego in general. Of an ego in general: for every possibility of another ego must keep within the frame of analogy, and as far as analogy reaches, so far reaches a unity of essence. Every possible variation I can devise in another I can devise, and still more originally, in myself. Analogy and essence coincide in extent: an ego I could not understand by analogy would not share the essence ego, so the limit of empathy is the limit of the eidos.

(155–156) If I am thus an absolute, uncancellable fact, the question is how far this reaches. My sphere of the past is likewise, rightly understood, uncancellable. Recollection can deceive, but it can show itself false only through conflict with other recollections, and it makes no sense that all memory deceives and that there is no past here that would be mine. Apodicticity extends to the existence of a past, though not to its content: single memories can be false, but only against other memories, so a past as such cannot be cancelled. This is the claim Husserl's critique of the apodicticity of recollection in First Philosophy (Hua VIII) tests.

(156) But can consciousness not begin, can consciousness not end? Consciousness as stream of experience, as the place of being in the form of immanent time. Can immanent time be bounded? Or, as we can also put it: can there be a first experience of the past that as such has no horizon of memory, and a last experience that has no horizon of expectation before it? Or is only the horizon of memory simply uncancellable as to being, but not the horizon of expectation, so that at the last experience the pre-expectation would be unfulfillable? The question of a first and last experience is posed through horizons: retention and protention would have to be empty.

(156) Can what appears as the new now appear without a background of the past, as continuous continuation of something just past? And can what appears as now be without passing concretely into a new now? How can an expectation be disappointed (and it belongs to the essence of the stream to carry protention in every phase) except in relation to something newly arriving that is other than expected? Is the non-being of something posited as future thinkable otherwise than in conflict with a new present? Can there be a breaking off without fulfilment or disappointment? Breaking off, what is that to mean? Nothing comes, and then there is nothing, neither the expected nor its other. But what does it mean that there is nothing? When I state or consider this in questioning, I must, as a living, knowing subject, represent something in the future from the now and consider the possibilities of its negation, its striking out. But how could the not be motivated if the field of future immanence were completely empty? With what would the positing conflict? Of course with something that is; so something is there after all. A note asks whether recourse to intersubjectivity is not missing here: the cessation of foreign life as a possible objection. Negation of a future requires a present that negates it, so an end of the stream is unrepresentable from within; the note concedes that I do experience the ending of others' lives, which might supply the missing evidence of an end.

(156–157) Immanent time must therefore continue endlessly into the future as filled time. I cannot cease to live; my stream of consciousness cannot break off; such a breaking off has no sense for absolute consciousness. I can be a "dead", unawakened ego, sleeping completely without dreams, one that has a wholly undifferentiated stream in which there is nothing that fulfils the conditions of affection and so of action either. The ego is then out of function. It is not therefore nothing, and it is inseparable from its stream even when out of function. The ego, this "pole", cannot arise and cannot perish; it can only be awakened. As with expectation, so all the more with memory. What could the nothing before a memory I still have mean? Nothing is a black night in which nothing happens. But this black night is itself again something, a kind of positive filling of the form of immanent time. The ego's immortality is derived from the structure of the stream: the undifferentiated stream of dreamless sleep is a positive filling, the "black night", so death of the pure ego is replaced by dormancy, which applies the separation of standing-out and affection from Appendix IV.

(157) So the ego cannot arise and perish; it always lives ("always thinks"). The ego, the pure monadic subjectivity that the phenomenological reduction yields in purity, is "eternal", in a certain sense immortal. But only what is natural can be born and die in the natural sense, the human being as member of nature. That it is unthinkable that a purely grasped ego, or more concretely a monadic subjectivity, should not be means, according to these discussions, that it is unthinkable that my monadic stream should not fill out its form of time infinite on both sides, and that the ego-pole belonging to this infinity should not be. "Always thinks" echoes Descartes's claim that the soul always thinks, which Leibniz defended against Locke; birth and death belong to the human being as natural object, not to the transcendental ego, the position Husserl keeps in the late texts on generativity (Hua XLII), where birth and death become "limit-problems".

(157) Is it then thinkable that an ego never awakes, that a stream of experience is the same uniform grey through its two-sided infinity? And, further, is it thinkable that all egos are in this state, the limiting state of absolute dullness, in which there is no development, no differing habituality, no differing passivity and activity? The question is whether the eternal ego could be eternally dormant, a world of sleeping monads.

(157) Does the possibility of cognition, which must be demanded of everything existing, object to this? The premise of Nr. 4, that to be is to be possibly known, is invoked against the sleeping universe.

(157) Only an ego that can know itself can know any other ego, and anything else at all, a nature, a world. An ego dull absolutely and from eternity to eternity could not know itself and could not be a subject of knowledge at all, obviously. So at least a second ego would have to exist beside the dull one as possible knower. Can it be known by another ego, which we must now add in thought as a knowing, awake ego? One can see that this too is impossible: how could it come to an expression? It would have to be awakened, and then the absolute dullness would be over. The possibility of the existence of an infinitely dead ego would therefore presuppose, for its knowability, that it was not infinitely dead, that it had expressed itself, expressed itself bodily, and can do so again. If ceasing becomes possible, only one conclusion remains: since this ego was attested bodily and the attestation ceases with bodily death, it is for essential reasons in a state of continuing "involution", without any further determination of this ego being possible. It exists only as the night following life, and the remaining problem is only to what extent a dead ego can awaken again and what conditions of possibility would have to be fulfilled for identifying the possibly awakened one (furnished with a new body), if such conditions are thinkable. At any rate an ego "dead" into infinity would be knowable neither for itself nor for any other ego, nor, of course, would a totality of such egos. It cannot be, therefore, since it is unthinkable. The argument excludes an eternally dormant ego, since it could be known neither by itself nor by another; but it allows the dead to be in "involution", Leibniz's word for death in the Monadology (§73), a state of enfolding without expression. Resurrection is raised as a question of the conditions of re-identification with a new body, which shows how far the premise of knowability drives the speculation.

(157–158) One could say that the seemingly legitimate representation of such an absolutely dead ego arises from our thinking dreamless sleep continued in infinitum in both directions, covering every experience with the black night. It is representable only for an ego that has awakened, which can then extend this night "in infinitum" and push itself out beyond it as awake. But the "infinity" of continuation does not mean an actual infinity, which is absurd here as well. Properly speaking, an absolutely dead ego is unthinkable. The representation of infinite sleep is parasitic on a waking ego that projects it, so it cannot be the representation of an ego that never wakes. Potential infinity is the only legitimate infinity here.

(158) But do we not operate in our considerations with actual and therefore false infinities? An infinity of immanent and filled time is supposed to belong to every ego. It exists as a lasting habitus, as the possibility of chains of recollection with ever-open horizons. But then it must be asked, for this as for other infinities, what can make it determinate for cognition, since we, or the knower, can never run through infinite time and look to see what is in it (Brouwer). What can make a single monadic subject determinate in infinitum; can a single one be determinate at all, and are not several egos already necessary in relation to this? Is not the animation of nature in infinitum, an infinity of egos (an infinite set), absurd? The reference to Brouwer's intuitionism, then much discussed through Hermann Weyl's *Über die neue Grundlagenkrise der Mathematik* (1921), turns the infinite stream into a problem of constructive determinability. If no one can survey an infinite time, the determinacy of a monad's infinite past may require other monads, and an actually infinite set of egos may be illegitimate, a doubt about the "open totality" Appendix XVII asked about.

(158) If we take it as granted that every ego must necessarily be at times awake, "living", and that there are several egos, then the questions of a common nature arise. The plurality can be knowable only if an ego has actually constituted nature. Is it thinkable that there are several egos and that they have never constituted a common nature, or that none of them has? The answer will be no, for then the possibility of knowledge would be lacking altogether. A world that is unknowable cannot be. That does not mean it must always be known. But it is knowable if an ego develops toward knowledge, even partial knowledge, if it or other egos develop so that they can also arrive at this knowledge, penetrate into the unknown past, as well as into the future and the unknown present parts of the world. Nr. 4's conclusion returns: several waking egos require a common nature for their plurality to be knowable. Knowability is defined developmentally: a world is knowable if some ego could develop to know it, which links Appendix XIV's teleology to the premise of the deduction.

### Appendix XXI. All Monadic Possibilities Are Relative in Existence to the Monadic Actualities (Around 1922)

(158–160) A monad can think itself as being otherwise but is given to itself absolutely as existing. The positing of its being otherwise presupposes the positing of its being. That it is a monad of this essence, of these determinations that determine it concretely as this monad, more precisely of this stream of life, these habitual peculiarities, is contingent insofar as it could have been otherwise, lived another life. But here every possibility stands in absolute, not reserved, conflict with what is given absolutely in perception. My non-being is representable only in the form of being otherwise, whereas I can think a thing as simply not existing even while it is perceived. I can now form the general essence of a monad in general and recognize with insight that it belongs to it by essence that, if it exists, its possible non-being can only have the form of a possible being otherwise, cancelled by the absolute being it has for itself. Likewise it belongs by essence to a monad in general that, as pure possibility, it is either a being-otherwise of my own monad (that of the knower) or a being-otherwise of a psychic monad I can conceive, namely the monad of a bodiliness I can conceive in my surroundings. But then I again re-imagine myself and all actual monads. The same holds if I posit any other actual monad as knower. The non-being of a monad stands in absolute conflict with the given monads; the possible being of every conceivable monad is either actual being or stands in conflict, "directly or indirectly", with every actual monad. In this respect the essence of every monad is inseparable from monadic existence. Its possibility and actuality stand in another relation than the possibility and actuality of "things". A given nature actually contains a thing, and the thing is factual actuality. But nature could also be otherwise, such that this thing did not belong to it, did not exist. Here possibilities precede actualities. The monad, however, is given to itself absolutely, and every other monad is posited from it as absolutely given for itself, rationally, though with reservations. All monadic possibilities are relative in existence to the monadic actualities. Everything monadic that does not exist is non-existent by absolute cancellation. All possibilities are absolutely decided. But is not everything possible in nature also decided in advance? The fiction of an arbitrary nature, a purely possible nature in general, conflicts with no actual nature. But the fiction of arbitrary monads conflicts with the actual monads. One could also try to say that a monad is an individual. It cannot be twice. To represent it concretely is to represent something unique that could only be identified in repetition. Here I cannot separate the repeatable concrete essence and the individual with forms of individuality. The essence includes the individuality. How can individuality be concretely representable as mere possibility? What ultimately individuates is the monadic individual, whose possibility includes its actuality in such a way that either this individual exists or a "being otherwise", a variation of an existing individual. Whether these are usable thoughts must be considered anew. For monads, possibility does not precede actuality: every possible monad is either actual or a variant of an actual one, since possibilities are relative to the actual ground that imagines them. A possible nature conflicts with nothing, a possible monad conflicts with the actual monads, which gives monadic essence the structure the ontological argument gives God's: essence includes existence, here because the monad's essence includes its individuality, Leibniz's complete concept read from the inside. Husserl's last sentence leaves the thought on trial.

### Appendix XXII. On the Re-imagining of My Ego (Mid-1920s)

(160) One must be careful here, when one has come as far as we have. It is of course not as if re-imaginings of my ego, by which I could obtain other concrete egos, preceded empathy or could precede it. Does re-imagining some piece of my life already create, in place of me who am, the ego as if I were another ego? I would have to think the piece of fiction "through to the end" consistently. Written several years later, the note withdraws the priority Nr. 7 and Appendix XVI gave to variation over empathy. A re-imagined fragment does not yet yield another ego; only a fiction carried through completely would, and that cannot be done.

(160) If I already have others, I of course gain for each other only a little piece of personal being in personal life, but also a horizon of being, open and endless, with unknown pulses of life and the like, which constantly have the mode "other". The other is given with an endless horizon in the mode "other", which re-imagining my own life could never supply: a fragment of variation is a modification of me, whereas a fragment of empathy opens an alien infinity.

(160) But if I have others factually, and with them as possibilities, then I can re-imagine myself, and so on. The order is reversed: the factual other makes my re-imagining possible as the imagining of another ego.

(160) On the other hand, if this yields a motivation from empathy, perhaps an essentially necessary one, it is implied that what I gain by re-imagining would not be a system of pure possibilities that are pure for themselves. Perhaps these are thoughts worth considering. If the eidos ego depends on empathy for its extension, eidetic variation of the ego is not purely a priori but rests on the fact of the other. This concedes that intersubjectivity conditions eidetic method itself: the a priori of the ego would rest on the fact of others.

### No. 8. I and the Other Ego in Conflict and in Coexistence (Around 1922)

(161) First, I am. There is the system of ideal possibilities of my ego's being otherwise, all mutually incompatible. Each is therefore cancelled, characterized as a non-existent ego by the I-am, and each, posited as existing, cancels every other as non-existent. The result of Nr. 7 and its appendices is restated as the first premise: possibilities of me exclude one another and are struck out by my actuality.

(161) Second, let us now take others beside me, coexisting with me, within the phenomenological reduction. I then have the I-am and the system of its variations of being otherwise, which coincide with it. In the understanding grasp of another, a co-subject, I have a presentifying positing of an ego, motivated by the familiar analogizing, which, as to its pure possibility, is contained in that system of the possible being-otherwise of my ego. The other is, as pure possibility, one of my variants; the question is why, unlike my variants, he is not struck out by my actuality.

(161–162) Consider the possibilities of the non-being of my ego and of an ego in general. Regarding my own ego, my monad, I have inner consciousness, inner impression, recollection, protention, and possibly a grasping in reflection. Of all other possibilities I have reproductive imagination. If I posit one of these possibilities as actuality, I posit it as present, past, or future. Here as elsewhere, to posit something as present means correlatively to posit it as perceived (or perceivable), and in the other cases as recollectable, and so on. The actual ego is posited as existing, and at the same time the possible ego coinciding with it is posited hypothetically. The hypothetical thesis with its content is cancelled by the actual thesis. In these cases what I posit is posited in coincidence and in one with my actual ego and its life, and the intuitive possible ego is itself posited directly. I cannot represent a consciousness and an ego intuitively without having it in coincidence with my actual one, and if I posit the intuitive directly, I have the conflict. The source of conflict is located: a variant of me, posited directly, must be posited where I am, in my present, my perception, my memory, and so collides with my actual life.

(162) It is otherwise with analogizing. I do not have the other in a direct presentification. It is not a recollection but precisely an analogue, admittedly an unclear word. Sensations, intuitions, judgments, and so on that are not mine, that I only "imagine", I nevertheless posit as co-present with a foreign body, and I do not posit them in coincidence with my life and in one with my body appearing to me. The solution to Nr. 7's problem lies in a single shift of reference: the other's life is posited as co-present with the foreign body, not with mine, and therefore does not occupy my place and does not conflict with me. Analogy relocates the presentified life to another body, which is how a variant of me becomes a coexisting other.

#### Empathy Is Not Presentation by Representation, Not Depiction

(162–163) What I really have intuitively necessarily coincides with my impressional givens of inner consciousness. Must I then distinguish this really intuitive content from what "presents itself" in it and, in the situation of motivation, is motivated as existing, co-existing with the foreign body? Is empathic presentification a kind of "presentation", related to pictorial presentation? If I have "the same" feeling as the one I empathize in my counterpart, one might say I have impressional presentation; if I have no such feeling, imaginative presentation. It is certainly no genuine imagery. My own feeling is not an image of the foreign feeling; otherwise my own experiential phantasm would be an image of the foreign one. But in imagery I can set the subject beside it in a second phantasy intuition; I can set what presents intuitively over against what is presented. Of that there is nothing here. Are there not similar difficulties for intuitive memory and expectation? Can I intuit something sensuously objective without the phantasy field of vision coinciding with the perceptually filled one? It makes a difference whether I posit something imagined as being now (in conflict with what is perceived) or have a positing of the same reproductive content, with its particular horizons, as a positing of the past. That is just a "presentification", a positing act. In the case of imagery I have something present, given as existing through an impression (perception), in which something else is depicted (perceived image, perceptual imagery). That can pass into memory, and I then have an image in memory; but also, modified, in imagination: I imagine an image as present (fictively present), in which something else is presented. When a phantasy tone, a phantasy melody, a phantasy house, and so on emerges, my corresponding perceptual tonal sphere, visual sphere, and so on is suppressed. My regard follows the imagined formations; I see, as it were, living in phantasy, hear as it were, and I can lose myself entirely in phantasy and become the phantasy-ego. But I can also hold fast the intention toward the actual spheres and am then the actual ego that is aware of following phantasies, contemplating phantasy notions, or shaping phantasies at will. So too I am either the actual ego of the present that presentifies something past, or I live in the past and am lost in it, no longer actually conscious of the present, though following the series of memories I finally come back to the immediate past and the present. How is it with empathy? When I "see" a foreign body, a foreign hand, as a hand, I have an external thing similar to my hand in a certain position; to it belong certain courses of sensation, certain free kinaesthetic courses and their results, certain apperceptions of its lying on the table and the table's modes of appearance in corresponding form, and so on; and all this is motivated by the similarity as an analogue, has the character of positing, and is co-existent with that thing which is another lived body, the body of a psychic life and an ego. If I went over there, sat down so, held such things, handled them so, and so on, I would, being there, have such and such psychic occurrences. But I have not gone there, and there is a thing that looks as I would look bodily to myself if my body were moved there as if it were a thing like any other. These are starting points for the theory of empathy. The appendix clears away the theory of empathy as picture-consciousness, the view, which Scheler also attacked, that my own felt state serves as an image of the other's. Imagery has a perceived image and a depicted subject that can be set side by side; empathy has nothing of the kind. Phantasy, memory, and empathy are compared as modes of presentification, each with the alternative of being "lost in" it or remaining the actual ego that performs it. The hand example gives empathy's actual structure: a hypothetical "if I were there" bound to a thing that looks as my body would look moved there.

(163–164) Possible experiences, represented intuitively as belonging hypothetically to certain psychophysical circumstances of mine and posited in this hypothetical way, play the mediating role. They are awakened associatively by the appearance of the foreign bodily thing, which first points to my body, alone originally given, and to the psychic element connected with it, to my corresponding ego-comportment and ego-doing insofar as it is connected with the appearance of my body and runs parallel to it. The foreign body is understood as a lived body in the sense of correspondingly motivated psychic occurrences, ego-activities, and the like, which do not function as "images of" but as apperceptive support. A feeling in me, or a sensing in phantasy, is not referred to another or somehow extrojected, any more than a present phantasm is projected into the past, which would be unintelligible. Rather, an I-think, I-feel, and so on is motivated in reproductive intuitiveness, or emptily, and this ego is posited, but given as a second ego coexisting with mine; it is given for me only through presentification and appresentatively, as an ego other than mine. Hypothetical experiences of mine are the "apperceptive support" of empathy, not its content. Husserl rejects "extrojection" exactly as the theory of memory rejects projecting a present image into the past: what is posited is an ego, given as second and coexisting, reached through my hypothetical self but not identical with it. The structure of the 1927 analyses, appresentation founded on "as if I were there", is complete here in outline.

### No. 9. Communal Spirit I: Person, Personal Wholes, Personal Communities of Effect. Community and Society (Freiburg, 1921)

(165) Husserl's summary names two themes. First, social acts and how communities are founded through them, in particular personalities of higher order; various forms of communities and higher-level personalities; communities that have grown naturally and their development (the family), as against communities founded "artificially", at will. Second, ethics and sociality, in detail: duty, responsibility, the functionary of a social body. The "communal spirit" texts are, as Kern's introduction noted, Husserl's engagement with Tönnies, Simmel, and Weber, and they work out what Ideen II (§51) only announced. The pairing of social acts with ethics shows that the person, for Husserl, is constituted socially and bears responsibility as a member.

#### §1. The Instinctual Subject before and after Empathy

(165) If we imagine a subject before empathy, constituting a spatial world instinctively, it is not a person, even if we add the freedom of deliberation, the suspension of instinctual realization, the weighing of what is best, of the best goals of pleasure or the best ways (pleasure in things and sensuous displeasure, things that give pleasure, and so on). At this level that is "reason", and there are "objects of reason" here: technical objects, tools, "devised methods" for reaching enjoyment and fleeing danger (danger stemming from things), which are transferred from one case to new ones and so become apperceptively determined methodical conceptions. Even a reasoning, tool-using solitary is not a person. Instrumental reason exists before sociality; personhood needs something more, which the next sections locate in social acts.

(165–166) We have not yet considered other subjects as goals of action, as subjects who "do us good" or "inflict harm". I can let myself be determined by others in the manner of imitation. I can want to help them instinctively. There is an instinctual "mother love", "parental love", an instinctual care that in its fulfilment is at once joy in their well-being, and naturally also an immediate instinctual suffering with their suffering. Looked at closely, this is not joy at their joy in the sense of rejoicing at the same thing, but joy that they rejoice, sorrow that they sorrow. But there is also a love of them themselves: a joy in their existence, a joy in living together with them, in their perceived existence, in them as they are given in experiencing empathy, in their ego-life and doing; a general joy that does not exclude taking offence at a single deed, evaluating it negatively, intervening against it, and so on. Joy in the subject as the identical subject of its comportment in its surroundings, showing its individual character in the ways of this comportment. Love determines an interest in promoting the other, hate an interest in harming, annihilating. The analysis of instinctual love distinguishes joy that the other rejoices from rejoicing at the same object, Scheler's distinction between sympathy and emotional contagion. Love's object is the person as "identical subject" showing a character, the individuality of Nr. 2, so even instinctual love is directed at what persons manifest in position-taking.

(166) These are not yet social acts, nor acts of genuine social love. Love, care, imitation, and hate are relations to others that need not be addressed to them; the social act requires address and its reception.

#### §2. Social Acts and Relations. The I–Thou Relation

(166) Husserl sets out cases. First: I do something with the intention that the other notice it and in the expectation, and so also with the intention, that the other behave in such and such a way. But that does not yet make a social act. The other need not notice my intention; I do not turn to him. Acting with an eye to another's reaction is strategic, not social.

(166) Second: I do it in the expectation that the other, noticing that I have this intention, will do this. Do I thereby already turn to him? I turn to him when my primary intention is one of communication. I make something known to him. When my wife lays an apple on my hat so that I remember to eat something before going out, I understand her intention. Is that a communication? A making known of her intention certainly lies in it. Another example: gypsies who lay branches at a crossroads so that their companions learn which way they have gone; these one will have to call communications. The apple on the hat (a domestic detail from Husserl's own life) and the branches at the crossroads test where communication begins. Communication requires that the intention to be understood is itself meant to be understood, Grice's later analysis of meaning, here reached through examples of signs left behind.

(166–167) Immediate communications, or better, contact establishing an original connection between I and Thou in originally experiencing empathy: we have the original experience of facing each other, and I "say" something to him, I "express myself", I perform an expressive movement or a vocal utterance or an externally visible, noticeable doing apt to awaken in the other the consciousness that I intend to make something known to him; the expression of this intention (as in a call) can be separated from what is then further to be made known and communicated. More exactly, what follows is indicated as "this is so and so", "you should do this", and the like. The communicated fact is the content of my intention that the other take note of it, and specifically of my intention made known to him. The form of the communication is understandable through the preceding call, for instance, and possibly also through the linguistic form, to which this form of communication adheres as such. The call ("hey!") separates out the second-order intention, the intention to be addressed, from the content; its function is pure address, which marks the social act as reflexive in structure.

(167) How is the I–Thou relation established, which is the personal relation of effect in the pregnant sense? The I–Thou relation is defined as a relation of effect between persons, the term "Wirkungsbeziehung" giving Husserl's sociality its practical rather than merely cognitive character.

(167) First, speaking figuratively: we both, I and you, "look each other in the eye"; he understands me, notices me, I notice him, simultaneously. Mutual and simultaneous awareness of being aware is the minimal I–Thou, the face-to-face situation that Alfred Schutz later called the "we-relationship".

(167–168) Second: I turn to you and communicate a fact to you. I experience a fact and draw the other's attention to it, since it also lies within his nearest range of experience, by "pointing". A certain movement of the hand and finger, throwing a piece of wood in that direction, and so on, awakens attention and "steers" it in this direction, where something interesting for the other (as for me earlier) is happening, and interest naturally slides of itself from the pointing movement to the new thing I wanted to draw attention to. Such means of awakening interest in an objectivity present in the external surroundings of I and Thou, if they repeatedly prove suitable in the same way, come to be used more often from both sides and finally are understood without further ado as produced intentionally by the other for the purpose of "pointing". In the given case the Thou understands: "I see that there and point the Thou to it so that he sees it too." My intention that he see it, my will, is realized by means of pointing (pointing as the way), which for me is a doing that he is to grasp and understand as the means by which I want to steer him there. I thereby act on the other, and not only in general, but within the empathy, performed reciprocally and simultaneously, that brings I and Thou into "contact"; and moreover in the special way that my acting on him (my will or instinctual striving to "determine" the other, to "move" him spiritually, to determine him to a striving or willing) is to be noticeable to the other and itself belongs to the "way", the means of the determination. Pointing becomes a convention by repetition: a natural means of steering attention is reused until it is understood as produced in order to be understood. The defining feature of the social act is that the influence is meant to be noticed as influence, so the other's understanding of my intention is part of the means.

(168) Normally this is an effective means, though not always, as in the case of fear of the enemy and the flight that may follow. Communication can trigger behaviour that is not uptake: fear of the one who addresses me is a response to the address, not its acceptance.

(168) Another case is descriptive communication, in the situation of "contact". Expressions, natural bodily reflexes of external events, of external physical or animal occurrences, become natural signs of these events; they draw the partner's attention to them if he has not seen them himself; and then, produced intentionally and emphasized before the other, made especially visible to him, they serve as indications, and at the same time as special ones, for special sides or parts of the event. Description arises from natural expression made deliberate, the genesis of signs from symptoms, which ties the phenomenology of language to the theory of indication in the First Logical Investigation.

(168–169) Then there are communications of recollections and of what is not present. Further, communication to the absent, or from the absent: communications to others at a temporal distance. My present will concerns here a future understanding of my communicating expression, which persists as a sensuous thing, and a future taking note (and so a doing) on the part of the other, in a "later" future lying outside the sphere of presence. I and Thou do not "touch" each other. They reach out the spiritual hand to each other across a stretch of time; the past ego is the subject of a communicating act, the giver, the later, future one the receiver. But it also belongs to the situation that the ego, in willing so now, founds a disposition of will, a habitual, lasting will, and as a rule at least "remains" with its will. And it is understood so. The receiver, for instance, grasps the giver as a human being not present but living in the present, who wanted then to let this communication reach him and still wants him to receive it, provided no counter-motives speak for a change of will and disposition. We note at the same time that a deceased person, recognized as such, and a living one can also reach each other the spiritual hand; a communication can come from the one to the other, understood as directed by the once-living to this now-living person and received by him. Communication at a distance, writing and its descendants, is analysed as a social act extended across time by the habitual will of the writer. The dead can communicate with the living, which makes tradition a social relation: the theme of "The Origin of Geometry" (Hua VI, Appendix III), where written sedimentation lets the founders of geometry address all later geometers.

#### §3. The Practical Community of Will

(169) So far we have dealt with the mere communication of facts; a certain community of will and a certain agreement already lie in it. But there is also a practical agreement in the pregnant sense, a practical community of will: in the status of contact the Thou lets himself be determined to perform not merely a taking note but another action, say an external action reaching into the physical or spiritual surroundings. The addresser makes known, and possibly describes, the wish or will that such and such happen, in the expectation that it will motivate the addressee. The situation is similar to that of mere communication, except that the communication itself becomes the means for performing a further action (as against the taking note contained in communication, which is itself already an action). Practical communication adds a further action to the uptake: the request and the command are communications whose intended effect is a deed.

(169–170) If he will not, I can want to compel him and count on his knowing it, or I can communicate that to him too; and he can, as he otherwise avoids painful external circumstances, act accordingly, submit to my will. My will communicated to him may be the motive of his lasting general will to do, once and for all, "in general", what I want, just as I can want in general that he submit, insofar as I believe I possess means of compulsion. Then the relation of master and servant arises as a lasting personal relation, lasting through a universal habitus of persons who had entered into contact or mediate spiritual hand-reaching, a relation of effect. Once the relation is founded, every action in which it appears is characterized as issuing from the founding interweaving of the wills of the two persons. I command, I as master; he follows "dutifully" as servant, in the consciousness of having submitted, of being subject, and as regards the deed, in the consciousness of its being dutiful. Domination is founded by compulsion but persists as a habitual will of the subordinate, a "submission" that is his own lasting act. Unlike Hegel's struggle for recognition, the master–servant relation here is a will-structure that persists only through the servant's habitual compliance, which prepares the analysis of its dissolution in §9.

(170) In a request we have a transient relation. The communication of my wish will, I hope, act as a motive for his will; my wish and my hope that his taking note will move him to comply are directed at the addressee. The wish concerns his striving, willing, doing; it is not yet thereby directed at him, but it becomes so through the communication, through the taking note accomplished with it, in the contact of I and Thou. A wish about someone becomes a request to him only through communication: address is what directs a wish at its object as at a person.

(170) Instead of immediate contact there can always be determination at a distance, in which the communication itself may take place in the contact of the subjects, as with a present wish and will directed at the future, or the communication may itself be a communication at a distance. Social acts admit every combination of present and distant communication and present and future fulfilment.

(170) A community of will, an agreement, can also be reciprocal, ending in a mutual arrangement. I fulfil your wish if you fulfil mine; I do this for your benefit if you do that for mine in return. Further: we both wish that something happen, we decide "together", I do one part, you the other part, and so on. S1 and S2 will the same G, but not each for himself: S1 wills G as also willed by S2; S2's will belongs to what S1 wills, and conversely. That S1 realizes part D1 and S2 part D2 is again decided in the will of both, and decided for both as "means" in the wider sense, or as belonging to the realization, but before that to the intention. Husserl gives a precise analysis of joint intention: each wills the goal as willed by the other, and the division of labour belongs to the content of each will. This anticipates by decades the analytic literature on collective intentionality (Searle, Gilbert, Bratman), with the further feature that the joint will arises from social acts of communication.

#### §4. The Acquisition of Personal Self-Consciousness in the I–Thou Relation

(170–172) The pole of all affections and actions, necessarily present in the subject imagined as solipsistic, the subject of motivation running through the stream of experience, which as such is the constant subject of a striving in manifold modalities, becomes an ego and thereby a personal subject; it gains personal "self-consciousness" in the I–Thou relation, in the community of striving and of will made possible by communication. Already in empathy the ego becomes aware of itself as subject of its life and of its surroundings, and the foreign ego is conscious as "other ego" (but at first only as another subject-pole of another life related, in other modes of givenness, to the same surroundings) through back-reference to one's own subject, which thereby reflects on itself.
