As subject of motivation I now enter the primally social I–Thou relation not merely beside the other as other, but I motivate him and he motivates me; and in the distinguished relation that the I–Thou relation establishes through social acts, there is a unity of striving or specific willing that encompasses both subjects, in which both are reciprocally related to each other in actual consciousness, so as to "act" on each other as subjects of striving, that is, to determine each other, in their striving directedness toward each other, to a doing or a suffering. In this community not only does each strive, but each is also objective to himself as striving; he is not merely pregiven to himself as such but given to himself objectively. Not, of course, objective as a theoretical theme, as a theme of taking note and of an intention directed at closer taking note or even theoretical knowledge (attentive contemplation, observation, perception seeking and taking knowledge), but practically objective, belonging to the practical theme. In the one case striving is directed at appropriating the object as a "matter", at exhibiting it, at producing the ideally identical unity of what is meant, at transforming what is meant into its self, which would be grasped as identical in ever new intended productions, and possibly further at conceptual grasp, predicative determination, theorems concerning the matter. In the other case striving is directed at "moving" the other to fulfil my request, my command: first, that he take note of this or that to which such things relate; then, that he take note of this command or request of mine, and that my command, given to him, my wish, come to his knowledge, should "move" and motivate him accordingly. Here, then, I myself belong to the practical theme, to the context of what the practical intention is directed at, to the context of what is to be surveyed practically and aimed at. So in every action of this kind I must keep the "way", the intermediate goals, and the end goal practically in view thematically. The pole-ego becomes a person in the I–Thou relation because there it becomes objective to itself practically: to move another, I must include myself, as the one who commands, in my own plan. Self-objectification is first practical, not theoretical, the move G. H. Mead made a few years later with the "me" arising from taking the other's attitude, and Fichte earlier with the "summons" (Aufforderung) as the condition of self-consciousness. Reflection gives the pole; the social act gives the person.

#### §5. Love

(172–174) Personal love is a lasting disposition, a lasting practical habitus, together with its realizing activity. First, it is an active pleasure in the personal individuality of the beloved, in its whole comportment in its passive and active behaviour toward its surroundings, in the expression of its individuality in the body, in its spiritualized bodiliness in general. Second, it is a striving not only for the richest possible exercise of this joy but for personal "contact" with it and for community in life and striving, in which his life is taken up into my life, his striving into my striving, insofar as my striving and my willing realize themselves in his and in his realizing doing, as his do in mine. The ego is a pole for affections and actions, and as such it is the pole of a unity of striving running through the whole streaming consciousness. Striving in its manifold modalities makes up the life of the ego; all intentionality that has the form of spiritual possession, of freely available, acquired unities of existence, and of valuable or useful existence, is a formation of striving and possibly of "conscious" willing. If I enter into a community of striving with another, I live as ego in him and he in me. But the kind and intimacy of this community then depend on the extent to which I and Thou are sheltered in each other, on the extent of the community of striving, and on various other points. Not every community is reciprocal, and not every reciprocal community is a community of love. Ideally it belongs to love that the lovers, as personal subjects, that is, as socially communicating subjects of striving, have founded a community of striving not only in single directions of striving but, properly and improperly, for all possible striving of the two. The lovers do not merely arrange some particular joint undertaking, though that may also occur; nor do they merely make mutual communications and practise a community of taking note concerning their common surroundings, and the like. Rather, in their having bound themselves into a community of love lies that all striving of the one enters, or has entered once and for all, into the striving of the other, and conversely, in a universal way. This means that as soon as the lovers' contact becomes actual and the one becomes aware of the other's striving, he explicitly (the "proper" case) seeks to take it into his own, takes part in it, perhaps with the other's will, and helps. And if the other prefers to manage things himself, what the other does on his own is nonetheless not merely his concern but also the lover's; in it the lover's wish and will are also realized, not for his own sake, as an isolated ego, but for the sake of the other, because this other, as subject of his life and striving, is wholly taken up into the range of the lover's intentionality of striving. Even in the life-activities of each of the lovers that take place outside contact, of which the other to the greatest extent knows nothing, the other's will lives implicitly. As lover I know that whatever I think, feel, strive for, or do is necessarily "in the sense" of my beloved, right with him, not only not blamed by him and in this sense acknowledged as right, but, as striven for by me, also in the sense of his striving. This sense would become actual if he were there: he would take part with joy and gladly stand by me, and where that was excluded or unwanted, he would gladly and lovingly put himself into my situation of striving and, in an approval of will, know his ego and its striving active in mine (the improper, implicit life and striving of the lover in the beloved). We can say that lovers live not beside and with one another but in one another, actually and potentially. They therefore also bear all responsibilities together; they are bound in solidarity, even in sin and guilt. Love is defined as a universal community of striving in which each lives "in" the other: the other's will is present implicitly even where he is absent, and approval replaces participation when participation is impossible. Love is thus the limit case of the social act, a standing interpenetration of wills, and its consequence, shared responsibility "even in sin and guilt", leads directly to the question whether such love is ethical.

#### §6. Ethical Love

(174–175) But it may be asked whether this love is not a limit case, and whether it makes up the concept of love in every good sense. The love described is perhaps a sinful love, or includes all sinful love. We think here of Christ's infinite love for all human beings and of the universal love of humanity that the Christian must awaken in himself and without which he cannot be a true Christian. Nothing ethical entered our description of that love. Loving parents chastise their children and do not take all the child's life and striving into their own life and striving in the way described. A friend is grieved when his friend falls away from his "true self". The Christian who practises love of enemies does not love the evil in the enemy, and the evil deed is not what he approves in his will. In every human soul there lies, this is the faith, a vocation, a germ to be unfolded spontaneously toward the good. In every soul an ideal ego is enclosed, the "true" ego of the person, which is realized only in "good" action. Every awakened human being (ethically awakened) posits his ideal ego in himself by will as an "infinite task". Special ethical forms of community result according to whether both of those bound together are ethically unawakened, or one is awakened and the other not, or both are awakened, and similarly for a plurality of personal subjects. As a truly (ethically) loving person, then, I love and live willingly in the budding soul, in the budding, growing, becoming ethical subject of the other; or I live in the subject that has awakened to maturity, wrestling and fighting and freely acting out its ethical life, in everything that springs from its positive ethical habituality as its ethical personal life (the ethically negative is against my wish and will). I live in it, first of all, I approve, I rejoice (or grieve). But ethical community is ethical friendship, the ethical relation between Christian and Christian, and the like. Ethical love loves the other's "true self", the ideal ego posited as "infinite task", the formula of the Kaizo articles and the ethics lectures of 1920/24 (Hua XXVII, XXXVII). Ethical love therefore discriminates: it lives in the other's growth toward the good and opposes the evil in him. Husserl's Christian vocabulary marks the source of the model, the love of neighbour and of enemy, while the phenomenological core is that love is directed at a person's ideal of itself.

#### §7. Love and the Community of Love

(175) Love of neighbour that consists merely in loving care for the other, for his ethical being and becoming, is a disposition toward others, toward communities, toward the whole of humanity, but not a relation of community, not a personal bond, or it founds no personal association. Universal benevolence is a disposition, not a social relation, since it involves no reciprocal address.

(175) We must therefore distinguish love and the community of love, the loving interpenetration of persons otherwise separate into a communal personality. A community of love is a personality of higher order formed by love; love alone is a one-sided disposition.

(175) Christian love is at first, of necessity, mere love. But it is bound up with a striving, necessarily motivated by love, to become a community of love to the greatest possible extent: a striving to "enter into relation" with human beings, to open oneself to them and to open them up for oneself, and so on, all according to practical possibility, whose limits are set ethically and thus themselves by ethical love. Love strives to become community, and ethics limits how far it may press; the missionary impulse of Christian love is described as love seeking reciprocity, restrained by respect for the other's freedom.

#### §8. The Origin of Personality in Sociality. The Community of Enjoyment, Care, the Shared Meal, and the Family

(175–176) Before the ethical lie the personal modes of comportment that are subject to ethical judgment, to approval and disapproval. All such comportments are not comportments of a subject in general (a jellyfish subject) but precisely of a personal subject. The origin of personality lies in empathy and in the social acts that grow from it. It is not enough for personality that the subject become aware of itself as pole of its acts; personality is constituted only when the subject enters into social relation with other subjects, in which it already becomes practically objective, as shown. It thereby becomes a subject that can enter, and does enter, into community, but that also enters into occasional personal relations with others and now practises in its life and striving not only self-preservation in relation to the world of things but also, as a person, in the world of persons. The thesis is stated with emphasis: the origin of personality lies in sociality. This contradicts the reading of Husserl as an egological solipsist: the person, unlike the pole-ego, is a social product, and the "jellyfish subject" of Nr. 6 is the example of a non-personal subject.

(176) Every person has a world of things, mere nature, as surroundings. If we screen off all personal relations, interests, and strivings, the question is what values and practical goals remain. Sensuous pleasure and the corresponding strivings and willings. Is a striving for observing knowledge and theoretical cognition not also conceivable; joy in beautiful appearances, in beautiful nature, in contemplating images of nature, especially those offering beautiful modes of appearance; joy in shaping the beautiful? Husserl asks whether theory and art are possible for a solitary: whether truth and beauty are values in the asocial stratum or presuppose others.

(176) What characterizes the sphere of naturally healthy sensuous pleasure and of normal, healthy sensuous striving, whose transgression announces itself in the consciousness of the anomalous, the unhealthy, the degrading, the base? Is that an original distinction or a derived one? Can scientific and aesthetic intention be motivated within this screening off, and can an order of rank of values be grounded there? The normality of Nr. 6 returns in the sphere of value: is the sense of the degrading original, or learned from others? The question whether an order of values can be founded solipsistically is left open.

(176) Lifting the screening off: I have not only a physical surrounding world; I find bodies in it and other subjects united with bodiliness, I find animals and human beings. The method of the "communal spirit" texts is that of the volume: abstract to the solitary stratum, then add others and see what changes.

(176) What relations are possible with respect to foreign animal and human subjectivity? What valuations, significations of value, strivings, goals of will? What connections as personal ones, into personal wholes? I can keep to the body of the foreign animal or human being and value it purely as an object of physical enjoyment. That is one thing. The subjective is then disregarded, although it is there for me. As when the body is killed for sensuous enjoyment, eaten, and the like. The lowest relation to others treats their bodies as things, with their subjectivity present but disregarded, which marks the possibility of objectification as a moral fact.

(176–177) But the soul and the subject are there, and the question is how their existence motivates me. Acting on the bodiliness, I must also determine the soul and can bring about sensuous pleasure or sensuous suffering in it. Treating the foreign body as an object of enjoyment, I can thereby also produce pleasure, or displeasure and pain, in the foreign subjectivity. And as in sexual enjoyment, not only can this take place, but both parties, enjoying, can be aware not only each of his own enjoyment; they can be there for one another as enjoying with and through one another, as striving for enjoying activity, and, striving for the enjoyment in this through-one-another, they can establish a unity of enjoying community. The other and his consent, or at least his compliance, is not merely a means of enjoyment (so not merely the foreign body, but the foreign subject as offering its bodiliness, which lies in its sphere of power, and permitting the activity of pleasure); rather, that the other takes part in an activity, that a unity of will spans both and brings about a unity of mutual doing, and that both enjoy pleasure in one, is itself an object of enjoyment, for each that the other enjoys. Sexual union is analysed as a community of enjoyment, a social act at the level of the senses: consent turns the other from a means into a participant, and the other's pleasure becomes part of what each enjoys. The analysis exemplifies the general structure of joint intention in §3 at the level of instinct.

(177–178) But it can also be that no regard is taken for the foreign subjectivity, that enjoyment is forced against its will, suffering thereby imposed on it, indeed that it is destroyed. Then the higher layer of joy and value not only falls away; a negative takes its place, of which it must be asked whether it does not bring about a conflict of values that not only diminishes the value but cancels it. In question then are compulsion to comply (and so subjection of the other's will), and the forcing of enjoyment against the other's will without even forced consent or subjection of will. It may be that on the compelled side there was no wish, but that in submission pleasure arises, a wish is awakened, and then satisfaction. It may be that there is no pleasure but suffering, accepted to avoid greater suffering. It may be that suffering is endured, borne, but not "accepted"; and finally that the suffering is not even endured but resisted with constant rebellion. Enduring is also a resisting, but it contains an acceptance in the heart that is lacking in unbroken and unyielding rebellion. Likewise for bodily pleasure that is biologically connected with reproduction and preservation of the species but does not concern the act itself (in animals sniffing, licking the young, and so on); and the compulsory washing, cleaning, holding back, and pulling away of children by those who raise them, by which annoyance and suffering are inflicted on them. Husserl describes violation as the cancellation of value: the negative of coercion does not merely subtract from the pleasure but negates its worth. The phenomenology of the coerced distinguishes acceptance, endurance, and rebellion as attitudes of the will toward imposed suffering; the passage then turns to the benign compulsion of child-rearing, setting the ethics of care against the ethics of consent.

(178) Instinctive care for bodiliness serves in advance healthy preservation and, of course, psychic well-being: in human beings instinctively and yet also with premeditated intention. As the child develops, it learns to understand the demand; it learns the ought of cleanliness and the like; it learns to submit, endure, obey; and finally it learns to will freely and to strive passively of its own accord for what was formerly imposed on it; it learns to understand the value of health itself and, for its sake, to resist a momentary stimulus, a momentary agreeableness or comfort. Education is the genesis of the ought: compulsion becomes obedience, then free willing, then passive habit, the internalization that Husserl's genetic phenomenology treats as the sedimentation of will into habitus.

(178) Comportment toward children, who are "not taken fully", who are regarded, valued, and loved under the aspect of their future development. The child is valued as a future person, the developmental horizon that Nr. 6 used in empathy now appearing in valuation.

(178) Comportment toward animals as irrational instinctual beings, and toward higher animals that approach the level of the human being. The levels of empathy in Nr. 6 become levels of moral relation.

(178) Developed human beings in relation to developed human beings. The transient relation of communication. The list of relations is ordered by the developmental level of the other, culminating in communication between equals.

(178–179) There is an instinctive striving for togetherness with immediate kin (the family), and communities of sociability. The shared meal: we assume that each can eat his fill, fully and undisturbed by the other, and that motives of envy and the like remain excluded. There is the joy in one's own eating, but also the pleasing resonance of the other's joy in eating, on the ground of natural love for the members of the family. That is a transient community. But it becomes a regulated institution, a regular coming together for the shared meal as a unity of social action. The shared meal is a basic piece of the founding of the family as a social institution, a lasting community, constituted as such for the consciousness of the family members. The family is not merely a habitual way of living side by side and keeping to one another in life, but a community of life with orders of life that have a social character. Each member not only knows the other beside it and seeks the other because it gives pleasure to be beside it; there are communities, reciprocal relatedness of striving life, of doing, of working together, of functioning in relation to one another, in which the doing of one enters into the doing of the other, each subject intervenes personally in the life and doing of the other, lives in it, unites with it as a person in manifold I–Thou relations. In short, a unity, a whole, is constituted, built from ego-subjects that interpenetrate in combining, insofar as the life of the one ego lives along in the life of the other and takes part in it: the egoity of the one is not beside that of the other but lives and acts in the other. In the family this happens in all the ego-relations that found the family's unity, which do not encompass all the relations that can set human beings in unity and connect them. The meal is chosen as the founding institution of the family: enjoyment shared, regularized, and so made an institution, a social action repeated. The family is defined by interpenetration ("the egoity of the one lives in the other"), the community-of-love structure of §5 extended to a lasting group.

(179) The father of the house can have the social function of master, the other members of the family the function of those who obey and serve. He can be a despot or the "head" who cares responsibly for the whole. And the mother of the house, as housewife in charge, can be the head of the household economy and the care of children, responsible for them and in this function mistress, yet on the other hand accountable for the rightness of her doing to the master responsible for the universal whole. So the other adult members can have their particular functions and act responsibly in them, again with the stated restriction: they may stand under the housewife as economic functionaries, or directly under the master as the highest "head". Each knows himself and every other as subject of his functions. The family of 1921 is described in its patriarchal form as a hierarchy of responsible functions; the phenomenological point is that each member knows himself as a functionary, the root of the concept of duty in §9.

(179) There are personal associations that grow naturally and instinctively (the family and those arising from it), transformed historically. Natural communities have a history: they grow from instinct and are reshaped by history.

(179) There are associations founded artificially for some purpose: clubs, associations of purpose. Tönnies's distinction between Gemeinschaft and Gesellschaft is restated as the difference between grown and founded associations, which §9 reconsiders.

#### §9. Sociality and Ethics. Function in the Community and Duty. Community and Society

(180) Reason in sociality. The ethical. The ethical on an instinctive basis. The ethical and personality. Can there be anything ethical where no actual or possible personal community is in play, no comportment of persons toward persons? The headings pose the question whether ethics is essentially social.

(180) Personal relations to oneself, however, come essentially into consideration here. In a constituted association I am not only an active subject but the subject of a personal action, and as such not only the subject of praxis but also an object-subject belonging to the content of the action (what is willed, striven for, planned). If I come to an agreement with another, if I take on a function, the other determines me to determine myself to take this or that on. The self-relation that ethics requires is socially mediated: I am object of my own action because the other determines me to determine myself, the structure of §4 now as the root of obligation.

(180–181) In the naturally growing family community we easily see that the first thing is the naturally and naively growing care of the mother for the children, of the man for the mother as wife and as mother of the children, and so on. Failure in natural care through oversight, momentary egoism, unreason, and the like leads to criticism and then to the personal demand, the command, and so on. The ought arises: the "he should", and on the part of the one who experiences and accepts the demand, the "I should". Nor does the father of the house lack motives for an "I should", or before it for self-judgment: his own neglect is felt by him, and a mistaken order harms his family and himself. The whole action, in which he as leader was objective to himself, and is so in memory, is blameworthy, and he as its author. He resolves on other, better orders, which have the character not only of being better but of being ought. Every member of the family is a responsible subject, a subject that has a generally circumscribed I-ought and, in the particular case, a determinate one falling within this generality. This lasting (habitual) I-ought here designates the naturally grown duty, and it has a general content that circumscribes in general what is dutiful, as the sense-content of the duty, as in the particular case the determinate particular duty. When we say that father, mother, and so on have such and such functions, we take the family as a personal connection carrying within it a communal value as goal, a communal end that is an end for each family member, each also having its particular ends included in the whole of the total end. Each member has the function of helping to realize the family's end by realizing its particular ends, and to that extent it has a function, it is the subject of an end in question. The ends are of course circumscribed in general, and this is what the functional expressions father, son, and so on designate. Each has his function and with it his duties, to fulfil his functions, and to do so as perfectly as possible. The ought arises genetically from failure: natural care is first instinctive; neglect provokes criticism; criticism becomes command; command becomes the habitual "I ought", which is duty. Function words (father, son) name roles defined by contribution to a common end, so duty is a role seen from within.

(181) Are function and duty, the ought, essentially different? Function designates the practical determination of the subject, its being directed to an end, under the aspect of a particular end serving a comprehensive end of the whole social association, here the family. The family in and for itself has no function, only perhaps in the life of a people and the like. But in the family the father has the function of head, the wife the function of spouse and mother, and so on. In the expression duty, ought, the passage through the negative resounds: deviation disturbs the concord of wills and conditions the reaction of blame. Function and duty name the same role, but duty carries the memory of possible failure and blame: the ought is function as answerable, which explains why duty has a negative ring.

(181–182) In "artificially" founded communities of coordination and subordination (master and servants, a club, a building company), the functions or duties are taken on voluntarily on the basis of agreement, or, as in slavery, imposed on those subordinated by compulsion. But here too a connection of wills, a unanimity of will, makes the personal bond. It is properly broken when the members refuse to fulfil their duty (whether well or badly, gladly or reluctantly and with negligence). The runaway slave is no longer really a slave (setting aside the legal question, which does not yet arise here). A slave revolt breaks the relation of master and slave. Compulsion, the suppression of the revolt, restores it, and the expression fits insofar as the new relation is conscious as the continuation of the old. The slave acknowledges the ought, and acknowledges it retroactively for the time in which he had broken it. Even slavery is analysed as a will-connection, real only while the slave's will complies; revolt dissolves it, and suppression restores it only if the slave again "acknowledges" the ought. The claim that the subdued slave acknowledges the ought retroactively shows the limit of the will-analysis: it describes the social relation as the participants constitute it, and leaves the question of right explicitly aside.

(182) There are changes in naturally grown personal communities (or personalities of higher order) through partial transformation into a community of will. In the state there is a community of will with respect to all full citizens. Those not yet entitled to vote, not yet taking part in the functions of the state, form a host of those growing up, who will subordinate themselves freely when they reach the appropriate maturity and so belong to the community of will. But they cannot cooperate by their will in the constitution of the state or its change. Husserl judges this insufficient and in need of separate consideration. The state is a partly voluntary community: full citizens as willing members, minors as members by anticipation; Husserl's doubt concerns how those without a voice belong to a community defined by will.

(182) Husserl refers to Tönnies on the concepts Gemeinschaft and Gesellschaft: Tönnies does not take Gemeinschaft as a community of will (will understood in Husserl's pregnant sense). Linguistic community, family community, marital community, national community. A linguistic community is no personal connection that creates a personal whole, but a marriage is, even a "modern" marriage. A state is a personal whole, although not everyone knows everyone, as is already the case in a larger association. The way a personal connection is established must, of course, start from actual empathy and actual agreement, or from a subordination that grows naturally but is founded in the status of personal contact or communication. But then the foundations of personal unions along mediate paths must be considered, in which the persons remain "unknown". They are at any rate communities of will of determinate persons, who therefore, as subjects of will, are in agreement, even if mediately. Husserl reverses Tönnies's criterion: for Tönnies Gemeinschaft is organic and Gesellschaft is willed (Kürwille); for Husserl the personal whole of higher order is defined by will in his sense, and a linguistic community, though "organic", is not a personal whole at all. The state is a personal whole because its members are in mediate agreement, which requires a theory of mediated social acts among strangers.

(182–183) A linguistic community is not a personal connection into a whole based on the unity of a purpose set at will or arising from "natural conditions", a whole that has received binding from the unity of a purpose and the coordination of function and duty. Likewise a community of estate, the community produced by the unity of a custom, a form of education, and so on. Finally the unity of a people, of a race, and so on. If the people is a state-people, the unity of the state is still distinct from the unity of the forms of custom and the like that constitute the community. Race is counted here insofar as the commonality of external physical habitus goes hand in hand with such characteristics of community; otherwise it does not belong here. Communities of custom, language, and education are not personal wholes because they lack a common end with functions and duties. Race enters the classification only as a community of custom correlated with appearance; as a merely physical commonality it is excluded from the social sphere altogether, which is the opposite of the biological theory of community then gaining ground.

(183–184) Community does not mean sameness of the kinds and forms of personal actions, of ways of thinking, opinions, scientific activities, and so on; persons stand in community when they stand, in such respects, in the unity of a spiritual nexus of effect, whether or not the effect becomes visible in the particular case. In the widest sense, in the unity of a "tradition", in a personal whole, a personality of higher order, I acts on Thou. The will of acting passes through the Thou as personal action, and in the functionary the personal will of all other functionaries acts implicitly. The member of a linguistic community acts through a coined expression that "catches on", far into the linguistic community, but not personally. By contrast the poet turns to his public with his linguistically coined work of art, as the actor does, as the teacher turns to his pupils, the scientist to his fellows. The community of scientific researchers is more than something like a linguistic community. Insofar as researchers think of their co-researchers, indeterminately co-thought as a universe, or possibly of known ones belonging to the present generation, turn to them spiritually and know them as turning to them, they form a personal connection, though not an association with a constitution. Different again is the community between the artist and his public, following or rejecting him, which he has before his mind's eye as an indeterminate generality. Further, the merchant and the craftsman and their actual and possible circle of customers. Here there are evidently personal relations running from ego to ego, but no association, no personality of higher order, a concept that would now have to be determined more closely. The audience of a speaker: most may have strayed in by chance. But as a professional speaker he can also have a more or less regular public, just as the craftsman, the merchant, and so on has his chance buyers and yet a regular clientele, in which the average number of chance buyers, who vary but whose number has its rule under given circumstances, is included. All such things must be thought through. Community is defined by a nexus of effect, not by similarity, and graded by the directness of address: the coiner of a word acts on the language impersonally; the poet, teacher, and scientist address an indeterminate public; the scientific community is a personal connection without a constitution, held together by mutual address across generations. This is Husserl's model of the community of science as a tradition of mutual address, the conception that underlies the Crisis's "community of researchers" and its idea of philosophy as a communal, infinite task.

### Appendix XXIII. The Effect of Ego-Subjects on One Another (Around 1920)

(184) First, carrying out an understanding of the other, I act on him: he is in need, I remove it; he is hungry, and I lay out food for him; he need know nothing of it himself and does not ask. All effect of ego on ego presupposes empathy and takes place constantly within empathy or within the mediate re-understanding that rests on it. Beneficence without address is an effect of ego on ego, made possible by empathy, but it is one-sided: the other need not know.

(184) The following case does not belong here: A is sick, and B, recognizing it, avoids him. That is a special case. I also avoid a nettle; but I understand the other's illness through empathy into his bodiliness and through the knowledge that such illness is transmitted. Yet here there is no effect of the ego on the ego, and only such cases were to be considered. Likewise the effects of animals on one another that are not reciprocal motivation of ego-acts, not the determination of one ego by another. Avoiding contagion treats the other as a natural cause; empathy is used, but the relation is to the body as thing, not to the ego.

(184) But this does belong here: A wants to do B harm, he may conceal it, but B sees through it and flees or takes precautions. A can in turn understand this and act accordingly. A and B need not face each other directly. A can also have indirect indications that B has such and such intentions regarding A. Strategic interaction, each reading and countering the other's intentions, is ego-to-ego, even without address, the domain of game theory before game theory.

(184) Second, suggestion. I do something, and the other imitates it. That can lead to influencing the other's will. I know that the other likes to imitate what I do, and I want it to be so in a particular case; so I do this or that before him. We have two cases of suggestive effect: A does something and B is suggestively influenced by it, perhaps quite unknowingly; or A knows that B (and others in general) let themselves be influenced suggestively (prior to any intention), and this knowledge motivates A's will to influence B's willing and doing in a definite way by means of suggestion. Suggestion is effect through the passive layer of imitation; when exploited deliberately it becomes manipulation, an effect of will on will that bypasses the other's will.

(185) Distinguished from all these cases are the interactions through social acts. I direct my will, my "I will", at the "I will" of the other, so that I ask him, command him, and thereby direct myself at him himself and his doing, in the manner of reciprocal ego-contact. I know not only that he will act so and so, that he will let himself be motivated by me, and not only that I want this (as in the previous case); he also knows that I have this intention and takes this intention up into his will as determining his will. He wants to satisfy my will, and I for my part presuppose and know that he wants this, in the actual act and in habitual decisions of will alike. These, then, are the specifically social acts, which presuppose a mutual understanding of the kind that brings ego into contact with ego. The definition Nr. 9 used: in the social act my intention is known by the other as intention and taken up by him as a reason for his will, and I know that he takes it up. Mutual knowledge and uptake distinguish it from manipulation and strategy.

### Appendix XXIV. Empathy and Sympathy. Sympathy and the Performance of Acts Concordant with Empathized, Remembered, etc. Acts. Empathy and Putting Oneself into Something in Phantasy and in Art (Around 1920, Perhaps Summer Semester 1920)

(185) Hume's theory of sympathy is of course false, Husserl says. One must see that sympathy as feeling-with the empathized feeling (actual pity, shared joy) is essentially akin to feeling-with in a reproductive act of consciousness. I can also "sympathize" with my own past. Hume's sympathy (*Treatise* II.i.11) is a communication of passions by which the idea of another's feeling is enlivened into an impression in me; Husserl rejects the mechanism and replaces it with a structural kinship between empathy and memory.

(185) One could say that normal memory is itself something like sympathy. The memory "it was so" is a reproductively modified perception. We have not merely a phantasy modification, a mere reproduction of the doxa, but at the same time an actual doxa. The reproductively modified doxa is not merely the "revived" one, as possible illusion of memory shows: I believed and still believe, or I now do not believe. In normal memory there lies a co-belief. One could call it the sympathy of belief. The same holds for every position-taking of liking, every desiring and willing, even past action: it still pleases now; I go along with the earlier desiring and having decided, I act along with it, or I do not. Memory contains a present position-taking on the remembered one: I co-believe what I believed, or not. "Sympathy" is generalized to every present endorsement of a presentified position-taking, my own past or another's.

(185) So too I sympathize with others, since empathy is a modification of memory. The analogy of Nr. 1 and Appendix XVI, empathy as a presentification like memory, now grounds a theory of co-performance.

(185–186) First, though: when I reflect in the midst of my present actual life and consider it, asking whether I value rightly, judge rightly, act or am about to act rightly, and likewise when I reflect on the past and ask how its right stands, it is a matter of bringing in counter-motives that might possibly strike it out. Self-examination is the same structure as co-performance: endorsing or striking out a position-taking by bringing in motives.

(186) Regarding the other, I try the hypothesis that empathizing is something like remembering oneself into the other, and accordingly a sympathizing would take place just as in memory in the ordinary sense. But is the situation really the same on both sides? In memory the present position-taking is a continuation, a prolongation, of my own old position-taking that has remained habitual in me; in empathy there can be no question of that. One would like to say that a certain mediacy lies in empathy, a presentification through indication. The analogy breaks at habituality: my present endorsement of a past belief continues a habit that is mine; my endorsement of another's belief continues nothing of mine, so empathy is mediated by indication, not continuity.

(186) How then does sympathy come about, the going along or not going along with acts? Through "putting oneself into", which, however, belongs to every empathy; empathy itself is putting oneself into. It is not that another is first there for me and I then put myself into him. What is so called is only a deeper living oneself into him, empathizing. The apperception "foreign human being" has its support in my self-perception; it is an analogical apperception, and since I am always there, I am always in actual coincidence of similarity with the other, who is there for me only through analogizing. Greater precision is of course needed here. The other is constituted as a reproductive variation of me, while I am at the same time myself there and present. To understand the other, to represent and have him more or less clearly, is as it were to represent, feel, and will so and so. Against Lipps and the common view, there is no first perceiving the other and then transposing oneself: transposition is constitutive of perceiving him, and what is called "putting oneself in" is only its deepening. The other as "reproductive variation of me, while I am there" is the formula of Nr. 7–8.

(186–187) It is thus similar to re-imagining myself in phantasy, as if I perceived, represented, thought this and that and valued so, and so on. The mere phantasy-perceiving cannot be struck out. But if I place myself on its ground, a judgment, a feeling, a will "in phantasy" can be taken up with sympathy or antipathy, as with fairy tales, novellas, works of visual art. Here I am in the attitude of as-if. How is the phenomenon to be interpreted? As it were I live in the other, but now I judge, value, act "as it were" so and so; yet I, the actually empathizing one, have my actual motivation and possibly do not go along, or do go along. If I imagine myself into a phantasy situation, I know myself as ego there; here I have in fact a memory as-if. I, the actual ego, presentify myself in a presentified surrounding world, and discrepancies arise. I, the actual ego, have my actual past, my acquired peculiarities, my convictions, my habitual valuations, and so on, and if I think myself into a phantasy situation, that includes a positing, perhaps with an actual primal doxa, as when I transpose myself to England. There I have an "assumption", and "thereupon" I do this or that, I with my habitual stock of motivation. Everything fits under the assumption. But I can also imagine otherwise, and then I have discord. In these cases free phantasy will therefore necessarily run in accordance with or against my habitual ego. Things are of course much more complicated. The world into which I imagine myself is also an imagined world and presupposes my habituality. But I can live so one-sidedly in one stratum of it that the phantasy violates certain experiences and experiential convictions of a theoretical kind, and that can make itself felt to me, the actual ego, more or less sharply. I do not go along there; I react inwardly with "nonsense", "that is impossible". So in the stratum of imagined nature. Then, however, comes the stratum of act-spirituality, of valuation, of rational actions. Fiction is analysed by the same structure: within the as-if, my actual ego with its habits endorses or rejects what is imagined, and discord is felt as "nonsense". The stratification (imagined nature, then values and actions) explains why we accept impossible physics in a fairy tale but balk at a character's unmotivated choice.

(187) In phantasy in which I playfully imagine myself into phantasy worlds or situations and then act "dreamily", in this "memory as-if", letting myself go, letting it please me, and dreamily doing in phantasy all sorts of fitting things in the intuitive domain, no conflict will in general arise. Accordingly I probably have no "sympathy" here; but in the contrary case, when I deliberately imagine against these subjective possibilities, I have a striking out, a corresponding conflict. A distinguishable case is dreaming, where dream images arise in passivity and I really value and act so and so, feeling the "unfitting" only afterward. Daydreaming, deliberate counter-fiction, and dreaming are distinguished: in the dream I really value and act, without the as-if, and only afterwards notice the incongruity.

(187) Art: by the means of art, indications are created that draw me into a phantasizing as something demanded, and while this is accomplished, and a representing, valuing, doing as-if is carried out in accordance with the demand, what suits me or does not suit me comes forward. And I, who coincide in a certain way with the other ego, sympathize, as I reproduce intuitively what is first indicated and grasped as symbolically indicated, but so that the demanded position-taking, and so what is concretely demanded in the foreign inner life, gains such intuitive deepening that it really becomes one with the ego, appears as springing from it. Only then is it for me an intuitive and given possibility. This says only that only what is my own possibility for me, who have a foreign ego represented in empathy but am eo ipso in coincidence with it, creates a concordant coincidence with it, a co-positing, co-judging, co-valuing, co-desiring, and so on, and correspondingly a common surrounding world endowed with like spiritual characters. In the contrary case "I do not agree"; there is a conflict between how he takes positions, how he perceives, judges, values, and so on, and how I "would judge in his place". But with that I do not yet have sympathy. The artwork is a set of indications that "demand" a phantasy of me, and identification with a character succeeds only when his position-taking deepens until it seems to spring from me, a possibility of my own. Co-performance is therefore limited by my own possibilities, the limit of variation in Nr. 2; but even complete co-performance is not yet sympathy.

(187–188) Shared joy with the other is not "I would rejoice if I were the other, or if I think myself in his place" (and not, as in the contrary case, that I would behave quite differently, grieve), but I really rejoice. In pity I really suffer. Is it then wrong to relate sympathy so directly to memory? In memory of the past I judge, value, and will along with it and in doing so have, as actual ego, an actual position-taking. That seemed to be what was common. But in empathy (we always take fully intuitive empathy, which incidentally is probably not necessary), as in a hypothetical putting of oneself into a possible situation, an actual position-taking is in a certain way also contained, and indeed as mine, the empathizing subject's. There lies in it a co-doing, a co-living in perceiving, thinking, feeling, deciding. It is not, of course, as if I put myself hypothetically into such a situation and then acted, so to speak in the apodosis (which of course is no inference; no representation of a "necessary consequence" is present here). But it lies in the essence of the situation of empathy that, while I posit the other according to the indications as perceiving, feeling, and so on, I myself, in presentifyingly performing that "I perceive", "I value", am in coincidence with the other ego and as actual ego have a commonly given ground. Sympathy is real feeling, not hypothetical feeling; the task is to locate the real position-taking. Empathy already contains a co-living that is not an inference from "if I were there", but a coinciding on a common ground.

(188) I already have the actual positing of the other's body in the peculiar mediacy that the positing of my body is constantly involved in it, in a peculiar "coincidence" with the foreign body. It is the same with my surrounding world of things and the other's, which is the same as mine, only seen from there, my body as it were coinciding with the one there and my ego, coinciding with the one belonging to the body there, seeing this and that from there and as it were with the foreign bodily organ; or, what is "contained" in it, what can be said evidently on the basis of the situation: he sees from there and has such and such aspects; I would have just the same from there. That gives the common "ground", namely for the other's act-life and for me. The passive surrounding world is one and the same, but the ego is not the same. Still, in empathy I coincide as ego with the other ego, and in his being affected and being active I, coinciding with him, am as it were affected and active. I am "really" co-active when the foreign acts run as my own prefigurations of motivation would permit, when they run just as I would really act on the ground, and in the situation changed from my actual one, from there and under certain presuppositions that just this situation brings with it (he is attacked, and I am not, and so on). I can say that the other is determined for me only by what I can find as common. Knowledge must be excluded, at least in a first consideration. The common ground is the one passive world seen from two places; the ego differs. My co-activity is real when the other's acts follow my own motivational possibilities from his position, so I understand only what I find common, the limit of empathy that Nr. 6 drew for animals.

(189) I do not have a hypothesis, as when I arbitrarily give myself a fictive ground. But, as in art, I have a ground given through indication, on which I place myself. In art I have an artistic demand on me, issuing from the work, which I accept; I thereby have a ground that I allow myself and that from then on is "there" for me, that I hold fast and shape further according to the demands (indications) of the work; and as soon as persons appear in it, I am in coincidence with these "assumed" ones, and now I agree with them or not. Either I have unity: I as actual ego, "transposed" onto this ground, go along, though as transposed ego I am of course not the actual one. But to this belongs the essential turn: I would behave so and so, I could behave similarly, I could think so there, and so on. On the other hand there belongs to it: I can approve such behaviour. In the opposite case I experience, as transposed ego, the discord, the no; I can say as present ego: I would not do that. I can also deliberate and clarify and only then as it were do, go along or not. These are thus phenomena of approval and possibly of approving judgment, evaluation, volition, in higher acts of reason. That yields no sympathy. Empathy's ground is given by indication, like the work's demand, not chosen like a hypothesis. Approval of the other's conduct ("I would do the same") is a rational judgment on a transposed ego's behaviour; it is still not sympathy, which is a feeling of my own.

(189–190) How does it come about, then, that I not only judge, suffer, value, and will along with the other while transposed into him, but judge, feel, and will myself as actual ego (in my situation, on my ground), and in the same sense as the other? What he judges I now judge too; I go along with judgments about his surroundings, which are also mine. He suffers from what has happened to him; I suffer not only in the modified form that I would suffer in the other's place, but I now suffer myself: I am sorry that this and that happens to the other. He is saddened that his mother has died, that his fortune is lost, and so on, and I am saddened by it too. But I am also saddened that he is saddened. What is primary here? Is his grieving, the fact that he grieves, the object of my grief, or is what is grievous for him primarily grievous for me? When I judge as he judges, judging "in" the other passes over into my own judging about the same state of affairs. That is an essential conversion. It lies in the situation of empathy, in the case of concordance, that if I were in his situation I would judge so. But his situation, insofar as the common surrounding world is the basis, is one of those situations possible for me that the sense of the judgment "it is so" includes in all cases where I do not make an immediate judgment of perception but a judgment on the basis of mediacies such as intuitive indications, in which it is implied that if I went there, as I can, I could convince myself of it in a judgment of perception. Here, then, the conversion can occur at any time to my own judgment about the same state of affairs that the other judged, a judgment I had gone along with empathically and "implicitly" made along with him. For judgment the passage from co-judging to judging is direct, because the other's standpoint is one of my possible standpoints on the same world: his evidence is evidence I could have by going there. The question about grief, whether its object is the other's grieving or the loss, is resolved below.

(190) It cannot be quite similar in the case of will. I cannot turn the other's decision into one of my own, however much I may go along with it in empathy. What stands before him as willed practically can, of course, now stand before me too, and by a similar conversion an actual "this should be" and "I will do it" can arise for me from the empathized one. But if he has taken hold, that in general excludes my taking hold, and this produces a peculiar discord that has nothing to do with disapproval. Perhaps, if he wants to push a rock, I can join in, though that already brings in a new complication. In his effort to carry out a proof I can have a concordant will; there it is not disturbed, and my intervention does not necessarily have the character of helping him. Will differs from judgment: a decision is someone's, and the deed excludes a second agent of the same deed. Proving a theorem is an exception, since its achievement is ideal and can be shared without rivalry.

(190) In helping lies: I will that his "will be done", that his will reach its goal in action; and to this of course belongs my action, which, wanting to realize the same thing, lends a binding force to the achievement, by which the achievement becomes a common one in which each one's will "is done". Helping is defined as willing that the other's will be done: my will is directed at his will's success, and the joint achievement fulfils both, the structure of joint intention in Nr. 9 seen from the helper's side. The echo of the Lord's Prayer ("thy will be done") is deliberate.

(190) It is similar to judging with the valuing of something objectively valuable and valued. But there is also something of value for me that is not of value without further ado for the other, something agreeable or pleasing to me that is not so for the other. What I enjoy and what is used up in enjoyment is not enjoyable or agreeable for anyone else. The possession I have, as long as I have it, cannot be another's, and so its value for me is not an actual value for the other. Values divide into objective ones, shareable like judgments, and subjective-possessive ones, which are mine and cannot be co-valued by conversion: sympathy with such goods must take another form.

(190) Grief over the death of my mother is not for the other the same, grief over the death of my mother, a grief of the same content, possible independently in each of us like the same judgment about the same state of affairs, say even a mathematical one (at any rate an objective one), where I can reach the same judgment by way of empathy but my judgment would also be possible without empathy. If, on the basis of empathy with the other's grief over his mother's death, I now find it sad myself that she is dead, this grief of mine has an intentional and essential relation to the other's grieving, and I take part in it; that is, I am sad that he grieves and that he has suffered the loss, and for that reason I also grieve over the death of his mother. The question left open is answered: my grief is primarily grief at his grief, and through it grief at its object. Sympathetic grief is founded on the other's grief, which distinguishes it from shared objective judgment.

(190–191) If I grasp through empathy that a higher value has fallen to him and become his own, an important insight, say, or something aesthetically beautiful, I may perhaps appropriate exactly the same theory to myself, gain an exactly similar subjective realization of the theory in the form of an insight, and likewise an exactly equally perfect "appropriation" of the ideal work of art; but every appropriation is something new, and so is every subjectively appropriated value as such. I can now value the fact that the other has such an appropriated possession, a spiritual good appropriated to him; I can rejoice at it, and grieve that he has lost it. Sympathy is a feeling and valuing directed at such things. In sympathy I do not live in empathy; I do not think, feel, will in the other, and do not merely perform, as an ego coinciding with him, my approvals and disapprovals, namely my thinking-with, judging-with, and so on (possibly my yes and no). Rather, in "sympathy" the other ego and its conduct in acts is the theme of my valuing, my loving, and so on. Here is the proper domain of the concepts love and hate. The appendix ends with a fourfold distinction: empathy (co-living in the other), co-performance (going along on a common ground), approval (judging his conduct as I would), and sympathy (a feeling of mine whose theme is the other and his acts). Love and hate belong to the last, as acts directed at persons, which is the conclusion Scheler reached on other grounds in *Wesen und Formen der Sympathie* (1913, revised 1923).

### No. 10. Communal Spirit II: Personal Unities of Higher Order and Their Correlates of Effect (Bernau, 1918, or St. Märgen, 1921)

#### §1. Communal Acts, Actions in the Social Community

(192) Husserl sets out the themes. First, "spiritual" objects arising from subject-acts, from activities of subjects, their "being", their actuality (cultural objects): as intersubjective, specifically in relation to a given normal human community and a given, identifiable temporal actuality; and as actuality of reason. The human sciences (Geisteswissenschaften) as sciences of spiritual objects in general. Cultural objects have two modes of being: actual for a given community, and valid for reason. The human sciences are defined by their objects, the products of acts, following Dilthey and Ideen II's "world of spirit".

(192) Second, the subjects themselves and the social unities of subjects of higher level. Subjects of higher order are the second theme, correlated with their products.

(192) The parallel constitution of communal subjects and communal achievements. The thesis of the text is announced: the communal subject and its works are constituted together, as ego and world are.

(192–193) Communal acts: communal valuations, and before them communal representations and convictions, communal practical oughts or joint decisions, and so on. Every individual subject, as member of a community, has its representations, its convictions, its valuations and willings (decisions), its actions. But in the communal context I have convictions (as already before representations) as lasting ones, arising from my experience and possibly from the mediated experience of others, which I can at the same time grasp as convictions of others: that is, they too have, as I know, convictions (arising in themselves, or transmitted, possibly first transmitted by me to them) that agree with mine. They mean and believe the same, and conversely they know that this is also the case for me (with respect to definite contents). We know one another mutually as "judging" the same (in the lasting sense). Likewise with valuations. And again there is a possible realm of common lasting needs (wishes, desires), lasting strivings and decisions, common and conscious to us as common. These have a special position, and we shall have to speak of them more closely. At any rate there is a communal conviction, a communal esteem, a communal decision, a communal action. A communal conviction is not mere agreement but mutually known agreement: each believes, knows the others believe, and knows they know he believes. This is the iterated mutual knowledge that later philosophy calls "common knowledge", here as the condition of communal acts.

(193) As regards action in particular, my will can be directed at the other's willing, whether I have commanded him and regard him as under my command, or have determined him by my will in another way to do something that is practically willed by me. His deed is then mediately also my deed; and if the relation is reciprocal, my deed and his activity are at once a complex deed for me, only part of which was done or to be done immediately by him and part by me. The whole action and achievement is my action and also his action in a higher, founded sense, while each acts immediately on the matter in "his part" and performs a primary action that is exclusively his own, but which is part of the secondary, founded action that is the full action of each of us. So with all communal works. The distinction between primary action (my part) and founded action (the whole) gives a precise sense to "our" deed: each does only his part, yet the whole is fully the deed of each.

(193) In other communal "achievements" the conscious "common" will is lacking, as in language, in science, and to the widest extent also in law, and so on. Then I may take up the other's achievements as a starting point for achievements of my own built on them, and he likewise. It is then similar to the way in my individual consciousness I take over an achievement I had performed as finished and use it as starting point for higher achievements, without having to think back to my earlier achieving and explicate its constitutive sense to myself. Language, science, and law are achievements without a common will: each takes over others' results as finished, as I take over my own earlier results without reactivating them. This is the theory of sedimentation that "The Origin of Geometry" makes the problem of the Crisis: tradition transmits sense without its originating evidence, and its reactivation is a task.

#### §2. The Constitution of Personal Unities of Higher Level

(194) In communication "we" all touch one another; we form a personal unity of higher level. In what sense? The question is whether "we" is a real subject or a façon de parler.

(194) Every person, once it has apperceived itself as communicating with other persons and in a special way as member of an indeterminately open community of communication, has as its field of will and of achievement generally (in the field of its remote spiritual effects reaching beyond its goals of will) not merely its individual surroundings, the things given to it, its body, its acts springing from its ego and its possible acts as belonging to its horizon of effect; and it has not merely its own personality, its own dispositions, abilities, and so on, before its eyes as standing at its service; it also has the foreign surroundings, the foreign psychophysical acts and possibilities of acts, the foreign personalities, and so on. The communicating person's practical field includes others' powers: I can do through others what I cannot do myself, so the field of my will extends over theirs.

(194) Every personal subject acts not only immediately and originally in the form of its own self-lived intentionalities, in the form of strivings and willings that spring from it, but in communication it acts through the foreign ego of consciousness in the form of the foreign strivings and willings. Correlatively it knows itself, and also vice versa, as medium of every foreign personal willing and intending in general. It is a personal nexus founded through empathy, in which (in every genuinely social respect, constituting a stratum of its own sociality) "one conviction", "one valuation", "one will" lives, with all their presuppositions of unity of an analogous kind. And as correlates we have the unity of "one" achievement, "one" work, possibly a unity extending and developing through the open infinity of temporal extension: the unity of a state, a religion, a language, a literature, an art, and so on; with limited socialities such as clubs we have the unity of a club's goal and of each club achievement, which takes its place in the system of successive achievements motivated by the unity of the goal. Each person is both agent through others and "medium" for others' agency; the result is a single will living in many, correlated with single works, a state or a language, that extend through indefinite time.

(194–195) The following difference is important. First, I act through a foreign will in the sense that my goal of will lies in the other's goal of will, that I want to reach my goal through his willing and acting (personal connections in the unity of a communal will). Second, I act through the foreign willing in the sense that what is produced, say a technical work carried out according to my idea, becomes a starting point for the spiritual action of others. It is imitated by others, and a type of acting and working spreads in the culture, or it is then improved, and so it goes on. My spiritual effect propagates itself, without my intention, into unknown persons and surroundings, which need know nothing of me (personal communities of effect without the unity of an encompassing communal willing and acting). Two forms of collective unity are separated: communities of will (association, state), where my goal lies in yours; and communities of effect (culture, technology), where my work spreads by imitation and improvement without anyone's common intention. Tönnies's pair is reworked as will versus diffusion.

(195) These are peculiar formations of unity. The unity of a culture of effect is neither a collective subject nor a mere aggregate, which leaves its ontological status open.

#### §3. The Habitual in the Isolated Subject and the Personal

(195) In the isolated subject, formations of unity come first: over against single acts of conviction following one another in manifold ways, the unity "my conviction", which I have lastingly even when I perform no act of that particular content. Every new act of this content is grasped as a new case in which my conviction becomes actual; and every possible act, when it is considered (when I put myself into a situation in which I would judge so), is recognized in the same style as a possible or hypothetical actualization. Likewise my "esteem", my striving, my will. "Disposition" is not a usable expression for this, nor is "lasting habitus", for these words express differently directed, though correlative, apperceptions. Insofar as I have a "firm conviction", I also have a lasting property of the ego, a relatively lasting hexis, from which acts of this content habitually arise under suitable circumstances. But the conviction I have; I am not it, and I am not in it as I am in my "properties". A conviction is an identical unity of sense across its actualizations, something I have; the hexis from which it arises is a property I am. Husserl separates the noematic unity of a position-taking from the egoic habitus, a distinction that the Cartesian Meditations (§32) compresses into "abiding convictions" as properties of the ego.

(195–196) In the sequence of my single achievements I perform total achievements. Every achievement is a deed, possibly an external work; but even when we say it is past, that I have done and achieved it, each remains mine: an achievement, deed, work fixed in the form of temporal objectivity, a chain, a system, a more or less coherent sequence of objectivities that have their belonging to a subject. They are all my practical world of deeds, worked out of a constantly belonging horizon of practical possibilities. Underlying this is my intuitive world, constituted passively in me and for me, objective as a world of things, as mere nature, but nature for me, my solipsistic surrounding nature. A person has a world of deeds as its own correlate: past achievements remain "mine" as objective products, built on the passively constituted nature.

(196) I cannot be a human being, cannot be a person, if I follow every stimulus blindly, if I merely react instinctively along the same lines, if I am merely the subject of psychophysical dispositions that direct me passively in the same types of reaction. I can be a person only insofar as I have not merely lasting apperceptions, and through them a world that stands firm and faces me as ego-alien, but lasting "convictions", self-acquired, gained by my own activity, through active position-takings from the ego; lasting valuations, lasting will, lasting in the sense that this identical element is constituted for me myself: this conviction, for example, that the idea of the German imposes duties on me, that I must satisfy it, this lasting will, this firm direction of will, really to satisfy it, and so on. But in small matters this guides us in every personal sphere, however slight, insofar as we act personally. Not from outside but from within, from the ego, "the" conviction, "the" will is a unity, a given and leading unity, and only through its mediation can personal achievements, actions, works come about. So for an "isolated" subject. Personhood requires self-acquired convictions from active position-taking, not instinct or disposition; this is the doctrine of Nr. 2 and Nr. 3. The example, "the idea of the German imposes duties on me", dates the text, if Bernau 1918 is right, to the last year of the war, when Husserl's Fichte lectures (1917–18, Hua XXV) spoke in the same idiom; the philosophical point is that a person is constituted by commitments he holds as his own.

#### §4. The Constitution of a Common Sensory World

(196–197) In the communicative community of personal subjects we do not have a merely collective plurality of subjects, nor merely a unity in the sense that communication establishes mutual understanding and with it, for each subject, the consciousness of facing indeterminately many other subjects who face it, and of having in common with them one single surrounding world to which they are all related in the "we" and to which they all count themselves, since each belongs for each to what faces him, has his place, his localization in the foreign body facing him. The minimal unity of a community is a shared surrounding world in which all are localized for each other, the result of Nr. 4; Husserl seeks more than this.

(197) That, of course, is the fundamental lower level, which deserves closer discussion. As the individual subject has its surrounding world with open horizons, so a communicating plurality of subjects has a common surrounding world as "theirs". Each individual has his sensibility, his apperceptions and lasting unities; the communicating plurality also has, in a certain way, a sensibility, a lasting apperception, and as correlate a world with a horizon of indeterminacy. I see, I hear, I experience not only with my senses but also with those of the other, and the other experiences not only with his but also with mine; that happens through the transmission of takings-note. And that is not merely an objective manner of speaking but a fact of consciousness, for me and for every other, something constantly effective for me in my conduct, already in the sphere of my passivity, my affection and mere reception. We all orient ourselves by it in our sensuous life; we orient ourselves by "our" experiences, and each not only by his own. Communicative sensibility is real in consciousness: I orient myself by what others have seen, so my world includes their perceptions as mine "through" them. This is the phenomenological basis of testimony as a source of knowledge.

(197) We are many subjects of sense, but as communicating ones all the senses benefit each, and in such a way that each, as a result, has a world facing him that is formed by all these senses, and knows an identical world facing him as the same for all. It is thus in this respect as if there were one subject as correlate of this communal world; the communicating plurality functions analogously to a subject that gains a unity of experience through the individual subjects and their senses. Each individual subject gains it for itself by experiencing through the others and knowing them as experiencing through itself the same world, knowable as the same. The communicating plurality is "as if" one subject with many senses, each member experiencing through the others. Objectivity for all is the correlate of this quasi-subject, which the next sections argue is more than "as if".

#### §5. The Constitution of the Common Personal World and of Communal Achievements. One-Sided and Reciprocal Communicative Relations

(198) Now to the higher level. The preceding paragraph took no account of the sensibility of feeling and drive, or of animal doing, the specifically "animal", the constitution of the world of subjective and communal needs. A level of needs between sense and culture is noted and skipped.

(198) But let us pass to the truly higher level, the level of communal personality and of the social world, the world of personal achievements, culture in the lower and higher sense. Wherever we know ourselves bound in agreement, one-sided or reciprocal, so that a unity of achieving action appears related to the common surrounding world (into which every product of achievement at once takes its place, helping to create ever higher-level surrounding worlds), the individual person does achieve, but it encompasses in consciousness the achieving and the achieved of the other persons. Each achievement enters the common world as a new layer, so the surrounding world is historically stratified by achievements.

(198–199) We have one-sided relations of communication with constitutive function, and reciprocal ones. First, all unity of historical spirit as historical is a one-sided relation. My life and Plato's is one. I continue his life's work; the unity of his achievements is a member of the unity of my achievements; his striving, his willing, his shaping continues in mine. Science as historical unity is the correlate of the unity of achieving that passes through a plurality of persons; the later ones practise empathy into the life of the earlier ones insofar as they re-understand their achievements as achievements, insofar as they understand in terms of the history of ideas what those ultimately aimed at in their theories, what remained open in them and must now be improved, what we descendants want to continue, improve, and bring to completion. Second, every activity of a club, every working together in coexistence, is an example of the constitution of communal achievements in reciprocal comprehension, in which one-sided comprehension may also partly function, as of course in the historical connection the reciprocal comprehension of those working and dealing together at one point in time plays its role. Everywhere here we have a plurality of persons with many personal powers, with many streams of consciousness, many acts of consciousness entering and fitting into them, and yet "one spirit", a personality "of higher level" as ideal bearer of a character, a capacity (the character of a people and so on), with a consciousness that encompasses all the individual consciousnesses in a certain selection. "My life and Plato's is one": the sentence gives the phenomenology of tradition. History of science is one-sided empathy with the dead, re-understanding what they aimed at and continuing it, which makes the historical "community of researchers" of Nr. 9 a single achieving spirit, the conception the Crisis calls the teleological history of philosophy.

(199) Communication creates unity. Separate things remain external to one another; they can lie side by side and touch, but they can never have something identical in common. Consciousness, however, really coincides with consciousness: consciousness re-understanding another consciousness constitutes in itself the same thing that the other constituted; both are one in the same. So already in my own consciousness, where I now remember the past and in the form of memory have a present consciousness that encloses the past one and is conscious of its content, the identical one. Comprehension, this other basic form of presentification, is coincidence of my consciousness with the foreign one; my presentification is in itself as it were a memory of the foreign consciousness, drawing it into itself and having its content as something identical, and exactly as far as it reaches in its clarity, its determinacy, and so on. As in every presentification, there is an element of "as it were" in it; presentification is "modification", this wonderful way in which consciousness mirrors itself in consciousness, finds this mirroring in itself in a conscious way, "coincides" with the mirror image, and thereby has the objectively identical. The windows of monads are given a precise sense: things cannot share an identical part, but consciousness can, through presentification, constitute the same sense as another. "Mirroring" takes over Leibniz's image (monads as living mirrors) and makes it a description of memory and empathy alike.

(199–200) Consciousness thus unites with consciousness, reaching across all time, across time in the form of simultaneity as in the form of succession. Personal consciousness becomes one with another consciousness, necessarily separate from it individually, and so arises the unity of a supra-personal consciousness. Consciousness as act is the act of a person. But the act need not be merely representing; it can also be "achieving" in the sense of acts standing under the idea of active reason. Then, as the intentional essence of acts makes possible, one act enters into others; a living act reaches over other living acts, or acts that were living or will be living, always, however, positing acts as acts consciously within the overreaching act. My thinking, as actively achieving act, reaches over other thinking, my own as well as foreign. My willing reaches over foreign willing, and my deed will include the foreign deed or be included in it. "Overreaching" (Übergreifen) names the inclusion of acts in acts: my judgment includes the other's as a premise, my will his as a part, which is how supra-personal consciousness is built from personal acts without a supra-personal stream.

(200) When I feel myself into Aristotle, it is the past Aristotle. I can no longer act on him, but his earlier thoughts now act in me; the thought he once produced is the same one I now reproduce, and it now acts on me, motivating further. A co-present human being can experience effects from me, and I from him.
