The past always acts on the present, and the present ego, as now positing an act, acts on the same or another ego of the present or future (as now or in future "living") with respect to later acts and dispositions. The temporality of effect (into the future) is an essential fact; personal effect relates to personality in its most proper continuity of duration, but presupposes the continuity of time, of immanent times and of the world's extension. Influence runs only forward in time: Aristotle acts on me, I cannot act on him. The asymmetry of tradition is grounded in the temporality of effect, which presupposes one continuous world-time, the result of Nr. 4.

#### §6. The Communicative Plurality of Persons as Substrate of Communal Acts or Achievements. Communal Spirit

(200–201) So in the general connection of consciousness, which can be founded through empathy in various ways, extents, and directions, we have a stratum of a general, supra-personal and yet personal achieving consciousness, living in all participating persons, streaming through them or rather streaming out of them and yet through them, as if there were a unity of a person with one consciousness and one personal achieving. The common, the connected personality as "subject" of the common achievement is on the one hand an analogue of an individual subject; on the other hand it is not merely an analogue. It is a connected plurality of persons that has, in its connection, a unity of consciousness (a communicative unity). Within the plurality of wills distributed over the individual persons it has a will constituted identically for them all, which has no other place, no other substrate, than the communicative plurality of persons; and likewise for other "unified", socially constituted acts. Every ego is subject of the action, but each in a function, and so the connected unity of all is the full subject. It is a unified substrate: as the ego, the person, is substrate for its individual acts and lasting acts, so the communicative plurality of persons is substrate: it is not a plurality there but a unity founded in pluralities, and it is substrate for "acts" as single acts and for lasting acts, acts that are themselves constitutive unities of higher level, having their founding lower levels in the corresponding acts of the individual persons. The communal subject is real but founded: its will exists only in the individuals' functions, yet it is one will. Each individual is a partial subject, a functionary; the whole is the "full subject". Husserl thus avoids both a group mind with its own stream (which his monadology forbids) and methodological individualism (which would deny the unity).

(201) It is therefore no mere analogy when we speak of a communal spirit, for example, no mere image, any more than when we speak correlatively of a formation such as language or custom and the like. A faculty has convictions, wishes, decisions of will; it performs actions, as does a club, a people, a state. And we can speak in a strict, though correspondingly higher-level, sense of capacity, character, disposition, and so on. "A faculty has convictions" (the faculty of a university, Husserl's own milieu) is meant literally: collective predicates are not metaphors but higher-level predicates of a founded subject.

(201) But a distinction must be drawn between personalities of higher order, genuine personal associations, and merely communicative communities, communities of effect. A language does not arise the way a constitution does in a parliamentary state. The distinction of §2 is reaffirmed: a personality of higher order requires founding by will, which a language lacks.

(201–202) Physical nature is constituted through the reciprocal "overreaching" of the personal subjects of a communicating community. In my manifold appearances the same thing, now appearing so, now so, is constituted as a unity. The appearances pass, and yet those that have passed "achieve"; they are gone, and yet they are still living and constitutively effective. The subject as sensuous subject reaches over present and past. It is not as in nature, where past states of things are nothing and only the present is real and really "achieves". That is still more important, however, in other respects for the subject in general. My past decisions become alive again reproductively and act now anew, and immediately. It is not as in nature, where there is continuous temporal effect and each moment carries within it the sediment of the effect of the preceding ones, each temporal "moment" alone being real and containing all causality within itself. Every conception of the soul that deals in dispositions and lets only the momentary present be effective and real falsifies the basic fact that through the medium of reproduction the past itself acts in the present. Every decision acts further into the future: not in the sense that it awakens in the "soul" something continuously changing, at the end of which the future effect appears, as in nature, but in the sense that the will posited for the "future" becomes present and realizes itself. The past ego exercises action at a distance on itself. This is a decisive argument against dispositional psychology: in nature only the present is real and carries the past as a trace; in mind the past itself acts again through memory, and a decision acts on the future directly, as "action at a distance on itself". Motivation is thus not causation by stored traces, a thesis close to Bergson's against associationism, grounded here in the structure of presentification.

(202) In my appearances the sensuous world is constituted as my counterpart for me, and in a certain way in me, the world that is mine. But the other reaches over my sensibility and his own, and in this overreaching the one world is there in consciousness, the one for both of us, and correlate of the "both", finally correlate of an open plurality. And so it goes on with all objectities. The objective world is constituted by overreaching: each subject includes others' sensibility in his own, so the one world is the correlate of the "both" and then of open plurality.

(202) Subjectivities too are constituted as unities of various levels; here too we have, but in quite another sense, manifolds and unities: the lasting subject of lasting convictions, and also of lasting apperceptions (of the one objective world), lasting valuations, needs, willings. The manifold here is the single experiences flowing into one another, the pulses of life; but these settle down as temporalities, stand under the law of immanent temporality, the law of entering immanent "associative" interweavings, of forming apperceptions, of creating, before all reflection, unity and starting points for possible affection (conditions of coherent personal ego-activity and personal development). Subjects are constituted unities, as things are, but their manifold is the flow of life-pulses sedimenting associatively, which gives the person a genesis rather than an appearing.

(202–203) If we then consider that persons touch one another in agreement and unite, those united are still individual persons, but united ones, there for one another, each conscious of itself as united with others and encompassed by them. In the consciousness of each person, it itself is a unity and each other person is a unity and a person comprehensively united with it; and in the consciousness of each person the foreign consciousness is connected with its own connection of consciousness constituted as a stream, and united into a common stream belonging to the united person. Further: in consciousness there belongs to this stream a character, an individual nature, a developing group of lasting acts, of common achievements, of communal works, and so on. The communal stream exists only in each member's consciousness, as each one's awareness of being united; the united person has a character and a history of works, but its stream is the connection of the individual streams as each represents it.

(203) The communal person, the communal spirituality (if one prefers to narrow the word person in one sense or another), is really and truly personal. There is a higher essential concept that binds the individual person and the communal person; there is analogy, just as there is analogy between a cell and an organism built of cells, not a mere image but a community of genus. The cell and organism comparison makes "person" a genus with two species, individual and communal, so the claim is generic identity, not metaphor; the organic image, however, sits uneasily with the stress on will, and Husserl immediately tests it on the people.

(203) With a people one will indeed speak of the unity of a pervasive consciousness; but can one speak of a (lasting) unity of communal action encompassing and connecting all ego-subjects? Yet it must be asked whether the unity of a people is not after all an analogue of personality. For example, the people flames up in passion and seeks forms of practical unity when a group of the people is oppressed for the sake of its language, and so on. A people is a community of effect that can become a will-community in crisis; the example of a linguistic minority oppressed (the national conflicts of 1918–21 in Central Europe) shows how a language community acquires a will.

#### §7. The Constitution of Subjectivity

(203–204) In the constitutive sense of all life, in which the origin of all being lies, we find that subjectivities and objectivities are constituted in parallel, and that subjectivities are constitutive unities just as much as their objectivities. The wonder of subjectivity, that it can become an "object", or, as Husserl prefers to say, a Gegenstand, shows itself in the fact that, subjectively and ultimately in pure consciousness, two streams of constitutive formations can be separated, standing to each other as subject and object at different levels, and that a definite concept of "object" is thereby marked out. The objects here are, first, things (Sachen), that is, impressional objects, but objects stemming from personal achievements; and over against them, second, subjects themselves as correlates of all things. Subjects in turn are Gegenstände insofar as they are, logically speaking, subjects of predicates characterizing them, but again so that all specifically personal properties, though lasting, are only relatively lasting. Persons are unities of development infinite in principle; they are not absolutely persisting substances such as must be ascribed to natures in the sphere of things, which is why there can be no "mathematical natural science" of persons. But persons necessarily become, for persons, objects of their surroundings, both individual persons and all constituted personalities of higher level. Necessarily the subjects of all achieving are also possible themes for achievements, for possible transformations, whereby the in itself radical distinction between person and objectivity as object is relativized. In the wider sense persons too are, and become, objects for personal achievements. Persons are constituted in parallel with things but are not substances: their properties are only relatively lasting, so persons admit no mathematical science, the thesis of the 1911 "Philosophy as Rigorous Science" against naturalistic psychology. Persons become objects of others' achievements (education, government, persuasion), which "relativizes" the distinction of subject and object without collapsing it.

(204) In what does the "constitution" of subjectivity consist? Together with the formation of the unity of immanent time, a lasting surrounding world of things and lasting position-takings toward it are constituted. The ego acquires lasting peculiarities in relation to what is pregiven; it develops for itself and develops in community with others. In relative passivity communities are constituted that presuppose the individual subjects as lastingly constituted and developing, but that also determine them in their development. In pure activity of the subjects involved, clubs and other self-conscious personalities of higher order, posited by themselves, are constituted; highest of all, the idea of an "ethical" humanity as against a mere community of effect. So too the ideal of reason for an individual human being is the human being who wills and acts himself as a subject of reason. Likewise a community of subjects who posit themselves as subjects of reason, not in isolation, but who, in a universal will, posit, strive for, and reason one another as such subjects. The constitution of subjectivity has three levels: passively grown communities that shape their members; actively founded associations; and at the top the idea of an ethical humanity, a community of rational subjects who will one another as rational. This is the ideal of the Kaizo articles (Hua XXVII), a community of "renewal" modelled on Kant's kingdom of ends but founded on mutual will rather than on law.

### Appendix XXV. Communalized Humanity as Supra-Personal Whole (1921/1922)

(205) Culture. Communalized humanity is a concrete historical reality, a living, spiritual reality, a whole of life formed out of persons through the looser or closer communalization of their personal life; a supra-personal whole that is a supra-personal spiritual individuality, or rather reality, and that can be more or less analogous to an individual personality. At any rate, as an individual subject has its psychic functions, more precisely its personal functions (cogito) of I-represent, I-think, I-feel, I-will, so does the supra-personal spiritual reality (or supra-personal subjectivity); and in distinguished cases, where one can speak in exact analogy of purposeful willing, acting, creating, we speak of a personality of higher order. Humanity is a real, if supra-personal, subject with cogito-functions; "personality of higher order" is reserved for cases of purposeful collective agency, the will-criterion of Nr. 9–10.

(205) The supra-personal subjectivity, the unity of humanity living on and acting on, resembles a physical organism in its construction from cells, to which we also ascribe the unity of a life in metabolism and so on. A humanity lives in the individual human beings and builds itself up out of them; they would be the cells, the last organically functioning elements. Several human beings communalize temporarily or lastingly and form in this connection a multicellular organ, and so on. The organic unity of humanity maintains itself through the birth of new individual human beings into it and the dying off of others, likewise through the new formation of multicellular organs and their dying off. The organism model of society (Spencer, Schäffle) is taken up for one feature: persistence of the whole through replacement of members, like the metabolism of a body or the rainbow of Appendix XIII.

(205) On the other hand something new of a different kind appears here, because these are persons, subjects of a consciousness and of a conscious self, and of what takes "psychic" shape in conscious life: lasting memories, convictions, habits, definitely formed ways of functioning or of acting, ways of carrying out purposive activities. Every personal subject is permeated by an abundance of spiritual structures, and Husserl means here functional forms that become objective for it as forms, which it itself also takes as theme of intentions, of transformations, and so on; the "method" is itself an object, and is invented, newly shaped, and so on. The disanalogy with cells: persons reflect on their own functional forms and change them deliberately. A method is a habit that has become an object of design, so social structure is reflexively modifiable, which no organism's is.

(205–206) The communal-spiritual is also a communal-spiritual psychic life, with communal-spiritual memories (tradition), communal-spiritual habits, ways of functioning, and so on (likewise called tradition). Language, for example: if one calls it an "organ" of spirit, it is an organ in quite another sense than when we speak of organs of a plant, or of the hand as a part, an organically functioning part, of the whole body. Tradition is communal memory and habit; language is an "organ" only metaphorically, since it is a habit of a communal subject, not a part of a body.

(206) With the title "body for a psychic, and specifically personal, subject", moreover, a new concept of organism enters. It must now be considered that, as the individual human being is the spirit of a body, and this body is a system of available bodily members, accessible to immediate bodily effects, and so represents an immediate system of free effects into the physical world and thereby on other subjects, so too the "human being writ large" has something like bodiliness and a physical surrounding world as a world of bodily mediated effects. Plato's "man writ large" (Republic II, 368d) is reinterpreted: the communal subject has a "body", the organized system of means (institutions, techniques, territory) through which it acts on the world as the individual acts through his limbs.

(206) The human being writ large, humanity as historical reality, or a humanity such as a people, has a spirituality as a really unified soul borne by bodiliness, something of a unified ego-centring or an analogue of it, a unity of communal-spiritual psychic life living through the individual subjects, in that the acts of one subject connect in communal consciousness (empathy) with the acts of another and become, in the form of peculiar social acts, syntheses of subjects as collective subjects of a social consciousness and social thinking and acting connecting several egos. But as far as empathy, being-there-for-one-another, being-there in a common horizon of life, reaches, it holds that no personal doing is really isolated, that it is always at least ready to motivate other subjects, just as every ego is there for every other ego not merely but socially, and all are functional elements of the whole for one another and gain actual and possible social function for one another. Within the reach of empathy every act is potentially social: no deed is isolated, since each can motivate others. The communal soul is the network of such potential motivations, centred in an "analogue" of an ego.

(206) In progressive communalization an ever more highly organized communal soul and communal subjectivity, and possibly personality, is constituted, with an ever richer structure built from personal associations (marriages, families, clubs, and so on) of relatively lasting functionality, functioning in the whole and in an ever higher sense for the whole as "organ". Beside these there are more fleeting connections. In the communal soul (the all-encompassing one) we find "habits", traditions, and so on, lasting forms or ways of active communal life (custom, language, customary law, and so on). An external physical world arises, reshaped by purposeful work and laden with meanings of purpose, things that have significance for all from the communal consciousness, are significant for all, and so on. Institutions are organs of the whole; customs and customary law are its habits; the humanized physical world, laden with purpose, is its body and work.

(206–207) All this is cultural life, for personal life is purposive life, and communal life is purposive life in community and according to communal meanings. Everything that is there for the community and for each individual subject (and association) as member of the community is itself a substrate or motive for purposive action, stands under the power of will, is "cultivated". And that concerns the community itself for itself; it concerns all forms of community, as the styles of active and generally of active life that have come to be; it concerns language and custom; it concerns the systems of communal convictions, religious, ethical, and so on; it concerns the works that are substrates of communal valuation, love, admiration, as models for new works or as motives for forming new works after modified models on the basis of criticism, and so on. Culture is defined as everything under the power of will: even the community's own forms are objects of possible cultivation, the reflexive feature from the cell analogy now generalized, and the root of Husserl's idea of "renewal" as deliberate self-cultivation of a culture.

(207) Culture is a title for the creating life of humanity, objectifying itself in communal achievements. But humanity itself, which is the achieving one, and the whole form of its soul, of its communal subjectivity, and of its progressing life, is itself achievement and, as such, objectifying itself in itself. "History" is the history of humanity or of a humanity (a people, a state, a humanity closed relatively or absolutely by communalization). It is eo ipso also history of culture, just as history of culture is at the same time history of humanity, in full concretion. In speaking of culture, however, the regard is directed at the great systems and connections of human achievement; in speaking of the history of humanity, at the achieving subjectivity. Culture and history are the same process seen from its products and from its subject; humanity is itself an achievement, self-objectifying, which makes the communal subject the product of its own history, the structure of the individual person's self-constitution (Appendix XXVI) at the collective level.

### Appendix XXVI. Communal Spirit II: Cultural and Communal Life (Autumn 1922)

(207) Husserl's summary lists the themes. The spiritual life of humanity is not the whole psychic life (not that of passivity) but goal-directed, purposive life, spiritualizing the world. True being and truth as cultural formations. Personal life in community: self-determination, and socially, I–Thou determination. Social acts, on the basis of the communal relation of experience to the world. Practical agreement, social union. Communities and societies. The unity of communalization. Lasting communal relations. The practical surrounding world; various practical surrounding worlds; practical present, practical future. Earthly humanity as a practical all-community ("human world"). Personal space as form, and personal (historical) time. Within this universe the particular communities, the community of communication (linguistic community). The appendix restates Nr. 9–10 in a more systematic order, adding truth as a cultural formation and the idea of a "human world" with its personal space and historical time.

(207–208) Personal life is ego-life in relation to a world that is conscious, experienced, represented emptily, thought, valued, and treated in work. Through empathy other ego-subjects enter this surrounding world, and indeed as human beings, as bodily-psychic unities, each of which harbours an ego that has a surrounding world as I do, a world common apart from the different egos and egoities, "oriented" differently according to the different egoities, conscious in different modes of givenness, representation, valuation, and so on. But greater precision would be needed here. For we must distinguish nature, as the world of objects unconditionally common to all personal subjects who enter into a connection of understanding with one another, to which belongs everything that I and everyone experience in concordant experience and determine thinkingly in legitimate, self-confirming and confirmable thought; and on the other hand the manifold of bodies that are not merely nature but have "animation". These too are objects, insofar as each can, ideally speaking, be given to each in the identical way in bodily expression and be determined as existing in itself. Every ego finds itself in subjective reflection on itself in original form, every ego every other in the presentifying form of empathy. Each identifies itself as the same one that the other recognizes. The two kinds of objectivity of Nr. 5 are restated: nature, unconditionally common, and animated bodies, objective insofar as identically given in expression. Self-identification with the self the other recognizes is the mechanism of objective subjectivity from Appendix VIII.

(208) This objective world becomes the substrate of spiritual, subjective predicates as a valued, practically shaped world, whereby a new kind of predicates, namely intersubjective predicates, is constituted: intersubjective characters of value, intersubjective characters of purpose (usefulness for everyone), and so on. These predicates, however, do not have the same unconditional objectivity as the predicates of nature, physical and psychophysical. Cultural predicates are intersubjective but not unconditionally objective: usefulness "for everyone" is relative to a community of needs, whereas the predicates of nature hold for any possible observer, the distinction between relative and strict objectivity at the end of Nr. 5.

(208) The spiritual life of humanity is not the whole of psychic life, not the life of merely dull passivity. We have in view specifically the domain of active life, of freely active life, of the individual human being in the connection of community, goal-directed, purposive life, directed at self-set ends. "Spirit" is restricted to active, purposive life, which excludes passivity from the human sciences' object while keeping it as their foundation.

(208) Purposive activity can be directed at physical things and processes. It can be a momentary purposive activity and give the thing a lasting changed shape, which can be understood as suited to the particular purpose of the particular individual, as destined for it, or, after the purpose is fulfilled, as having been destined and as having fulfilled the purpose, pointing back to a past fulfilment (the remains of a fruit). A natural object can be suited to a purpose from the outset and be seen as such; it can be suited lastingly and again and again for a kind of purpose; and it can be suited not only for one subject to which it is related back, but for every subject, or for many subjects motivated by the same needs, in the same way. Likewise a subject can have reshaped an object for its single or repeatable purpose and given it a lasting purposive form suited to repeated needs, and at the same time this form can be serviceable for others and for any subjects driven by the same needs. The core of a cultural object is a sense of purpose legible in its form. The remains of a fruit are a cultural object in the minimal sense, a trace pointing back to a fulfilled purpose, the seed of the theory of the "trace" and of the archaeological reading of artefacts.

(208–209) Objects thus receive a form of value and purpose, a form stemming from the valuing and purpose-setting acts of the individual concerned or of individuals thought along with him, possibly a form they first acquired through a reshaping activity that stamped them as means intentionally shaped for such purposes. Individual predicates of subjective origin thus arise, and also general, empirically general subjective predicates, well distinguished from the predicates that the objects of the surrounding world and common world have in themselves, independently of the valuing and practical position-takings and working achievements of the subjects, independently of intentions subjects have to shape things according to purposes (as means for their fulfilment). Subjective predicates divide into individual ones (my keepsake) and empirically general ones (a hammer's use for anyone with the need), both distinct from natural predicates.

(209) The surrounding world and common world thus contain mere objects and "spiritual" objects, objects with predicates of value and purpose, objects that can also be regarded purely as objects and then belong to the mere world of objects. On the other hand, they are objects in whose objective determinations (in certain of them) a "spiritual" significance expresses itself, including significance of general validity. But objective determinations are not the only bearers of such predicates; so when an object is dear to me for the sake of a "beautiful" mode of appearance in a certain position relative to me, and has possibly been shaped so as to be able to offer this beautiful mode of appearance. Aesthetic value can attach to modes of appearance rather than to objective properties: the beauty of a view belongs to the thing-from-here, which is why art can work by shaping appearances.

#### True Being and Truth as Cultural Formations

(209) Consider active, freely acting subjects. The human being is truly human only as awake, and as such he is constantly affected and active according to his affections. He is active as experiencing, thinking, insightfully knowing, and in being so he strives, values, aims at what is valued, and attains it in realizing doing. What is attained here is true being, what has come to cognition "itself" in cognitive appropriation. But there is also an anticipating attainment, as in deciding on a goal in the form of an uninsightful certainty, or in deciding as determination of a questionable, indeterminate being-so, in attaining this being-so but only as certainty, not as insight. Then, however, the drive can arise toward the final decision, toward grounding cognition, toward the ultimately fulfilling self, true being. In general, systems of the modalities of belief and of the various logical forms of propositions and of cognitive decision in cognizing certainty. And the change of attitude in which the goal is the true probability, and so on. Cognition is treated as purposive activity: truth is the goal of a striving whose achievement is the "self" of the object in evident appropriation. Truth is thus a cultural formation, the product of a practice, which places logic within the theory of culture, the thesis of Formal and Transcendental Logic's "logic as theory of science as achievement".

(209–210) Nature: the existence believed, possibly experienced and cognized in thought, which is now valued in its turn. Joy that it is so; what is so is pleasing as such. Confirmation: intention toward an "it is really so" and its fulfilment (realization of this "it is really"). Intention toward "it is really pleasing". Value has its own confirmation, parallel to the confirmation of being: "it is really pleasing" is an intention that can be fulfilled or disappointed, the axiological analogue of evidence in the ethics lectures.

(210) Is love a joy not merely "on the basis of" or "in" existence, but in "possession"? That would mean: joy in grasping the object "itself". The intention of love would be desire. Desire is desire for possession, striving for possession. A wish is not without further ado a wish for possession but a wish that it be, so more general. But is desiring a food a loving striving, and is enjoying it in eating a loving? The candidate definition of love as joy in possession is tested and found too broad, since appetite would become love.

(210) Love aims at something lasting, at the possession of something I can grasp myself again and again and enjoy valuingly in grasping it. But joy in this ability, in possession, is not love. I can love something I do not possess. Love is a habitual, a lasting conviction of joy. I love something if, whenever I behold this lasting thing itself, I rejoice in it, and as often as I think of it, I long for it, desire it, to rejoice in it, beholding it itself. Love is a habitual "conviction of joy", structurally parallel to a conviction of belief: a lasting position-taking that is actualized in joy at presence and in longing in absence. The analysis of love in Nr. 9, §5 as a community of striving applies to persons; this one applies to any lasting object, including works and ideals.

#### Personal Life in Community

(210) The self-determination of the ego: I in my thinking, feeling (valuing), desiring (striving), willing directed at myself, I as causa sui. Self-determination is the ego's practical relation to itself; "causa sui", the scholastic attribute of God, here names the person as maker of himself, as in Appendix XXII and Nr. 2's conversion.

(210) The I–Thou acts: I as exercising "effects" on the other, the alter ego, I determining him. The I–Thou relation is the social counterpart of self-determination: determining another as I determine myself.

(210) Determining myself, I do not merely represent myself; more distinctly, I do not merely practise self-contemplation, nor merely self-evaluation. In self-evaluation I do not value what I represent or know of, but I value that I think so, that I know, but also that I value so and so, desire this or that, will. So in general I value this: that I perform such and such acts, perform such and such ego cogito, possibly that I take such and such positions, hold this or that to be true or know it as true, value this and that, desire so and so, decide by will for it, but also that I realize such things (or have realized them in the past). Self-evaluation, however, is not only the evaluation of the momentary act; it is evaluation of the ego itself, which manifests itself as the identical person in its acts and proves, in behaving so in its respective acts, how it is. That I once willed or did this or that, indeed only that I took pleasure in indulging in such and such games of phantasy, I may reproach myself for my whole life; it shames me. I who am now am the same, the same person who was earlier; it is the same who proved itself in its past act-life and proves itself in its present acts, for instance also in this act of shame at itself. Shame is the paradigm of self-evaluation: in it the person judges itself as the identical author of past acts, even of imagined pleasures, so self-evaluation discloses personal identity across time as moral continuity. The shame of having merely enjoyed a phantasy carries Appendix XXIV's claim that imagined position-takings manifest the person into ethics.

(210–211) Self-evaluation is thus the evaluation of the ego itself, performed by this same ego in the direction of itself; and in the self-evaluation of an act I have performed lies the evaluation of the ego itself as determining itself (proving itself) in such an act, as how it is. Every evaluation of my act is an evaluation of me, since the act manifests what I am.

(211) But self-determination is not the determination of being, ontic and predicative determination; determination is here a "practical" act. "Determining" myself means making myself so, not describing myself so; the ambiguity of "Bestimmung" (determination as predication and as decision) is resolved in favour of the practical.

(211) I cannot will (nor even strive or try to will) to be another, but I can will to be otherwise, to change myself. In the change I am still the same, the same but possibly a better or a less good one. I wish myself otherwise; I miss a being-so; I value it negatively; I recognize that a being-otherwise is possible, and possible for me; I myself, in my lasting habitualities, am in my power; I am able to do something to myself; I determine myself practically; I become my own work. The limit of self-determination repeats the result of Nr. 7 and Appendix XIX: I can be otherwise, never another. Within that limit the person "becomes his own work", the formula of Husserl's ethics of self-shaping (Hua XXVII, "Renewal as Individual-Ethical Problem").

(211) But this is only a distinguished case of self-determination, the case of "changing oneself". What is self-determination in general? Every case of a will directed at my ego that is founded in an "I direct my regard at myself and evaluate myself". Self-determination in general is any will toward myself founded on self-evaluation, of which self-change is the special case.

(211) What does an I–Thou determination look like? Not every willing and doing directed by one ego at another is an I–Thou determining. Not every representing or thinking directed by one ego at another is an I–Thou cognizing. The I–Thou relation is narrower than any relation of one ego to another, as Nr. 9 had found.

(211) I can see the other bodily, see his eyes in doing so, and yet not "look him in the eyes". I can see the other bodily and re-understand a piece of his inner life in the expression of his bodiliness; I can be directed at him and his acts in doing so, and yet I am not with him and in him in the special sense indicated by "looking the other in the eyes" and turning to "him". An utterance I make so that another may understand it, a written one for example, is not an utterance "turning to him", in which I address myself to him. Seeing eyes is not meeting a gaze; understanding someone is not addressing him. Even a text written to be understood is not, by that alone, addressed; address requires the reciprocal structure that follows.

(211) In an act in which one ego turns to another, what lies at the basis is above all this: I1 grasps I2 empathically, and I2 grasps I1 empathically; but not only that. I1 experiences (understands) I2 as experiencing I1 understandingly, and conversely. I see the other as seeing and understanding me, and it further lies in this that I "know" that the other also, for his part, knows himself seen by me. We understand each other and are spiritually together in mutual understanding, in contact. To look each other in the eyes mutually, to find each other related to one another in perceiving consciousness, to be there originarily for one another and, grasping and attending, to be directed at one another in mutual spiritual contact. The I–Thou is defined by second-order reciprocal empathy: each understands the other as understanding him, and knows the other knows this. The gaze is the original form of this mutual knowledge, the analysis Sartre's look and Levinas's face would later turn in opposite directions.

(211–212) Each of these egos is directed, experiencing, valuing, striving, and willing, at its surrounding world, at this or that single objectivity (things, human beings, animals); each is the subject of a life, a living present that has its horizon of the past and its horizon of the future toward which it lives. Each partner in contact remains the centre of a whole life with its temporal horizons, which the contact does not merge.

(212) When I see the other, I grasp him as subject of his life, which is, however, given to me only empathically, in an imperfect, more or less indeterminate, unintuitive, unclear presentification (everything that is given in original self-givenness as horizons, unclarities, emptinesses enters the presentification in a modification of a new level). The other's horizons are presentified as horizons: his unclarities appear to me as his unclarities, a modification of a "new level" over my own, which explains why the other is always given with more indeterminacy than my own life.

(212) I can feel pleasure or displeasure at the other, in contact with him. We can also touch one another in pleasure. He looks at me with pleasure, I at him; I can wish to awaken in him a lively, deeper, lasting interest in me by presenting myself in certain ways, speaking so, choosing such forms of speech and expression as are likely to arouse his pleasure. Or I can wish to arouse his displeasure or heighten his visible displeasure; I hate him and want to annoy him, humiliate him, and so on. Social life includes self-presentation aimed at the other's regard, Goffman's theme half a century early; hatred, too, is a social act, directed at the other's experience of me.

(212) We are related to a surrounding world actually common to us. He tries to appropriate things for himself and shape them according to his purposes. While he is directed at that, I am with him and help him, before any agreement; I push toward him what he wants so that he can take hold of it immediately; it is in my sphere of immediate reach, for him it is not, or is hard to reach. He acts "in my presence", and I am for him in his presence. We are not merely in the same connection of things at all, in the same "place", but in a "common" present, in an actually common place, a surrounding world common in consciousness; we are its subjects and are constituted together as "we"; and we are there for one another, each as ego, the other as other. The "we two" is given to me in the form "I and he" and given to him in the form I express as "he and I", but which he expresses as "I and he". Spontaneous help, before any agreement, is the simplest communal act; it presupposes a "common present", which is more than being in the same place. The "we" is indexical: each gives it as "I and he", so the first-person perspective is preserved within the shared "we", the structure the Fifth Meditation needed to avoid a supra-individual subject.

(212) If I have consciousness of the other's existence as communicating with me, in original communicative life, I need not be directed attentively at the other; I need not look him in the eyes, nor he me. It suffices that the other is in my surrounding field in consciousness, though unnoticed, and as the one in whose field I am likewise. Attention, as ego-directedness, will, however, necessarily have preceded. But when I reach for something in his presence, I am directed specially at it, while the other is conscious secondarily. Yet he observes this and helps me. When I help another, I strive "with" him in his striving; I "take part" in his action; I give it a piece done from my side; I act in a certain way in one with him, and the end for him, as his goal, is also my goal; my goal is that he reach his goal. Communicative co-presence can be marginal, once established; the communal situation persists in the background. Helping is defined again as willing that the other reach his goal, as in Appendix XXIV.

(213) Progressing so: practical agreement. The wish for help, the request for help. Turning to the other so that he does this and that. A general consideration of the possibilities that arise from my and the other's being in a community of consciousness, in a common present and spatiality, and being so as living, practical subjects. The ascent from spontaneous help to request and agreement follows Nr. 9's order.

(213) In particular, striving for, desiring, wanting to appropriate the same things, which only one subject can have. Things consumed in "having"; things that can be used "again and again", but when one uses them, no other can; things that can be used by several at once, and so on. Relatively lasting social connections of community of purpose and of conflict of purpose (war). Social unions (clubs, companies, trading companies, and so on). The social personality ("we") with purposive valuing, wishing, willing, acting according to the social purpose. The partners (fellows) as coordinated functionaries of the society. Relations of coordination and subordination. Unions according to subordination and superordination. Master and servant, master and slave. Likewise such relations in a community of coordination: one takes over the "leadership" by agreement with the others, in the single case or in general. Consideration of possible relations of this kind. Rivalry is grounded in the exclusivity of goods (consumed, exclusive in use, shareable), which yields community of purpose and its conflict, war, the economic phenomenology of scarcity. Leadership by agreement is the form of authority in a community of equals.

(213) Possible types of community. Possible purposes to be pursued communally. Communities of cognition, economic communities, communities for promoting common religious life. Communities for ordering an intersubjective life (a life of individuals as free as possible, to be ordered in sociable forms). Communities that exclude strife as far as possible and settle strife in an orderly way, so that peaceful life and action are possible for all. The state. The state is defined by its purpose, the exclusion and orderly settlement of strife so that all can live and act in peace: Hobbes's sovereign without the state of nature, a functional definition within a teleology of communities.

(213) Communities that are not societies. The community of scientific researchers of a science. Here there is no unity of will, no obligation assumed by agreement with fellows, no cooperative organization, no social personality (no union of will in a voluntary agreement, no convention). The communal life of language, economic life, and so on. The scientific community is again a community without a social will, as in Nr. 9; economic life is a community of effect, the market as a nexus of mutual effect without a common will.

(213–214) The objectivity of societies (communities) and their mode of givenness for each member. They are only what they are as possibly given in the members, by virtue of the acts constituting sociality and their habitualities. Accordingly every sociality has its original givenness as oriented, from the standpoint of the member concerned. But in representing the community with its members, the member represents every other member along with it as centre of its possible orientation, which, turned back, also intentionally encloses every other as such a centre, and so reciprocally in every respect. And all these modes of givenness, as intersubjectively accessible by putting oneself into the other members, the fellows, are apprehended intersubjectively as "modes of appearance" of the same social whole and stand in relations of identification. The true self, however, is an intentional unity and a true being that must be worked out, but has its infinitely open horizons. Social wholes appear perspectivally, as things do: each member has an oriented view of the community, with himself at the centre, and the community is the identical unity of all these perspectives, an "intentional unity" to be determined in infinite inquiry. This is the phenomenological basis for treating institutions as objects of a science, without reifying them.

(214) Communal consciousness of a communal subjectivity (a communalized one), communal nature, communal bodily-personal beings, human beings and animals attached in the communal nature, the communal objective world. The levels of communal constitution are listed: a communal subject, its nature, its embodied members, its world.

(214–215) The unity of communalization. Every subject is subject of the communal world as an open infinity with openly determinable, indeterminate horizons, a world that in its truth is the same for every subject and knowable to each as the same one that every other subject it finds in its world as surroundings would know. To this world belongs an open infinity of subjects who are subjects of this world. For every subject there are not only other subjects, and it has not only experience, acquaintance, knowledge of them, and not only does its valuing and willing extend to them in their life, their passive and active life; rather, every ego can also enter into communal relation with other subjects as personal ego-subjects in social acts. It can express itself intentionally, speak out, communicate; it can also produce effects in the common world, first of all the physical one, that not only belong as such to the domain of others' possible acquaintance but are understandable by others as effects this subject intended and produced, and produced for the purpose that the other ego (or several other egos) have advantage or disadvantage from them, be furthered or hindered in their purposes. As I address myself to the other in expressing myself for him, so my other effects of this kind, or the activities producing them, address themselves to the other, or to an indeterminately open plurality of others, as when I turn not only "personally" to the other in a letter but write a text so that others may read it on a given occasion (which I reckon with) or may presumably find occasion to read it, and then experience desired effects from it. Or I build a fence around my garden, or a wall, which means that I do so in order that others know that I want to deny others free access to and free use of what is enclosed. So processes produced by subjects in definite ways and with corresponding content, or even lasting things, have for subjects an address that points back to subjects who gave them a certain meaning directed at other subjects, and on the other hand they bear this very meaning, a meaning of purpose, directed at subjects and their conscious life and at the definite or indeterminately general purposive activities guiding these subjects. Objects can carry an "address": the fence says "keep out" to anyone, the book is written to unknown readers. The concept of address extends social acts to things and to indefinite publics, the theory of institutional signs (property marks, public texts) as sedimented social acts.

(215) There thus exist between the subjects of the world manifold social relations of effect that have a relatively lasting character, beside momentary actual relations of immediate intercourse. Social structure is the set of lasting relations of effect, as distinct from encounters.

(215) But not all subjects of the world stand in such relations, though there is a vague possibility of founding relations of intercourse and lasting relations of effect. Every subject has an actual surrounding world, a sphere of natural objects and human beings that it actually experiences in the actual present and that, more precisely, makes up a circle of its actual or possible actual purposive activity. And in addition a practical present in a wider sense and a practical surrounding world, which marks out a circle, a horizon of the present, of practical possibilities: the totality of what I can influence in the surrounding present, in which I can intervene purposively, in which I can have goals. A concept of its own is therefore needed: that of an actual present sphere of effect, or of a potential practical environmental present. The practical surrounding world is defined by reach: what I can affect now, a concept of the present measured by possible action rather than perception.

(215) But this present has its practical future. Every ego has its open and yet not infinite horizon of its practical life and, belonging to it, its practical surrounding world for its whole life. In it it has at every moment its practical present, what it could realize practically now, "immediately"; then the concept of the present widens to today, to the next time and what I could do then and what is my practical surrounding world for it, and finally to my whole life. Not all fellow human beings belong to this practical surrounding world, but those to whom I can communicate myself, to whom I can turn somehow in social acts. That I cannot do for the human beings on Mars, nor for all human beings on earth itself. Every other subject of my practical surrounding world has its own practical surrounding world in turn, and that too belongs mediately to my practical surrounding world insofar as I can act through the other. The practical horizon is finite, bounded by a lifetime and by communicability; it extends mediately through others' reach. The "men on Mars" mark the limit of practical community: possible subjects outside any chain of address.

(215–216) Since about the second half of the nineteenth century, earthly humanity forms, by virtue of its organization of state power, a maximally extended practical community: a totality of ego-subjects for whom there is a real possibility of immediate or mediate mutual understanding and practical social effect, in such a way that an extension by new subjects is no longer possible; or, what is the same, subjects connected by a common practical surrounding world, in this case the earthly world (world in the sense of world-history). Such a maximal practical community forms a practical all-community, a personal universe, a human world. Before the nineteenth century there was not one but several human worlds on earth, human all-communities or total communities, and correspondingly different practical worlds. The practical world is the same for every member of a universal practical community and encloses all the other members and the universal practical community itself. It is at the same time subjectivity for this world and belongs to it as practical objectivity, much as a subject considered solitarily includes itself in its practical surrounding world. The subject is for itself at the same time a practical object. Husserl dates the unification of humanity into one practical world to the later nineteenth century, through the global reach of state power: before that there were several "human worlds", each closed. Globalization is described phenomenologically as the closure of a practical all-community, and the plurality of earlier human worlds anticipates the "home-world" and "alien world" of the 1930s (Hua XV).

(216) In a practical all-community every personal life is connected with every other, every consciousness with every other, immediately or mediately. All persons of this community have, or can have, one and the same personal horizon, a personal space, so to speak, as the form of the all-common surrounding world of subjects, and a world of persons as the personal actuality belonging to this form. Every subject can penetrate into this horizon and bring the persons to givenness step by step, advancing from the "nearest" to the "more distant". Each is nearest to himself; he is the zero point of personal orientation in the human world. To the communal stream of life connecting all (a synthetically connected or connectable manifold, or rather totality, of subjective streams, each centred in an ego) belongs the external communal surrounding world as intentional correlate, together with the totality of possible modes of givenness, modes of appearance, of this surrounding world (physical nature, psychophysical nature), but practically reachable, intuitable in modes of experience of just these persons, knowable for them within the frame of their practical possibilities, and thereby determining all actuality of valuing and of real reshaping. "Personal space" with its zero point transfers spatial orientation to the social world: each person is the "here" of a space of persons ordered by nearness and distance. The social world is oriented as the perceptual world is, which grounds Schutz's later concentric zones of consociates, contemporaries, and predecessors.

(216) Into this universe all possible particular forms of community inscribe themselves, all possible particular communities and connections of communities (practical unions and practical strife, enmities), "naturally grown" communities of communication with the unity of a language as a system of communicating signs and connections of signs, suited to bring the unity of a manifold connected sense to communicating expression. Language as the form of communication of a linguistically closed community. Members of such a community can learn other languages and so become members of several such communities of communication, and thereby different such communities can enter into relation with one another at the switching points of persons who master these different languages. But language also belongs in the practical sphere of objects. It can be formed and reformed arbitrarily, and it is itself a communal formation, grown from arbitrary acts of individuals and taken over by tradition. Language communities are closed but connected through multilingual persons, the "switching points" between human worlds. Language is both a natural community and a practical object that can be reshaped, grown from individual acts sedimented in tradition, the dual status of language that Nr. 10 assigned to communities of effect.

(217) There is a narrower community of life and effect within the system of expression, or systematic form of expression, of a single language. A language community is a closer community of life and effect within the human world, defined by its shared expressive system.
